<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> For every irrational angle, we construct a continuous nonnegative circle weight with exactly one zero and logarithmic integral $`-\infty`$ whose weighted rotation has no nontrivial invariant projection in the associated hyperfinite type II<sub>1</sub> factor. This answers negatively the question of Zhu, Fang, and Shi, with the angle prescribed in advance. The resulting operator is nonzero and norm-quasinilpotent.

- **claim-prescribed-angle-weight** (construction, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): For every irrational $\theta\in(0,1)$ there exists a continuous function $f:\mathbb T\to[0,1]$ such that $$f^{-1}(\{0\})=\{1\},\qquad \int_\mathbb T\log f\,dm=-\infty,$$ and every projection $p\in R_\theta$ satisfying $(1-p)Uf(V)p=0$ is either $0$ or $1$.
  - Explanation: The weight is constructed after the irrational angle is prescribed. Its logarithmic integral diverges negatively, and its weighted rotation excludes every nontrivial invariant projection in the containing factor.
- **claim-commutant-transitivity** (classification, [source](#stmt-lem-commutant-algebra)): If $T\in B(\mathcal H)$ is nonscalar, then $\{T\}'$ is a proper, strong-operator closed, unital complex algebra. It is transitive exactly when $T$ has no nontrivial hyperinvariant subspace.
  - Explanation: Nonscalarity makes the commutant proper. Its transitivity is exactly the absence of nontrivial hyperinvariant subspaces.
- **claim-hyperinvariant-projection-membership** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-hyperinvariant-projection)): Let $\mathcal M\subset B(\mathcal H)$ be a von Neumann algebra and $T\in\mathcal M$. The orthogonal projection onto any hyperinvariant subspace for $T$ belongs to $\mathcal M$ and is invariant for $T$.
  - Explanation: Hyperinvariance forces the subspace to reduce commutant unitaries, placing its orthogonal projection in the containing von Neumann algebra.
- **claim-quasinilpotent-transitive-commutant** (construction, [source](#stmt-cor-transitive-commutant)): Every separable infinite-dimensional complex Hilbert space carries a nonzero norm-quasinilpotent operator with no nontrivial hyperinvariant subspace. Its commutant is a proper strongly closed transitive unital algebra.
  - Explanation: The factor construction supplies a nonzero norm-quasinilpotent operator without nontrivial hyperinvariant subspaces, and unitary equivalence transfers it to every Hilbert space in the stated class.
- **claim-scalar-sampling-estimates** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-scalar-sampling-estimates)): Let $m\ge200$ be an integer, put $h=m^{-3/5}$, and let $u=w_m^{(r)}$ for either $r=1$ or $r=2$. Write $\bar u(s)=\int_0^1u(s,v)\,dv$. Uniformly in $s$, the supremum plus total variation of $v\mapsto u(s,v)$ is $O(m^{13/5})$, hence $O(m^{10})$, and $$\label{eq:kernel-estimates} \begin{aligned} &\\|\bar u\\|_\infty\le2,\qquad \int_0^1\bar u=1-2m^{-2},\\ &(1-2m^{-2})^2\le\int_0^1\bar u^2 \le1+\frac{h^2}{(1-h)^2},\qquad \iint_{[0,1]^2}u^2=\frac2h\log\frac{m^2}{2}. \end{aligned}$$* *For the sampling estimate let $0<N_0\le N\le m^2$, $Q=m^{(7/10)m}$ and $H=m^{300}$. Let $\lambda$ be uniform on $[1/N_0,2/N_0]$. If $n\in[Q/2,Q]\cap\mathbb N$, the density $p_n$ of $\{n\lambda\}$ satisfies $$\label{eq:scalar-marginal} \|p_n-1\|\le N_0/n\quad\hbox{almost everywhere}.$$ If two distinct such powers $n,n'$ satisfy $$\|vn+v'n'\|>Nm^{1000} \quad(0<\|v\|+\|v'\|,\ \|v\|,\|v'\|\le H,\ v,v'\in\mathbb Z),$$ then, for intervals $I,J\subset[0,1)$, $$\label{eq:scalar-rectangles} \left\|\mathbb P(\{n\lambda\}\in I,\{n'\lambda\}\in J) -\|I\|\|J\|\right\|=O(m^{-80}).$$ Consequently, for complex functions $f,g$ of bounded variation on $[0,1]$, with $B(f)=\\|f\\|_\infty+\operatorname{Var}(f)$, $$\label{eq:scalar-bv} \left\|\mathbb E[f(\{n\lambda\})g(\{n'\lambda\})] -\left(\int_0^1f\right)\left(\int_0^1g\right)\right\| \le C m^{-80}B(f)B(g).$$ All implicit constants are absolute, for sufficiently large $m$, and uniform in $N_0,N,n,n'$ and the intervals or functions.
  - Explanation: The kernels have controlled marginal mass, square integrals and variation. Separated powers give rectangle and bounded-variation discrepancy estimates without requiring a joint density.
- **claim-finite-convolution-lower-bound** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-polynomial-coefficient)): For every integer $K\ge0$ and $D\ge\delta>0$ there is $\varepsilon=\varepsilon(K,D,\delta)>0$ such that complex Laurent polynomials $A,B$ supported in $[-K,K]$, with coefficients of modulus at most $D$ and $\|A_0\|,\|B_0\|\ge\delta$, satisfy $$\max_{0\le n\le2K}\|(AB)_{-n}\|\ge\varepsilon.$$
  - Explanation: Nonvanishing central coefficients force a nonzero product coefficient in the indicated window. Compactness makes its lower bound uniform over the bounded admissible polynomial pairs.
- **claim-prescribed-angle-parameters** (construction, [source](#stmt-prop-parameters)): For the fixed $\theta$ there are positive integers $m_j,K_j,G_j,q_i$, positive real numbers $L_j,C_j,\varepsilon_j$, and consecutive blocks $J_j=\{d_{j-1}+1,\ldots,d_j\}$ of size $m_j$, with $d_0=0$, having the following properties. Set $$\begin{gathered} q_0=1,\qquad u_i(x)=\{q_ix\},\qquad\alpha_i=\{q_i\theta\},\\ \kappa_j=(100j)^{-1},\qquad N_j=m_jK_j,\\ a_j=G_j/m_j,\qquad b_j=m_j^{-19/20},\qquad Q_j=m_j^{(7/10)m_j}. \end{gathered}$$ The frequencies are strictly increasing and divisible, and $$\label{eq:phases} \frac1{N_j}<\alpha_i<\frac2{N_j},\qquad q_i\mid q_{i+1},\qquad \frac{q_{i+1}}{q_i}\ge N_j \quad(i\in J_j).$$ For every integer $k$ with $N_j/4\le \|k\|\le m_j^{(4/5)m_j}$, at least $m_j/20$ indices in $J_j$ satisfy $$\label{eq:spread} 1-\{k\alpha_i\}\in[b_j,1-b_j].$$ If $G_jK_j\le \|k\|\le N_j/4$, every index in $J_j$ has $1-\{k\alpha_i\}\in[a_j,1-a_j]$.* *The initial values are $K_1=100,G_1=1$. The numbers $L_j\ge1$ are nondecreasing, $C_j=\max(L_j,K_j^2)$, and the sizes satisfy $$\begin{aligned} \label{eq:scales} &m_j\ge\max(K_j,2^j,4G_j,200),\qquad K_{j+1}=\lceil m_j^{(3/4)m_j}\rceil>2K_j,\notag\\ &G_{j+1}K_{j+1}<m_j^{(4/5)m_j},\qquad \kappa_jm_j^{1/40}\ge L_j,\qquad \kappa_{j+1}\sqrt{G_{j+1}}\ge L_j,\notag\\ &h_{j+1}^*/K_{j+1}\le2^{-j}. \end{aligned}$$ Put $D_j=\exp((K_j+1)\sum_{s<j}C_sm_s)$. If $A,B$ are Laurent polynomials supported in $[-K_j,K_j]$, every coefficient has modulus at most $D_j$, and $\|A_0\|,\|B_0\|\ge1/j$, then $$\label{eq:polynomial} \max_{0\le n\le2K_j}\|(AB)_{-n}\|\ge\varepsilon_j, \qquad (2K_j+1)3e^{-L_j/10000}\le\varepsilon_j/j.$$* *Finally put $p_*=d_{j-1}$ and $\ell_j=((j+1)^2(1+p_*)q_{p_*})^{-1}$. For every $t=r/q_{p_*}$ with $r\in\mathbb Z$, $0\le r<q_{p_*}$, there is a finite nonempty set $\mathcal N_{j,t}$ of positive integers in $[Q_j/2,Q_j]$, with $\{n\theta-t\}\in(0,\ell_j)$, such that for $r'=1,2$, $$\label{eq:sampling} \left\\|\frac1{\|\mathcal N_{j,t}\|}\sum_{n\in\mathcal N_{j,t}} \prod_{i\in J_j}w_{m_j}^{(r')}(u_i(x),\{n\alpha_i\})-1 \right\\|_{L^2(\mu)}<\frac1j.$$
  - Explanation: Successive choices provide divisible frequencies, spread ranges, attenuation costs and finite sampling sets at the unchanged irrational angle.
- **claim-scalar-multiplier-field-decomposition** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-scalar-multiplier-fields)): Let $(Y,\nu)$ be a probability space, let $K=\ell^2(\mathbb Z)$, and let $A\in B(L^2(Y,\nu;K))$ commute with every scalar multiplication operator $M_g\zeta(y)=g(y)\zeta(y)$, $g\in L^\infty(Y,\nu)$. There is a field $A(y)\in B(K)$, unique almost everywhere, such that $$(A\zeta)(y)=A(y)\zeta(y),\qquad \\|A(y)\\|\le\\|A\\|\quad\text{almost everywhere}.$$ The field is measurable in the sense that $y\mapsto A(y)v$ is measurable for every $v\in K$. Products and adjoints of such operators are computed fiberwise. In particular, an orthogonal projection is represented by orthogonal projections outside one null set.
  - Explanation: Commutation with scalar multiplication yields an essentially bounded measurable operator field, with fiberwise products, adjoints and projection identities.
- **claim-strict-central-diagonal** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-positive-diagonal)): Let $\sigma$ be an invertible ergodic probability-preserving transformation of $(Y,\nu)$. On $L^2(Y,\nu;\ell^2(\mathbb Z))$, define $(U\zeta)(y)=S\zeta(y)$ and $\pi(a)(y)e_k=a(\sigma^ky)e_k$, where $Se_k=e_{k+1}$. Suppose a von Neumann algebra $M$ containing $U$ and $\pi(L^\infty(Y))$ consists of measurable fields with covariance $A(\sigma y)=S^{-1}A(y)S$ and has faithful normal tracial state $$\tau(A)=\int_Y\langle A(y)e_0,e_0\rangle\,d\nu(y).$$ Let $f\in L^\infty(Y,\nu)$ be positive almost everywhere, put $T=U\pi(f)$, and let $p\in M$ be an orthogonal projection with $(1-p)Tp=0$. If $0<\tau(p)<1$, then $0<\langle p(y)e_0,e_0\rangle<1$ almost everywhere.
  - Explanation: Positive weights propagate the extreme diagonal events along the transformation. Ergodicity and intermediate trace exclude either event from having full measure; this is pointwise strictness, not a uniform separation from the endpoints.
- **claim-weak-limit-identity-transfer** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-compact-transfer)): Let $X$ be a compact metric space with Borel probability measure $\mu$. Let $p(x)$ be a Borel field of orthogonal projections on $\ell^2(\mathbb Z)$, meaning that every column $x\mapsto p(x)e_k$ is Borel, and let $F_k:X\to(0,\infty)$, $k\in\mathbb Z$, be finite Borel functions. Suppose finite positive Borel measures $\beta_l$ on $X^2$ converge weakly to $\beta$. Assume that both families of marginals of $\beta_l$ are uniformly absolutely continuous with respect to $\mu$, and that both marginals of $\beta$ are absolutely continuous. Suppose also that some $0<B<\infty$ satisfies $$F_k(y)/F_k(x)\le B\quad(k\in\mathbb Z)$$ almost everywhere for each $\beta_l$ and for $\beta$. If $$\label{eq:compact-transfer-identity} (1-p(y))\operatorname{diag}_k\bigl(F_k(y)/F_k(x)\bigr)p(x)=0$$ holds $\beta_l$-almost everywhere for every $l$, then it holds $\beta$-almost everywhere.
  - Explanation: Uniform absolute continuity and a common ratio cap allow measurable projection identities to survive weak convergence. Simultaneous compact-set approximation supplies the needed continuity.
- **claim-rational-translation-identities** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-rational-graphs)): Let $p\in M$ be a projection satisfying $(1-p)T_*p=0$. For every integer $A\ge1$ and every $t\in X$ that is an integer multiple of $1/q_A$, the identity $$\label{eq:rational-graph-identity} (1-p(x+t))\operatorname{diag}_v R_{x,x+t}(v)p(x)=0$$ holds for almost every $x$. The exceptional sets may be chosen simultaneously for this countable family of translations.
  - Explanation: Sampling and trimming extend the positive-orbit projection identity to the specified rational translations, with a common exceptional null set for the countable family.
- **claim-product-density-energy-bound** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-product-density-bound)): Let $(\Omega_i,\mathcal F_i,\mu_i)$, $i\ge1$, be probability spaces and let $h_i\ge0$ satisfy $\int h_i\,d\mu_i=1$. If $$\sum_{i\ge1}\\|h_i-1\\|_{L^2(\mu_i)}^2\le C<\infty,$$ then the product probability measure $\nu=\bigotimes_{i\ge1}(h_i\mu_i)$ has a density with respect to $\mu=\bigotimes_{i\ge1}\mu_i$ whose $L^2(\mu)$ norm is at most $e^{C/2}$.* *In particular, suppose two probability densities $h_i^0,h_i^1$ at each coordinate satisfy $$\\|h_i^a-1\\|_{L^2(\mu_i)}^2\le b_i\quad(a=0,1), \qquad \sum_{i\ge1}b_i\le C.$$ For every deterministic sequence $\epsilon_i\in\{0,1\}$, the product law with coordinate densities $h_i^{\epsilon_i}$ has the same bound.
  - Explanation: Summable squared coordinate density deviations bound the square-integrable density of the infinite product, including deterministic selections between alternative coordinate marginals.
- **claim-digit-marginal-uniformity** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-digit-marginals)): There is a finite constant $H_0$, independent of $j$, such that the marginal laws of $x$ and $y$ have densities with respect to $\mu$ of $L^2(\mu)$ norm at most $H_0$. The same assertion holds for an endpoint formed by any deterministic choice of the $x$-digit or the $y$-digit at each coordinate, retaining the common uniform initial digit.
  - Explanation: The independent digit construction gives uniformly square-integrable endpoint densities, also for deterministic hybrid endpoints.
- **claim-ordered-circle-pair-identity** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-ordered-couplings)): Let $p\in M$ be a projection satisfying $(1-p)T_*p=0$. For every $j$, $$\label{eq:coupling-identity} (1-p(y))\operatorname{diag}_{k\in\mathbb Z}\bigl(R_{x,y}(k)\bigr)p(x)=0 \quad\text{for }\mathbb P_j\text{-almost every pair}.$$ Moreover $0<R_{x,y}(k)\le1$ for all $k\in\mathbb Z$ almost surely.
  - Explanation: Finite digit replacements are rational translations. Their limit preserves the projection identity because hybrid marginal densities stay controlled and coordinate order caps every ratio.
- **claim-conditional-digit-crossings** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-conditional-labels)): Conditional on $x$, all coordinate labels are independent and each posterior depends only on the corresponding digit $A_i$. The analogous statement holds conditional on $y$, using $B_i$.* *For $i\in J_s$, $w\in\{a_s,b_s\}$, and $u\in[w,1-w]$, the following bounds hold almost surely: $$\begin{aligned} \mathbb P_j(\text{top label of width }w\text{ at }i\mid x) &\ge\rho_s(w)/10 &&\text{if }u_i(x)<u, \label{eq:conditional-crossing}\\ \mathbb P_j(\text{bottom label of width }w\text{ at }i\mid y) &\ge\rho_s(w)/10 &&\text{if }u_i(y)\ge u. \label{eq:conditional-crossing-bottom} \end{aligned}$$ In the first case that label forces $u_i(y)\ge u$; in the second it forces $u_i(x)<u$.
  - Explanation: Conditioning on an entire endpoint fixes its digits without destroying independence of the coordinate label posteriors. Eligible top or bottom labels then force threshold crossings.
- **claim-conditional-convolution-obstruction** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-conditional-convolution)): Let $K\ge1$ be an integer, $D\ge a>0$, $c>0$, and $\eta,\delta\ge0$. On a probability space with endpoints $x,y$, let $\xi(x),\gamma(y)$ be measurable vectors in the unit ball of $\ell^2(\mathbb Z)$, and let $F_k$ be positive finite measurable functions of an endpoint, with $F_0=1$. Set $$A_v=\frac{\xi_v(x)}{F_v(x)},\qquad B_w=F_{-w}(y)\overline{\gamma_{-w}(y)},\qquad R(k)=\frac{F_k(y)}{F_k(x)}.$$ Suppose, almost surely, for $0\le n\le2K$ and every $v\in\mathbb Z$, $$\label{eq:generic-convolution} \sum_{v\in\mathbb Z}A_vB_{-n-v}=0,\qquad 0<\frac{F_{n+v}(y)}{F_v(x)} \le\min\{R(v),R(n+v)\}\le1.$$ Let $E$ be an event of probability at least $c$ on which $$\|A_v\|,\|B_v\|\le D\quad(\|v\|\le K),\qquad \|A_0\|,\|B_0\|\ge a.$$ For each $\|k\|>K$, suppose there are events $H_k^{(1)},H_k^{(2)}$ with $$\label{eq:generic-tail} R(k)\le\eta+\mathbf1_{H_k^{(1)}}+\mathbf1_{H_k^{(2)}}\quad\text{on }E, \qquad \mathbb P(H_k^{(1)}\mid x)\le\delta,\quad \mathbb P(H_k^{(2)}\mid y)\le\delta.$$ The conditional bounds are under the full probability law, before restriction to $E$. If $\varepsilon(K,D,a)$ is the positive constant from Lemma <a href="#lem:polynomial-coefficient" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:polynomial-coefficient">7</a>, then $$\label{eq:generic-obstruction} c\varepsilon(K,D,a)\le(2K+1)(\eta+2\sqrt\delta).$$
  - Explanation: A positive-probability finite coefficient lower bound cannot exceed the attenuated tail budget. Complementary endpoint conditioning controls the infinite masked sums without summing exceptional probabilities.
- **claim-measurable-projection-exclusion** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-measurable)): The bounded operator $T_*=U\pi(f_*)$ has no projection $p\in M$ satisfying $0<\tau(p)<1$ and $(1-p)T_*p=0$.
  - Explanation: The constructed measurable weighted rotation has no invariant projection of intermediate trace in its containing factor.
- **claim-positive-event-truncation** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-truncation-bound)): There is a constant $c_*>0$ such that, for all sufficiently large $j$, $$\label{eq:finite-lower-bound} \mathbb P_j(E_j)\ge c_*,\qquad \mathbb E_j\left[\mathbf 1_{E_j} \sum_{n=0}^{2K_j}\|c_{j,n}\|\right]\ge c_*\varepsilon_j.$$ The point after which these inequalities hold may depend on $p$.
  - Explanation: A tagged admissible block has positive probability, while future exclusions and extreme diagonal events lose vanishing mass. Polynomial compactness then gives the expected finite convolution lower bound.
- **claim-circle-tail-attenuation** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-tail-attenuation)): For every $j$ and every integer $k$ with $\|k\|>K_j$, there are measurable events $H_k^{(1)},H_k^{(2)}$ in the labelled coupling space such that $$\label{eq:conditional-bad-events} \mathbb P_j(H_k^{(1)}\mid x)\le e^{-L_j/400},\qquad \mathbb P_j(H_k^{(2)}\mid y)\le e^{-L_j/400} \quad\text{almost surely},$$ and on $E_j$, $$\label{eq:tail-ratio-bound} R_{x,y}(k)\le e^{-L_j} +\mathbf 1_{H_k^{(1)}}+\mathbf 1_{H_k^{(2)}}.$$ The conditional bounds hold before any restriction to $E_j$.
  - Explanation: Tags attenuate the near tail. Spread ranges cover the remaining indices, and complementary endpoint conditioning makes the no-crossing exceptions uniformly small under the original law.
- **claim-affiliated-trace-preserving-transport** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-affiliated-transport)): Let $(\mathcal M,\tau)$ be a finite von Neumann algebra acting on a Hilbert space, with faithful normal tracial state $\tau$. Let $H$ be an injective positive self-adjoint operator affiliated with $\mathcal M$, and let $A,B\in\mathcal M$. Suppose $$A(\operatorname{Dom}H)\subseteq\operatorname{Dom}H, \qquad HA\zeta=BH\zeta\quad(\zeta\in\operatorname{Dom}H).$$ For every projection $p\in\mathcal M$ with $A\operatorname{ran}p\subseteq \operatorname{ran}p$, the projection $P$ onto $$\overline{H(\operatorname{ran}p\cap\operatorname{Dom}H)}$$ belongs to $\mathcal M$, is invariant under $B$, and satisfies $\tau(P)=\tau(p)$.
  - Explanation: An injective positive affiliated intertwiner transports invariant ranges after taking the appropriate domain intersection and closure. Spectral cutoffs and tracial dimension estimates preserve the projection trace.
- **claim-continuous-weight-quasinilpotence** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cor-quasinilpotent)): The constructed operator $T_f$ is nonzero and quasinilpotent.
  - Explanation: Uniform irrational-rotation averaging of truncated continuous logarithms makes the operator power norms decay at every prescribed exponential rate.
- **claim-product-rotation-realization** (construction, [source](#stmt-p-thm-model)): There are angles $0<\theta_i<1$ and costs $c_i>0$, with $\sum_i\theta_i<\infty$ and $1,\theta_1,\theta_2,\ldots$ rationally independent, such that the measurable weight $$f(x)=\exp\left(-\sum_{i\ge1}c_i\lfloor x_i+\theta_i\rfloor\right)$$ is positive almost everywhere and has $\int_X\log f\,d\mu=-\infty$. The algebra $M$ is a type $\mathrm{II}_1$ factor with separable predual, and $T=U\pi(f)$ is nonzero and norm-quasinilpotent, with $\Delta(T)=0$. Every projection $p\in M$ satisfying $(1-p)Tp=0$ is $0$ or $1$.* *Moreover, $\|T\|$ has pure-point spectral type. The abelian algebra generated by any finite family of polar conjugates $U^n\|T\|U^{-n}$ is atomic, whereas $W^*(U^n\|T\|U^{-n}:n\in\mathbb Z)$ is diffuse.
  - Explanation: Independent product coordinates give another nonzero norm-quasinilpotent weighted rotation without nontrivial factor-invariant projections. Finite polar-conjugate families generate atomic algebras, while the full orbit algebra is diffuse.
- **claim-product-phase-ranges** (bound, [source](#stmt-p-prop-phases)): Almost surely, for every sufficiently large $j$ the following hold simultaneously for all integers $k$ in the indicated ranges, with $m=m_j$:* 1. *If $G_jK_j\le \|k\|\le N_j/4$, then every $i\in J_j$ satisfies $$1-\{k\theta_i\}\in[a_j,1-a_j].$$* 2. *If $N_j/4\le \|k\|\le m^{(4/5)m}$, then at least $m/20$ coordinates $i\in J_j$ satisfy $$1-\{k\theta_i\}\in[b_j,1-b_j].$$* *The first assertion in fact holds at every stage and for every realization of the angles in their prescribed intervals.
  - Explanation: The smaller phase range is controlled deterministically. Independence and a summable failure estimate supply the larger range simultaneously at all sufficiently late stages.
- **claim-product-ordered-orbit-sampling** (construction, [source](#stmt-p-prop-sampling)): With probability one in the choice of $\theta$, the following holds for every finite $I\subset\mathbb N$. There are positive finite Borel measures $\eta_{I,l}$ on $X\times X$ converging weakly to $\lambda_I$, each carried by ordered pairs of the form $$(x,y)=(x,\sigma_\theta^n x)$$ with $n$ in a finite set of positive integers. Both marginals of $\eta_{I,l}$ have $L^2(\mu)$ densities, and their $L^2$ norms are bounded uniformly in $l$. The bound may depend on $I$ and on the fixed realization of $\theta$. Weak convergence here means convergence against every continuous function on the compact space $X\times X$.
  - Explanation: A single probability-one angle event supplies weak approximation of every finite-coordinate triangular law by ordered positive orbit pairs, with uniformly square-integrable endpoint marginals.
- **claim-product-factor-field-characterization** (classification, [source](#stmt-p-lem-factor)): The algebra $M$ is a factor of type $\mathrm{II}_1$ with separable predual. Its elements are essentially bounded measurable fields $H(x)\in\mathcal B(\mathcal K)$ satisfying $$\label{p:eq:covariance} H(\sigma x)=S^{-1}H(x)S\quad\text{almost everywhere},$$ and its faithful normal tracial state is $$\tau(H)=\int_X H(x)_{0,0}\,d\mu(x).$$
  - Explanation: The crossed product is a finite factor with separable predual. Covariant measurable fields characterize its elements, and integration of the central entry gives its faithful normal trace.
- **claim-product-norm-quasinilpotence** (theorem, [source](#stmt-p-lem-quasinilpotent)): The weight satisfies $\int_X\log f\,d\mu=-\infty$ and $\Delta(T)=0$. The operator $T$ is nonzero and norm-quasinilpotent: $\lim_{n\to\infty}\\|T^n\\|^{1/n}=0$.
  - Explanation: Finite prefixes give uniform upper bounds on power norms whose exponential rates tend to zero as more blocks are retained. The same divergent cost calculation gives the logarithmic integral and determinant.
- **claim-triangular-pair-projection-identity** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-p-prop-pair-identity)): Suppose $p\in M$ is a projection and $(1-p)Tp=0$. For every finite $I\subset\mathbb N$, $$\label{p:eq:pair-identity} (1-p(y))\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}_{k\in\mathbb Z}\bigl(R_{x,y}(k)\bigr)p(x)=0 \quad\text{for }\lambda_I\text{-almost every }(x,y).$$
  - Explanation: The orbit identity passes to each finite-coordinate triangular law using ordered sampling, marginal absolute continuity and a common ratio cap.
- **claim-product-coupling-marginals-crossings** (bound, [source](#stmt-p-lem-coupling-marginals)): Put $$\label{p:eq:uniform-product-density} H^2=\exp\left(20\left(2\sum_{s\ge1}\kappa_s^2+1\right)\right)<\infty.$$ For every $j\ge1$, the law just constructed has the following properties.* 1. *Every one-coordinate endpoint density is at most $10$. Both marginals of $\nu_j$ have $L^2(\mu)$ densities of norm at most $H$. The same bound holds for the product law formed by any deterministic choice of the $x$-marginal or the $y$-marginal at each coordinate.* 2. *Conditional on $x$, the coordinates $y_i$ are independent. Conditional on $y$, the coordinates $x_i$ are independent. If $i\in J_s$, $w=a_s$ or $b_s$, and $u\in[w,1-w]$, then $$\begin{aligned} \mathbb P_j(x_i<u\le y_i\mid x)&\ge\rho_s(w)/10 &&\text{on }\{x_i<u\},\label{p:eq:crossing-given-x}\\ \mathbb P_j(x_i<u\le y_i\mid y)&\ge\rho_s(w)/10 &&\text{on }\{y_i\ge u\}. \label{p:eq:crossing-given-y} \end{aligned}$$ The assertions hold almost surely, simultaneously for any specified countable family of such thresholds.
  - Explanation: The biased independent-coordinate mixture controls both endpoint densities and deterministic hybrids. Explicit conditional kernels preserve coordinate independence and force eligible crossings.
- **claim-biased-product-pair-identity** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-p-prop-biased-identity)): Let $p\in M$ be an orthogonal projection satisfying $(1-p)Tp=0$ in the product model. For every $j\ge1$, $$\label{p:eq:biased-identity} (1-p(y))\operatorname{diag}_{k\in\mathbb Z}\bigl(R_{x,y}(k)\bigr)p(x)=0 \qquad\text{for }\nu_j\text{-almost every }(x,y).$$ Moreover, $0<R_{x,y}(k)\le1$ for all $k\in\mathbb Z$ almost surely.
  - Explanation: Finite prefix replacement laws are dominated by triangular laws with an explicit biased tail density. Uniform hybrid marginal control then transfers the identity to the full coupling.
- **claim-finite-atomic-full-diffuse-orbit** (classification, [source](#stmt-p-prop-joint-structure)): For the operator $T$ constructed above, its polar decomposition is $T=U\|T\|$, with $\|T\|=\pi(f)$ and $\ker\|T\|=\{0\}$. The spectral measure of $\|T\|$ is countably atomic. For every finite $F\subset\mathbb Z$, the abelian von Neumann algebra $$\mathcal A_F=W^*(U^n\|T\|U^{-n}:n\in F)$$ is atomic. The full algebra $$\mathcal A=W^*(U^n\|T\|U^{-n}:n\in\mathbb Z)$$ is diffuse.
  - Explanation: The weight has countable essential range, so finite tuples of polar conjugates give atomic joint level sets. Total ergodicity and nonconstancy exclude atoms from the full orbit algebra.

          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-commutant-algebra</summary>\n\n*Proof.* The commutation equation is preserved by addition, multiplication and scalar multiplication, and the identity commutes with $T$. If $A_\\lambda\\to A$ strongly and $A_\\lambda T=TA_\\lambda$, then for every $\\zeta\\in\\mathcal H$ both sides converge, giving $AT\\zeta=TA\\zeta$. Thus the algebra is strongly closed. If every bounded operator commuted with $T$, then every rank-one orthogonal projection would commute with $T$, so every nonzero vector would be an eigenvector. Applying this to $x,y,x+y$ for linearly independent vectors shows that the eigenvalues are equal; hence $T$ would be scalar. Properness follows. The last assertion is precisely the two definitions. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "If $T\\in B(\\mathcal H)$ is nonscalar, then $\\{T\\}'$ is a proper, strong-operator closed, unital complex algebra. It is transitive exactly when $T$ has no nontrivial hyperinvariant subspace.",
      "explanation": "Nonscalarity makes the commutant proper. Its transitivity is exactly the absence of nontrivial hyperinvariant subspaces.",

          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-hyperinvariant-projection",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-hyperinvariant-projection</summary>\n\n*Proof.* Every unitary $W\\in\\mathcal M'$ and its inverse commute with $T$. Hyperinvariance therefore makes the subspace reducing for $W$. Its orthogonal projection commutes with all such unitaries and hence belongs to $\\mathcal M''=\\mathcal M$. Since $T\\in\\{T\\}'$, its range is invariant under $T$. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-cor-transitive-commutant",
        "exact_quote": "Every separable infinite-dimensional complex Hilbert space carries a nonzero norm-quasinilpotent operator with no nontrivial hyperinvariant subspace. Its commutant is a proper strongly closed transitive unital algebra.",
        "source_id": "PM-293.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-cor-transitive-commutant",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-cor-transitive-commutant</summary>\n\n*Proof.* The tracial representation of $R_\\theta$ is separable and infinite-dimensional. Apply Theorem <a href=\"#thm:main\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"thm:main\">1</a>, Corollary <a href=\"#cor:quasinilpotent\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"cor:quasinilpotent\">22</a>, and Lemma <a href=\"#lem:hyperinvariant-projection\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:hyperinvariant-projection\">3</a> there. A nonzero quasinilpotent operator is nonscalar, so Lemma <a href=\"#lem:commutant-algebra\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:commutant-algebra\">2</a> applies. Unitary equivalence transfers these properties to any separable infinite-dimensional complex Hilbert space. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-scalar-sampling-estimates",
        "exact_quote": "Let $m\\ge200$ be an integer, put $h=m^{-3/5}$, and let $u=w_m^{(r)}$ for either $r=1$ or $r=2$. Write $\\bar u(s)=\\int_0^1u(s,v)\\,dv$. Uniformly in $s$, the supremum plus total variation of $v\\mapsto u(s,v)$ is $O(m^{13/5})$, hence $O(m^{10})$, and $$\\label{eq:kernel-estimates}\n \\begin{aligned}\n &\\|\\bar u\\|_\\infty\\le2,\\qquad\n   \\int_0^1\\bar u=1-2m^{-2},\\\\\n &(1-2m^{-2})^2\\le\\int_0^1\\bar u^2\n       \\le1+\\frac{h^2}{(1-h)^2},\\qquad\n   \\iint_{[0,1]^2}u^2=\\frac2h\\log\\frac{m^2}{2}.\n \\end{aligned}$$*\n\n\n\n\n*For the sampling estimate let $0<N_0\\le N\\le m^2$, $Q=m^{(7/10)m}$ and $H=m^{300}$. Let $\\lambda$ be uniform on $[1/N_0,2/N_0]$. If $n\\in[Q/2,Q]\\cap\\mathbb N$, the density $p_n$ of $\\{n\\lambda\\}$ satisfies $$\\label{eq:scalar-marginal}\n |p_n-1|\\le N_0/n\\quad\\hbox{almost everywhere}.$$ If two distinct such powers $n,n'$ satisfy $$|vn+v'n'|>Nm^{1000}\n \\quad(0<|v|+|v'|,\\ |v|,|v'|\\le H,\\ v,v'\\in\\mathbb Z),$$ then, for intervals $I,J\\subset[0,1)$, $$\\label{eq:scalar-rectangles}\n \\left|\\mathbb P(\\{n\\lambda\\}\\in I,\\{n'\\lambda\\}\\in J)\n             -|I||J|\\right|=O(m^{-80}).$$ Consequently, for complex functions $f,g$ of bounded variation on $[0,1]$, with $B(f)=\\|f\\|_\\infty+\\operatorname{Var}(f)$, $$\\label{eq:scalar-bv}\n \\left|\\mathbb E[f(\\{n\\lambda\\})g(\\{n'\\lambda\\})]\n           -\\left(\\int_0^1f\\right)\\left(\\int_0^1g\\right)\\right|\n       \\le C m^{-80}B(f)B(g).$$ All implicit constants are absolute, for sufficiently large $m$, and uniform in $N_0,N,n,n'$ and the intervals or functions.",
        "source_id": "PM-293.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-scalar-sampling-estimates",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-scalar-sampling-estimates</summary>\n\n*Proof.* For the first orientation the function of $v$ is a constant height at most $m^{13/5}$ on an interval, so the variation bound is immediate. For the second, nonzero values require $v<s$ in representatives $s,v\\in[0,1)$: if $v>s$, then $\\Delta(s-v)\\le v-s<v$, whereas $h<1$. On $0<v<s$, the conditions $\\Delta(s-v)\\ge m^{-2}$ and $v<h\\Delta(s-v)$ describe an interval. The reciprocal of $\\Delta(s-v)$ has at most two monotone pieces there, with the same height bound. This proves the asserted uniform bound in both orientations.\n\nIn the first orientation $\\bar u$ is the cutoff indicator, and $$\\iint u^2=\\frac2h\\int_{m^{-2}}^{1/2}\\frac{ds}{s}\n          =\\frac2h\\log(m^2/2).$$ The change $(s,v)\\mapsto(s-v,v)$ preserves double integrals, giving the same total mass and square integral for the second orientation. To bound its averaged density, first omit the cutoff. The resulting density $d$ is the law of $z+t\\Delta(z)$ for independent uniform $z\\in[0,1)$ and $t\\in(0,h)$. For fixed $t$ this is an increasing piecewise-linear bijection of $[0,1]$, with slopes $1+t$ and $1-t$. Thus $\\int d=1$ and $|d-1|\\le h/(1-h)$, whence $$\\int d^2=1+\\int(d-1)^2\\le1+\\frac{h^2}{(1-h)^2}.$$ Restoring the cutoff only decreases this nonnegative density; also $d\\le(1-h)^{-1}\\le2$. These observations give the upper bounds in <a href=\"#eq:kernel-estimates\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:kernel-estimates\">[eq:kernel-estimates]</a>, while Cauchy–Schwarz gives the lower bound from its exact mass. The cancellation of the linear term here is essential when multiplying estimates over $m$ coordinates.\n\nPeriodizing a uniform interval of length $n/N_0$ gives <a href=\"#eq:scalar-marginal\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:scalar-marginal\">[eq:scalar-marginal]</a>; in particular $p_n\\le2$ for large $m$. For the separated powers and any permitted nonzero frequency pair, direct integration on $[1/N_0,2/N_0]$ gives $$\\left|\\mathbb E e^{2\\pi i(vn+v'n')\\lambda}\\right|\n \\le\\frac{N_0}{\\pi|vn+v'n'|}\\le\\frac1\\pi m^{-1000}.$$ To pass from characters to rectangles, convolve each interval indicator with the Fejér kernel $$F_H(s)=\\frac1H\\left(\\frac{\\sin(\\pi Hs)}{\\sin(\\pi s)}\\right)^2.$$ Expanding the squared geometric sum shows that $F_H$ is nonnegative, has mass one, and has frequencies of magnitude below $H$. The convolved indicators lie in $[0,1]$, preserve their means, and have Fourier coefficients of modulus at most one. Outside strips of width $\\eta=m^{-80}$ about interval endpoints, their error is $O((H\\eta)^{-1})$, by integrating the kernel tail. The marginal bound $p_n,p_{n'}\\le2$ shows that replacing both indicators in a product costs $O(\\eta+(H\\eta)^{-1})$, both for the actual joint law and for Haar product measure. The remaining Fourier error is $O(H^2m^{-1000})$. This proves <a href=\"#eq:scalar-rectangles\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:scalar-rectangles\">[eq:scalar-rectangles]</a>; no joint density, or independence of the sampled powers, is used.\n\nFinally, a bounded-variation function is, up to a null set, a constant plus an integral of indicators of intervals $[a,1)$ against its complex Stieltjes measure. The constant and measure have total size at most $B(f)$; the same holds for $g$. Apply <a href=\"#eq:scalar-rectangles\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:scalar-rectangles\">[eq:scalar-rectangles]</a> to the resulting products of interval indicators, allowing the full interval. Integration against total variation gives <a href=\"#eq:scalar-bv\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:scalar-bv\">[eq:scalar-bv]</a>. Changes at the countably many possible discontinuities do not matter, because the marginals have densities. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Let $m\\ge200$ be an integer, put $h=m^{-3/5}$, and let $u=w_m^{(r)}$ for either $r=1$ or $r=2$. Write $\\bar u(s)=\\int_0^1u(s,v)\\,dv$. Uniformly in $s$, the supremum plus total variation of $v\\mapsto u(s,v)$ is $O(m^{13/5})$, hence $O(m^{10})$, and $$\\label{eq:kernel-estimates}\n \\begin{aligned}\n &\\|\\bar u\\|_\\infty\\le2,\\qquad\n   \\int_0^1\\bar u=1-2m^{-2},\\\\\n &(1-2m^{-2})^2\\le\\int_0^1\\bar u^2\n       \\le1+\\frac{h^2}{(1-h)^2},\\qquad\n   \\iint_{[0,1]^2}u^2=\\frac2h\\log\\frac{m^2}{2}.\n \\end{aligned}$$*\n\n\n\n\n*For the sampling estimate let $0<N_0\\le N\\le m^2$, $Q=m^{(7/10)m}$ and $H=m^{300}$. Let $\\lambda$ be uniform on $[1/N_0,2/N_0]$. If $n\\in[Q/2,Q]\\cap\\mathbb N$, the density $p_n$ of $\\{n\\lambda\\}$ satisfies $$\\label{eq:scalar-marginal}\n |p_n-1|\\le N_0/n\\quad\\hbox{almost everywhere}.$$ If two distinct such powers $n,n'$ satisfy $$|vn+v'n'|>Nm^{1000}\n \\quad(0<|v|+|v'|,\\ |v|,|v'|\\le H,\\ v,v'\\in\\mathbb Z),$$ then, for intervals $I,J\\subset[0,1)$, $$\\label{eq:scalar-rectangles}\n \\left|\\mathbb P(\\{n\\lambda\\}\\in I,\\{n'\\lambda\\}\\in J)\n             -|I||J|\\right|=O(m^{-80}).$$ Consequently, for complex functions $f,g$ of bounded variation on $[0,1]$, with $B(f)=\\|f\\|_\\infty+\\operatorname{Var}(f)$, $$\\label{eq:scalar-bv}\n \\left|\\mathbb E[f(\\{n\\lambda\\})g(\\{n'\\lambda\\})]\n           -\\left(\\int_0^1f\\right)\\left(\\int_0^1g\\right)\\right|\n       \\le C m^{-80}B(f)B(g).$$ All implicit constants are absolute, for sufficiently large $m$, and uniform in $N_0,N,n,n'$ and the intervals or functions.",
      "explanation": "The kernels have controlled marginal mass, square integrals and variation. Separated powers give rectangle and bounded-variation discrepancy estimates without requiring a joint density.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-polynomial-coefficient",
        "exact_quote": "For every integer $K\\ge0$ and $D\\ge\\delta>0$ there is $\\varepsilon=\\varepsilon(K,D,\\delta)>0$ such that complex Laurent polynomials $A,B$ supported in $[-K,K]$, with coefficients of modulus at most $D$ and $|A_0|,|B_0|\\ge\\delta$, satisfy $$\\max_{0\\le n\\le2K}|(AB)_{-n}|\\ge\\varepsilon.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-293.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-polynomial-coefficient",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-polynomial-coefficient</summary>\n\n*Proof.* The admissible polynomial pairs form a nonempty compact set. For each pair, the first nonzero exponents of $A$ and $B$ are at most zero, and their sum belongs to $[-2K,0]$. The corresponding product coefficient is nonzero. The continuous displayed maximum therefore has a positive minimum on the compact set. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "For every integer $K\\ge0$ and $D\\ge\\delta>0$ there is $\\varepsilon=\\varepsilon(K,D,\\delta)>0$ such that complex Laurent polynomials $A,B$ supported in $[-K,K]$, with coefficients of modulus at most $D$ and $|A_0|,|B_0|\\ge\\delta$, satisfy $$\\max_{0\\le n\\le2K}|(AB)_{-n}|\\ge\\varepsilon.$$",
      "explanation": "Nonvanishing central coefficients force a nonzero product coefficient in the indicated window. Compactness makes its lower bound uniform over the bounded admissible polynomial pairs.",

        {

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-parameters",
        "exact_quote": "For the fixed $\\theta$ there are positive integers $m_j,K_j,G_j,q_i$, positive real numbers $L_j,C_j,\\varepsilon_j$, and consecutive blocks $J_j=\\{d_{j-1}+1,\\ldots,d_j\\}$ of size $m_j$, with $d_0=0$, having the following properties. Set $$\\begin{gathered}\n q_0=1,\\qquad u_i(x)=\\{q_ix\\},\\qquad\\alpha_i=\\{q_i\\theta\\},\\\\\n \\kappa_j=(100j)^{-1},\\qquad N_j=m_jK_j,\\\\\n a_j=G_j/m_j,\\qquad b_j=m_j^{-19/20},\\qquad Q_j=m_j^{(7/10)m_j}.\n\\end{gathered}$$ The frequencies are strictly increasing and divisible, and $$\\label{eq:phases}\n \\frac1{N_j}<\\alpha_i<\\frac2{N_j},\\qquad\n q_i\\mid q_{i+1},\\qquad \\frac{q_{i+1}}{q_i}\\ge N_j\n \\quad(i\\in J_j).$$ For every integer $k$ with $N_j/4\\le |k|\\le m_j^{(4/5)m_j}$, at least $m_j/20$ indices in $J_j$ satisfy $$\\label{eq:spread}\n 1-\\{k\\alpha_i\\}\\in[b_j,1-b_j].$$ If $G_jK_j\\le |k|\\le N_j/4$, every index in $J_j$ has $1-\\{k\\alpha_i\\}\\in[a_j,1-a_j]$.*\n\n\n\n*The initial values are $K_1=100,G_1=1$. The numbers $L_j\\ge1$ are nondecreasing, $C_j=\\max(L_j,K_j^2)$, and the sizes satisfy $$\\begin{aligned}\n\\label{eq:scales}\n &m_j\\ge\\max(K_j,2^j,4G_j,200),\\qquad\n K_{j+1}=\\lceil m_j^{(3/4)m_j}\\rceil>2K_j,\\notag\\\\\n &G_{j+1}K_{j+1}<m_j^{(4/5)m_j},\\qquad\n \\kappa_jm_j^{1/40}\\ge L_j,\\qquad\n \\kappa_{j+1}\\sqrt{G_{j+1}}\\ge L_j,\\notag\\\\\n &h_{j+1}^*/K_{j+1}\\le2^{-j}.\n\\end{aligned}$$ Put $D_j=\\exp((K_j+1)\\sum_{s<j}C_sm_s)$. If $A,B$ are Laurent polynomials supported in $[-K_j,K_j]$, every coefficient has modulus at most $D_j$, and $|A_0|,|B_0|\\ge1/j$, then $$\\label{eq:polynomial}\n \\max_{0\\le n\\le2K_j}|(AB)_{-n}|\\ge\\varepsilon_j,\n \\qquad\n (2K_j+1)3e^{-L_j/10000}\\le\\varepsilon_j/j.$$*\n\n\n*Finally put $p_*=d_{j-1}$ and $\\ell_j=((j+1)^2(1+p_*)q_{p_*})^{-1}$. For every $t=r/q_{p_*}$ with $r\\in\\mathbb Z$, $0\\le r<q_{p_*}$, there is a finite nonempty set $\\mathcal N_{j,t}$ of positive integers in $[Q_j/2,Q_j]$, with $\\{n\\theta-t\\}\\in(0,\\ell_j)$, such that for $r'=1,2$, $$\\label{eq:sampling}\n \\left\\|\\frac1{|\\mathcal N_{j,t}|}\\sum_{n\\in\\mathcal N_{j,t}}\n       \\prod_{i\\in J_j}w_{m_j}^{(r')}(u_i(x),\\{n\\alpha_i\\})-1\n       \\right\\|_{L^2(\\mu)}<\\frac1j.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-293.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-prop-parameters",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-prop-parameters</summary>\n\n*Proof.* Suppose $K_j,G_j$ and all earlier blocks are fixed. Apply Lemma <a href=\"#lem:polynomial-coefficient\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:polynomial-coefficient\">7</a> with $K=K_j$, $D=D_j$ and $\\delta=1/j$ to choose $\\varepsilon_j$. Choose $L_j\\ge\\max(1,L_{j-1})$ to satisfy the second inequality in <a href=\"#eq:polynomial\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:polynomial\">[eq:polynomial]</a> (omit $L_{j-1}$ when $j=1$), set $C_j=\\max(L_j,K_j^2)$, and then choose an integer $G_{j+1}$ with $\\kappa_{j+1}\\sqrt{G_{j+1}}\\ge L_j$. All requirements of <a href=\"#eq:scales\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:scales\">[eq:scales]</a> now hold for sufficiently large $m=m_j$. In what follows earlier data remain fixed while $m\\to\\infty$; in particular $p_*,q_{p_*},\\ell_j$ and the finite list of shifts $t$ are fixed. Write $N=mK_j$, $Q=m^{(7/10)m}$, $a=G_j/m$, and $b=m^{-19/20}$.\n\n\n###### Separated sampling powers.\n\nEquidistribution gives at least a fixed positive multiple of $\\ell_j Q$ integers $n\\in[Q/2,Q]$ with $\\{n\\theta-t\\}\\in(0,\\ell_j)$ for each required $t$, once $m$ is sufficiently large. Set $H=m^{300}$ and $R=Nm^{1000}$. We can select at least $m^{0.69m}$ such powers, separately for every $t$, so that distinct selected powers obey $$\\label{eq:power-separation}\n |vn+v'n'|>R\\quad\n (|v|,|v'|\\le H,\\ (v,v')\\ne(0,0),\\ v,v'\\in\\mathbb Z).$$ Indeed, if $v'\\ne0$, each previously chosen $n$ excludes at most $2R+2$ integers $n'$ for each frequency pair $(v,v')$. There are at most $(2H+1)^2$ pairs, and $N\\le m^2$. A greedy selection thus loses only a polynomial factor in $m$ from the available number. Since $Q=m^{0.70m}$, it eventually leaves at least $m^{0.69m}$ powers. If $v'=0$ and $v\\ne0$, then $|vn|\\ge Q/2>R$ for large $m$, so this case causes no exclusion. Fix the resulting sets $\\mathcal N_{j,t}$.\n\n\n###### Trial phases and the spread condition.\n\nFor $i\\in J_j$, take independent uniform $\\lambda_i\\in[1/N,2/N]$. For each integer $k$ in the range of <a href=\"#eq:spread\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:spread\">[eq:spread]</a>, the law of $\\{k\\lambda_i\\}$ has density at most $5$: it is the periodization of a uniform interval of length $|k|/N\\ge1/4$, whose density is at most $1+N/|k|$. The probability that an index fails the strict interior version of <a href=\"#eq:spread\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:spread\">[eq:spread]</a> is therefore at most $10b$. If fewer than $m/20$ indices succeed, more than $19m/20$ fail. Independence and a union bound give failure probability at most $2^m(10b)^{(19/20)m}$. A further union over both signs of $k$, up to $m^{(4/5)m}$, tends to zero: the coefficient of $m\\log m$ in its logarithm is $4/5-(19/20)^2<0$. The lower range in the proposition requires no probabilistic choice. When $G_jK_j\\le|k|\\le N/4$, every trial phase has $a\\le|k|\\lambda_i\\le1/2$; the same observation will hold for the eventual $\\alpha_i$.\n\n\n###### One-coordinate and two-power estimates.\n\nFix an orientation, put $u=w_m^{(r')}$, and set $\\bar u(s)=\\int_0^1u(s,v)\\,dv$ and $A_*=\\int_0^1\\bar u(s)^2\\,ds$. Lemma <a href=\"#lem:scalar-sampling-estimates\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:scalar-sampling-estimates\">6</a>, with $N_0=N$, gives $\\|\\bar u\\|_\\infty\\le2$, $(1-2m^{-2})^2\\le A_*\\le1+4h^2$ and $\\iint u^2\\le4h^{-1}\\log m$, where $h=m^{-3/5}$. Its bounded-variation estimate also gives, uniformly in $s$, $$\\label{eq:two-power}\n \\mathbb E[u(s,\\{n\\lambda_i\\})u(s,\\{n'\\lambda_i\\})]\n       =\\bar u(s)^2+O(m^{-50})\\quad(n\\ne n').$$ Indeed, the error from <a href=\"#eq:scalar-bv\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:scalar-bv\">[eq:scalar-bv]</a> is $O(m^{-60})$. The one-power density estimate <a href=\"#eq:scalar-marginal\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:scalar-marginal\">[eq:scalar-marginal]</a> likewise gives $\\mathbb E[u(s,\\{n\\lambda_i\\})\\bar u(s)]\n=\\bar u(s)^2+O(m^{-50})$, since $n\\ge Q/2$.\n\n\n###### The product sampling test.\n\nWe now have one-coordinate and two-power estimates. Their role is to control the second moment of an average of products over a whole block; the diagonal terms will be absorbed by the large number of sampling powers, while the distinct-power terms approach the ideal product law. For independent Haar variables $z=(z_i)_{i\\in J_j}$, and a fixed power set of size $P$, put $$Z(z)=\\frac1P\\sum_{n\\in\\mathcal N_{j,t}}\n                 \\prod_{i\\in J_j}u(z_i,\\{n\\lambda_i\\}),\n \\qquad Z^{\\rm id}(z)=\\prod_{i\\in J_j}\\bar u(z_i).$$ By independence of the trial phases over $i$, the distinct-power terms of $\\mathbb E\\|Z\\|_2^2$, and the terms of $\\mathbb E\\langle Z,Z^{\\rm id}\\rangle$, are products of $m$ factors $A_*+O(m^{-50})$. They equal $A_*^m+O(m^{-49})$ by telescoping, because $(1+4h^2+O(m^{-50}))^m$ stays bounded. The equal-power terms are each at most $(8h^{-1}\\log m)^m$ by <a href=\"#eq:kernel-estimates\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:kernel-estimates\">[eq:kernel-estimates]</a> and the one-phase density bound, and their total averaging weight is $1/P$. Thus $$\\mathbb E\\|Z-Z^{\\rm id}\\|_2^2\n \\le O(m^{-49})+P^{-1}(8h^{-1}\\log m)^m\\longrightarrow0,$$ since $P\\ge m^{0.69m}$ and $h^{-1}=m^{0.60}$. Also $$\\|Z^{\\rm id}-1\\|_2^2\n =A_*^m-2(1-2m^{-2})^m+1\\longrightarrow0$$ by <a href=\"#eq:kernel-estimates\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:kernel-estimates\">[eq:kernel-estimates]</a> and $mh^2=m^{-1/5}\\to0$. It follows that $\\mathbb E\\|Z-1\\|_2^2\\to0$. There are only finitely many $t$ at this stage. Markov’s inequality, the two orientations, and the spread estimate therefore let us fix trial phases with strict spread inequalities and sampling norms below $1/(2j)$, simultaneously for every test. We can also avoid the finitely many equalities $n\\lambda_i\\in\\mathbb Z$ and the endpoints $1/N,2/N$, since each has probability zero.\n\n\n###### Realizing the phases by circle characters.\n\nNow fix this $m$ and these trial phases. Successively choose $q_i$ as multiples of the preceding frequency so that $\\{q_i\\theta\\}$ is as close to $\\lambda_i$ as desired and $q_i/q_{i-1}$ is as large as desired. This is possible because the positive multiples of $q_{i-1}\\theta$ are dense. More formally, make a sequence of such finite block choices for which all new ratios tend to infinity and all $\\alpha_i\\to\\lambda_i$. The Haar law of $(u_i(x))_{i\\in J_j}$ then converges weakly to product Haar measure. For each fixed nonzero integer character vector, the last nonzero coefficient times its frequency dominates the sum of the earlier terms, once the ratios are sufficiently large. That Fourier coefficient is therefore exactly zero eventually. Trigonometric approximation proves the weak convergence. The joint law including the deterministic phases converges to product Haar in the coordinates and the fixed vector of trial phases.\n\nFor fixed $m$ the finitely many squared sampling tests are bounded functions of these coordinates and phases. Their discontinuities have zero limiting measure: every selected $\\{n\\lambda_i\\}$ avoids the phase cut, and for that phase only finitely many coordinate values can lie on a cutoff or interval boundary. Weak convergence therefore gives convergence of their integrals. This familiar extension from continuous tests follows, for example, by continuous upper and lower approximations outside open neighborhoods of the null discontinuity sets. It applies to the squared differences from one as well. Choose a sufficiently late finite block choice. Its sampling norms are below $1/j$, all the strict spread and angle bounds persist, and all frequency ratios determined so far satisfy their required bounds. The ratio connecting the previous block to this one can be made large enough to meet that block’s outstanding condition; the ratio at the end of this block is imposed at the next stage. This completes the induction. ◻",

        }

      "exact_statement": "For the fixed $\\theta$ there are positive integers $m_j,K_j,G_j,q_i$, positive real numbers $L_j,C_j,\\varepsilon_j$, and consecutive blocks $J_j=\\{d_{j-1}+1,\\ldots,d_j\\}$ of size $m_j$, with $d_0=0$, having the following properties. Set $$\\begin{gathered}\n q_0=1,\\qquad u_i(x)=\\{q_ix\\},\\qquad\\alpha_i=\\{q_i\\theta\\},\\\\\n \\kappa_j=(100j)^{-1},\\qquad N_j=m_jK_j,\\\\\n a_j=G_j/m_j,\\qquad b_j=m_j^{-19/20},\\qquad Q_j=m_j^{(7/10)m_j}.\n\\end{gathered}$$ The frequencies are strictly increasing and divisible, and $$\\label{eq:phases}\n \\frac1{N_j}<\\alpha_i<\\frac2{N_j},\\qquad\n q_i\\mid q_{i+1},\\qquad \\frac{q_{i+1}}{q_i}\\ge N_j\n \\quad(i\\in J_j).$$ For every integer $k$ with $N_j/4\\le |k|\\le m_j^{(4/5)m_j}$, at least $m_j/20$ indices in $J_j$ satisfy $$\\label{eq:spread}\n 1-\\{k\\alpha_i\\}\\in[b_j,1-b_j].$$ If $G_jK_j\\le |k|\\le N_j/4$, every index in $J_j$ has $1-\\{k\\alpha_i\\}\\in[a_j,1-a_j]$.*\n\n\n\n*The initial values are $K_1=100,G_1=1$. The numbers $L_j\\ge1$ are nondecreasing, $C_j=\\max(L_j,K_j^2)$, and the sizes satisfy $$\\begin{aligned}\n\\label{eq:scales}\n &m_j\\ge\\max(K_j,2^j,4G_j,200),\\qquad\n K_{j+1}=\\lceil m_j^{(3/4)m_j}\\rceil>2K_j,\\notag\\\\\n &G_{j+1}K_{j+1}<m_j^{(4/5)m_j},\\qquad\n \\kappa_jm_j^{1/40}\\ge L_j,\\qquad\n \\kappa_{j+1}\\sqrt{G_{j+1}}\\ge L_j,\\notag\\\\\n &h_{j+1}^*/K_{j+1}\\le2^{-j}.\n\\end{aligned}$$ Put $D_j=\\exp((K_j+1)\\sum_{s<j}C_sm_s)$. If $A,B$ are Laurent polynomials supported in $[-K_j,K_j]$, every coefficient has modulus at most $D_j$, and $|A_0|,|B_0|\\ge1/j$, then $$\\label{eq:polynomial}\n \\max_{0\\le n\\le2K_j}|(AB)_{-n}|\\ge\\varepsilon_j,\n \\qquad\n (2K_j+1)3e^{-L_j/10000}\\le\\varepsilon_j/j.$$*\n\n\n*Finally put $p_*=d_{j-1}$ and $\\ell_j=((j+1)^2(1+p_*)q_{p_*})^{-1}$. For every $t=r/q_{p_*}$ with $r\\in\\mathbb Z$, $0\\le r<q_{p_*}$, there is a finite nonempty set $\\mathcal N_{j,t}$ of positive integers in $[Q_j/2,Q_j]$, with $\\{n\\theta-t\\}\\in(0,\\ell_j)$, such that for $r'=1,2$, $$\\label{eq:sampling}\n \\left\\|\\frac1{|\\mathcal N_{j,t}|}\\sum_{n\\in\\mathcal N_{j,t}}\n       \\prod_{i\\in J_j}w_{m_j}^{(r')}(u_i(x),\\{n\\alpha_i\\})-1\n       \\right\\|_{L^2(\\mu)}<\\frac1j.$$",

        "anchor": "stmt-lem-scalar-multiplier-fields",
        "exact_quote": "Let $(Y,\\nu)$ be a probability space, let $K=\\ell^2(\\mathbb Z)$, and let $A\\in B(L^2(Y,\\nu;K))$ commute with every scalar multiplication operator $M_g\\zeta(y)=g(y)\\zeta(y)$, $g\\in L^\\infty(Y,\\nu)$. There is a field $A(y)\\in B(K)$, unique almost everywhere, such that $$(A\\zeta)(y)=A(y)\\zeta(y),\\qquad\n \\|A(y)\\|\\le\\|A\\|\\quad\\text{almost everywhere}.$$ The field is measurable in the sense that $y\\mapsto A(y)v$ is measurable for every $v\\in K$. Products and adjoints of such operators are computed fiberwise. In particular, an orthogonal projection is represented by orthogonal projections outside one null set.",
        "source_id": "PM-293.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-scalar-multiplier-fields",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-scalar-multiplier-fields</summary>\n\n*Proof.* If $B\\in B(L^2(Y,\\nu))$ commutes with all multipliers, put $b=B1$. For every measurable set $E$, $$B\\mathbf1_E=\\mathbf1_E b,\\qquad\n \\int_E|b|^2\\,d\\nu\\le\\|B\\|^2\\nu(E).$$ Thus $|b|\\le\\|B\\|$ almost everywhere. The identity $Bf=bf$ follows first for simple functions and then, by density, for all $f\\in L^2(Y)$. Apply this observation to the matrix entries of $A$ between the coordinate copies of $L^2(Y)$, obtaining measurable functions $a_{ij}$.\n\nLet $v$ range over the countable set of finitely supported vectors with rational real and imaginary parts. Commutation with $M_{\\mathbf1_E}$ and the operator bound give $$\\int_E\\sum_{i\\in\\mathbb Z}\n       \\left|\\sum_j a_{ij}(y)v_j\\right|^2d\\nu(y)\n \\le\\|A\\|^2\\|v\\|^2\\nu(E).$$ Consequently the integrand is at most $\\|A\\|^2\\|v\\|^2$ almost everywhere. Outside the union of these countably many null sets, the matrix defines an operator $A(y)$ on $K$ with norm at most $\\|A\\|$. Its columns, and then its values on every vector, are measurable by finite-coordinate approximation. The represented field agrees with $A$ on finite-coordinate simple functions, hence on all of $L^2(Y;K)$.\n\nEquality of two such fields follows by applying their represented operators to each constant basis field. Products act fiberwise by the representation formula, and the entries of the adjoint field are $\\overline{a_{ji}}$. This proves the assertions about products and adjoints. The identities $p=p^*=p^2$ then give orthogonal projections on a common conull set. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }

There is a field $A(y)\\in B(K)$, unique almost everywhere, such that $$(A\\zeta)(y)=A(y)\\zeta(y),\\qquad\n \\|A(y)\\|\\le\\|A\\|\\quad\\text{almost everywhere}.$$ The field is measurable in the sense that $y\\mapsto A(y)v$ is measurable for every $v\\in K$.         "exact_quote": "If $0<\\tau(p)<1$, then $0<\\langle p(y)e_0,e_0\\rangle<1$ almost everywhere.",

      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-positive-diagonal",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-positive-diagonal</summary>\n\n*Proof.* Put $r(y)=\\langle p(y)e_0,e_0\\rangle$ and $D_y=\\operatorname{diag}_k f(\\sigma^ky)$. Invariance and covariance give $D_y\\operatorname{ran}p(y)\\subseteq\\operatorname{ran}p(\\sigma y)$. If $r(\\sigma y)=0$, every vector in the latter range has zero zeroth coordinate. Since $(D_yv)_0=f(y)v_0$ and $f(y)>0$, every vector in the former range also has zero zeroth coordinate, so $r(y)=0$. If $r(y)=1$, then $e_0$ belongs to the former range, and its image $f(y)e_0$ shows that $r(\\sigma y)=1$.\n\nThus $\\sigma^{-1}\\{r=0\\}\\subseteq\\{r=0\\}$ and $\\{r=1\\}\\subseteq\\sigma^{-1}\\{r=1\\}$ modulo null sets. Probability preservation makes both inclusions equalities modulo null sets. Ergodicity makes each event null or conull. The trace formula and $0<\\tau(p)<1$ exclude both conull cases. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Let $\\sigma$ be an invertible ergodic probability-preserving transformation of $(Y,\\nu)$. On $L^2(Y,\\nu;\\ell^2(\\mathbb Z))$, define $(U\\zeta)(y)=S\\zeta(y)$ and $\\pi(a)(y)e_k=a(\\sigma^ky)e_k$, where $Se_k=e_{k+1}$. Suppose a von Neumann algebra $M$ containing $U$ and $\\pi(L^\\infty(Y))$ consists of measurable fields with covariance $A(\\sigma y)=S^{-1}A(y)S$ and has faithful normal tracial state $$\\tau(A)=\\int_Y\\langle A(y)e_0,e_0\\rangle\\,d\\nu(y).$$ Let $f\\in L^\\infty(Y,\\nu)$ be positive almost everywhere, put $T=U\\pi(f)$, and let $p\\in M$ be an orthogonal projection with $(1-p)Tp=0$. If $0<\\tau(p)<1$, then $0<\\langle p(y)e_0,e_0\\rangle<1$ almost everywhere.",

        "anchor": "stmt-lem-compact-transfer",
        "exact_quote": "Let $X$ be a compact metric space with Borel probability measure $\\mu$. Let $p(x)$ be a Borel field of orthogonal projections on $\\ell^2(\\mathbb Z)$, meaning that every column $x\\mapsto p(x)e_k$ is Borel, and let $F_k:X\\to(0,\\infty)$, $k\\in\\mathbb Z$, be finite Borel functions. Suppose finite positive Borel measures $\\beta_l$ on $X^2$ converge weakly to $\\beta$. Assume that both families of marginals of $\\beta_l$ are uniformly absolutely continuous with respect to $\\mu$, and that both marginals of $\\beta$ are absolutely continuous. Suppose also that some $0<B<\\infty$ satisfies $$F_k(y)/F_k(x)\\le B\\quad(k\\in\\mathbb Z)$$ almost everywhere for each $\\beta_l$ and for $\\beta$. If $$\\label{eq:compact-transfer-identity}\n (1-p(y))\\operatorname{diag}_k\\bigl(F_k(y)/F_k(x)\\bigr)p(x)=0$$ holds $\\beta_l$-almost everywhere for every $l$, then it holds $\\beta$-almost everywhere.",
        "source_id": "PM-293.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-compact-transfer",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-compact-transfer</summary>\n\n*Proof.* First replace every diagonal entry by its minimum with $B$, so that the diagonal field is bounded everywhere. Given an arbitrarily small $\\varepsilon>0$, simultaneous Lusin approximation supplies a compact $Q\\subset X$ on which every $F_k$ and every vector map $x\\mapsto p(x)e_k$ is continuous, while $\\mu(X\\setminus Q)$ is sufficiently small to make the $\\beta_l$-mass outside $Q^2$ less than $\\varepsilon$ for all $l$. For completeness, the simultaneous assertion follows by applying Lusin’s property to the countably many maps with summable exceptional measures and intersecting the resulting compact sets. The usual property for maps into separable metric spaces follows by finite-valued approximation and measure regularity.\n\nOn $Q^2$, the capped diagonal field is strongly continuous: continuity on the basis vectors and its uniform bound imply continuity on every vector. The same argument applies to $p(x)$ and $p(y)$. Products of strongly continuous, uniformly bounded fields are strongly continuous. Therefore the zero set $Z_Q$ of the capped version of <a href=\"#eq:compact-transfer-identity\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:compact-transfer-identity\">[eq:compact-transfer-identity]</a> in $Q^2$ is compact. The assumed identity implies $\\beta_l(Z_Q)\\ge\\beta_l(X^2)-\\varepsilon$. The compact-set upper bound under weak convergence and convergence of total masses give $$\\beta(Z_Q)\\ge\\limsup_l\\beta_l(Z_Q)\n \\ge\\beta(X^2)-\\varepsilon.$$ The capped global zero set thus has full $\\beta$-measure, since $\\varepsilon$ was arbitrary. It is measurable by its countably many matrix entries. Finally the cap changes no diagonal entry $\\beta$-almost everywhere. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Let $X$ be a compact metric space with Borel probability measure $\\mu$. Suppose finite positive Borel measures $\\beta_l$ on $X^2$ converge weakly to $\\beta$. Assume that both families of marginals of $\\beta_l$ are uniformly absolutely continuous with respect to $\\mu$, and that both marginals of $\\beta$ are absolutely continuous. Suppose also that some $0<B<\\infty$ satisfies $$F_k(y)/F_k(x)\\le B\\quad(k\\in\\mathbb Z)$$ almost everywhere for each $\\beta_l$ and for $\\beta$. If $$\\label{eq:compact-transfer-identity}\n (1-p(y))\\operatorname{diag}_k\\bigl(F_k(y)/F_k(x)\\bigr)p(x)=0$$ holds $\\beta_l$-almost everywhere for every $l$, then it holds $\\beta$-almost everywhere.",

        "exact_quote": "Let $p\\in M$ be a projection satisfying $(1-p)T_*p=0$. For every integer $A\\ge1$ and every $t\\in X$ that is an integer multiple of $1/q_A$, the identity $$\\label{eq:rational-graph-identity}\n (1-p(x+t))\\operatorname{diag}_v R_{x,x+t}(v)p(x)=0$$ holds for almost every $x$.       "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-rational-graphs",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-rational-graphs</summary>\n\n*Proof.* Fix $A,t$. In every sufficiently late stage $j$, put $a=d_{j-1}\\ge A$. Since $q_A$ divides $q_a$, the sampling set $\\mathcal N_{j,t}$ occurs in Proposition <a href=\"#prop:parameters\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prop:parameters\">8</a>. Write $w=w_{m_j}$ and $w^{(2)}=w_{m_j}^{(2)}$ for this stage’s kernels in <a href=\"#eq:sampling-kernel\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:sampling-kernel\">[eq:sampling-kernel]</a>. Average over $n\\in\\mathcal N_{j,t}$ the graph measures $x\\mapsto(x,\\sigma^n x)$ with starting density $$\\prod_{i\\in J_j}w\\bigl(u_i(x),\\{n\\alpha_i\\}\\bigr).$$ By <a href=\"#eq:sampling\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:sampling\">[eq:sampling]</a>, both marginal densities tend to $1$ in $L^2(\\mu)$. For the second marginal this uses the second orientation $w^{(2)}(z,v)=w(z-v,v)$. As every translation $n\\theta$ lies within $\\ell_j$ of $t$ and $\\ell_j\\to0$, testing continuous functions on $X^2$ shows that these measures converge weakly to the Haar-first graph measure of $x\\mapsto x+t$. At every point of positive density, $$0<\\{n\\alpha_i\\}<m_j^{-3/5}\\Delta(u_i(x))\\quad(i\\in J_j),$$ so there is no wrap and all current-block coordinates are ordered.\n\nFor every selected $n$, remove the same set of starting points, namely those satisfying at least one of $$\\begin{array}{ll}\n u_i(x)\\ge1-q_a\\ell_j & (A\\le i\\le a),\\\\\n u_i(x)\\ge1-2Q_j/N_s & (i\\in J_s,\\ s>j).\n \\end{array}$$ The removed Haar measure is at most $$a q_a\\ell_j+2Q_j\\sum_{s>j}K_s^{-1}\n \\le (j+1)^{-2}+4Q_j/K_{j+1}\\longrightarrow0.$$ The $L^2$ bound on the averaged first marginal and Cauchy–Schwarz show that the removed weighted mass tends to zero. Thus the trimmed measures have the same weak limit. Each trimmed marginal is dominated by its untrimmed counterpart, so both retain uniform $L^2$ bounds and hence uniform absolute continuity.\n\nFor $A\\le i\\le a$, the number $q_i t$ is integral. The coordinate advance is therefore $q_i\\{n\\theta-t\\}<q_a\\ell_j$, and trimming prevents a wrap. For future coordinates it is $n\\alpha_i<2Q_j/N_s$, again without a wrap. Together with the current block, this orders every coordinate $i\\ge A$. Hence, on all trimmed measures, $$R_{x,y}(v)\\le\n \\exp\\left(\\sum_{i<A}C_{s(i)}\\right)\\quad(v\\in\\mathbb Z),\n \\qquad i\\in J_{s(i)}.$$ The bound is independent of $j$ and $v$. It also holds on the limiting graph, because $u_i(x+t)=u_i(x)$ for $i\\ge A$. The positive-orbit identity holds on the approximants, so Lemma <a href=\"#lem:compact-transfer\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:compact-transfer\">11</a> gives <a href=\"#eq:rational-graph-identity\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:rational-graph-identity\">[eq:rational-graph-identity]</a>. There are only countably many pairs $(A,t)$ under consideration. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Let $p\\in M$ be a projection satisfying $(1-p)T_*p=0$. For every integer $A\\ge1$ and every $t\\in X$ that is an integer multiple of $1/q_A$, the identity $$\\label{eq:rational-graph-identity}\n (1-p(x+t))\\operatorname{diag}_v R_{x,x+t}(v)p(x)=0$$ holds for almost every $x$. The exceptional sets may be chosen simultaneously for this countable family of translations.",

        "anchor": "stmt-lem-product-density-bound",
        "exact_quote": "Let $(\\Omega_i,\\mathcal F_i,\\mu_i)$, $i\\ge1$, be probability spaces and let $h_i\\ge0$ satisfy $\\int h_i\\,d\\mu_i=1$. If $$\\sum_{i\\ge1}\\|h_i-1\\|_{L^2(\\mu_i)}^2\\le C<\\infty,$$ then the product probability measure $\\nu=\\bigotimes_{i\\ge1}(h_i\\mu_i)$ has a density with respect to $\\mu=\\bigotimes_{i\\ge1}\\mu_i$ whose $L^2(\\mu)$ norm is at most $e^{C/2}$.*\n\n*In particular, suppose two probability densities $h_i^0,h_i^1$ at each coordinate satisfy $$\\|h_i^a-1\\|_{L^2(\\mu_i)}^2\\le b_i\\quad(a=0,1),\n \\qquad \\sum_{i\\ge1}b_i\\le C.$$ For every deterministic sequence $\\epsilon_i\\in\\{0,1\\}$, the product law with coordinate densities $h_i^{\\epsilon_i}$ has the same bound.",
        "source_id": "PM-293.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-product-density-bound",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-product-density-bound</summary>\n\n*Proof.* The mean-one assumption gives $\\|h_i\\|_2^2=1+\\|h_i-1\\|_2^2$. Hence each finite product density obeys $$\\left\\|\\prod_{i=1}^n h_i\\right\\|_{L^2(\\mu)}^2\n =\\prod_{i=1}^n\\bigl(1+\\|h_i-1\\|_2^2\\bigr)\n \\le \\exp\\left(\\sum_{i=1}^n\\|h_i-1\\|_2^2\\right)\\le e^C.$$ For a bounded cylinder function $\\varphi$ depending on the first $n$ coordinates, integration under $\\nu$ is therefore bounded in absolute value by $e^{C/2}\\|\\varphi\\|_{L^2(\\mu)}$. These cylinder functions are dense in $L^2(\\mu)$, so the integration functional extends continuously to that space. The Riesz representation theorem gives a real $H\\in L^2(\\mu)$ with norm at most $e^{C/2}$ representing the extension. The finite signed measure $H\\mu$ and the probability measure $\\nu$ agree on all cylinder sets. Uniqueness of finite measures on the product sigma-algebra gives $H\\mu=\\nu$; thus $H\\ge0$ almost everywhere and $\\int H\\,d\\mu=1$. Applying the first assertion to $h_i=h_i^{\\epsilon_i}$ proves the hybrid assertion. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-digit-marginals",
        "exact_quote": "There is a finite constant $H_0$, independent of $j$, such that the marginal laws of $x$ and $y$ have densities with respect to $\\mu$ of $L^2(\\mu)$ norm at most $H_0$. The same assertion holds for an endpoint formed by any deterministic choice of the $x$-digit or the $y$-digit at each coordinate, retaining the common uniform initial digit.",
        "source_id": "PM-293.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-digit-marginals",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-digit-marginals</summary>\n\n*Proof.* Either raw marginal density is at most $10$. For each width its top and bottom contributions together are at most $4\\rho_s(w)/w$, the tag contributes at most $2$, and the diagonal component contributes at most $1$.\n\n\nWe also need a sharper integral estimate. A uniform density on an interval of length at least $w/2$ differs from $1$ by a mean-zero function of squared $L^2$ norm at most $2/w$. For a tagged interval the corresponding bound is $2m_s$. Express a raw marginal density minus $1$ as the sum of its at most five weighted mean-zero perturbations. The finite-sum Cauchy–Schwarz inequality gives $$\\label{eq:one-digit-square}\n \\int_0^1 d_i(t)^2\\,dt\n \\le 1+50\\left(\\frac{\\kappa_s^2}{m_s}\n          +\\frac{\\mathbf 1_{\\{s=j\\}}}{m_s}\\right),\n \\qquad i\\in J_s.$$ In fact the four width terms contribute at most $40\\kappa_s^2/m_s$, and the tag at most $10/m_s$. Passing from raw variables to digits replaces $d_i$ by its average on each radix bin, so Jensen’s inequality preserves <a href=\"#eq:one-digit-square\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:one-digit-square\">[eq:one-digit-square]</a>.\n\nUse the uniform law at each digit as the reference probability measure. The pairs of digits are independent across coordinates, and each endpoint digit density has mean one. Since $|J_s|=m_s$, summing <a href=\"#eq:one-digit-square\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:one-digit-square\">[eq:one-digit-square]</a> minus one over the coordinates gives the common bound $50(\\sum_s\\kappa_s^2+1)$, for either endpoint and every deterministic choice between them. The initial digit has density one. Lemma <a href=\"#lem:product-density-bound\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:product-density-bound\">13</a> therefore gives the bound $$H_0^2:=\\exp\\left(50\\left(\\sum_{s\\ge1}\\kappa_s^2+1\\right)\\right)<\\infty.$$ Outside the null sets of ambiguous expansions, the mixed-radix map is a measure-preserving bijection from the reference digit space to $X$. Transporting the density through this map gives a Haar density with the same $L^2$ norm. This proves the assertion for both endpoints and all the deterministic hybrids. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "There is a finite constant $H_0$, independent of $j$, such that the marginal laws of $x$ and $y$ have densities with respect to $\\mu$ of $L^2(\\mu)$ norm at most $H_0$.         "anchor": "stmt-prop-ordered-couplings",
        "exact_quote": "Let $p\\in M$ be a projection satisfying $(1-p)T_*p=0$. For every $j$, $$\\label{eq:coupling-identity}\n (1-p(y))\\operatorname{diag}_{k\\in\\mathbb Z}\\bigl(R_{x,y}(k)\\bigr)p(x)=0\n \\quad\\text{for }\\mathbb P_j\\text{-almost every pair}.$$ Moreover $0<R_{x,y}(k)\\le1$ for all $k\\in\\mathbb Z$ almost surely.",

      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-prop-ordered-couplings",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-prop-ordered-couplings</summary>\n\n*Proof.* Fix $D$. The rational shifts $y^{(D)}-x$ have only finitely many possible values. Lemma <a href=\"#lem:rational-graphs\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:rational-graphs\">12</a> holds outside a common Haar-null set for all these shifts, so the absolute continuity of the $x$ marginal implies the required identity at $(x,y^{(D)})$. This remains valid even though the selected shift depends on the pair.\n\nThe coordinate order and <a href=\"#eq:ratio-crossing\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:ratio-crossing\">[eq:ratio-crossing]</a> bound every ratio by $1$, both for these pairs and for their limit $(x,y)$. Their measures converge weakly by the almost-sure convergence $y^{(D)}\\to y$. Lemma <a href=\"#lem:digit-marginals\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:digit-marginals\">14</a> supplies uniform $L^2$ bounds for both marginals. In particular their masses on a set of Haar measure $\\delta$ are at most $H_0\\sqrt\\delta$. Lemma <a href=\"#lem:compact-transfer\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:compact-transfer\">11</a> now passes the identity to the limit. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-conditional-labels",
        "exact_quote": "Conditional on $x$, all coordinate labels are independent and each posterior depends only on the corresponding digit $A_i$. The analogous statement holds conditional on $y$, using $B_i$.*\n\n*For $i\\in J_s$, $w\\in\\{a_s,b_s\\}$, and $u\\in[w,1-w]$, the following bounds hold almost surely: $$\\begin{aligned}\n \\mathbb P_j(\\text{top label of width }w\\text{ at }i\\mid x)\n   &\\ge\\rho_s(w)/10 &&\\text{if }u_i(x)<u,\n   \\label{eq:conditional-crossing}\\\\\n \\mathbb P_j(\\text{bottom label of width }w\\text{ at }i\\mid y)\n   &\\ge\\rho_s(w)/10 &&\\text{if }u_i(y)\\ge u.\n   \\label{eq:conditional-crossing-bottom}\n\\end{aligned}$$ In the first case that label forces $u_i(y)\\ge u$; in the second it forces $u_i(x)<u$.",
        "source_id": "PM-293.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-conditional-labels",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-conditional-labels</summary>\n\n*Proof.* The entire endpoint determines all its digits almost surely. For any finite collection of labels, conditioning on all $x$-digits gives the product of the individual posteriors $$\\frac{\\mathbb P_j(\\text{label }\\ell\\text{ at }i)\\,\n       \\mathbb P_j(A_i=a\\mid\\text{label }\\ell\\text{ at }i)}\n      {\\mathbb P_j(A_i=a)}.$$ One verifies this formula first against cylinder tests in the digit sequence, using independence of coordinate pairs, and then against all bounded digit-measurable tests by the monotone class theorem. The common initial digit is independent of those pairs. This proves conditional independence given $x$; the proof for $y$ is the same. In particular, the dependence of $u_i(x)$ on later digits does not change the label posteriors after the entire endpoint has been conditioned on.\n\nThe parameters satisfy $$\\frac1{r_i^*}\\le\\frac1{N_s}<\\frac w2.$$ If $u_i(x)<u\\le1-w$, the whole radix bin containing $X_i$ lies in $[0,1-w/2)$. On that bin the top component’s $X_i$-density is at least $1$, while the total raw marginal density is at most $10$. Its posterior probability is therefore at least $\\rho_s(w)/10$. Under this label, <a href=\"#eq:coordinate-order\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:coordinate-order\">[eq:coordinate-order]</a> gives $$u_i(y)\\ge1-w/2-1/r_i^*>1-w\\ge u.$$ For the second assertion, $u_i(y)\\ge u\\ge w$ places the entire $Y_i$ bin in $[w/2,1)$. The bottom component again has density at least $1$ there, so its posterior is at least $\\rho_s(w)/10$. Its $X_i$ value satisfies $$u_i(x)\\le w/2+1/r_i^*<w\\le u.$$ These estimates include thresholds at radix-bin endpoints. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Conditional on $x$, all coordinate labels are independent and each posterior depends only on the corresponding digit $A_i$. The analogous statement holds conditional on $y$, using $B_i$.*\n\n*For $i\\in J_s$, $w\\in\\{a_s,b_s\\}$, and $u\\in[w,1-w]$, the following bounds hold almost surely: $$\\begin{aligned}\n \\mathbb P_j(\\text{top label of width }w\\text{ at }i\\mid x)\n   &\\ge\\rho_s(w)/10 &&\\text{if }u_i(x)<u,\n   \\label{eq:conditional-crossing}\\\\\n \\mathbb P_j(\\text{bottom label of width }w\\text{ at }i\\mid y)\n   &\\ge\\rho_s(w)/10 &&\\text{if }u_i(y)\\ge u.\n   \\label{eq:conditional-crossing-bottom}\n\\end{aligned}$$ In the first case that label forces $u_i(y)\\ge u$; in the second it forces $u_i(x)<u$.",

          "source_id": "PM-293.1"

        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-conditional-labels",
          "exact_quote": "The analogous statement holds conditional on $y$, using $B_i$.*\n\n*For $i\\in J_s$, $w\\in\\{a_s,b_s\\}$, and $u\\in[w,1-w]$, the following bounds hold almost surely: $$\\begin{aligned}\n \\mathbb P_j(\\text{top label of width }w\\text{ at }i\\mid x)\n   &\\ge\\rho_s(w)/10 &&\\text{if }u_i(x)<u,\n   \\label{eq:conditional-crossing}\\\\\n \\mathbb P_j(\\text{bottom label of width }w\\text{ at }i\\mid y)\n   &\\ge\\rho_s(w)/10 &&\\text{if }u_i(y)\\ge u.\n   \\label{eq:conditional-crossing-bottom}\n\\end{aligned}$$ In the first case that label forces $u_i(y)\\ge u$; in the second it forces $u_i(x)<u$.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "eq:generic-obstruction",
        "exact_quote": "Let $K\\ge1$ be an integer, $D\\ge a>0$, $c>0$, and $\\eta,\\delta\\ge0$. On a probability space with endpoints $x,y$, let $\\xi(x),\\gamma(y)$ be measurable vectors in the unit ball of $\\ell^2(\\mathbb Z)$, and let $F_k$ be positive finite measurable functions of an endpoint, with $F_0=1$. Set $$A_v=\\frac{\\xi_v(x)}{F_v(x)},\\qquad\n B_w=F_{-w}(y)\\overline{\\gamma_{-w}(y)},\\qquad\n R(k)=\\frac{F_k(y)}{F_k(x)}.$$ Suppose, almost surely, for $0\\le n\\le2K$ and every $v\\in\\mathbb Z$, $$\\label{eq:generic-convolution}\n \\sum_{v\\in\\mathbb Z}A_vB_{-n-v}=0,\\qquad\n 0<\\frac{F_{n+v}(y)}{F_v(x)}\n       \\le\\min\\{R(v),R(n+v)\\}\\le1.$$ Let $E$ be an event of probability at least $c$ on which $$|A_v|,|B_v|\\le D\\quad(|v|\\le K),\\qquad\n |A_0|,|B_0|\\ge a.$$ For each $|k|>K$, suppose there are events $H_k^{(1)},H_k^{(2)}$ with $$\\label{eq:generic-tail}\n R(k)\\le\\eta+\\mathbf1_{H_k^{(1)}}+\\mathbf1_{H_k^{(2)}}\\quad\\text{on }E,\n \\qquad\n \\mathbb P(H_k^{(1)}\\mid x)\\le\\delta,\\quad\n \\mathbb P(H_k^{(2)}\\mid y)\\le\\delta.$$ The conditional bounds are under the full probability law, before restriction to $E$. If $\\varepsilon(K,D,a)$ is the positive constant from Lemma <a href=\"#lem:polynomial-coefficient\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:polynomial-coefficient\">7</a>, then $$\\label{eq:generic-obstruction}\n c\\varepsilon(K,D,a)\\le(2K+1)(\\eta+2\\sqrt\\delta).$$",
        "source_id": "PM-293.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-conditional-convolution",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-conditional-convolution</summary>\n\n*Proof.* The multiplier bound in <a href=\"#eq:generic-convolution\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:generic-convolution\">[eq:generic-convolution]</a> and Cauchy–Schwarz make every convolution there absolutely convergent. Put $$c_n=\\sum_{\\substack{|v|\\le K\\\\|n+v|\\le K}}A_vB_{-n-v}\n       \\quad(0\\le n\\le2K).$$ On $E$, Lemma <a href=\"#lem:polynomial-coefficient\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:polynomial-coefficient\">7</a> applied to the two truncated Laurent polynomials gives $\\sum_{n=0}^{2K}|c_n|\\ge\\varepsilon(K,D,a)$. Its expected value on $E$ is therefore at least $c\\varepsilon(K,D,a)$.\n\n\nFix $n$. For an omitted index $v$, choose $k(v)=v$ if $|v|>K$, and $k(v)=n+v$ otherwise. This is a deterministic choice with $|k(v)|>K$. The zero convolution and the two multiplier bounds give $$\\label{eq:masked-tail}\n \\mathbf1_E|c_n|\\le\\eta+\n \\sum_{b=1}^2\\sum_{v\\text{ omitted}}\n |\\xi_v(x)\\gamma_{n+v}(y)|\\mathbf1_{H_{k(v)}^{(b)}}.$$ The unmasked sum of products of vector coordinates is at most one, which accounts for $\\eta$. We dropped $\\mathbf1_E$ from the nonnegative exceptional terms so that their full-law conditional bounds remain applicable.\n\nFor the first exceptional sum, Cauchy–Schwarz on probability times counting measure bounds its expectation by $$\\left(\\mathbb E\\sum_{v\\text{ omitted}}\n       |\\xi_v(x)|^2\\mathbf1_{H_{k(v)}^{(1)}}\\right)^{1/2}\n \\left(\\mathbb E\\sum_{v\\text{ omitted}}\n       |\\gamma_{n+v}(y)|^2\\right)^{1/2}\n \\le\\sqrt\\delta.$$ Indeed, condition the first factor’s square on $x$, apply <a href=\"#eq:generic-tail\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:generic-tail\">[eq:generic-tail]</a> to each deterministic $k(v)$, and use $\\sum_v|\\xi_v(x)|^2\\le1$. The second factor is at most one. For the other exceptional sum put its indicator with $|\\gamma_{n+v}(y)|^2$ and condition on $y$, giving the same bound. Tonelli’s theorem justifies every interchange of the nonnegative sums and integrals. No sum of the individual exceptional probabilities is required. Taking expectations in <a href=\"#eq:masked-tail\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:masked-tail\">[eq:masked-tail]</a> and summing in $n$ proves <a href=\"#eq:generic-obstruction\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:generic-obstruction\">[eq:generic-obstruction]</a>. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Let $K\\ge1$ be an integer, $D\\ge a>0$, $c>0$, and $\\eta,\\delta\\ge0$. On a probability space with endpoints $x,y$, let $\\xi(x),\\gamma(y)$ be measurable vectors in the unit ball of $\\ell^2(\\mathbb Z)$, and let $F_k$ be positive finite measurable functions of an endpoint, with $F_0=1$. Set $$A_v=\\frac{\\xi_v(x)}{F_v(x)},\\qquad\n B_w=F_{-w}(y)\\overline{\\gamma_{-w}(y)},\\qquad\n R(k)=\\frac{F_k(y)}{F_k(x)}.$$ Suppose, almost surely, for $0\\le n\\le2K$ and every $v\\in\\mathbb Z$, $$\\label{eq:generic-convolution}\n \\sum_{v\\in\\mathbb Z}A_vB_{-n-v}=0,\\qquad\n 0<\\frac{F_{n+v}(y)}{F_v(x)}\n       \\le\\min\\{R(v),R(n+v)\\}\\le1.$$ Let $E$ be an event of probability at least $c$ on which $$|A_v|,|B_v|\\le D\\quad(|v|\\le K),\\qquad\n |A_0|,|B_0|\\ge a.$$ For each $|k|>K$, suppose there are events $H_k^{(1)},H_k^{(2)}$ with $$\\label{eq:generic-tail}\n R(k)\\le\\eta+\\mathbf1_{H_k^{(1)}}+\\mathbf1_{H_k^{(2)}}\\quad\\text{on }E,\n \\qquad\n \\mathbb P(H_k^{(1)}\\mid x)\\le\\delta,\\quad\n \\mathbb P(H_k^{(2)}\\mid y)\\le\\delta.$$ The conditional bounds are under the full probability law, before restriction to $E$. If $\\varepsilon(K,D,a)$ is the positive constant from Lemma <a href=\"#lem:polynomial-coefficient\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:polynomial-coefficient\">7</a>, then $$\\label{eq:generic-obstruction}\n c\\varepsilon(K,D,a)\\le(2K+1)(\\eta+2\\sqrt\\delta).$$",

        "anchor": "stmt-thm-measurable",
        "exact_quote": "The bounded operator $T_*=U\\pi(f_*)$ has no projection $p\\in M$ satisfying $0<\\tau(p)<1$ and $(1-p)T_*p=0$.",
        "source_id": "PM-293.1"
