    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-p-lem-coupling-marginals",
        "exact_quote": "Put $$\\label{p:eq:uniform-product-density}\n H^2=\\exp\\left(20\\left(2\\sum_{s\\ge1}\\kappa_s^2+1\\right)\\right)<\\infty.$$ For every $j\\ge1$, the law just constructed has the following properties.*\n\n1.  *Every one-coordinate endpoint density is at most $10$. Both marginals of $\\nu_j$ have $L^2(\\mu)$ densities of norm at most $H$. The same bound holds for the product law formed by any deterministic choice of the $x$-marginal or the $y$-marginal at each coordinate.*\n\n\n\n2.  *Conditional on $x$, the coordinates $y_i$ are independent. Conditional on $y$, the coordinates $x_i$ are independent. If $i\\in J_s$, $w=a_s$ or $b_s$, and $u\\in[w,1-w]$, then $$\\begin{aligned}\n      \\mathbb P_j(x_i<u\\le y_i\\mid x)&\\ge\\rho_s(w)/10\n           &&\\text{on }\\{x_i<u\\},\\label{p:eq:crossing-given-x}\\\\\n      \\mathbb P_j(x_i<u\\le y_i\\mid y)&\\ge\\rho_s(w)/10\n           &&\\text{on }\\{y_i\\ge u\\}.\n           \\label{p:eq:crossing-given-y}\n     \n    \\end{aligned}$$ The assertions hold almost surely, simultaneously for any specified countable family of such thresholds.",
        "source_id": "PM-293.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-p-lem-coupling-marginals",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-p-lem-coupling-marginals</summary>\n\n*Proof.* For a top or bottom component of width $w$, each endpoint interval has length at least $w/2$, so its density is at most $2/w$. A tagged endpoint density is at most $m$. Consequently either one-coordinate marginal density $h_i$ satisfies $$0<q_{j,s}\\le h_i\n \\le1+4\\sum_{w=a_s,b_s}\\frac{\\rho_s(w)}w+\\mathbf 1_{\\{s=j\\}}\n \\le2+8\\kappa_s<10.$$ Throughout these sums the two widths are counted with their labels.\n\n\nIf $v$ is the uniform density on an interval of length at least $w/2$, then $\\int(v-1)=0$ and $\\|v-1\\|_2^2\\le2/w$. The corresponding bound for a tagged interval is $m$. Subtracting $1$ from $h_i$ expresses it as the sum of four weighted mean-zero perturbations and, when $s=j$, one tagged perturbation. Cauchy–Schwarz for these at most five summands gives $$\\label{p:eq:marginal-energy}\n \\begin{split}\n \\|h_i-1\\|_2^2\n &\\le20\\sum_{w=a_s,b_s}\\frac{\\rho_s(w)^2}{w}\n                    +5\\frac{\\mathbf 1_{\\{s=j\\}}}{m_s}\\\\\n &\\le20\\left(\\frac{2\\kappa_s^2}{m_s}\n                    +\\frac{\\mathbf 1_{\\{s=j\\}}}{m_s}\\right).\n \\end{split}$$ This estimate holds for either endpoint. Summing over the $m_s$ coordinates of every block gives the bound $20(2\\sum_s\\kappa_s^2+1)$ for every deterministic selection of endpoint marginals. Each selected coordinate density is nonnegative and has integral one, and the coordinate laws are independent. Thus Lemma <a href=\"#lem:product-density-bound\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:product-density-bound\">13</a> supplies the asserted $L^2$ density with norm at most $H$. In particular, for each such product law $\\eta$ and every Borel set $A\\subseteq X$, $$\\label{p:eq:uniform-absolute-continuity}\n \\eta(A)\\le H\\mu(A)^{1/2}.$$\n\n\nTo verify the assertions after conditioning on an entire endpoint, we describe the conditional kernels. At coordinate $i\\in J_s$, let $\\ell$ run through the replacement labels, including the tag when present. Write $\\pi_\\ell$ for its mixture probability and $u_\\ell,v_\\ell$ for the normalized interval densities of its two endpoints. The marginal densities in this coordinate are $$h_i^x(t)=q_{j,s}+\\sum_\\ell\\pi_\\ell u_\\ell(t),\n \\qquad\n h_i^y(t)=q_{j,s}+\\sum_\\ell\\pi_\\ell v_\\ell(t).$$ They are strictly positive. A conditional law of $y_i$ given $x_i=t$ is $$\\label{p:eq:conditional-kernel}\n K_i^x(t,dz)=\\frac{q_{j,s}}{h_i^x(t)}\\delta_t(dz)\n       +\\sum_\\ell\\frac{\\pi_\\ell u_\\ell(t)}{h_i^x(t)}\n                              v_\\ell(z)\\,dz.$$ The reverse kernel $K_i^y$ interchanges $u_\\ell$ and $v_\\ell$. Independence of the coordinate pairs shows, first for cylinder tests, that $\\bigotimes_i K_i^x(x_i,\\cdot)$ is a conditional law of $y$ given $x$. The monotone class theorem extends the defining identity to all Borel tests on $X^2$. The corresponding product of reverse kernels is a conditional law of $x$ given $y$. These formulas prove both assertions of conditional independence.\n\nIf $x_i=t<u\\le1-w$, then $t\\in[0,1-w/2)$, where the top component of width $w$ has $x_i$-density $(1-w/2)^{-1}\\ge1$. Its whole $y_i$ interval lies above $u$. Since $h_i^x\\le10$, its contribution to <a href=\"#p:eq:conditional-kernel\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:conditional-kernel\">[p:eq:conditional-kernel]</a> is at least $\\rho_s(w)/10$. This proves <a href=\"#p:eq:crossing-given-x\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:crossing-given-x\">[p:eq:crossing-given-x]</a>. If $y_i=t\\ge u\\ge w$, the bottom component has $y_i$-density $(1-w/2)^{-1}\\ge1$ at $t$, and its whole $x_i$ interval lies below $u$. The reverse kernel therefore proves <a href=\"#p:eq:crossing-given-y\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:crossing-given-y\">[p:eq:crossing-given-y]</a>. Intersecting the full-measure sets for any specified countable family of thresholds gives the final assertion. ◻",

For $D\\ge1$, define $$y_i^{(D)}=\n \\begin{cases}\n  y_i,&i\\le D,\\\\\n  x_i,&i>D,\n \\end{cases}$$ and let $\\beta_D$ be the law of $(x,y^{(D)})$ under $\\mathbb P_j$. We claim that $$\\label{p:eq:finite-prefix-domination}\n \\beta_D\\ll\\sum_{I\\subseteq\\{1,\\ldots,D\\}}\\lambda_I.$$ Condition on a pattern $c$ of mixture labels in the first $D$ coordinates. There are finitely many patterns. Let $I(c)$ consist of the coordinates carrying replacement labels. At each $i\\in I(c)$ the conditioned pair has density $$r_{c,i}(t,z)=\n \\frac{\\mathbf 1_{U_{c,i}}(t)\\mathbf 1_{V_{c,i}}(z)}\n {|U_{c,i}|\\,|V_{c,i}|}$$ relative to the unnormalized triangular measure $\\mathbf 1_{\\{0\\le t<z<1\\}}\\,dt\\,dz$. Here $U_{c,i},V_{c,i}$ are the two intervals of the selected component. Their lengths are positive and their rectangle is supported on $t<z$. The remaining prefix coordinates are uniformly diagonal.\n\nFor the diagonal tail, let $g$ be the full $x$-marginal density from Lemma <a href=\"#p:lem:coupling-marginals\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"p:lem:coupling-marginals\">29</a>. Write $\\mu=\\mu_{\\le D}\\otimes\\mu_{>D}$ and define $$\\label{p:eq:tail-density}\n g_D(z)=\\int g(u,z)\\,d\\mu_{\\le D}(u),\n \\qquad\n \\|g_D\\|_{L^2(\\mu_{>D})}\\le\\|g\\|_{L^2(\\mu)}\\le H.$$ Fubini identifies $g_D$ as the density of $x_{>D}$, and Jensen’s inequality proves the norm bound. Independence across coordinates ensures that conditioning on $c$ leaves this tail law unchanged. Therefore the conditional law of $(x,y^{(D)})$ has density $$\\label{p:eq:conditioned-prefix-density}\n g_D(x_{>D})\\prod_{i\\in I(c)}r_{c,i}(x_i,y_i)$$ with respect to $\\lambda_{I(c)}$. This follows on cylinder sets from the coordinate construction, and hence on all Borel sets. The rectangle factors form a finite product and are bounded. The tail factor is integrable and need not equal one. Summing the conditional laws over the finitely many patterns proves <a href=\"#p:eq:finite-prefix-domination\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:finite-prefix-domination\">[p:eq:finite-prefix-domination]</a>.\n\nProposition <a href=\"#p:prop:pair-identity\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"p:prop:pair-identity\">28</a> now gives the pair identity $\\beta_D$-almost everywhere. Since $X^2$ is compact metrizable, bounded convergence against continuous functions proves $\\beta_D\\Rightarrow\\nu_j$. Every one of these laws is carried by ordered pairs. Their absolutely continuous marginals put both endpoints in the common conull set on which the cocycles are finite and positive. The ordered-coordinate formula for the cocycle ratios consequently gives $0<R_{x,y^{(D)}}(k)\\le1$ and $0<R_{x,y}(k)\\le1$, simultaneously for every signed $k$.           "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-p-prop-joint-structure</summary>\n\n*Proof.* Since $f>0$ almost everywhere, $T^*T=\\pi(f)^2$ has zero kernel, and $|T|=\\pi(f)$. The unitary $U$ is therefore its polar factor. On the conull set where the sum in <a href=\"#p:eq:weights\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:weights\">[p:eq:weights]</a> has finite support, the value of $f$ is determined by a finite subset of the countable coordinate set. There are only countably many such subsets. Consequently $f$ takes values in a countable subset of $(0,1]$, up to a null set. Its nonnull level sets give countably many spectral projections whose sum is $1$, which proves the assertion about spectral type.\n\nThe representation convention gives $$U\\pi(g)U^*=\\pi(g\\circ\\sigma^{-1}).$$ For a finite $F$, the tuple $(f\\circ\\sigma^{-n})_{n\\in F}$ has countable range on a common conull set. The nonnull joint level sets therefore give an atomic partition of the generated algebra $\\mathcal A_F$. This includes $F=\\varnothing$, when the generated unital algebra is scalar.\n\nFor the infinite family, $\\mathcal A$ is an invariant abelian subalgebra of $\\pi(L^\\infty(X))$. The rotation is totally ergodic. Indeed, for any integer $q\\ne0$ and any nonconstant finite-support character indexed by $v=(v_i)$, rational independence implies $q\\sum_i v_i\\theta_i\\notin\\mathbb Z$. The Fourier argument used in Section <a href=\"#p:sec:scales\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"p:sec:scales\">7.1</a> thus proves ergodicity of $\\sigma^q$.\n\nSuppose $e$ were an atom of $\\mathcal A$. Its integer translates under $\\operatorname{Ad}U$ are atoms of equal positive trace. Distinct atoms in an abelian algebra are orthogonal, so only finitely many can occur. The orbit of $e$ is therefore finite, and for some positive integer $q$, $U^q eU^{-q}=e$. Because $e\\in\\pi(L^\\infty(X))$, total ergodicity forces $e=1$. An abelian algebra whose identity is an atom is scalar. But $\\mathcal A$ contains the nonconstant multiplication operator $\\pi(f)$: a positive almost-everywhere constant would have finite logarithmic integral, contrary to Lemma <a href=\"#p:lem:quasinilpotent\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"p:lem:quasinilpotent\">27</a>. <a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Weighted circle rotation** ([source](#sec:introduction)): For a continuous function $f$ on the unit circle $\mathbb T$, write $T_f=Uf(V)$.
- **Affiliated invariant subspace** ([source](#sec:introduction)): A closed invariant subspace is *affiliated with $R_\theta$* when its orthogonal projection belongs to $R_\theta$.
- **Scalar-weight Fuglede–Kadison determinant** ([source](#sec:introduction)): The Fuglede–Kadison determinant of the scalar weight is $$\Delta(f(V))=\exp\left(\int_\mathbb T\log\|f(z)\|\,dm(z)\right),$$ with $\log0=-\infty$ and $\exp(-\infty)=0$.
- **Operator commutant** ([source](#pm-h-3)): For a bounded operator $T$ on a complex Hilbert space $\mathcal H$, write $\{T\}'=\{A\in B(\mathcal H):AT=TA\}$.
- **Hyperinvariant subspace** ([source](#pm-h-3)): A closed subspace is *hyperinvariant* for $T$ if every member of $\{T\}'$ leaves it invariant.
- **Transitive operator algebra** ([source](#pm-h-3)): An operator algebra is *transitive* if its only common closed invariant subspaces are $0$ and $\mathcal H$.
- **Oriented sampling kernels** ([source](#eq:sampling-kernel)): Define on two circles $$\label{eq:sampling-kernel} w_m(z,v)=\mathbf1_{\{\Delta(z)\ge m^{-2}\}} \frac{\mathbf1_{\{0<v<h\Delta(z)\}}}{h\Delta(z)}, \qquad w_m^{(1)}(z,v)=w_m(z,v),\quad w_m^{(2)}(z,v)=w_m(z-v,v),$$ where $v$ is represented in $[0,1)$ and the expression is zero when the cutoff fails.
- **Uniform absolute continuity of measures** ([source](#pm-h-15)): A family of finite measures is *uniformly absolutely continuous* with respect to $\mu$ if, for each $\varepsilon>0$, one can choose $\delta>0$ such that every set of $\mu$-measure below $\delta$ has measure below $\varepsilon$ for every member of the family.
- **Measurable circle cocycles** ([source](#pm-h-16)): For each $k\in\mathbb Z$, define $$\label{eq:cocycle-definition} F_k(x)=\exp\left(-\sum_j C_j\sum_{i\in J_j} \lfloor u_i(x)+k\alpha_i\rfloor\right).$$
- **Endpoint cocycle ratio** ([source](#eq:ratio-crossing)): For endpoints in the common conull set, write $$R_{x,y}(k)=F_k(y)/F_k(x).$$
- **Affiliated positive self-adjoint operator** ([source](#pm-h-27)): A positive self-adjoint operator is *affiliated* with a von Neumann algebra if all its spectral projections belong to that algebra.
- **Triangular finite-coordinate law** ([source](#p:sec:sampling)): For a finite set $I\subset\mathbb N$, define a finite Borel measure $\lambda_I$ on $X\times X$ as follows.
- **Finite polar-conjugate algebra** ([source](#stmt-p-prop-joint-structure)): For every finite $F\subset\mathbb Z$, the abelian von Neumann algebra $$\mathcal A_F=W^*(U^n\|T\|U^{-n}:n\in F)$$ is atomic.
- **Full polar-orbit algebra** ([source](#stmt-p-prop-joint-structure)): The full algebra $$\mathcal A=W^*(U^n\|T\|U^{-n}:n\in\mathbb Z)$$ is diffuse.

      "exact_definition": "Define on two circles $$\\label{eq:sampling-kernel}\n w_m(z,v)=\\mathbf1_{\\{\\Delta(z)\\ge m^{-2}\\}}\n       \\frac{\\mathbf1_{\\{0<v<h\\Delta(z)\\}}}{h\\Delta(z)},\n \\qquad\n w_m^{(1)}(z,v)=w_m(z,v),\\quad\n w_m^{(2)}(z,v)=w_m(z-v,v),$$ where $v$ is represented in $[0,1)$ and the expression is zero when the cutoff fails.",
      "interpretation": "The kernel selects a small forward move without wrapping at the circle cut; its second orientation expresses the same density from the endpoint.",
      "kind": "definition",
      "name": "Oriented sampling kernels",
      "scope": "Finite sampling tests in the circle and product constructions."
    },

          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }

| q-kernel-marginal-supremum | \\|\bar u\\|_\infty | ≤ | $2$ | The integer scale and width are those fixed in the scalar sampling lemma. |
| q-kernel-total-mass | \int_0^1\bar u | = | $1-2m^{-2}$ | The cutoff is the one in the sampling-kernel definition. |
| q-kernel-marginal-second-moment | \int_0^1\bar u^2 | ≤ | $1+\frac{h^2}{(1-h)^2}$ | The bound applies to both kernel orientations. |
| q-kernel-square-integral | \iint_{[0,1]^2}u^2 | = | $\frac2h\log\frac{m^2}{2}$ | The change of variables preserves the square integral between orientations. |
| q-single-phase-density-error | \|p_n-1\| | ≤ | $N_0/n$ | The bound is almost everywhere for a power in the specified interval. |
| q-finite-convolution-positive-minimum | \max_{0\le n\le2K}\|(AB)_{-n}\| | ≥ | $\varepsilon$ | The positive minimum depends on the support window, coefficient cap and central lower bound. |
| q-circle-reciprocal-cutoff-tail | \sum_{s>j}\frac1{K_s} | ≤ | $\frac2{K_{j+1}}$ | The reciprocal tail is dominated by the next cutoff. |
| q-product-density-l2-cap | Product density norm | ≤ | $e^{C/2}$ | The cap depends on the summed squared deviations. |
| q-conditional-convolution-budget | c\varepsilon(K,D,a) | ≤ | $(2K+1)(\eta+2\sqrt\delta)$ | The finite central lower bound is compared with attenuation and conditional exception budgets. |
| q-circle-tail-exception | \mathbb P_j(H_k^{(1)}\mid x) | ≤ | $e^{-L_j/400}$ | Uniformly over omitted signed indices at the chosen stage. |
| q-product-sampling-discrepancy | \mathbb E_\theta\\|Z_l-Z_l^0\\|_{L^2(\mu)}^2 | ≤ | $O(dm^{-40})+\frac{(c h^{-1}\log m)^d}{P_l}$ | Either endpoint orientation, for a fixed rational rectangle test or no test. |
| q-product-tail-trimming-loss | \sum_{i>d}n\theta_i | ≤ | $4m^{-m/20}$ | Uniformly over the finitely selected positive powers at a sufficiently late stage. |
| q-product-coupling-uniform-cap | H^2 | = | $\exp\left(20\left(2\sum_{s\ge1}\kappa_s^2+1\right)\right)$ | The constant is independent of the tagged block. |
| q-product-power-spectral-radius | \lim_{n\to\infty}\\|T^n\\|^{1/n} | = | $0$ | The conclusion uses divergent accumulated costs over finite prefixes. |

          "exact_quote": "Let $(\\Omega_i,\\mathcal F_i,\\mu_i)$, $i\\ge1$, be probability spaces and let $h_i\\ge0$ satisfy $\\int h_i\\,d\\mu_i=1$.           "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }

On a probability space with endpoints $x,y$, let $\\xi(x),\\gamma(y)$ be measurable vectors in the unit ball of $\\ell^2(\\mathbb Z)$, and let $F_k$ be positive finite measurable functions of an endpoint, with $F_0=1$. Set $$A_v=\\frac{\\xi_v(x)}{F_v(x)},\\qquad\n B_w=F_{-w}(y)\\overline{\\gamma_{-w}(y)},\\qquad\n R(k)=\\frac{F_k(y)}{F_k(x)}.$$ Suppose, almost surely, for $0\\le n\\le2K$ and every $v\\in\\mathbb Z$, $$\\label{eq:generic-convolution}\n \\sum_{v\\in\\mathbb Z}A_vB_{-n-v}=0,\\qquad\n 0<\\frac{F_{n+v}(y)}{F_v(x)}\n       \\le\\min\\{R(v),R(n+v)\\}\\le1.$$ Let $E$ be an event of probability at least $c$ on which $$|A_v|,|B_v|\\le D\\quad(|v|\\le K),\\qquad\n |A_0|,|B_0|\\ge a.$$ For each $|k|>K$, suppose there are events $H_k^{(1)},H_k^{(2)}$ with $$\\label{eq:generic-tail}\n R(k)\\le\\eta+\\mathbf1_{H_k^{(1)}}+\\mathbf1_{H_k^{(2)}}\\quad\\text{on }E,\n \\qquad\n \\mathbb P(H_k^{(1)}\\mid x)\\le\\delta,\\quad\n \\mathbb P(H_k^{(2)}\\mid y)\\le\\delta.$$ The conditional bounds are under the full probability law, before restriction to $E$. <a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-commutant-algebra** [claim](#stmt-lem-commutant-algebra) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-commutant-algebra): Preserve the commutation equation under algebra operations and strong limits; use rank-one projections to test properness. -> A proper strongly closed unital commutant, with the stated transitivity equivalence.
- **step-hyperinvariant-membership** [claim](#stmt-lem-hyperinvariant-projection) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-hyperinvariant-projection): Use commutant unitaries and their inverses to obtain reduction, then apply the bicommutant identity. -> Its invariant orthogonal projection belongs to the algebra.
- **step-scalar-kernel-estimates** [claim](#stmt-lem-scalar-sampling-estimates) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-scalar-sampling-estimates): Compute kernel moments and variation, periodize a uniform interval, and use Fourier separation and interval approximation. -> Marginal, moment and bounded-variation discrepancy bounds.
- **step-finite-coefficient-compactness** [claim](#stmt-lem-polynomial-coefficient) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-polynomial-coefficient): Find a nonzero coefficient from the first nonzero exponents, then minimize the continuous maximum on the admissible compact set. -> A strictly positive uniform finite-convolution lower bound.
- **step-circle-parameter-selection** [claim](#stmt-prop-parameters) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-parameters): Select separated powers and successful independent trial phases, then approximate those phases by divisible characters of the prescribed rotation. -> All phase, scale, attenuation and finite sampling requirements.
- **step-measurable-field-decomposition** [claim](#stmt-lem-scalar-multiplier-fields) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-scalar-multiplier-fields): Represent scalar matrix entries by multipliers and use a countable dense family of vectors to obtain a common fiberwise operator bound. -> A unique measurable operator field with fiberwise products and adjoints.
- **step-strict-diagonal** [claim](#stmt-lem-positive-diagonal) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-positive-diagonal): Propagate extreme diagonal events using positive weights and covariance; exclude conull extremes by ergodicity and trace. -> Strictly interior central diagonal entries almost everywhere.
- **step-compact-limit-transfer** [claim](#stmt-lem-compact-transfer) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-compact-transfer): Cap the diagonal, apply simultaneous compact-set continuity approximation, and use the compact-set bound under weak convergence. -> The projection identity holds under the limiting measure.
- **step-rational-graph-transfer** [claim](#stmt-lem-rational-graphs) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-rational-graphs): Average weighted positive orbit graphs, trim wrap events, and pass to the rational-translation graph limit. -> Simultaneous almost-everywhere identities on the specified rational graphs.
- **step-product-density-control** [claim](#stmt-lem-product-density-bound) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-product-density-bound): Bound finite product density norms, extend integration from cylinder functions, and identify the resulting density. -> A square-integrable infinite-product density and the same hybrid bound.
- **step-digit-marginal-control** [claim](#stmt-lem-digit-marginals) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-digit-marginals): Bound raw marginal perturbation energy, average on radix bins, and transport product densities through the digit expansion. -> Uniform endpoint and deterministic-hybrid marginal density bounds.
- **step-ordered-circle-identity** [claim](#stmt-prop-ordered-couplings) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-ordered-couplings): Approximate the second endpoint by finite digit replacements and transfer the rational-graph identity to the ordered limit. -> The ordered-pair projection identity with capped positive ratios.
- **step-conditional-digit-labels** [claim](#stmt-lem-conditional-labels) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-conditional-labels): Compute coordinate label posteriors from endpoint digits and use whole-bin containment to force interior crossings. -> Conditional label independence and eligible crossing lower bounds.
- **step-abstract-convolution-obstruction** [claim](#stmt-lem-conditional-convolution) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-conditional-convolution): Compare the finite polynomial lower bound with omitted terms, conditioning each masked square sum on its matching endpoint. -> The central-mass versus tail-budget inequality.
- **step-circle-positive-event** [claim](#stmt-lem-truncation-bound) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-truncation-bound): Retain an admissible tagged block, subtract future and diagonal losses, and bound the truncated polynomial coefficients on the retained event. -> A fixed positive event mass and expected finite-convolution lower bound at late stages.
- **step-circle-conditional-tail** [claim](#stmt-lem-tail-attenuation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-tail-attenuation): Use tags near the cutoff and covering phase ranges farther away; split no-crossing according to complementary eligible endpoint coordinates. -> Attenuated ratios with small full-law conditional exceptions for every omitted index.
- **step-measurable-exclusion** [claim](#stmt-thm-measurable) / [proof](#pm-h-25): Derive exact convolutions from ordered-pair invariance and apply the abstract obstruction with the chosen attenuation budget. -> A contradiction to any intermediate-trace invariant projection.
- **step-affiliated-transport** [claim](#stmt-lem-affiliated-transport) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-affiliated-transport): Establish algebra membership and range invariance through commutant unitaries; compare traces using spectral cutoffs of the intertwiner and its inverse. -> A transported invariant projection with equal trace.
- **step-continuous-main-construction** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#pm-h-30): Smooth drops, relocate them into shrinking arcs, add a diverging background, and use the resulting measurable coboundary to transport invariant projections. -> The continuous prescribed-angle weight with the required zero set, logarithmic integral and projection exclusion.
- **step-continuous-quasinilpotence** [claim](#stmt-cor-quasinilpotent) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-quasinilpotent): Truncate the logarithm, use uniform continuous-function rotation averages, and apply the spectral-radius formula. -> Nonzero norm-quasinilpotence of the circle operator.
- **step-transitive-commutant-corollary** [claim](#stmt-cor-transitive-commutant) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-transitive-commutant): Combine factor projection exclusion with hyperinvariant projection membership and transfer by unitary equivalence. -> The stated Hilbert-space existence and transitive commutant conclusion.
- **step-product-phase-control** [claim](#stmt-p-prop-phases) / [proof](#proof-stmt-p-prop-phases): Control the lower phase range directly and apply independence, a union bound and summable failure probabilities to the upper range. -> Simultaneous eventual phase-range coverage.
- **step-product-ordered-sampling** [claim](#stmt-p-prop-sampling) / [proof](#proof-stmt-p-prop-sampling): Average separated positive powers, estimate growing products in both marginal orientations, intersect countable full-measure events, and trim unordered tail coordinates. -> Ordered orbit-pair approximants to every finite-coordinate triangular law.
- **step-product-factor** [claim](#stmt-p-lem-factor) / [proof](#proof-stmt-p-lem-factor): Recover covariant fields from matrix diagonals by strong averaging; verify trace faithfulness, traciality and scalar center. -> The finite factor, its field characterization and its trace.
- **step-product-norm-determinant** [claim](#stmt-p-lem-quasinilpotent) / [proof](#proof-stmt-p-lem-quasinilpotent): Use finite cost prefixes to bound operator powers and use the nonnegative integral calculation for the logarithmic integral. -> Nonzero norm-quasinilpotence and zero determinant.
- **step-product-triangular-identity** [claim](#stmt-p-prop-pair-identity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-p-prop-pair-identity): Divide the positive-power orbit inclusion by the cocycle and transfer to the triangular law under the common ordered ratio cap. -> The projection identity under every finite-coordinate triangular law.
- **step-product-biased-marginals** [claim](#stmt-p-lem-coupling-marginals) / [proof](#proof-stmt-p-lem-coupling-marginals): Bound mean-zero mixture perturbations and describe independent conditional kernels in both endpoint orientations. -> Uniform hybrid marginal density bounds and conditional crossing estimates.
- **step-product-biased-identity** [claim](#stmt-p-prop-biased-identity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-p-prop-biased-identity): Condition on finite prefix labels, retain the biased tail density explicitly, and pass to the full endpoint by compact transfer. -> The identity under each full biased coupling.
- **step-product-projection-exclusion** [claim](#stmt-p-thm-model) / [proof](#p:sec:obstruction): Verify exact convolutions, a positive tagged event, finite coefficient bounds and complementary conditional tail estimates, then cancel the positive coefficient constant at a fixed late stage. -> Exclusion of every nontrivial invariant projection in the product factor.
- **step-atomic-diffuse-structure** [claim](#stmt-p-prop-joint-structure) / [proof](#proof-stmt-p-prop-joint-structure): Use finite-support weight values for atomic finite tuples; use equal-trace atom orbits and total ergodicity to exclude atoms in the full orbit algebra. -> The polar decomposition, atomic spectral type, finite-family atomicity and full-orbit diffuseness.
- **step-product-model-assembly** [claim](#stmt-p-thm-model) / [proof](#p:sec:structure): Assemble the factor, weight, norm, determinant, projection exclusion and polar-orbit structure results. -> The complete product realization theorem.

          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-affiliated-transport</summary>\n\n*Proof.* Every commutant unitary preserves $\\operatorname{ran}p$ and $\\operatorname{Dom}H$, and commutes with $H$ on that domain. Thus $B$ preserves the unclosed image and, by boundedness, its closure.\n\nIt remains to prove the trace assertion; bounded similarity cannot be assumed. Put $E_N=\\mathbf1_{[1/N,N]}(H)$. Letting $N$ increase gives $\\tau(P)\\ge\\tau(p)$.\n\nFor the reverse inequality, set $q'_N=(1-p)\\wedge E_N$. The bounded operator $H^{-1}q'_N=(H^{-1}E_N)q'_N$ is likewise bounded below on its initial space and has closed image. This image is orthogonal to $\\operatorname{ran}P$. Indeed, for $\\eta\\in\\operatorname{ran}q'_N$ and $\\zeta\\in\\operatorname{ran}p\\cap\\operatorname{Dom}H$, self-adjointness gives $$\\langle H^{-1}\\eta,H\\zeta\\rangle=\\langle\\eta,\\zeta\\rangle=0;$$ here $H^{-1}\\eta$ lies in $\\operatorname{Dom}H$. Polar decomposition and the same meet estimate yield $$\\tau(1-P)\\ge\\tau((1-p)\\wedge E_N)\n \\ge\\tau(1-p)+\\tau(E_N)-1.$$ Passing to the limit proves the opposite inequality for $\\tau(P)$. ◻",

          "exact_quote": "#### A background cost diverging at the zero\n\nLet $W_j=2^{j+1}$. For $x\\ne0$, put $$g_0(x)=1+\\sum_j\\left(v_j(x)-\\frac1{W_j}\n                              \\sum_{n=1}^{W_j}v_j(\\sigma^{-n}x)\\right).$$ For each $j$, disjointness bounds the norm of the subtracted function by $1/W_j$. Its integral is finite, since $\\sum_j\\int v_j\\,d\\mu\\le\\sum_j\\mu(I_j)<\\infty$ and the negative series is uniformly bounded.\n\nDefine $$b_0(x)=\\sum_j\\frac1{W_j}\\sum_{n=1}^{W_j}\\sum_{r=1}^n\n                                      v_j(\\sigma^{-r}x).$$ The $j$th summand is supported in $\\bigcup_{r=1}^{W_j}\\sigma^r I_j$. The assumed summability makes this sum finite almost everywhere. On a conull set where the sums at $x$ and $\\sigma x$ are finite, telescoping shows $$g_0=1+b_0\\circ\\sigma-b_0.$$ Now let $$g(x)=g_0(x)+\\sum_i h'_i(x)\\quad(x\\ne0),\\qquad g(0)=\\infty,\n \\qquad b=b_0-\\sum_i(d_i^{\\rm sm}+d'_i).$$ The preceding arguments prove that $g$ is nonnegative, finite and continuous off $0$, tends to infinity at $0$, and has infinite integral. The transfer $b$ is real and finite almost everywhere. We may omit finitely many initial stages; alternatively, each of their densities is bounded for fixed $l$, so they can be included by enlarging the bound. The total masses of $\\widetilde\\eta_{I,l}$ are consequently uniformly bounded.\n\n\nFor a fixed cylinder rectangle $\\mathcal R$, using the first orientation with its test shows that $$\\left|\\widetilde\\eta_{I,l}(\\mathcal R)\n       -\\int_X Z_l^0\\,d\\mu\\right|\n \\le\\|Z_l-Z_l^0\\|_2\\longrightarrow0.$$ The ideal integral has a direct product interpretation on the first $d$ coordinates.           "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-commutant-algebra",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-commutant-algebra</summary>\n\n*Proof.* The commutation equation is preserved by addition, multiplication and scalar multiplication, and the identity commutes with $T$. If $A_\\lambda\\to A$ strongly and $A_\\lambda T=TA_\\lambda$, then for every $\\zeta\\in\\mathcal H$ both sides converge, giving $AT\\zeta=TA\\zeta$. Thus the algebra is strongly closed. If every bounded operator commuted with $T$, then every rank-one orthogonal projection would commute with $T$, so every nonzero vector would be an eigenvector. Applying this to $x,y,x+y$ for linearly independent vectors shows that the eigenvalues are equal; hence $T$ would be scalar. Properness follows. The last assertion is precisely the two definitions. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }

          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-hyperinvariant-projection",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-hyperinvariant-projection</summary>\n\n*Proof.* Every unitary $W\\in\\mathcal M'$ and its inverse commute with $T$. Hyperinvariance therefore makes the subspace reducing for $W$. Its orthogonal projection commutes with all such unitaries and hence belongs to $\\mathcal M''=\\mathcal M$. Since $T\\in\\{T\\}'$, its range is invariant under $T$. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }
      ],

      "claim_anchor": "stmt-lem-scalar-sampling-estimates",

        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-scalar-sampling-estimates",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-scalar-sampling-estimates</summary>\n\n*Proof.* For the first orientation the function of $v$ is a constant height at most $m^{13/5}$ on an interval, so the variation bound is immediate. For the second, nonzero values require $v<s$ in representatives $s,v\\in[0,1)$: if $v>s$, then $\\Delta(s-v)\\le v-s<v$, whereas $h<1$. On $0<v<s$, the conditions $\\Delta(s-v)\\ge m^{-2}$ and $v<h\\Delta(s-v)$ describe an interval. The reciprocal of $\\Delta(s-v)$ has at most two monotone pieces there, with the same height bound. This proves the asserted uniform bound in both orientations.\n\nIn the first orientation $\\bar u$ is the cutoff indicator, and $$\\iint u^2=\\frac2h\\int_{m^{-2}}^{1/2}\\frac{ds}{s}\n          =\\frac2h\\log(m^2/2).$$ The change $(s,v)\\mapsto(s-v,v)$ preserves double integrals, giving the same total mass and square integral for the second orientation. To bound its averaged density, first omit the cutoff. The resulting density $d$ is the law of $z+t\\Delta(z)$ for independent uniform $z\\in[0,1)$ and $t\\in(0,h)$. For fixed $t$ this is an increasing piecewise-linear bijection of $[0,1]$, with slopes $1+t$ and $1-t$. Thus $\\int d=1$ and $|d-1|\\le h/(1-h)$, whence $$\\int d^2=1+\\int(d-1)^2\\le1+\\frac{h^2}{(1-h)^2}.$$ Restoring the cutoff only decreases this nonnegative density; also $d\\le(1-h)^{-1}\\le2$. These observations give the upper bounds in <a href=\"#eq:kernel-estimates\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:kernel-estimates\">[eq:kernel-estimates]</a>, while Cauchy–Schwarz gives the lower bound from its exact mass. The cancellation of the linear term here is essential when multiplying estimates over $m$ coordinates.\n\nPeriodizing a uniform interval of length $n/N_0$ gives <a href=\"#eq:scalar-marginal\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:scalar-marginal\">[eq:scalar-marginal]</a>; in particular $p_n\\le2$ for large $m$. For the separated powers and any permitted nonzero frequency pair, direct integration on $[1/N_0,2/N_0]$ gives $$\\left|\\mathbb E e^{2\\pi i(vn+v'n')\\lambda}\\right|\n \\le\\frac{N_0}{\\pi|vn+v'n'|}\\le\\frac1\\pi m^{-1000}.$$ To pass from characters to rectangles, convolve each interval indicator with the Fejér kernel $$F_H(s)=\\frac1H\\left(\\frac{\\sin(\\pi Hs)}{\\sin(\\pi s)}\\right)^2.$$ Expanding the squared geometric sum shows that $F_H$ is nonnegative, has mass one, and has frequencies of magnitude below $H$. The convolved indicators lie in $[0,1]$, preserve their means, and have Fourier coefficients of modulus at most one. Outside strips of width $\\eta=m^{-80}$ about interval endpoints, their error is $O((H\\eta)^{-1})$, by integrating the kernel tail. The marginal bound $p_n,p_{n'}\\le2$ shows that replacing both indicators in a product costs $O(\\eta+(H\\eta)^{-1})$, both for the actual joint law and for Haar product measure. The remaining Fourier error is $O(H^2m^{-1000})$. This proves <a href=\"#eq:scalar-rectangles\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:scalar-rectangles\">[eq:scalar-rectangles]</a>; no joint density, or independence of the sampled powers, is used.\n\nFinally, a bounded-variation function is, up to a null set, a constant plus an integral of indicators of intervals $[a,1)$ against its complex Stieltjes measure. The constant and measure have total size at most $B(f)$; the same holds for $g$. Apply <a href=\"#eq:scalar-rectangles\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:scalar-rectangles\">[eq:scalar-rectangles]</a> to the resulting products of interval indicators, allowing the full interval. Integration against total variation gives <a href=\"#eq:scalar-bv\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:scalar-bv\">[eq:scalar-bv]</a>. Changes at the countably many possible discontinuities do not matter, because the marginals have densities. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }
      ],
      "id": "step-scalar-kernel-estimates",

      "output": "Marginal, moment and bounded-variation discrepancy bounds.",
      "proof_anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-scalar-sampling-estimates"

      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-polynomial-coefficient",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-polynomial-coefficient</summary>\n\n*Proof.* The admissible polynomial pairs form a nonempty compact set. For each pair, the first nonzero exponents of $A$ and $B$ are at most zero, and their sum belongs to $[-2K,0]$. The corresponding product coefficient is nonzero. The continuous displayed maximum therefore has a positive minimum on the compact set. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }
      ],
      "id": "step-finite-coefficient-compactness",
      "inputs": [
        "Bounded Laurent polynomial coefficients",

      "method": "Find a nonzero coefficient from the first nonzero exponents, then minimize the continuous maximum on the admissible compact set.",

    {
      "claim_anchor": "stmt-prop-parameters",
      "depends_on": [
        "step-scalar-kernel-estimates",
        "step-finite-coefficient-compactness"
      ],
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-prop-parameters",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-prop-parameters</summary>\n\n*Proof.* Suppose $K_j,G_j$ and all earlier blocks are fixed. Apply Lemma <a href=\"#lem:polynomial-coefficient\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:polynomial-coefficient\">7</a> with $K=K_j$, $D=D_j$ and $\\delta=1/j$ to choose $\\varepsilon_j$. Choose $L_j\\ge\\max(1,L_{j-1})$ to satisfy the second inequality in <a href=\"#eq:polynomial\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:polynomial\">[eq:polynomial]</a> (omit $L_{j-1}$ when $j=1$), set $C_j=\\max(L_j,K_j^2)$, and then choose an integer $G_{j+1}$ with $\\kappa_{j+1}\\sqrt{G_{j+1}}\\ge L_j$. All requirements of <a href=\"#eq:scales\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:scales\">[eq:scales]</a> now hold for sufficiently large $m=m_j$. In what follows earlier data remain fixed while $m\\to\\infty$; in particular $p_*,q_{p_*},\\ell_j$ and the finite list of shifts $t$ are fixed. Write $N=mK_j$, $Q=m^{(7/10)m}$, $a=G_j/m$, and $b=m^{-19/20}$.\n\n\n###### Separated sampling powers.\n\nEquidistribution gives at least a fixed positive multiple of $\\ell_j Q$ integers $n\\in[Q/2,Q]$ with $\\{n\\theta-t\\}\\in(0,\\ell_j)$ for each required $t$, once $m$ is sufficiently large. Set $H=m^{300}$ and $R=Nm^{1000}$. We can select at least $m^{0.69m}$ such powers, separately for every $t$, so that distinct selected powers obey $$\\label{eq:power-separation}\n |vn+v'n'|>R\\quad\n (|v|,|v'|\\le H,\\ (v,v')\\ne(0,0),\\ v,v'\\in\\mathbb Z).$$ Indeed, if $v'\\ne0$, each previously chosen $n$ excludes at most $2R+2$ integers $n'$ for each frequency pair $(v,v')$. There are at most $(2H+1)^2$ pairs, and $N\\le m^2$. A greedy selection thus loses only a polynomial factor in $m$ from the available number. Since $Q=m^{0.70m}$, it eventually leaves at least $m^{0.69m}$ powers. If $v'=0$ and $v\\ne0$, then $|vn|\\ge Q/2>R$ for large $m$, so this case causes no exclusion. Fix the resulting sets $\\mathcal N_{j,t}$.\n\n\n###### Trial phases and the spread condition.\n\nFor $i\\in J_j$, take independent uniform $\\lambda_i\\in[1/N,2/N]$. For each integer $k$ in the range of <a href=\"#eq:spread\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:spread\">[eq:spread]</a>, the law of $\\{k\\lambda_i\\}$ has density at most $5$: it is the periodization of a uniform interval of length $|k|/N\\ge1/4$, whose density is at most $1+N/|k|$. The probability that an index fails the strict interior version of <a href=\"#eq:spread\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:spread\">[eq:spread]</a> is therefore at most $10b$. If fewer than $m/20$ indices succeed, more than $19m/20$ fail. Independence and a union bound give failure probability at most $2^m(10b)^{(19/20)m}$. A further union over both signs of $k$, up to $m^{(4/5)m}$, tends to zero: the coefficient of $m\\log m$ in its logarithm is $4/5-(19/20)^2<0$. The lower range in the proposition requires no probabilistic choice. When $G_jK_j\\le|k|\\le N/4$, every trial phase has $a\\le|k|\\lambda_i\\le1/2$; the same observation will hold for the eventual $\\alpha_i$.\n\n\n###### One-coordinate and two-power estimates.\n\nFix an orientation, put $u=w_m^{(r')}$, and set $\\bar u(s)=\\int_0^1u(s,v)\\,dv$ and $A_*=\\int_0^1\\bar u(s)^2\\,ds$. Lemma <a href=\"#lem:scalar-sampling-estimates\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:scalar-sampling-estimates\">6</a>, with $N_0=N$, gives $\\|\\bar u\\|_\\infty\\le2$, $(1-2m^{-2})^2\\le A_*\\le1+4h^2$ and $\\iint u^2\\le4h^{-1}\\log m$, where $h=m^{-3/5}$. Its bounded-variation estimate also gives, uniformly in $s$, $$\\label{eq:two-power}\n \\mathbb E[u(s,\\{n\\lambda_i\\})u(s,\\{n'\\lambda_i\\})]\n       =\\bar u(s)^2+O(m^{-50})\\quad(n\\ne n').$$ Indeed, the error from <a href=\"#eq:scalar-bv\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:scalar-bv\">[eq:scalar-bv]</a> is $O(m^{-60})$. The one-power density estimate <a href=\"#eq:scalar-marginal\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:scalar-marginal\">[eq:scalar-marginal]</a> likewise gives $\\mathbb E[u(s,\\{n\\lambda_i\\})\\bar u(s)]\n=\\bar u(s)^2+O(m^{-50})$, since $n\\ge Q/2$.\n\n\n###### The product sampling test.\n\nWe now have one-coordinate and two-power estimates. Their role is to control the second moment of an average of products over a whole block; the diagonal terms will be absorbed by the large number of sampling powers, while the distinct-power terms approach the ideal product law. For independent Haar variables $z=(z_i)_{i\\in J_j}$, and a fixed power set of size $P$, put $$Z(z)=\\frac1P\\sum_{n\\in\\mathcal N_{j,t}}\n                 \\prod_{i\\in J_j}u(z_i,\\{n\\lambda_i\\}),\n \\qquad Z^{\\rm id}(z)=\\prod_{i\\in J_j}\\bar u(z_i).$$ By independence of the trial phases over $i$, the distinct-power terms of $\\mathbb E\\|Z\\|_2^2$, and the terms of $\\mathbb E\\langle Z,Z^{\\rm id}\\rangle$, are products of $m$ factors $A_*+O(m^{-50})$. They equal $A_*^m+O(m^{-49})$ by telescoping, because $(1+4h^2+O(m^{-50}))^m$ stays bounded. The equal-power terms are each at most $(8h^{-1}\\log m)^m$ by <a href=\"#eq:kernel-estimates\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:kernel-estimates\">[eq:kernel-estimates]</a> and the one-phase density bound, and their total averaging weight is $1/P$. Thus $$\\mathbb E\\|Z-Z^{\\rm id}\\|_2^2\n \\le O(m^{-49})+P^{-1}(8h^{-1}\\log m)^m\\longrightarrow0,$$ since $P\\ge m^{0.69m}$ and $h^{-1}=m^{0.60}$. Also $$\\|Z^{\\rm id}-1\\|_2^2\n =A_*^m-2(1-2m^{-2})^m+1\\longrightarrow0$$ by <a href=\"#eq:kernel-estimates\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:kernel-estimates\">[eq:kernel-estimates]</a> and $mh^2=m^{-1/5}\\to0$. It follows that $\\mathbb E\\|Z-1\\|_2^2\\to0$. There are only finitely many $t$ at this stage. Markov’s inequality, the two orientations, and the spread estimate therefore let us fix trial phases with strict spread inequalities and sampling norms below $1/(2j)$, simultaneously for every test. We can also avoid the finitely many equalities $n\\lambda_i\\in\\mathbb Z$ and the endpoints $1/N,2/N$, since each has probability zero.\n\n\n###### Realizing the phases by circle characters.\n\nNow fix this $m$ and these trial phases. Successively choose $q_i$ as multiples of the preceding frequency so that $\\{q_i\\theta\\}$ is as close to $\\lambda_i$ as desired and $q_i/q_{i-1}$ is as large as desired. This is possible because the positive multiples of $q_{i-1}\\theta$ are dense. More formally, make a sequence of such finite block choices for which all new ratios tend to infinity and all $\\alpha_i\\to\\lambda_i$. The Haar law of $(u_i(x))_{i\\in J_j}$ then converges weakly to product Haar measure. For each fixed nonzero integer character vector, the last nonzero coefficient times its frequency dominates the sum of the earlier terms, once the ratios are sufficiently large. That Fourier coefficient is therefore exactly zero eventually. Trigonometric approximation proves the weak convergence. The joint law including the deterministic phases converges to product Haar in the coordinates and the fixed vector of trial phases.\n\nFor fixed $m$ the finitely many squared sampling tests are bounded functions of these coordinates and phases. Their discontinuities have zero limiting measure: every selected $\\{n\\lambda_i\\}$ avoids the phase cut, and for that phase only finitely many coordinate values can lie on a cutoff or interval boundary. Weak convergence therefore gives convergence of their integrals. This familiar extension from continuous tests follows, for example, by continuous upper and lower approximations outside open neighborhoods of the null discontinuity sets. It applies to the squared differences from one as well. Choose a sufficiently late finite block choice. Its sampling norms are below $1/j$, all the strict spread and angle bounds persist, and all frequency ratios determined so far satisfy their required bounds. The ratio connecting the previous block to this one can be made large enough to meet that block’s outstanding condition; the ratio at the end of this block is imposed at the next stage. This completes the induction. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }
      ],
      "id": "step-circle-parameter-selection",

      "method": "Select separated powers and successful independent trial phases, then approximate those phases by divisible characters of the prescribed rotation.",
      "output": "All phase, scale, attenuation and finite sampling requirements.",
      "proof_anchor": "proof-stmt-prop-parameters"

      "claim_anchor": "stmt-lem-scalar-multiplier-fields",
      "depends_on": [],
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-scalar-multiplier-fields",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-scalar-multiplier-fields</summary>\n\n*Proof.* If $B\\in B(L^2(Y,\\nu))$ commutes with all multipliers, put $b=B1$. For every measurable set $E$, $$B\\mathbf1_E=\\mathbf1_E b,\\qquad\n \\int_E|b|^2\\,d\\nu\\le\\|B\\|^2\\nu(E).$$ Thus $|b|\\le\\|B\\|$ almost everywhere. The identity $Bf=bf$ follows first for simple functions and then, by density, for all $f\\in L^2(Y)$. Apply this observation to the matrix entries of $A$ between the coordinate copies of $L^2(Y)$, obtaining measurable functions $a_{ij}$.\n\nLet $v$ range over the countable set of finitely supported vectors with rational real and imaginary parts. Commutation with $M_{\\mathbf1_E}$ and the operator bound give $$\\int_E\\sum_{i\\in\\mathbb Z}\n       \\left|\\sum_j a_{ij}(y)v_j\\right|^2d\\nu(y)\n \\le\\|A\\|^2\\|v\\|^2\\nu(E).$$ Consequently the integrand is at most $\\|A\\|^2\\|v\\|^2$ almost everywhere. Outside the union of these countably many null sets, the matrix defines an operator $A(y)$ on $K$ with norm at most $\\|A\\|$. Its columns, and then its values on every vector, are measurable by finite-coordinate approximation. The represented field agrees with $A$ on finite-coordinate simple functions, hence on all of $L^2(Y;K)$.\n\nEquality of two such fields follows by applying their represented operators to each constant basis field. Products act fiberwise by the representation formula, and the entries of the adjoint field are $\\overline{a_{ji}}$. This proves the assertions about products and adjoints. The identities $p=p^*=p^2$ then give orthogonal projections on a common conull set. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }
      ],
      "id": "step-measurable-field-decomposition",
      "inputs": [
        "Commutation with every scalar multiplier"
      ],
      "method": "Represent scalar matrix entries by multipliers and use a countable dense family of vectors to obtain a common fiberwise operator bound.",
      "output": "A unique measurable operator field with fiberwise products and adjoints.",
      "proof_anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-scalar-multiplier-fields"
    },
    {
      "claim_anchor": "stmt-lem-positive-diagonal",
      "depends_on": [
        "step-measurable-field-decomposition"

      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-positive-diagonal",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-positive-diagonal</summary>\n\n*Proof.* Put $r(y)=\\langle p(y)e_0,e_0\\rangle$ and $D_y=\\operatorname{diag}_k f(\\sigma^ky)$. Invariance and covariance give $D_y\\operatorname{ran}p(y)\\subseteq\\operatorname{ran}p(\\sigma y)$. If $r(\\sigma y)=0$, every vector in the latter range has zero zeroth coordinate. Since $(D_yv)_0=f(y)v_0$ and $f(y)>0$, every vector in the former range also has zero zeroth coordinate, so $r(y)=0$. If $r(y)=1$, then $e_0$ belongs to the former range, and its image $f(y)e_0$ shows that $r(\\sigma y)=1$.\n\nThus $\\sigma^{-1}\\{r=0\\}\\subseteq\\{r=0\\}$ and $\\{r=1\\}\\subseteq\\sigma^{-1}\\{r=1\\}$ modulo null sets. Probability preservation makes both inclusions equalities modulo null sets. Ergodicity makes each event null or conull. The trace formula and $0<\\tau(p)<1$ exclude both conull cases. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }
      ],
      "id": "step-strict-diagonal",
      "inputs": [
        "Covariant projection fields",
        "Positive almost-everywhere weight",
        "Intermediate trace"
      ],
      "method": "Propagate extreme diagonal events using positive weights and covariance; exclude conull extremes by ergodicity and trace.",
      "output": "Strictly interior central diagonal entries almost everywhere.",
      "proof_anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-positive-diagonal"
    },
    {
      "claim_anchor": "stmt-lem-compact-transfer",
      "depends_on": [],
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-compact-transfer",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-compact-transfer</summary>\n\n*Proof.* First replace every diagonal entry by its minimum with $B$, so that the diagonal field is bounded everywhere. Given an arbitrarily small $\\varepsilon>0$, simultaneous Lusin approximation supplies a compact $Q\\subset X$ on which every $F_k$ and every vector map $x\\mapsto p(x)e_k$ is continuous, while $\\mu(X\\setminus Q)$ is sufficiently small to make the $\\beta_l$-mass outside $Q^2$ less than $\\varepsilon$ for all $l$. For completeness, the simultaneous assertion follows by applying Lusin’s property to the countably many maps with summable exceptional measures and intersecting the resulting compact sets. The usual property for maps into separable metric spaces follows by finite-valued approximation and measure regularity.\n\nOn $Q^2$, the capped diagonal field is strongly continuous: continuity on the basis vectors and its uniform bound imply continuity on every vector. The same argument applies to $p(x)$ and $p(y)$. Products of strongly continuous, uniformly bounded fields are strongly continuous. Therefore the zero set $Z_Q$ of the capped version of <a href=\"#eq:compact-transfer-identity\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:compact-transfer-identity\">[eq:compact-transfer-identity]</a> in $Q^2$ is compact. The assumed identity implies $\\beta_l(Z_Q)\\ge\\beta_l(X^2)-\\varepsilon$. The compact-set upper bound under weak convergence and convergence of total masses give $$\\beta(Z_Q)\\ge\\limsup_l\\beta_l(Z_Q)\n \\ge\\beta(X^2)-\\varepsilon.$$ The capped global zero set thus has full $\\beta$-measure, since $\\varepsilon$ was arbitrary. It is measurable by its countably many matrix entries. Finally the cap changes no diagonal entry $\\beta$-almost everywhere. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }
      ],
      "id": "step-compact-limit-transfer",
      "inputs": [
        "Weakly convergent endpoint measures",
        "Uniformly absolutely continuous marginals",
        "A common positive ratio cap"
      ],
      "method": "Cap the diagonal, apply simultaneous compact-set continuity approximation, and use the compact-set bound under weak convergence.",
      "output": "The projection identity holds under the limiting measure.",
      "proof_anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-compact-transfer"
    },

      "claim_anchor": "stmt-lem-rational-graphs",
      "depends_on": [
        "step-circle-parameter-selection",
        "step-measurable-field-decomposition",
        "step-compact-limit-transfer"
      ],
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-rational-graphs",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-rational-graphs</summary>\n\n*Proof.* Fix $A,t$. In every sufficiently late stage $j$, put $a=d_{j-1}\\ge A$. Since $q_A$ divides $q_a$, the sampling set $\\mathcal N_{j,t}$ occurs in Proposition <a href=\"#prop:parameters\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prop:parameters\">8</a>. Write $w=w_{m_j}$ and $w^{(2)}=w_{m_j}^{(2)}$ for this stage’s kernels in <a href=\"#eq:sampling-kernel\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:sampling-kernel\">[eq:sampling-kernel]</a>. Average over $n\\in\\mathcal N_{j,t}$ the graph measures $x\\mapsto(x,\\sigma^n x)$ with starting density $$\\prod_{i\\in J_j}w\\bigl(u_i(x),\\{n\\alpha_i\\}\\bigr).$$ By <a href=\"#eq:sampling\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:sampling\">[eq:sampling]</a>, both marginal densities tend to $1$ in $L^2(\\mu)$. For the second marginal this uses the second orientation $w^{(2)}(z,v)=w(z-v,v)$. As every translation $n\\theta$ lies within $\\ell_j$ of $t$ and $\\ell_j\\to0$, testing continuous functions on $X^2$ shows that these measures converge weakly to the Haar-first graph measure of $x\\mapsto x+t$. At every point of positive density, $$0<\\{n\\alpha_i\\}<m_j^{-3/5}\\Delta(u_i(x))\\quad(i\\in J_j),$$ so there is no wrap and all current-block coordinates are ordered.\n\nFor every selected $n$, remove the same set of starting points, namely those satisfying at least one of $$\\begin{array}{ll}\n u_i(x)\\ge1-q_a\\ell_j & (A\\le i\\le a),\\\\\n u_i(x)\\ge1-2Q_j/N_s & (i\\in J_s,\\ s>j).\n \\end{array}$$ The removed Haar measure is at most $$a q_a\\ell_j+2Q_j\\sum_{s>j}K_s^{-1}\n \\le (j+1)^{-2}+4Q_j/K_{j+1}\\longrightarrow0.$$ The $L^2$ bound on the averaged first marginal and Cauchy–Schwarz show that the removed weighted mass tends to zero. Thus the trimmed measures have the same weak limit. Each trimmed marginal is dominated by its untrimmed counterpart, so both retain uniform $L^2$ bounds and hence uniform absolute continuity.\n\nFor $A\\le i\\le a$, the number $q_i t$ is integral. The coordinate advance is therefore $q_i\\{n\\theta-t\\}<q_a\\ell_j$, and trimming prevents a wrap. For future coordinates it is $n\\alpha_i<2Q_j/N_s$, again without a wrap. Together with the current block, this orders every coordinate $i\\ge A$. Hence, on all trimmed measures, $$R_{x,y}(v)\\le\n \\exp\\left(\\sum_{i<A}C_{s(i)}\\right)\\quad(v\\in\\mathbb Z),\n \\qquad i\\in J_{s(i)}.$$ The bound is independent of $j$ and $v$. It also holds on the limiting graph, because $u_i(x+t)=u_i(x)$ for $i\\ge A$. The positive-orbit identity holds on the approximants, so Lemma <a href=\"#lem:compact-transfer\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:compact-transfer\">11</a> gives <a href=\"#eq:rational-graph-identity\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:rational-graph-identity\">[eq:rational-graph-identity]</a>. There are only countably many pairs $(A,t)$ under consideration. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }
      ],

      "inputs": [
        "Circle sampling sets",

      "method": "Average weighted positive orbit graphs, trim wrap events, and pass to the rational-translation graph limit.",
      "output": "Simultaneous almost-everywhere identities on the specified rational graphs.",

      "claim_anchor": "stmt-lem-product-density-bound",
      "depends_on": [],
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-product-density-bound",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-product-density-bound</summary>\n\n*Proof.* The mean-one assumption gives $\\|h_i\\|_2^2=1+\\|h_i-1\\|_2^2$. Hence each finite product density obeys $$\\left\\|\\prod_{i=1}^n h_i\\right\\|_{L^2(\\mu)}^2\n =\\prod_{i=1}^n\\bigl(1+\\|h_i-1\\|_2^2\\bigr)\n \\le \\exp\\left(\\sum_{i=1}^n\\|h_i-1\\|_2^2\\right)\\le e^C.$$ For a bounded cylinder function $\\varphi$ depending on the first $n$ coordinates, integration under $\\nu$ is therefore bounded in absolute value by $e^{C/2}\\|\\varphi\\|_{L^2(\\mu)}$. These cylinder functions are dense in $L^2(\\mu)$, so the integration functional extends continuously to that space. The Riesz representation theorem gives a real $H\\in L^2(\\mu)$ with norm at most $e^{C/2}$ representing the extension. The finite signed measure $H\\mu$ and the probability measure $\\nu$ agree on all cylinder sets. Uniqueness of finite measures on the product sigma-algebra gives $H\\mu=\\nu$; thus $H\\ge0$ almost everywhere and $\\int H\\,d\\mu=1$. Applying the first assertion to $h_i=h_i^{\\epsilon_i}$ proves the hybrid assertion. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }

          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-digit-marginals",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-digit-marginals</summary>\n\n*Proof.* Either raw marginal density is at most $10$. For each width its top and bottom contributions together are at most $4\\rho_s(w)/w$, the tag contributes at most $2$, and the diagonal component contributes at most $1$.\n\n\nWe also need a sharper integral estimate. A uniform density on an interval of length at least $w/2$ differs from $1$ by a mean-zero function of squared $L^2$ norm at most $2/w$. For a tagged interval the corresponding bound is $2m_s$. Express a raw marginal density minus $1$ as the sum of its at most five weighted mean-zero perturbations. The finite-sum Cauchy–Schwarz inequality gives $$\\label{eq:one-digit-square}\n \\int_0^1 d_i(t)^2\\,dt\n \\le 1+50\\left(\\frac{\\kappa_s^2}{m_s}\n          +\\frac{\\mathbf 1_{\\{s=j\\}}}{m_s}\\right),\n \\qquad i\\in J_s.$$ In fact the four width terms contribute at most $40\\kappa_s^2/m_s$, and the tag at most $10/m_s$. Passing from raw variables to digits replaces $d_i$ by its average on each radix bin, so Jensen’s inequality preserves <a href=\"#eq:one-digit-square\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:one-digit-square\">[eq:one-digit-square]</a>.\n\nUse the uniform law at each digit as the reference probability measure. The pairs of digits are independent across coordinates, and each endpoint digit density has mean one. Since $|J_s|=m_s$, summing <a href=\"#eq:one-digit-square\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:one-digit-square\">[eq:one-digit-square]</a> minus one over the coordinates gives the common bound $50(\\sum_s\\kappa_s^2+1)$, for either endpoint and every deterministic choice between them. The initial digit has density one. Lemma <a href=\"#lem:product-density-bound\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:product-density-bound\">13</a> therefore gives the bound $$H_0^2:=\\exp\\left(50\\left(\\sum_{s\\ge1}\\kappa_s^2+1\\right)\\right)<\\infty.$$ Outside the null sets of ambiguous expansions, the mixed-radix map is a measure-preserving bijection from the reference digit space to $X$. Transporting the density through this map gives a Haar density with the same $L^2$ norm. This proves the assertion for both endpoints and all the deterministic hybrids. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }
      ],

          "anchor": "proof-stmt-prop-ordered-couplings",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-prop-ordered-couplings</summary>\n\n*Proof.* Fix $D$. The rational shifts $y^{(D)}-x$ have only finitely many possible values. Lemma <a href=\"#lem:rational-graphs\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:rational-graphs\">12</a> holds outside a common Haar-null set for all these shifts, so the absolute continuity of the $x$ marginal implies the required identity at $(x,y^{(D)})$. This remains valid even though the selected shift depends on the pair.\n\nThe coordinate order and <a href=\"#eq:ratio-crossing\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:ratio-crossing\">[eq:ratio-crossing]</a> bound every ratio by $1$, both for these pairs and for their limit $(x,y)$. Their measures converge weakly by the almost-sure convergence $y^{(D)}\\to y$. Lemma <a href=\"#lem:digit-marginals\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:digit-marginals\">14</a> supplies uniform $L^2$ bounds for both marginals. In particular their masses on a set of Haar measure $\\delta$ are at most $H_0\\sqrt\\delta$. Lemma <a href=\"#lem:compact-transfer\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:compact-transfer\">11</a> now passes the identity to the limit. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }

          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-conditional-labels",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-conditional-labels</summary>\n\n*Proof.* The entire endpoint determines all its digits almost surely. For any finite collection of labels, conditioning on all $x$-digits gives the product of the individual posteriors $$\\frac{\\mathbb P_j(\\text{label }\\ell\\text{ at }i)\\,\n       \\mathbb P_j(A_i=a\\mid\\text{label }\\ell\\text{ at }i)}\n      {\\mathbb P_j(A_i=a)}.$$ One verifies this formula first against cylinder tests in the digit sequence, using independence of coordinate pairs, and then against all bounded digit-measurable tests by the monotone class theorem. The common initial digit is independent of those pairs. This proves conditional independence given $x$; the proof for $y$ is the same. In particular, the dependence of $u_i(x)$ on later digits does not change the label posteriors after the entire endpoint has been conditioned on.\n\nThe parameters satisfy $$\\frac1{r_i^*}\\le\\frac1{N_s}<\\frac w2.$$ If $u_i(x)<u\\le1-w$, the whole radix bin containing $X_i$ lies in $[0,1-w/2)$. On that bin the top component’s $X_i$-density is at least $1$, while the total raw marginal density is at most $10$. Its posterior probability is therefore at least $\\rho_s(w)/10$. Under this label, <a href=\"#eq:coordinate-order\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:coordinate-order\">[eq:coordinate-order]</a> gives $$u_i(y)\\ge1-w/2-1/r_i^*>1-w\\ge u.$$ For the second assertion, $u_i(y)\\ge u\\ge w$ places the entire $Y_i$ bin in $[w/2,1)$. The bottom component again has density at least $1$ there, so its posterior is at least $\\rho_s(w)/10$. Its $X_i$ value satisfies $$u_i(x)\\le w/2+1/r_i^*<w\\le u.$$ These estimates include thresholds at radix-bin endpoints. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }
      ],

      "proof_anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-conditional-labels"

      "depends_on": [

      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-conditional-convolution",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-conditional-convolution</summary>\n\n*Proof.* The multiplier bound in <a href=\"#eq:generic-convolution\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:generic-convolution\">[eq:generic-convolution]</a> and Cauchy–Schwarz make every convolution there absolutely convergent. Put $$c_n=\\sum_{\\substack{|v|\\le K\\\\|n+v|\\le K}}A_vB_{-n-v}\n       \\quad(0\\le n\\le2K).$$ On $E$, Lemma <a href=\"#lem:polynomial-coefficient\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:polynomial-coefficient\">7</a> applied to the two truncated Laurent polynomials gives $\\sum_{n=0}^{2K}|c_n|\\ge\\varepsilon(K,D,a)$. Its expected value on $E$ is therefore at least $c\\varepsilon(K,D,a)$.\n\n\nFix $n$. For an omitted index $v$, choose $k(v)=v$ if $|v|>K$, and $k(v)=n+v$ otherwise. This is a deterministic choice with $|k(v)|>K$. The zero convolution and the two multiplier bounds give $$\\label{eq:masked-tail}\n \\mathbf1_E|c_n|\\le\\eta+\n \\sum_{b=1}^2\\sum_{v\\text{ omitted}}\n |\\xi_v(x)\\gamma_{n+v}(y)|\\mathbf1_{H_{k(v)}^{(b)}}.$$ The unmasked sum of products of vector coordinates is at most one, which accounts for $\\eta$. We dropped $\\mathbf1_E$ from the nonnegative exceptional terms so that their full-law conditional bounds remain applicable.\n\nFor the first exceptional sum, Cauchy–Schwarz on probability times counting measure bounds its expectation by $$\\left(\\mathbb E\\sum_{v\\text{ omitted}}\n       |\\xi_v(x)|^2\\mathbf1_{H_{k(v)}^{(1)}}\\right)^{1/2}\n \\left(\\mathbb E\\sum_{v\\text{ omitted}}\n       |\\gamma_{n+v}(y)|^2\\right)^{1/2}\n \\le\\sqrt\\delta.$$ Indeed, condition the first factor’s square on $x$, apply <a href=\"#eq:generic-tail\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:generic-tail\">[eq:generic-tail]</a> to each deterministic $k(v)$, and use $\\sum_v|\\xi_v(x)|^2\\le1$. The second factor is at most one. For the other exceptional sum put its indicator with $|\\gamma_{n+v}(y)|^2$ and condition on $y$, giving the same bound. Tonelli’s theorem justifies every interchange of the nonnegative sums and integrals. No sum of the individual exceptional probabilities is required. Taking expectations in <a href=\"#eq:masked-tail\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:masked-tail\">[eq:masked-tail]</a> and summing in $n$ proves <a href=\"#eq:generic-obstruction\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:generic-obstruction\">[eq:generic-obstruction]</a>. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }
      ],

      "claim_anchor": "stmt-lem-truncation-bound",
      "depends_on": [
        "step-strict-diagonal",

      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-truncation-bound",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-truncation-bound</summary>\n\n*Proof.* At a current-block coordinate, a tag has probability $1/m$ and satisfies both endpoint exclusions. Since each raw marginal density is at most $10$, the probability of an inadmissible outcome is at most $60/m$. Thus a nontagged admissible outcome has probability at least $1-61/m$. Independence across the block shows that exactly one tag and admissible nontagged outcomes at the other coordinates have probability at least $$m\\frac1m(1-61/m)^{m-1}\\ge e^{-90}\\qquad(m\\ge200).$$ For example, the last estimate follows from $\\log(1-t)\\ge-t/(1-t)$ with $t=61/m$.\n\nThe total probability excluded by the future-block inequalities is at most $$60K_j\\sum_{s>j}\\frac1{K_s}\n \\le\\frac{120K_j}{K_{j+1}}\\longrightarrow0.$$ By <a href=\"#eq:diagonal-interior\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:diagonal-interior\">[eq:diagonal-interior]</a>, $0<r<1$ Haar-almost everywhere. Lemma <a href=\"#lem:digit-marginals\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:digit-marginals\">14</a> therefore bounds the probability lost from the last line of the definition of $E_j$ by $$H_0\\mu\\{r<1/j\\}^{1/2}\n +H_0\\mu\\{1-r<1/j\\}^{1/2}\\longrightarrow0.$$ Consequently $c_*=e^{-90}/2$ works for all sufficiently large $j$.\n\nOn $E_j$, rounding changes the raw endpoint inequalities by at most $1/N_s$. Since $K\\ge1$, they imply $$u_i(x)<1-2K/N_s,\\qquad u_i(y)>2K/N_s\n       \\quad(i\\in J_s,\\ s\\ge j).$$ For $0\\le v\\le K$, the bound $\\alpha_i<2/N_s$ makes the floors of $u_i(x)+v\\alpha_i$ and $u_i(y)-v\\alpha_i$ zero in every such block. Earlier-block floors have absolute value at most $K+1$. Hence $$1/F_v(x)\\le D_j,\n \\qquad F_{-v}(y)\\le D_j\\quad(0\\le v\\le K).$$ For $v<0$ these two quantities are at most $1$, by monotonicity and $F_0=1$. Since the vector coordinates have modulus at most $1$, all coefficients of the Laurent polynomials $$A^{[K]}(z)=\\sum_{|v|\\le K}A_vz^v,\n \\qquad B^{[K]}(z)=\\sum_{|v|\\le K}B_vz^v$$ have modulus at most $D_j$. Their constant coefficients are respectively $r(x)$ and $1-r(y)$, both at least $1/j$ on $E_j$. The coefficient of $z^{-n}$ in their product is $c_{j,n}$. The polynomial bound <a href=\"#eq:polynomial\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:polynomial\">[eq:polynomial]</a> now gives $\\sum_{n=0}^{2K}|c_{j,n}|\\ge\\varepsilon_j$ on $E_j$. Integration proves the second assertion. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }
      ],

          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-tail-attenuation",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-tail-attenuation</summary>\n\n*Proof.* First suppose $K_j<|k|\\le N_j/4$. A tagged coordinate in $J_j$ satisfies, after rounding, $$u_i(x)<1/m_j,\\qquad u_i(y)>1-1/m_j.$$ Here $\\beta=|k|\\alpha_i$ lies in $(1/m_j,1/2)$. The threshold $1-\\{k\\alpha_i\\}$ equals $1-\\beta$ for $k>0$ and $\\beta$ for $k<0$. It is crossed in either case, so <a href=\"#eq:ratio-crossing\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:ratio-crossing\">[eq:ratio-crossing]</a> gives $R_{x,y}(k)\\le e^{-C_j}\\le e^{-L_j}$ on $E_j$. Set both exceptional events to be empty in this range.\n\nWe next cover all larger $|k|$. Put $M_s=m_s^{(4/5)m_s}$. The intervals $$[N_j/4,M_j],\\qquad [G_sK_s,M_s]\\quad(s>j)$$ cover $[N_j/4,\\infty)$. Indeed the next left endpoint is below the preceding right endpoint by <a href=\"#eq:scales\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:scales\">[eq:scales]</a>, the right endpoints tend to infinity, and $G_sK_s\\le N_s/4$ because $m_s\\ge4G_s$. Split each later interval at $N_s/4$. In its lower part the thresholds belong to $[a_s,1-a_s]$ at every coordinate of $J_s$. In its upper part, <a href=\"#eq:spread\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:spread\">[eq:spread]</a> places them in $[b_s,1-b_s]$ at at least $m_s/20$ coordinates. The initial interval uses the upper-part assertion for $s=j$.\n\n\nThus for each fixed $k$ in this tail we can choose, deterministically, a block $J_s$, a width $w\\in\\{a_s,b_s\\}$, and a set $I\\subset J_s$ of at least $m_s/20$ indices such that $$u_i^*:=1-\\{k\\alpha_i\\}\\in[w,1-w]\\quad(i\\in I).$$ The lower-part choice always has $s>j$. In either type of choice, $$\\label{eq:crossing-intensity}\n m_s\\rho_s(w)\\ge L_j,\n \\qquad C_s\\ge L_j.$$ For $w=b_s$ the first quantity is $\\kappa_sm_s^{1/40}\\ge L_s\\ge L_j$; for $w=a_s$ it is $\\kappa_s\\sqrt{G_s}\\ge L_{s-1}\\ge L_j$.\n\nLet $H_k$ be the event of no crossing at any index in $I$, and define $$H_k^{(1)}=H_k\\cap\n    \\left\\{\\#\\{i\\in I:u_i(x)<u_i^*\\}\\ge|I|/2\\right\\},\n \\qquad H_k^{(2)}=H_k\\setminus H_k^{(1)}.$$ Conditional on $x$, the first event forbids the top label at every index in the indicated $x$-measurable set. Conditional independence and Lemma <a href=\"#lem:conditional-labels\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:conditional-labels\">16</a> therefore give $$\\mathbb P_j(H_k^{(1)}\\mid x)\n \\le(1-\\rho_s(w)/10)^{|I|/2}\n \\le e^{-m_s\\rho_s(w)/400}\\le e^{-L_j/400}.$$ If the $x$-measurable set has fewer than $|I|/2$ elements, the event is empty, so the same bound holds.\n\nFor the other conditional estimate, note explicitly that coordinate order and the absence of crossings imply $$u_i(x)<u_i^*\\quad\\Longleftrightarrow\\quad u_i(y)<u_i^*\n \\qquad(i\\in I).$$ Thus $H_k^{(2)}$ requires at least $|I|/2$ indices with $u_i(y)\\ge u_i^*$. Conditional on $y$, the bottom labels at all those indices are forbidden. The same product estimate proves the second bound in <a href=\"#eq:conditional-bad-events\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:conditional-bad-events\">[eq:conditional-bad-events]</a>. These arguments use the original law, without conditioning on $E_j$. Figure <a href=\"#fig:conditioning\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"fig:conditioning\">1</a> displays the complementary split.\n\n<figure id=\"fig:conditioning\">\n\n<figcaption>The two parts of the no-crossing event are small after conditioning on complementary endpoints. Each estimate controls the matching masked square sum in Lemma <a href=\"#lem:conditional-convolution\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:conditional-convolution\">17</a>; no simultaneous avoidance over all indices is required.</figcaption>\n</figure>\n\n\n**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:conditioning`)\n\nTikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:\n\n```latex\n\\begin{tikzpicture}[\n  box/.style={draw,rounded corners,align=center,text width=5.3cm,\n              minimum height=1.1cm,inner sep=6pt},\n  arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=2mm]},thick}]\n\\node[box,text width=8.2cm] (b) at (0,0)\n {$H_k$: no listed threshold is crossed\\\\\n  On this event, $u_i(x)<u_i^*\\iff u_i(y)<u_i^*$.};\n\\node[box] (x) at (-3.1,-2)\n {$H_k^{(1)}$: at least $|I|/2$ of the $u_i(x)$ lie below $u_i^*$};\n\\node[box] (y) at (3.1,-2)\n {$H_k^{(2)}$: more than $|I|/2$ of the $u_i(y)$ lie above or at $u_i^*$};\n\\node[box] (cx) at (-3.1,-4)\n {Condition on all of $x$;\\\\\n  top labels can cross.\\\\\n  $\\mathbb P_j(H_k^{(1)}\\mid x)\\le e^{-L_j/400}$};\n\\node[box] (cy) at (3.1,-4)\n {Condition on all of $y$;\\\\\n  bottom labels can cross.\\\\\n  $\\mathbb P_j(H_k^{(2)}\\mid y)\\le e^{-L_j/400}$};\n\\draw[arr] (b.south) -- (x.north);\n\\draw[arr] (b.south) -- (y.north);\n\\draw[arr] (x) -- (cx);\n\\draw[arr] (y) -- (cy);\n\\end{tikzpicture}\n```\n\nVisual witness description of PDF page 22 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):\n\n```text\nFlowchart diagram showing the decomposition of the event $H_k$ into two branches across three levels:\n- Top level: A single rectangular box containing:\n  \"$H_k$: no listed threshold is crossed\"\n  \"On this event, $u_i(x) < u_i^* \\iff u_i(y) < u_i^*$.\"\n- Middle level: Two rectangular boxes connected from the top box by branching arrows:\n  - Left branch arrow points to box: \"$H_k^{(1)}$: at least $|I|/2$ of the $u_i(x)$ lie below $u_i^*$\"\n  - Right branch arrow points to box: \"$H_k^{(2)}$: more than $|I|/2$ of the $u_i(y)$ lie above or at $u_i^*$\"\n- Bottom level: Two rectangular boxes connected by vertical downward arrows from the middle boxes:\n  - Left box below $H_k^{(1)}$: \"Condition on all of $x$; top labels can cross.\" and \"$\\mathbb{P}_j(H_k^{(1)} \\mid x) \\le e^{-L_j/400}$\"\n  - Right box below $H_k^{(2)}$: \"Condition on all of $y$; bottom labels can cross.\" and \"$\\mathbb{P}_j(H_k^{(2)} \\mid y) \\le e^{-L_j/400}$\"\n```\n\n\nOutside $H_k$, at least one chosen coordinate crosses. All coordinates are ordered, so <a href=\"#eq:ratio-crossing\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:ratio-crossing\">[eq:ratio-crossing]</a> and <a href=\"#eq:crossing-intensity\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:crossing-intensity\">[eq:crossing-intensity]</a> give $R_{x,y}(k)\\le e^{-L_j}$. On $H_k$ the ratio is at most $1$. This proves <a href=\"#eq:tail-ratio-bound\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:tail-ratio-bound\">[eq:tail-ratio-bound]</a> and the lemma. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }
      ],

      "claim_anchor": "stmt-thm-measurable",
      "depends_on": [

      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "pm-h-25",
          "exact_quote": "#### Completion of the contradiction\n\nApply Lemma <a href=\"#lem:conditional-convolution\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:conditional-convolution\">17</a> to the labelled law $\\mathbb P_j$, with $$K=K_j,\\quad D=D_j,\\quad a=1/j,\\quad c=c_* =e^{-90}/2,\n \\qquad \\eta=e^{-L_j},\\quad \\delta=e^{-L_j/400}.$$ Equations <a href=\"#eq:full-convolution\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:full-convolution\">[eq:full-convolution]</a> and <a href=\"#eq:ratio-minimum\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:ratio-minimum\">[eq:ratio-minimum]</a> verify its algebraic assumptions. The proof of Lemma <a href=\"#lem:truncation-bound\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:truncation-bound\">19</a> supplies the event of mass at least $c_*$ and all central-coefficient bounds for sufficiently large $j$. Lemma <a href=\"#lem:tail-attenuation\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:tail-attenuation\">20</a> gives the tail estimate with conditional probabilities under the full law. Consequently $$c_*\\varepsilon_j\n \\le(2K_j+1)\\bigl(e^{-L_j}+2e^{-L_j/800}\\bigr)\n \\le\\frac{\\varepsilon_j}{j},$$ where the last inequality is the parameter choice <a href=\"#eq:polynomial\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:polynomial\">[eq:polynomial]</a>. Since $\\varepsilon_j>0$ and eventually $1/j<c_*$, this is impossible. This proves Theorem <a href=\"#thm:measurable\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"thm:measurable\">18</a>.",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }

        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-affiliated-transport",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-affiliated-transport</summary>\n\n*Proof.* Every commutant unitary preserves $\\operatorname{ran}p$ and $\\operatorname{Dom}H$, and commutes with $H$ on that domain. It therefore maps the displayed closed space onto itself. Its projection commutes with the commutant, proving $P\\in\\mathcal M$. If $\\zeta\\in\\operatorname{ran}p\\cap\\operatorname{Dom}H$, both invariance assumptions give $A\\zeta\\in\\operatorname{ran}p\\cap\\operatorname{Dom}H$, and $BH\\zeta=HA\\zeta$. Thus $B$ preserves the unclosed image and, by boundedness, its closure.\n\nIt remains to prove the trace assertion; bounded similarity cannot be assumed. Put $E_N=\\mathbf1_{[1/N,N]}(H)$. Since $H$ is injective, $E_N\\uparrow1$. For $q_N=p\\wedge E_N$, the bounded element $Hq_N=(HE_N)q_N$ of $\\mathcal M$ is bounded below by $1/N$ on its initial space. Its image is closed, and its polar decomposition identifies the initial projection $q_N$ with its final projection, which is dominated by $P$. Therefore $$\\tau(P)\\ge\\tau(p\\wedge E_N)\n \\ge\\tau(p)+\\tau(E_N)-1.$$ The last inequality follows directly from the polar decomposition of $(1-E_N)p$: its initial support is $p-(p\\wedge E_N)$ and its final support is at most $1-E_N$. Letting $N$ increase gives $\\tau(P)\\ge\\tau(p)$.\n\nFor the reverse inequality, set $q'_N=(1-p)\\wedge E_N$. The bounded operator $H^{-1}q'_N=(H^{-1}E_N)q'_N$ is likewise bounded below on its initial space and has closed image. This image is orthogonal to $\\operatorname{ran}P$. Indeed, for $\\eta\\in\\operatorname{ran}q'_N$ and $\\zeta\\in\\operatorname{ran}p\\cap\\operatorname{Dom}H$, self-adjointness gives $$\\langle H^{-1}\\eta,H\\zeta\\rangle=\\langle\\eta,\\zeta\\rangle=0;$$ here $H^{-1}\\eta$ lies in $\\operatorname{Dom}H$. Polar decomposition and the same meet estimate yield $$\\tau(1-P)\\ge\\tau((1-p)\\wedge E_N)\n \\ge\\tau(1-p)+\\tau(E_N)-1.$$ Passing to the limit proves the opposite inequality for $\\tau(P)$. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }
      ],
