        {
          "anchor": "eq:continuous-coboundary",
          "exact_quote": "We claim that there exist a nonnegative function $g$, finite and continuous on $X\\setminus\\{0\\}$ and tending to infinity at $0$, and an almost everywhere finite real measurable function $b$ such that $$\\label{eq:continuous-coboundary}\n g=g_*+1+b\\circ\\sigma-b\\quad\\text{almost everywhere},\n \\qquad \\int_Xg\\,d\\mu=\\infty.$$ The construction has three parts: smooth each drop, move its support into a shrinking arc, and add an integrable background cost that guarantees divergence at the center of those arcs. Apart from the constant $1$, all changes will be explicit coboundaries.",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        },
        {
          "anchor": "pm-h-30",
          "exact_quote": "#### Conclusion and spectral consequences\n\n\nDefine the function on the unit circle by $$f(e^{2\\pi i x})=e^{-g(x)},\\qquad e^{-\\infty}=0.$$ It is continuous, is positive away from $1$, and vanishes at $1$. Its modulus is nonconstant and its zero set has Haar measure zero. Moreover, $$\\label{eq:zero-log-integral}\n \\int_{\\mathbb T}\\log f\\,dm=-\\infty.$$ In particular, its Fuglede–Kadison determinant is zero (Fuglede and Kadison 1952) [[fuglede-kadison](#ref-fuglede-kadison)]: the spectral distribution of $\\pi(f)$ is the Haar distribution of $f$, so the determinant definition is precisely the exponential of <a href=\"#eq:zero-log-integral\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:zero-log-integral\">[eq:zero-log-integral]</a>. Also $|U\\pi(f)|=\\pi(f)$, which gives the same determinant for $T_f=U\\pi(f)$. This conclusion uses the nonnegative integral calculation <a href=\"#eq:infinite-cost-integral\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:infinite-cost-integral\">[eq:infinite-cost-integral]</a>; it does not require the transfer $b$ to be integrable.\n\nChoose a finite Borel version of $b$ and discard all integer translates of the null set where the coboundary identity fails. Let $H_b$ be multiplication on the $k$th fiber coordinate by $e^{b(\\sigma^k x)}$, with its natural square-integrability domain. It is positive, self-adjoint, and injective. Its spectral projections are $\\pi$-images of spectral indicators of $e^b$, hence lie in $M$. Thus $H_b$ is affiliated with $M$. The coboundary identity gives the pointwise factor equality $$e^{b(\\sigma^{k+1}x)}e^{-g(\\sigma^k x)}\n       =e^{-1}f_*(\\sigma^k x)e^{b(\\sigma^k x)}.$$ For every $\\zeta\\in\\operatorname{Dom}H_b$, the right-hand side defines the $k+1$ coordinate of $e^{-1}T_*H_b\\zeta$, a square-integrable vector because $T_*$ is bounded. Therefore $$T_f\\zeta\\in\\operatorname{Dom}H_b,\n \\qquad H_bT_f\\zeta=e^{-1}T_*H_b\\zeta.$$ Lemma <a href=\"#lem:affiliated-transport\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:affiliated-transport\">21</a> sends any invariant projection of $T_f$ to an invariant projection of $e^{-1}T_*$ having the same trace. Multiplication by the nonzero scalar $e^{-1}$ does not change invariant ranges. Theorem <a href=\"#thm:measurable\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"thm:measurable\">18</a> therefore excludes traces strictly between $0$ and $1$. This proves Theorem <a href=\"#thm:main\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"thm:main\">1</a> for the arbitrary irrational $\\theta$ fixed at the start.\n\nThe same continuous weight has a stronger spectral property, which we record with an elementary proof. This is the zero-determinant case of Zhu, Fang, and Shi (2017, Theorem 2.3) [[zfs](#ref-zfs)]. We include the truncated-logarithm argument, which uses uniform averaging under irrational rotation.\n\n\n<div id=\"cor:quasinilpotent\" class=\"corollary\">\n\n**Corollary 22**. *The constructed operator $T_f$ is nonzero and quasinilpotent.*\n\n</div>\n\n<details id=\"proof-stmt-cor-quasinilpotent\"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-quasinilpotent</summary>\n\n*Proof.* For $M>0$, the function $\\ell_M(x)=\\log\\max(f(e^{2\\pi i x}),e^{-M})$ is continuous. Its integrals decrease to $-\\infty$ by <a href=\"#eq:zero-log-integral\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:zero-log-integral\">[eq:zero-log-integral]</a>. Irrational rotation averages of a continuous function converge uniformly to its integral: this follows for Fourier monomials from their geometric sums and then for continuous functions by uniform trigonometric approximation. Hence, for any $a>0$, first choosing $M$ with $\\int_X\\ell_M\\,d\\mu<-2a$ and then taking $n$ sufficiently large gives $$\\sup_{x\\in X}\\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}f(e^{2\\pi i\\sigma^k x})\n \\le\\sup_{x\\in X}\\exp\\left(\\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\\ell_M(\\sigma^k x)\\right)\n \\le e^{-an}.$$ The expression on the left is $\\|T_f^n\\|$, because the power is a unitary $U^n$ times multiplication by that continuous product. The spectral-radius formula yields radius zero, since $a$ was arbitrary. Finally $T_f\\ne0$ because $f>0$ almost everywhere. ◻\n\n</details>",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }
      ],

          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-cor-quasinilpotent</summary>\n\n*Proof.* For $M>0$, the function $\\ell_M(x)=\\log\\max(f(e^{2\\pi i x}),e^{-M})$ is continuous. Its integrals decrease to $-\\infty$ by <a href=\"#eq:zero-log-integral\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:zero-log-integral\">[eq:zero-log-integral]</a>. Irrational rotation averages of a continuous function converge uniformly to its integral: this follows for Fourier monomials from their geometric sums and then for continuous functions by uniform trigonometric approximation. Hence, for any $a>0$, first choosing $M$ with $\\int_X\\ell_M\\,d\\mu<-2a$ and then taking $n$ sufficiently large gives $$\\sup_{x\\in X}\\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}f(e^{2\\pi i\\sigma^k x})\n \\le\\sup_{x\\in X}\\exp\\left(\\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\\ell_M(\\sigma^k x)\\right)\n \\le e^{-an}.$$ The expression on the left is $\\|T_f^n\\|$, because the power is a unitary $U^n$ times multiplication by that continuous product. The spectral-radius formula yields radius zero, since $a$ was arbitrary. Finally $T_f\\ne0$ because $f>0$ almost everywhere. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"

          "anchor": "proof-stmt-cor-transitive-commutant",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-cor-transitive-commutant</summary>\n\n*Proof.* The tracial representation of $R_\\theta$ is separable and infinite-dimensional. Apply Theorem <a href=\"#thm:main\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"thm:main\">1</a>, Corollary <a href=\"#cor:quasinilpotent\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"cor:quasinilpotent\">22</a>, and Lemma <a href=\"#lem:hyperinvariant-projection\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:hyperinvariant-projection\">3</a> there. A nonzero quasinilpotent operator is nonscalar, so Lemma <a href=\"#lem:commutant-algebra\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:commutant-algebra\">2</a> applies. Unitary equivalence transfers these properties to any separable infinite-dimensional complex Hilbert space. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }

      "claim_anchor": "stmt-p-prop-phases",
      "depends_on": [

      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-p-prop-phases",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-p-prop-phases</summary>\n\n*Proof.* In the first range, put $t=|k|\\theta_i$. The angle bounds give $$a_j=G_j/m_j\\le t\\le1/2.$$ For $k>0$ the threshold is $1-t$, and for $k<0$ it is $t$. Both belong to $[a_j,1-a_j]$ because $a_j\\le1/4$.\n\nFor the second range, the uniform law of $k\\theta_i$ on an interval of length $|k|/N_j$ has, after reduction modulo one, a density bounded by $$1+N_j/|k|\\le5.$$ This follows by counting the integer translates covering a point: the covering multiplicity of an interval of length $r$ is at most $r+1$. The statement also applies to negative $k$ by reflection. Thus a given coordinate misses $[b_j,1-b_j]$ with probability at most $10b_j$. If fewer than $m/20$ coordinates satisfy the assertion, more than $19m/20$ coordinates miss the interval. Independence over $i\\in J_j$ and a union bound over the possible sets of misses show that, for a fixed $k$, this failure probability is at most $2^m(10b_j)^{(19/20)m}$ for large $m$. A further union bound over the at most $2m^{(4/5)m}$ nonzero integers in the range gives $$\\mathbb P(\\text{failure at stage }j)\n \\le2m^{(4/5)m}2^m(10b_j)^{(19/20)m}\n =\\exp\\left(-\\frac{41}{400}m\\log m+O(m)\\right).$$ These probabilities are summable because $m_j\\ge2^j$. The first Borel–Cantelli Lemma now gives the second assertion for all sufficiently large $j$, simultaneously for every integer in each stated range. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }
      ],

    {
      "claim_anchor": "stmt-p-prop-sampling",
      "depends_on": [
        "step-scalar-kernel-estimates",

      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-p-prop-sampling",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-p-prop-sampling</summary>\n\n*Proof.* We give the construction and its estimates before choosing the probability-one event. The notation $O(\\cdot)$ in this proof has absolute constants, uniform in the coordinate, the selected powers, and the interval endpoints involved in a rectangle test.\n\nWe average many separated orbit powers so that both marginal densities approach those of an ideal model with independent uniform phases. The coordinate weights impose triangular laws on $I$ and concentrate near the diagonal elsewhere, while their marginal bounds remain uniform in the growing product. A final restriction to ordered tail coordinates removes asymptotically negligible mass.\n\n\n###### Separated powers and phase discrepancy\n\nTake $l$ sufficiently large that $m_l\\ge200$, as required by the scalar lemma. At stage $l$ write $m=m_l$, $d=d_l$, and set $$Q=m^{(7/10)m},\\qquad H=m^{300},\\qquad R=N_lm^{1000}.$$ For all sufficiently large $l$ we can choose, deterministically, a set $\\mathcal N_l$ of integers in $[Q/2,Q]$ such that $$\\label{p:eq:sampling-separation}\n \\begin{gathered}\n P_l:=|\\mathcal N_l|\\ge m^{0.69m},\\\\\n |vn+v'n'|>R\\quad\n \\left(\\begin{array}{l}\n n,n'\\in\\mathcal N_l,\\ n\\ne n',\\\\\n v,v'\\in\\mathbb Z,\\ |v|,|v'|\\le H,\\ (v,v')\\ne(0,0)\n \\end{array}\\right).\n \\end{gathered}$$ Indeed, given a previously selected $n$, a fixed frequency pair with $v'\\ne0$ forbids an interval of possible $n'$ of length $2R/|v'|\\le2R$, hence at most $2R+2$ integers. There are $O(H^2)$ frequency pairs. Since $N_l\\le m^2$, each selection therefore excludes at most $O(m^{1602})$ integers. When $v'=0$ and $v\\ne0$, the inequality is automatic because $n\\ge Q/2>R$ eventually; likewise, a zero first frequency creates no obstruction. The constraints are symmetric in the selected powers, since the full set of frequency pairs is used. Greedy selection from the $\\asymp Q$ available integers yields at least $cQ/m^{1602}\\ge m^{0.69m}$ choices for large $m$.\n\n\n\n\nFor a coordinate $i\\in J_s$ with $s\\le l$, the angle $\\theta_i$ is uniform on $[1/N_s,2/N_s]$. Apply the separated-phase part of Lemma <a href=\"#lem:scalar-sampling-estimates\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:scalar-sampling-estimates\">6</a> with $$N_0=N_s\\le N_l=N\\le m^2.$$ The powers just selected satisfy its separation hypothesis. Thus $t_n=\\{n\\theta_i\\}$ has a density $\\varphi_{i,n}$ satisfying $$\\label{p:eq:single-phase-density}\n \\|\\varphi_{i,n}-1\\|_\\infty\\le N_s/n\\le N_l/n.$$ In particular, that density is at most two for large $l$. For distinct selected powers and functions $g,g'$ of bounded variation, the same lemma gives $$\\label{p:eq:bv-discrepancy}\n \\begin{split}\n \\left|\\mathbb E_\\theta g(t_n)g'(t_{n'})\n       -\\left(\\int_0^1g\\right)\\left(\\int_0^1g'\\right)\\right|\n &\\le c m^{-80}\n   (\\|g\\|_\\infty+\\operatorname{Var}g)\n   (\\|g'\\|_\\infty+\\operatorname{Var}g').\n \\end{split}$$ No independence between $t_n$ and $t_{n'}$ is asserted. The imported estimate uses their Fourier coefficients and one-dimensional marginal densities, and so applies even when their joint law is singular. We shall also use the direct consequence of <a href=\"#p:eq:single-phase-density\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:single-phase-density\">[p:eq:single-phase-density]</a> $$\\label{p:eq:single-phase-test}\n \\left|\\mathbb E_\\theta g(t_n)-\\int_0^1g(t)\\,dt\\right|\n \\le\\frac{N_l}{n}\\int_0^1|g(t)|\\,dt.$$\n\n\n###### The weighted measures and both marginal orientations\n\nFix a finite $I$ and restrict to sufficiently large $l$ that $I\\subset\\{1,\\ldots,d\\}$. Set $$h=m^{-3/5},\\qquad \\Delta(s)=\\min(s,1-s),\\qquad \\alpha=m^{-2}.$$ For $0\\le s,t<1$ define $$\\label{p:eq:sampling-weights}\n w_i(s,t)=\n \\begin{cases}\n \\mathbf 1_{\\{0<t<1-s\\}},&i\\in I,\\\\[1mm]\n \\displaystyle\\mathbf 1_{\\{\\Delta(s)\\ge\\alpha\\}}\n \\frac{\\mathbf 1_{\\{0<t<h\\Delta(s)\\}}}{h\\Delta(s)},&i\\notin I.\n \\end{cases}$$ The second line is defined to be zero if $\\Delta(s)<\\alpha$. Let $\\widetilde\\eta_{I,l}$ be the average, over $n\\in\\mathcal N_l$, of the push-forward under $x\\mapsto(x,\\sigma_\\theta^n x)$ of $$\\label{p:eq:untrimmed-weight}\n W_n(x)\\,d\\mu(x),\\qquad\n W_n(x)=\\prod_{i\\le d}w_i(x_i,\\{n\\theta_i\\}).$$ Each weight is bounded for fixed $l$, so these are finite positive measures. They are carried by pairs satisfying $x_i<y_i$ for $i\\le d$, since $h\\Delta(x_i)\\le h(1-x_i)<1-x_i$ and $h<1$.\n\nWe will treat the marginals both without a test and after restriction to a cylinder rectangle $$\\mathcal R=\\{(x,y):x_i\\in E_i^x,\\ y_i\\in E_i^y\n                         \\text{ for every }i\\},$$ where the $E_i^x,E_i^y$ are intervals with rational endpoints and all but finitely many are the whole circle. Take $l$ large enough that all tested coordinates are at most $d$. A missing test is represented by using the whole circle for every interval. For the first and second marginal, respectively, define $$\\label{p:eq:oriented-weights}\n \\begin{split}\n u_i^{(1)}(s,t)\n &=w_i(s,t)\\mathbf 1_{E_i^x}(s)\\mathbf 1_{E_i^y}(\\{s+t\\}),\\\\\n u_i^{(2)}(s,t)\n &=w_i(\\{s-t\\},t)\\mathbf 1_{E_i^x}(\\{s-t\\})\\mathbf 1_{E_i^y}(s).\n \\end{split}$$ Changing variables $y=\\sigma_\\theta^n x$ preserves Haar measure. It follows that the two tested marginal densities are exactly the averages of the products of the corresponding $u_i^{(r)}$. There is no factor from coordinates past $d$, even in the second orientation, since their unweighted Haar law is translation invariant.\n\n\nWe check the extra factors introduced by triangular laws and interval tests. Write $u_i$ for either orientation and put $$\\bar u_i(s)=\\int_0^1u_i(s,t)\\,dt,\\qquad\n A_i=\\int_0^1\\bar u_i(s)^2\\,ds.$$ Uniformly in $s$, the coordinate, and the test intervals, we have $$\\label{p:eq:weight-variation}\n \\|u_i(s,\\cdot)\\|_\\infty+\n       \\operatorname{Var}_t u_i(s,\\cdot)\n =O(m^{13/5})=O(m^{10}).$$ For an untested triangular factor, the bound is $O(1)$: in the first orientation its support in $t$ is $0<t<1-s$, and in the second it is $0<t\\le s$. The possible value at $t=s$ affects no integral. For the untested near-diagonal factors, Lemma <a href=\"#lem:scalar-sampling-estimates\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:scalar-sampling-estimates\">6</a> gives the bound $O(m^{13/5})$. To include the tests, in either orientation the extra factor is a constant in $\\{0,1\\}$ times an interval indicator translated or reflected modulo one. Its supremum is at most one and its variation is bounded by an absolute constant. The inequality $$\\operatorname{Var}(ab)\\le\n \\|a\\|_\\infty\\operatorname{Var}(b)\n +\\|b\\|_\\infty\\operatorname{Var}(a)$$ therefore proves <a href=\"#p:eq:weight-variation\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:weight-variation\">[p:eq:weight-variation]</a>, including all jumps at the test endpoints.\n\n\nThe moment bounds needed below are $$\\label{p:eq:one-coordinate-moments}\n 0\\le\\bar u_i(s)\\le2,\\qquad\n 0\\le A_i\\le1+O(h^2),\\qquad\n \\int_0^1\\int_0^1u_i(s,t)^2\\,ds\\,dt\n       =O(h^{-1}\\log m).$$ For an untested triangular factor the two averages are exactly $1-s$ and $s$, respectively. In either orientation its total integral and squared integral are $1/2$, while $A_i=1/3$. For an untested near-diagonal factor, Lemma <a href=\"#lem:scalar-sampling-estimates\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:scalar-sampling-estimates\">6</a> gives $$\\|\\bar u_i\\|_\\infty\\le2,\\qquad\n A_i\\le1+\\frac{h^2}{(1-h)^2},\\qquad\n \\iint u_i(s,t)^2\\,ds\\,dt=\\frac2h\\log\\frac1{2\\alpha}.$$ Since tests only decrease the nonnegative factors and their averages, these statements imply <a href=\"#p:eq:one-coordinate-moments\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:one-coordinate-moments\">[p:eq:one-coordinate-moments]</a> for every tested factor. Their actual masses need not agree; in particular, the near-diagonal integral identities are not being imposed on the triangular factors or on the tested kernels. The quadratic error $O(h^2)$ is what keeps the product of the moment bounds controlled as the number of coordinates grows.\n\n\n###### The estimate in the growing product dimension\n\n\nFor either fixed orientation and a fixed rectangle test or no test, write $$\\begin{aligned}\n q_n(z)&=\\prod_{i\\le d}u_i(z_i,\\{n\\theta_i\\}),&\n Z_l(z)&=\\frac1{P_l}\\sum_{n\\in\\mathcal N_l}q_n(z),\\\\\n Z_l^0(z)&=\\prod_{i\\le d}\\bar u_i(z_i),&\n A&=\\prod_{i\\le d}A_i=\\|Z_l^0\\|_2^2.\n \\end{aligned}$$ We prove the estimate $$\\label{p:eq:sampling-L2}\n \\mathbb E_\\theta\\|Z_l-Z_l^0\\|_{L^2(\\mu)}^2\n \\le O(dm^{-40})+\\frac{(c h^{-1}\\log m)^d}{P_l}.$$ The independence used here is independence across coordinates $\\theta_i$. In a single coordinate two phases may still be dependent, as already accounted for in <a href=\"#p:eq:bv-discrepancy\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:bv-discrepancy\">[p:eq:bv-discrepancy]</a>.\n\nFor distinct $n,n'$, Tonelli’s Theorem, product Haar measure, and coordinate independence yield the exact factorization $$\\mathbb E_\\theta\\langle q_n,q_{n'}\\rangle\n =\\prod_{i\\le d}\\int_0^1\n   \\mathbb E_\\theta\\bigl[\n    u_i(s,\\{n\\theta_i\\})u_i(s,\\{n'\\theta_i\\})\\bigr]\\,ds.$$ By <a href=\"#p:eq:weight-variation\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:weight-variation\">[p:eq:weight-variation]</a> and <a href=\"#p:eq:bv-discrepancy\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:bv-discrepancy\">[p:eq:bv-discrepancy]</a>, its $i$th factor is $A_i+e_i$ with $|e_i|\\le c m^{-50}$. Similarly, $$\\mathbb E_\\theta\\langle q_n,Z_l^0\\rangle\n =\\prod_{i\\le d}\\int_0^1\n       \\bar u_i(s)\\mathbb E_\\theta u_i(s,\\{n\\theta_i\\})\\,ds.$$ Here <a href=\"#p:eq:single-phase-test\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:single-phase-test\">[p:eq:single-phase-test]</a> and $\\bar u_i\\le2$ bound the error in each factor from $A_i$ by $4N_l/n=O(m^{-50})$. Thus both sorts of factors have the same stated error bound.\n\n\nThe product errors do not require any lower bound on $A_i$. Telescoping gives $$\\label{p:eq:sampling-product-error}\n \\left|\\prod_{i\\le d}(A_i+e_i)-\\prod_{i\\le d}A_i\\right|\n \\le cdm^{-50}(1+ch^2+cm^{-50})^{d-1}\n =O(dm^{-50}),$$ because <a href=\"#p:eq:scale-consequences\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:scale-consequences\">[p:eq:scale-consequences]</a> gives $d\\le1.01m$, and hence $dh^2\\le1.01m^{-1/5}$. In particular, $A\\le(1+ch^2)^d=O(1)$.\n\nFor a self-pairing, use only the one-phase density bound at most $2$ and <a href=\"#p:eq:one-coordinate-moments\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:one-coordinate-moments\">[p:eq:one-coordinate-moments]</a>: $$\\mathbb E_\\theta\\|q_n\\|_2^2\n =\\prod_{i\\le d}\\int_0^1\n            \\mathbb E_\\theta u_i(s,\\{n\\theta_i\\})^2\\,ds\n \\le(c h^{-1}\\log m)^d.$$ To see the cancellation of all other main terms explicitly, expand the squared norm. The distinct-pair terms have total averaging weight $1-1/P_l$ and main term $(1-1/P_l)A$. The cross terms with $Z_l^0$ have main term $-2A$, and the final squared norm of $Z_l^0$ is $A$. Their sum is $-A/P_l\\le0$. By <a href=\"#p:eq:sampling-product-error\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:sampling-product-error\">[p:eq:sampling-product-error]</a>, their remaining contribution has absolute value $O(dm^{-50})$. The self-pairings have total weight $1/P_l$. Dropping $-A/P_l$ proves the slightly weaker bound <a href=\"#p:eq:sampling-L2\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:sampling-L2\">[p:eq:sampling-L2]</a>.\n\n\nThis estimate is summable in $l$. Indeed, $$\\log_m (c h^{-1}\\log m)^d\n \\le1.01m\\left(\\frac35+\\log_m(c\\log m)\\right)\n \\le0.65m$$ for large $m$, whereas $P_l\\ge m^{0.69m}$. Thus $$\\label{p:eq:sampling-summable}\n \\mathbb E_\\theta\\|Z_l-Z_l^0\\|_2^2\n \\le O(m^{-39})+m^{-0.04m},$$ whose sum is finite by $m_l\\ge2^l$.\n\n\n###### One probability-one event and the weak limit\n\nFor each fixed finite $I$, orientation, and rational cylinder rectangle or absent test, Tonelli’s Theorem applied to <a href=\"#p:eq:sampling-summable\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:sampling-summable\">[p:eq:sampling-summable]</a> shows that $$\\sum_l\\|Z_l-Z_l^0\\|_2^2<\\infty\n \\quad\\text{almost surely},$$ where only the sufficiently large admissible stages are included. In particular, $\\|Z_l-Z_l^0\\|_2\\to0$. Finite subsets of $\\mathbb N$ form a countable family, as do finite cylinder rectangles with rational endpoints, and there are only two orientations. Intersect these countably many probability-one events. For the remainder of the proof take any $\\theta$ in this intersection.\n\nWithout a test, $Z_l$ is a marginal density of $\\widetilde\\eta_{I,l}$. Since $\\|Z_l^0\\|_2^2=A=O(1)$, the $L^2$ convergence just proved bounds both marginal norms uniformly in $l$. We may omit finitely many initial stages; alternatively, each of their densities is bounded for fixed $l$, so they can be included by enlarging the bound. The total masses of $\\widetilde\\eta_{I,l}$ are consequently uniformly bounded.\n\n\nFor a fixed cylinder rectangle $\\mathcal R$, using the first orientation with its test shows that $$\\left|\\widetilde\\eta_{I,l}(\\mathcal R)\n       -\\int_X Z_l^0\\,d\\mu\\right|\n \\le\\|Z_l-Z_l^0\\|_2\\longrightarrow0.$$ The ideal integral has a direct product interpretation on the first $d$ coordinates. For $i\\in I$, integration of $w_i(x_i,t)$ in $x_i,t$ gives exactly the triangular measure under $y_i=x_i+t$. For $i\\notin I$, first take uniform $x_i$, discard $\\Delta(x_i)<\\alpha$, and, otherwise, take $t$ uniform in $(0,h\\Delta(x_i))$, setting $y_i=x_i+t$. Before the discard this is a probability law (the value at the two endpoint values of $x_i$ may be defined arbitrarily), and $|y_i-x_i|\\le h/2$. The total loss caused by all cutoffs is at most $$2d\\alpha=2dm^{-2}\\longrightarrow0,$$ by the union bound, with the finite triangular factors having mass at most one. Each fixed tested coordinate outside $I$ therefore converges to its uniform diagonal law. More explicitly, outside an $h$-neighborhood of a tested interval’s endpoints, membership of $y_i$ in that interval agrees with membership of $x_i$; the neighborhood has Lebesgue measure $O(h)$. Since a fixed rectangle tests only finitely many coordinates, all such errors vanish. Untested coordinates contribute only their masses, and the total cutoff estimate controls their product even as $d$ increases. It follows that $$\\label{p:eq:sampling-rectangle-limit}\n \\widetilde\\eta_{I,l}(\\mathcal R)\\longrightarrow\n                  \\lambda_I(\\mathcal R).$$\n\nThese limits imply weak convergence on $X\\times X$. Here are the topological details. The boundaries of the interval rectangles have zero $\\lambda_I$ measure, since every single-coordinate marginal of $\\lambda_I$ is absolutely continuous. Every continuous function on the compact product can be uniformly approximated by a continuous function of finitely many coordinates: in a summable product metric, replace the tail coordinates by fixed points and use uniform continuity. On the resulting finite torus, a sufficiently fine rational grid gives a step function, a finite linear combination of rational rectangle indicators, with arbitrarily small uniform error. Thus <a href=\"#p:eq:sampling-rectangle-limit\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:sampling-rectangle-limit\">[p:eq:sampling-rectangle-limit]</a> and the uniform bound on total masses give convergence of integrals for every continuous function. In particular, $$\\widetilde\\eta_{I,l}\\ \\Longrightarrow\\ \\lambda_I.$$\n\n\n###### Trimming the tail to impose order everywhere\n\n\nThe untrimmed measures now have the required weak limit and both marginal bounds. It remains to impose order past the first $d$ coordinates. Define $\\eta_{I,l}$ by restricting $\\widetilde\\eta_{I,l}$ to the Borel set where $x_i\\le y_i$ also for every $i>d$. For a fixed selected $n$, the weight $W_n$ depends only on the first $d$ coordinates, so the tail $x$-coordinates remain independent uniform coordinates under that summand, apart from multiplication by its total mass. For a uniform tail coordinate, the probability that $x_i>\\{x_i+n\\theta_i\\}$ is exactly $\\{n\\theta_i\\}\\le n\\theta_i$. Consequently, uniformly over $n\\in\\mathcal N_l$, the relative mass discarded from this summand is at most $$\\label{p:eq:sampling-tail-trim}\n \\begin{split}\n \\sum_{i>d}n\\theta_i\n &\\le 2m^{(7/10)m}\\sum_{s>l}\\frac1{K_s}\\\\\n &\\le\\frac{4m^{(7/10)m}}{K_{l+1}}\n \\le4m^{-m/20}\\longrightarrow0.\n \\end{split}$$ The union bound is valid for the countable tail by increasing finite unions. More precisely, the discarded mass of a summand equals its total mass times the Haar probability of its tail failure event, because the weight and that event depend on disjoint coordinates. This statement also holds for a summand of mass zero. Averaging over $n$ shows that the total discarded mass is bounded by <a href=\"#p:eq:sampling-tail-trim\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:sampling-tail-trim\">[p:eq:sampling-tail-trim]</a> times the uniformly bounded untrimmed mass. Hence the difference of the two measures tends to zero in total variation and $\\eta_{I,l}\\Longrightarrow\\lambda_I$.\n\nEach trimmed marginal is a submeasure of the corresponding untrimmed marginal. Its Radon–Nikodym density is therefore nonnegative and bounded almost everywhere by the untrimmed density, so its $L^2$ norm cannot increase. Trimming preserves the finite set of positive orbit powers and now imposes order in every coordinate. All conclusions hold on the single probability-one event already constructed, for every finite $I$, proving the Proposition. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }

          "anchor": "proof-stmt-p-lem-factor",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-p-lem-factor</summary>\n\n*Proof.* Each generator commutes with scalar multiplication by any function of $x$. It also commutes with the unitary $(J\\zeta)(x)=S\\zeta(\\sigma x)$. These commutation relations therefore hold for every $H\\in M$. Lemma <a href=\"#lem:scalar-multiplier-fields\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:scalar-multiplier-fields\">9</a> therefore represents $H$ by an essentially bounded measurable field $H(x)$, with products and adjoints computed fiberwise. Commutation with $J$ gives <a href=\"#p:eq:covariance\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:covariance\">[p:eq:covariance]</a>.\n\nConversely, let $H$ be an essentially bounded measurable field satisfying <a href=\"#p:eq:covariance\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:covariance\">[p:eq:covariance]</a>. All its integer-iterate covariance identities hold on one conull set. Put $h_r(x)=H(x)_{r,0}$ for $r\\in\\mathbb Z$. Then $$H(x)_{k+r,k}=h_r(\\sigma^k x),$$ so the $r$th matrix diagonal of $H$ is $U^r\\pi(h_r)\\in M$. To recover $H$ from these diagonals, use the constant-field unitaries $D_t e_k=e^{2\\pi i kt}e_k$, $t\\in\\mathbb T$. The strong integral $$H_N=\\int_0^1\\mathcal F_N(t)D_tHD_t^*\\,dt\n =\\sum_{|r|<N}\\left(1-\\frac{|r|}{N}\\right)U^r\\pi(h_r)$$ belongs to $M$, where $\\mathcal F_N$ is the Fejér kernel. The map $t\\mapsto D_tHD_t^*$ is strongly continuous and uniformly bounded. The positivity, unit integral and concentration at zero of $\\mathcal F_N$ imply $H_N\\to H$ strongly. The strong closure of $M$ therefore proves the converse field characterization.\n\nThe displayed state is the vector state associated with the constant field $e_0$, hence is normal. If $H\\geq0$ and $\\tau(H)=0$, its $00$ entry vanishes almost everywhere. Covariance then forces every diagonal entry to vanish almost everywhere. Positivity, or the inequality $|H_{k,l}|^2\\leq H_{k,k}H_{l,l}$, implies $H=0$. Thus the state is faithful. For $H,H'\\in M$, covariance and the change of variable $z=\\sigma^r x$ give $$\\int_X H(x)_{0,r}H'(x)_{r,0}\\,d\\mu(x)\n =\\int_X H'(z)_{0,-r}H(z)_{-r,0}\\,d\\mu(z).$$ The sums over $r$ are absolutely integrable by the row and column $\\ell^2$ bounds and Cauchy–Schwarz. Summing proves $\\tau(HH')=\\tau(H'H)$.\n\nThe rotation $\\sigma$ is ergodic by Section <a href=\"#p:sec:scales\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"p:sec:scales\">7.1</a>. If $H$ is central, it commutes with $\\pi(x\\mapsto e^{2\\pi i x_1})$. In each fiber this last operator has distinct diagonal entries, because $\\theta_1$ is irrational; hence $H(x)$ is diagonal. Commutation with $U$ makes all its diagonal entries equal. Equation <a href=\"#p:eq:covariance\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:covariance\">[p:eq:covariance]</a> and ergodicity make their common value constant almost everywhere. The center of $M$ is therefore scalar. The algebra contains the infinite-dimensional abelian algebra $\\pi(L^\\infty(X))$, so this finite factor is not a matrix algebra and is of type $\\mathrm{II}_1$. Finally, the predual of a von Neumann algebra on a separable Hilbert space is a quotient of the separable trace class, and is separable. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }

          "anchor": "proof-stmt-p-lem-quasinilpotent",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-p-lem-quasinilpotent</summary>\n\n*Proof.* For $n\\geq1$, direct multiplication and <a href=\"#p:eq:cocycle\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:cocycle\">[p:eq:cocycle]</a> give $T^n=U^n\\pi(F_n)$. All the floors defining $F_n$ are nonnegative. For each fixed $q$, the estimate $\\lfloor x_i+n\\theta_i\\rfloor\\geq n\\theta_i-1$ on the first $q$ blocks therefore gives $$\\limsup_{n\\to\\infty}\\|T^n\\|^{1/n}\n \\leq \\exp\\!\\left(-\\sum_{s\\leq q}C_s\n                              \\sum_{i\\in J_s}\\theta_i\\right)\n \\leq \\exp\\!\\left(-\\sum_{s\\leq q}\\frac{C_s}{K_s}\\right).$$ Since $C_s\\geq K_s^2$, the last expression tends to zero as $q\\to\\infty$. Also, Tonelli’s Theorem and the same angle bounds give $$-\\int_X\\log f\\,d\\mu\n =\\sum_s C_s\\sum_{i\\in J_s}\\theta_i\n \\ge\\sum_s\\frac{C_s}{K_s}=\\infty.$$ Since $|T|=\\pi(f)$ and the trace on $\\pi(L^\\infty(X))$ is integration against $\\mu$, the determinant formula gives $\\Delta(T)=0$. Positivity of $f$ almost everywhere ensures $T\\ne0$. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }

          "anchor": "proof-stmt-p-prop-pair-identity",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-p-prop-pair-identity</summary>\n\n*Proof.* Fix this $p$ and a Borel version of its projection field. We may shrink $X_0$ to a common invariant Borel set of full measure on which covariance, the cocycle identities, and all the following countably many power identities hold. Invariance of $\\mathop{\\mathrm{ran}}p(x)$ under $T(x)$ gives invariance under $T(x)^n$, for each $n\\geq1$. Applying $S^{-n}$ and using covariance yields $$\\label{p:eq:orbit-inclusion}\n (1-p(\\sigma^n x))\n \\mathop{\\mathrm{diag}}_{k\\in\\mathbb Z}\\bigl(F_n(\\sigma^k x)\\bigr)p(x)=0.$$ Division by $F_n(x)>0$ changes the diagonal entries to $F_k(\\sigma^n x)/F_k(x)$ by <a href=\"#p:eq:cocycle\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:cocycle\">[p:eq:cocycle]</a>. Proposition <a href=\"#p:prop:sampling\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"p:prop:sampling\">25</a> gives ordered orbit-pair measures converging to $\\lambda_I$ with uniformly $L^2$-bounded marginal densities. Their first marginals are absolutely continuous, so the countable collection of orbit identities holds almost everywhere on each approximant. The marginals of $\\lambda_I$ are absolutely continuous. The uniform $L^2$ bounds give uniform absolute continuity by Cauchy–Schwarz. Orderedness and <a href=\"#p:eq:ratio\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:ratio\">[p:eq:ratio]</a> give the common cap $B=1$ on every ratio, under both the approximants and the limit. Lemma <a href=\"#lem:compact-transfer\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:compact-transfer\">11</a> now proves <a href=\"#p:eq:pair-identity\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:pair-identity\">[p:eq:pair-identity]</a>. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }
      ],

      "claim_anchor": "stmt-p-lem-coupling-marginals",

          "anchor": "proof-stmt-p-lem-coupling-marginals",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-p-lem-coupling-marginals</summary>\n\n*Proof.* For a top or bottom component of width $w$, each endpoint interval has length at least $w/2$, so its density is at most $2/w$. A tagged endpoint density is at most $m$. Consequently either one-coordinate marginal density $h_i$ satisfies $$0<q_{j,s}\\le h_i\n \\le1+4\\sum_{w=a_s,b_s}\\frac{\\rho_s(w)}w+\\mathbf 1_{\\{s=j\\}}\n \\le2+8\\kappa_s<10.$$ Throughout these sums the two widths are counted with their labels.\n\n\nIf $v$ is the uniform density on an interval of length at least $w/2$, then $\\int(v-1)=0$ and $\\|v-1\\|_2^2\\le2/w$. The corresponding bound for a tagged interval is $m$. Subtracting $1$ from $h_i$ expresses it as the sum of four weighted mean-zero perturbations and, when $s=j$, one tagged perturbation. Cauchy–Schwarz for these at most five summands gives $$\\label{p:eq:marginal-energy}\n \\begin{split}\n \\|h_i-1\\|_2^2\n &\\le20\\sum_{w=a_s,b_s}\\frac{\\rho_s(w)^2}{w}\n                    +5\\frac{\\mathbf 1_{\\{s=j\\}}}{m_s}\\\\\n &\\le20\\left(\\frac{2\\kappa_s^2}{m_s}\n                    +\\frac{\\mathbf 1_{\\{s=j\\}}}{m_s}\\right).\n \\end{split}$$ This estimate holds for either endpoint. Summing over the $m_s$ coordinates of every block gives the bound $20(2\\sum_s\\kappa_s^2+1)$ for every deterministic selection of endpoint marginals. Each selected coordinate density is nonnegative and has integral one, and the coordinate laws are independent. Thus Lemma <a href=\"#lem:product-density-bound\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:product-density-bound\">13</a> supplies the asserted $L^2$ density with norm at most $H$. In particular, for each such product law $\\eta$ and every Borel set $A\\subseteq X$, $$\\label{p:eq:uniform-absolute-continuity}\n \\eta(A)\\le H\\mu(A)^{1/2}.$$\n\n\nTo verify the assertions after conditioning on an entire endpoint, we describe the conditional kernels. At coordinate $i\\in J_s$, let $\\ell$ run through the replacement labels, including the tag when present. Write $\\pi_\\ell$ for its mixture probability and $u_\\ell,v_\\ell$ for the normalized interval densities of its two endpoints. The marginal densities in this coordinate are $$h_i^x(t)=q_{j,s}+\\sum_\\ell\\pi_\\ell u_\\ell(t),\n \\qquad\n h_i^y(t)=q_{j,s}+\\sum_\\ell\\pi_\\ell v_\\ell(t).$$ They are strictly positive. A conditional law of $y_i$ given $x_i=t$ is $$\\label{p:eq:conditional-kernel}\n K_i^x(t,dz)=\\frac{q_{j,s}}{h_i^x(t)}\\delta_t(dz)\n       +\\sum_\\ell\\frac{\\pi_\\ell u_\\ell(t)}{h_i^x(t)}\n                              v_\\ell(z)\\,dz.$$ The reverse kernel $K_i^y$ interchanges $u_\\ell$ and $v_\\ell$. Independence of the coordinate pairs shows, first for cylinder tests, that $\\bigotimes_i K_i^x(x_i,\\cdot)$ is a conditional law of $y$ given $x$. The monotone class theorem extends the defining identity to all Borel tests on $X^2$. The corresponding product of reverse kernels is a conditional law of $x$ given $y$. These formulas prove both assertions of conditional independence.\n\nIf $x_i=t<u\\le1-w$, then $t\\in[0,1-w/2)$, where the top component of width $w$ has $x_i$-density $(1-w/2)^{-1}\\ge1$. Its whole $y_i$ interval lies above $u$. Since $h_i^x\\le10$, its contribution to <a href=\"#p:eq:conditional-kernel\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:conditional-kernel\">[p:eq:conditional-kernel]</a> is at least $\\rho_s(w)/10$. This proves <a href=\"#p:eq:crossing-given-x\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:crossing-given-x\">[p:eq:crossing-given-x]</a>. If $y_i=t\\ge u\\ge w$, the bottom component has $y_i$-density $(1-w/2)^{-1}\\ge1$ at $t$, and its whole $x_i$ interval lies below $u$. The reverse kernel therefore proves <a href=\"#p:eq:crossing-given-y\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:crossing-given-y\">[p:eq:crossing-given-y]</a>. Intersecting the full-measure sets for any specified countable family of thresholds gives the final assertion. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }
      ],
      "id": "step-product-biased-marginals",
      "inputs": [
        "The biased labelled coordinate mixture",
        "The product-density lemma"

      "method": "Bound mean-zero mixture perturbations and describe independent conditional kernels in both endpoint orientations.",
      "output": "Uniform hybrid marginal density bounds and conditional crossing estimates.",

    {
      "claim_anchor": "stmt-p-prop-biased-identity",
      "depends_on": [
        "step-product-triangular-identity",
        "step-product-biased-marginals",

      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-p-prop-biased-identity",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-p-prop-biased-identity</summary>\n\n\n*Proof.* Fix $j$. For $D\\ge1$, define $$y_i^{(D)}=\n \\begin{cases}\n  y_i,&i\\le D,\\\\\n  x_i,&i>D,\n \\end{cases}$$ and let $\\beta_D$ be the law of $(x,y^{(D)})$ under $\\mathbb P_j$. We claim that $$\\label{p:eq:finite-prefix-domination}\n \\beta_D\\ll\\sum_{I\\subseteq\\{1,\\ldots,D\\}}\\lambda_I.$$ Condition on a pattern $c$ of mixture labels in the first $D$ coordinates. There are finitely many patterns. Let $I(c)$ consist of the coordinates carrying replacement labels. At each $i\\in I(c)$ the conditioned pair has density $$r_{c,i}(t,z)=\n \\frac{\\mathbf 1_{U_{c,i}}(t)\\mathbf 1_{V_{c,i}}(z)}\n {|U_{c,i}|\\,|V_{c,i}|}$$ relative to the unnormalized triangular measure $\\mathbf 1_{\\{0\\le t<z<1\\}}\\,dt\\,dz$. Here $U_{c,i},V_{c,i}$ are the two intervals of the selected component. Their lengths are positive and their rectangle is supported on $t<z$. The remaining prefix coordinates are uniformly diagonal.\n\nFor the diagonal tail, let $g$ be the full $x$-marginal density from Lemma <a href=\"#p:lem:coupling-marginals\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"p:lem:coupling-marginals\">29</a>. Write $\\mu=\\mu_{\\le D}\\otimes\\mu_{>D}$ and define $$\\label{p:eq:tail-density}\n g_D(z)=\\int g(u,z)\\,d\\mu_{\\le D}(u),\n \\qquad\n \\|g_D\\|_{L^2(\\mu_{>D})}\\le\\|g\\|_{L^2(\\mu)}\\le H.$$ Fubini identifies $g_D$ as the density of $x_{>D}$, and Jensen’s inequality proves the norm bound. Independence across coordinates ensures that conditioning on $c$ leaves this tail law unchanged. Therefore the conditional law of $(x,y^{(D)})$ has density $$\\label{p:eq:conditioned-prefix-density}\n g_D(x_{>D})\\prod_{i\\in I(c)}r_{c,i}(x_i,y_i)$$ with respect to $\\lambda_{I(c)}$. This follows on cylinder sets from the coordinate construction, and hence on all Borel sets. The rectangle factors form a finite product and are bounded. The tail factor is integrable and need not equal one. Summing the conditional laws over the finitely many patterns proves <a href=\"#p:eq:finite-prefix-domination\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:finite-prefix-domination\">[p:eq:finite-prefix-domination]</a>.\n\nProposition <a href=\"#p:prop:pair-identity\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"p:prop:pair-identity\">28</a> now gives the pair identity $\\beta_D$-almost everywhere. To pass to the limit, the first marginal of $\\beta_D$ is the $x$-marginal of $\\nu_j$, while its second marginal uses the $y$-marginals in the first $D$ coordinates and the $x$-marginals thereafter. Lemma <a href=\"#p:lem:coupling-marginals\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"p:lem:coupling-marginals\">29</a> and <a href=\"#p:eq:uniform-absolute-continuity\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:uniform-absolute-continuity\">[p:eq:uniform-absolute-continuity]</a> therefore give uniform absolute continuity of both marginal families, and absolute continuity of the two marginals of $\\nu_j$.\n\nOn the common probability space, $y^{(D)}\\to y$ in the product topology. Since $X^2$ is compact metrizable, bounded convergence against continuous functions proves $\\beta_D\\Rightarrow\\nu_j$. Every one of these laws is carried by ordered pairs. Their absolutely continuous marginals put both endpoints in the common conull set on which the cocycles are finite and positive. The ordered-coordinate formula for the cocycle ratios consequently gives $0<R_{x,y^{(D)}}(k)\\le1$ and $0<R_{x,y}(k)\\le1$, simultaneously for every signed $k$. All hypotheses of Lemma <a href=\"#lem:compact-transfer\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:compact-transfer\">11</a> are now satisfied, with the fixed cap $B=1$. That lemma transfers the identity from $\\beta_D$ to $\\nu_j$, proving <a href=\"#p:eq:biased-identity\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:biased-identity\">[p:eq:biased-identity]</a>. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }
      ],

The unitary $U$ is therefore its polar factor. On the conull set where the sum in <a href=\"#p:eq:weights\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:weights\">[p:eq:weights]</a> has finite support, the value of $f$ is determined by a finite subset of the countable coordinate set. There are only countably many such subsets. Consequently $f$ takes values in a countable subset of $(0,1]$, up to a null set. Its nonnull level sets give countably many spectral projections whose sum is $1$, which proves the assertion about spectral type.\n\nThe representation convention gives $$U\\pi(g)U^*=\\pi(g\\circ\\sigma^{-1}).$$ For a finite $F$, the tuple $(f\\circ\\sigma^{-n})_{n\\in F}$ has countable range on a common conull set. The nonnull joint level sets therefore give an atomic partition of the generated algebra $\\mathcal A_F$. This includes $F=\\varnothing$, when the generated unital algebra is scalar.\n\nFor the infinite family, $\\mathcal A$ is an invariant abelian subalgebra of $\\pi(L^\\infty(X))$. The rotation is totally ergodic. Indeed, for any integer $q\\ne0$ and any nonconstant finite-support character indexed by $v=(v_i)$, rational independence implies $q\\sum_i v_i\\theta_i\\notin\\mathbb Z$. The Fourier argument used in Section <a href=\"#p:sec:scales\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"p:sec:scales\">7.1</a> thus proves ergodicity of $\\sigma^q$.\n\nSuppose $e$ were an atom of $\\mathcal A$. Its integer translates under $\\operatorname{Ad}U$ are atoms of equal positive trace. Distinct atoms in an abelian algebra are orthogonal, so only finitely many can occur. The orbit of $e$ is therefore finite, and for some positive integer $q$, $U^q eU^{-q}=e$. Because $e\\in\\pi(L^\\infty(X))$, total ergodicity forces $e=1$. An abelian algebra whose identity is an atom is scalar. But $\\mathcal A$ contains the nonconstant multiplication operator $\\pi(f)$: a positive almost-everywhere constant would have finite logarithmic integral, contrary to Lemma <a href=\"#p:lem:quasinilpotent\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"p:lem:quasinilpotent\">27</a>. Hence $\\mathcal A$ has no atoms, as claimed. ◻",

The algebra $M$ is a type $\\mathrm{II}_1$ factor with separable predual, and $T=U\\pi(f)$ is nonzero and norm-quasinilpotent, with $\\Delta(T)=0$. Every projection $p\\in M$ satisfying $(1-p)Tp=0$ is $0$ or $1$.*\n\n*Moreover, $|T|$ has pure-point spectral type. The abelian algebra generated by any finite family of polar conjugates $U^n|T|U^{-n}$ is atomic, whereas $W^*(U^n|T|U^{-n}:n\\in\\mathbb Z)$ is diffuse.",

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-prescribed-angle-factor-obstruction**: At each prescribed irrational angle, a continuous nonnegative circle weight with a single zero and divergent negative logarithmic integral excludes every nontrivial invariant projection in the specified containing factor.
- **inv-full-law-conditional-tail-control**: Tail attenuation uses exceptional events controlled after conditioning separately on the entire starting endpoint and the entire ending endpoint. These conditional estimates hold under the original coupling law before restriction to the favorable event.
- **inv-affiliated-transport-preserves-trace**: An injective positive affiliated intertwiner transports an invariant range by taking the closure of its image on the intertwining domain. The resulting projection belongs to the algebra, remains invariant under the target operator, and has the original trace.
- **inv-nonzero-quasinilpotent-continuous-operator**: The continuous weighted rotation constructed in the paper is nonzero and quasinilpotent.
- **inv-finite-atomic-full-diffuse-orbit**: In the product realization, the absolute value has countably atomic spectral measure. Every finite family of polar conjugates generates an atomic abelian algebra, while the full integer orbit generates a diffuse abelian algebra.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-ordinary-invariant-subspace-exclusion**: Conclude that the represented operator has no ordinary invariant subspaces. -> The conclusion excludes invariant projections in the containing factor. Ordinary invariant subspaces can have projections outside that factor.
- **anti-determinant-only-obstruction**: Use a vanishing determinant or point-mass Brown measure alone to exclude invariant projections. -> The source describes quasinilpotent examples with nontrivial affiliated invariant subspaces, so spectral degeneration alone does not supply the construction's obstruction.
- **anti-unbounded-bounded-similarity**: Treat the measurable transfer multiplier as a bounded similarity. -> The transfer function can produce an unbounded affiliated operator; the domain and trace arguments cannot be replaced by bounded-similarity reasoning.
- **anti-uniform-diagonal-gap**: Interpret strict interior central diagonal entries as a uniform separation from the endpoints. -> The diagonal is strictly interior almost everywhere, but the favorable-event threshold is obtained only at sufficiently late stages and may depend on the candidate projection.
- **anti-tail-union-bound**: Control the infinite convolution tail by summing the exceptional probabilities over all omitted indices. -> The proof bounds masked square sums by conditioning on the matching endpoint; it does not establish a summable family of exceptional probabilities over the entire tail.
- **anti-finite-orbit-diffuseness**: Infer diffuseness from a finite collection of polar conjugates. -> Every finite family generates an atomic algebra in this model; diffuseness is a property of the algebra generated by the full integer orbit.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `not_applicable`. The main constructions concern infinite-dimensional operator fields, infinite sequences of logarithmic drops, weak limits of coupling measures, and an affiliated intertwiner that may be unbounded. A finite truncation would not decide the absence of invariant projections in the containing factor. Even the finite convolution coefficient bound is obtained through compactness rather than an explicit finite computation: The continuous displayed maximum therefore has a positive minimum on the compact set. The source supplies no finite decision procedure or effective error contract converting truncated computations into a certificate for the full operator construction.

For every finite $F\\subset\\mathbb Z$, the abelian von Neumann algebra $$\\mathcal A_F=W^*(U^n|T|U^{-n}:n\\in F)$$ is atomic. The full algebra $$\\mathcal A=W^*(U^n|T|U^{-n}:n\\in\\mathbb Z)$$ is diffuse.",

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-prescribed-irrational-angle** (hypothesis): The circle construction requires an irrational angle and constructs a suitable weight after that angle is prescribed. It does not assert the same projection exclusion for rational rotations or arbitrary weights. Blocked use: ['Apply the construction to a rational rotation.', 'Conclude that every continuous weight with the stated zero set and logarithmic integral excludes invariant projections.']
- **limit-containing-factor-projections** (formal_scope): Projection exclusion concerns the specified containing factor. It does not exclude ordinary invariant subspaces whose orthogonal projections lie outside that factor. Blocked use: ['Claim the represented operator has no ordinary invariant subspaces.', 'Apply the projection obstruction without verifying algebra membership.']
- **limit-infinite-dimensional-existence** (formal_scope): The transitive-commutant existence corollary concerns separable infinite-dimensional complex Hilbert spaces. Blocked use: ['Treat a finite matrix truncation as an instance of the existence corollary.', 'Transfer the corollary to a Hilbert space outside its stated class without another argument.']
- **limit-determinant-not-projection-obstruction** (counterexample_scope): Point-mass Brown measure and quasinilpotence do not by themselves exclude affiliated invariant projections. The construction requires its separate convolution obstruction. Blocked use: ['Infer absence of invariant projections from a vanishing determinant alone.', 'Use point-mass Brown measure as a substitute for the ordered-pair argument.']
- **limit-sampling-scale-and-separation** (hypothesis): The scalar discrepancy estimates require the prescribed scale, uniform angle law and separation of the selected powers. They do not assert independence of phases obtained from distinct powers of one angle. Blocked use: ['Apply the bounded-variation discrepancy bound to unseparated powers.', 'Replace the actual joint phase law by independent phases without a justified discrepancy estimate.']
- **limit-compact-polynomial-minimum** (nonconstructive): The uniform convolution lower bound is obtained as a positive minimum on a compact set. The proof supplies no explicit formula or computational stopping contract for that minimum. Blocked use: ['Assign a numerical attenuation budget from the existence lemma without a separate effective lower bound.', 'Describe the compactness proof as an implemented coefficient-minimization algorithm.']
- **limit-successive-parameter-selection** (nonconstructive): Parameter selection uses successful trial phases and sufficiently accurate character approximations selected through probabilistic existence and convergence. Finite sampling tests do not provide a certificate for the entire infinite construction. Blocked use: ['Claim a finite executable construction with a proven stopping bound.', 'Replace the divisible characters by independent circle coordinates in the prescribed-angle proof.']
- **limit-scalar-multiplier-commutation** (hypothesis): The measurable-field representation requires commutation with every scalar multiplication operator. Its identities and operator bounds hold almost everywhere. Blocked use: ['Decompose an arbitrary bounded operator into the stated fields without checking multiplier commutation.', 'Treat an arbitrary choice of exceptional-point field values as part of the operator identity.']
- **limit-strict-diagonal-not-uniform-gap** (hypothesis): Strict interior central diagonal entries require ergodicity, a positive almost-everywhere weight, covariance, the faithful tracial state and an invariant projection of intermediate trace. The strictness is almost everywhere and supplies no uniform gap from the endpoints. Blocked use: ['Infer a uniform central-coordinate lower bound from strict interior values.', 'Use the lemma when the weight vanishes on a set of positive measure or the transformation is not ergodic.']
- **limit-weak-transfer-marginals-and-cap** (hypothesis): Weak-limit transfer requires uniform absolute continuity of both approximating marginal families, absolute continuity of both limiting marginals and a common finite cap on all cocycle ratios. Weak convergence alone does not establish the measurable identity. Blocked use: ['Pass projection identities to an arbitrary weak limit.', 'Use endpoint measures concentrating on exceptional sets.', 'Allow ratio caps to depend without bound on the approximation stage.']
- **limit-independent-density-energy** (hypothesis): The infinite-product density bound requires nonnegative mean-one coordinate densities and a finite sum of their squared deviations from the reference densities. Blocked use: ['Apply the product bound to dependent coordinate laws without another proof.', 'Assert the same square-integrable density bound when the summed deviation bound is absent.']
- **limit-digit-order-and-endpoint-conditioning** (hypothesis): The circle coupling uses independent digits, although character coordinates depend on overlapping digit tails. Conditional independence belongs to the coordinate labels after an entire endpoint has been fixed, and the crossing bounds require thresholds in the prescribed interior interval. Blocked use: ['Assume the circle character coordinates are independent.', 'Apply the posterior crossing bounds after conditioning only on an unrelated statistic.', 'Use the crossing lower bound for thresholds outside its allowed interval.']
- **limit-full-law-infinite-tail-control** (hypothesis): The convolution obstruction requires exact vanishing convolutions, positive finite cocycles, unit-ball vectors, the stated multiplier comparisons and a positive-probability finite-coefficient event. Exceptional-event bounds must hold under the full law before favorable-event restriction. Blocked use: ['Use conditional estimates proved only under the favorable-event law.', 'Replace exact convolution vanishing by unexplained numerical residuals.', 'Control the infinite masked tail by summing the individual exceptional probabilities.']
- **limit-projection-dependent-eventual-stage** (edge_case): The positive-event truncation bounds hold only at sufficiently late stages, and the threshold stage may depend on the candidate projection. The coefficient constants need not have a uniform positive lower bound across stages. Blocked use: ['Require the favorable-event bound at every stage.', 'Cancel coefficient constants after replacing their stagewise positivity by an unsupported uniform bound.', 'Interpret the contradiction as a uniform finite search over candidate projections.']
- **limit-smoothing-relocation-support-summability** (hypothesis): Smoothing and relocating the logarithmic drops require the prepared partition and summable exceptional support measures. The coboundary sums are legitimate on a common conull set where the transfer sums and original cost have finitely many nonzero terms. Blocked use: ['Sum arbitrary smoothing or relocation transfers without support control.', 'Treat the measurable coboundary identity as an everywhere identity for arbitrary representatives.']
- **limit-background-needed-at-the-zero** (edge_case): Local finiteness of the relocated drops away from the distinguished point does not alone guarantee divergence at that point. The background cost supplies this additional property. Blocked use: ['Omit the background and conclude that the resulting weight has exactly the required zero.', 'Infer divergence at the center solely from shrinking supports and an infinite integral.']
- **limit-affiliated-intertwining-domain** (hypothesis): Trace-preserving transport requires an injective positive self-adjoint affiliated operator, a finite algebra with faithful normal tracial state, domain preservation and the intertwining equality on that domain. The transported range is the closure of the image of the domain intersection. Blocked use: ['Replace the transport argument by bounded similarity without proving boundedness.', 'Apply the result to an intertwiner with a nontrivial kernel.', 'Ignore the domain intersection when defining the transported projection.']
- **limit-continuous-norm-averaging** (hypothesis): The circle norm-quasinilpotence argument uses continuous truncated logarithms and uniform averaging under irrational rotation, together with the divergent negative logarithmic integral. Pointwise or average numerical decay is not the asserted operator-norm conclusion. Blocked use: ['Replace uniform rotation averaging by convergence at sampled starting points.', 'Infer the norm conclusion for a general measurable circle weight from this continuous-weight proof.']
- **limit-product-probability-one-realization** (nonconstructive): The product sampling theorem holds on a probability-one set of angle choices. A realization satisfying rational independence, phase coverage and all finite-coordinate sampling conclusions is fixed before any projection is introduced. Blocked use: ['Assert that every angle sequence in the allowed intervals satisfies the sampling theorem.', 'Choose the rotation after inspecting a candidate invariant projection.', 'Treat finitely many successful tests as certification of the probability-one intersection.']
- **limit-product-prefix-biased-tail** (hypothesis): Finite-prefix domination retains the biased density of the unreplaced endpoint tail. Passing to the full coupling also requires uniform control of hybrid marginal densities and ordered positive cocycle ratios. Blocked use: ['Replace the unreplaced tail law by Haar measure without accounting for its density.', 'Transfer the finite-prefix identity without checking marginal control in the limit.']
- **limit-atomic-spectral-type-versus-spectrum** (formal_scope): Countably atomic spectral measure does not assert that every spectral point is an eigenvalue. Finite polar-conjugate families generate atomic algebras, while diffuseness is asserted for the full orbit algebra. Blocked use: ['Infer that a spectral accumulation point must be an eigenvalue.', 'Infer diffuseness from a finite family of polar conjugates.', 'Identify the full orbit algebra with the entire multiplication algebra.']
- **limit-no-finite-certification-contract** (unknown): ABSENT: a finite executable decision procedure, a truncation error contract certifying projection exclusion, and computational complexity bounds for the full operator construction. Blocked use: ['Publish finite numerical agreement as certification of the infinite-dimensional projection obstruction.', 'Assign implementation complexity to the full existence construction.']

To bound its averaged density, first omit the cutoff. The resulting density $d$ is the law of $z+t\\Delta(z)$ for independent uniform $z\\in[0,1)$ and $t\\in(0,h)$. For fixed $t$ this is an increasing piecewise-linear bijection of $[0,1]$, with slopes $1+t$ and $1-t$. Thus $\\int d=1$ and $|d-1|\\le h/(1-h)$, whence $$\\int d^2=1+\\int(d-1)^2\\le1+\\frac{h^2}{(1-h)^2}.$$ Restoring the cutoff only decreases this nonnegative density; also $d\\le(1-h)^{-1}\\le2$. These observations give the upper bounds in <a href=\"#eq:kernel-estimates\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:kernel-estimates\">[eq:kernel-estimates]</a>, while Cauchy–Schwarz gives the lower bound from its exact mass. The analogous statement holds conditional on $y$, using $B_i$.*\n\n*For $i\\in J_s$, $w\\in\\{a_s,b_s\\}$, and $u\\in[w,1-w]$, the following bounds hold almost surely: $$\\begin{aligned}\n \\mathbb P_j(\\text{top label of width }w\\text{ at }i\\mid x)\n   &\\ge\\rho_s(w)/10 &&\\text{if }u_i(x)<u,\n   \\label{eq:conditional-crossing}\\\\\n \\mathbb P_j(\\text{bottom label of width }w\\text{ at }i\\mid y)\n   &\\ge\\rho_s(w)/10 &&\\text{if }u_i(y)\\ge u.\n   \\label{eq:conditional-crossing-bottom}\n\\end{aligned}$$ In the first case that label forces $u_i(y)\\ge u$; in the second it forces $u_i(x)<u$.",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        },
        {
          "anchor": "eq:digit-expansion",
          "exact_quote": "Write $r_i^*=q_{i+1}/q_i$. A Haar-uniform point of $X$ has the expansion $$\\label{eq:digit-expansion}\n x=\\frac{A_0}{q_1}+\\sum_{i\\ge1}\\frac{A_i}{q_{i+1}},\n \\qquad\n A_0\\in\\{0,\\ldots,q_1-1\\},\\quad\n A_i\\in\\{0,\\ldots,r_i^*-1\\},$$ with independent uniform digits. Indeed, specifying a finite prefix selects an interval whose length is the reciprocal of the product of the relevant radices. Conversely these intervals determine the Haar measure. The ambiguity at endpoints of such intervals is a null set. In particular, $A_i=\\lfloor r_i^*u_i(x)\\rfloor$, and $u_i(x)$ is the tail of <a href=\"#eq:digit-expansion\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:digit-expansion\">[eq:digit-expansion]</a> starting with digit $A_i$, after multiplication by $q_i$. We couple the independent digits; each character coordinate $u_i$ depends on an overlapping tail of these digits.",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }
      ],

          "anchor": "stmt-lem-conditional-convolution",
          "exact_quote": "Let $K\\ge1$ be an integer, $D\\ge a>0$, $c>0$, and $\\eta,\\delta\\ge0$. On a probability space with endpoints $x,y$, let $\\xi(x),\\gamma(y)$ be measurable vectors in the unit ball of $\\ell^2(\\mathbb Z)$, and let $F_k$ be positive finite measurable functions of an endpoint, with $F_0=1$. Set $$A_v=\\frac{\\xi_v(x)}{F_v(x)},\\qquad\n B_w=F_{-w}(y)\\overline{\\gamma_{-w}(y)},\\qquad\n R(k)=\\frac{F_k(y)}{F_k(x)}.$$ Suppose, almost surely, for $0\\le n\\le2K$ and every $v\\in\\mathbb Z$, $$\\label{eq:generic-convolution}\n \\sum_{v\\in\\mathbb Z}A_vB_{-n-v}=0,\\qquad\n 0<\\frac{F_{n+v}(y)}{F_v(x)}\n       \\le\\min\\{R(v),R(n+v)\\}\\le1.$$ Let $E$ be an event of probability at least $c$ on which $$|A_v|,|B_v|\\le D\\quad(|v|\\le K),\\qquad\n |A_0|,|B_0|\\ge a.$$ For each $|k|>K$, suppose there are events $H_k^{(1)},H_k^{(2)}$ with $$\\label{eq:generic-tail}\n R(k)\\le\\eta+\\mathbf1_{H_k^{(1)}}+\\mathbf1_{H_k^{(2)}}\\quad\\text{on }E,\n \\qquad\n \\mathbb P(H_k^{(1)}\\mid x)\\le\\delta,\\quad\n \\mathbb P(H_k^{(2)}\\mid y)\\le\\delta.$$ The conditional bounds are under the full probability law, before restriction to $E$. If $\\varepsilon(K,D,a)$ is the positive constant from Lemma <a href=\"#lem:polynomial-coefficient\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:polynomial-coefficient\">7</a>, then $$\\label{eq:generic-obstruction}\n c\\varepsilon(K,D,a)\\le(2K+1)(\\eta+2\\sqrt\\delta).$$",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        },
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-tail-attenuation",
          "exact_quote": "For every $j$ and every integer $k$ with $|k|>K_j$, there are measurable events $H_k^{(1)},H_k^{(2)}$ in the labelled coupling space such that $$\\label{eq:conditional-bad-events}\n \\mathbb P_j(H_k^{(1)}\\mid x)\\le e^{-L_j/400},\\qquad\n \\mathbb P_j(H_k^{(2)}\\mid y)\\le e^{-L_j/400}\n \\quad\\text{almost surely},$$ and on $E_j$, $$\\label{eq:tail-ratio-bound}\n R_{x,y}(k)\\le e^{-L_j}\n       +\\mathbf 1_{H_k^{(1)}}+\\mathbf 1_{H_k^{(2)}}.$$ The conditional bounds hold before any restriction to $E_j$.",

          "anchor": "stmt-lem-truncation-bound",
          "exact_quote": "There is a constant $c_*>0$ such that, for all sufficiently large $j$, $$\\label{eq:finite-lower-bound}\n \\mathbb P_j(E_j)\\ge c_*,\\qquad\n \\mathbb E_j\\left[\\mathbf 1_{E_j}\n          \\sum_{n=0}^{2K_j}|c_{j,n}|\\right]\\ge c_*\\varepsilon_j.$$ The point after which these inequalities hold may depend on $p$.",

      "id": "limit-projection-dependent-eventual-stage",
      "kind": "edge_case",
      "statement": "The positive-event truncation bounds hold only at sufficiently late stages, and the threshold stage may depend on the candidate projection. The coefficient constants need not have a uniform positive lower bound across stages.",

    {
      "blocked_use": [
        "Sum arbitrary smoothing or relocation transfers without support control.",

      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "pm-h-28",
          "exact_quote": "#### Smoothing and moving the logarithmic drops\n\n\nWe claim that there exist a nonnegative function $g$, finite and continuous on $X\\setminus\\{0\\}$ and tending to infinity at $0$, and an almost everywhere finite real measurable function $b$ such that $$\\label{eq:continuous-coboundary}\n g=g_*+1+b\\circ\\sigma-b\\quad\\text{almost everywhere},\n \\qquad \\int_Xg\\,d\\mu=\\infty.$$ The construction has three parts: smooth each drop, move its support into a shrinking arc, and add an integrable background cost that guarantees divergence at the center of those arcs. Apart from the constant $1$, all changes will be explicit coboundaries.\n\n\nFix $i\\in J_j$ and put $\\delta_i=\\alpha_i/4$. For $u\\in X$, define the continuous periodic smoothing of the sawtooth by $$s_i(u)=\\frac1{2\\delta_i}\\int_{-\\delta_i}^{\\delta_i}\\{u+t\\}\\,dt.$$ It equals $\\{u\\}$ outside the $\\delta_i$-neighborhood of the cut at $0$. Set $$h_i(x)=C_j\\bigl(\\alpha_i+s_i(u_i(x))\n                         -s_i(u_i(x)+\\alpha_i)\\bigr).$$ The elementary identity $\\lfloor\\{u\\}+\\alpha_i\\rfloor=\n\\alpha_i+\\{u\\}-\\{u+\\alpha_i\\}$ shows that $h_i$ is the corresponding average of the original one-coordinate cost. It is nonnegative and continuous, with $$\\label{eq:smoothed-support}\n \\int_X h_i\\,d\\mu=C_j\\alpha_i,\n \\qquad \\mu(\\operatorname{supp}h_i)\\le\\alpha_i+2\\delta_i.$$ For $$d_i^{\\rm sm}(x)=C_j\\bigl(s_i(u_i(x))-u_i(x)\\bigr),$$ we have the exact measurable identity $$\\label{eq:smoothing-coboundary}\n h_i(x)=C_j\\lfloor u_i(x)+\\alpha_i\\rfloor\n                  +d_i^{\\rm sm}(x)-d_i^{\\rm sm}(\\sigma x).$$ The support of $d_i^{\\rm sm}$ has measure at most $2\\delta_i$.\n\n\nRecall the shrinking arcs $O_j$, the integers $h_j^*$, and the continuous partition of unity $\\phi_{j,n}$, $0\\le n\\le h_j^*$, prepared in the parameter construction, with $\\sigma^n(\\operatorname{supp}\\phi_{j,n})\\subset O_j$. Define $$h'_i(x)=\\sum_{n=0}^{h_j^*}(h_i\\phi_{j,n})(\\sigma^{-n}x).$$ This is continuous and nonnegative, supported in $O_j$, and has the same integral as $h_i$. Telescoping gives $$\\label{eq:relocation-coboundary}\n h'_i=h_i+d'_i-d'_i\\circ\\sigma,\n \\qquad\n d'_i(x)=\\sum_{n=1}^{h_j^*}\\sum_{r=1}^n\n                      (h_i\\phi_{j,n})(\\sigma^{-r}x).$$ Although this transfer contains a double sum, its support is contained in just $h_j^*$ translates of $\\operatorname{supp}h_i$, one for each possible $r$. Consequently $$\\sum_i\\left(\\mu\\{d_i^{\\rm sm}\\ne0\\}+\\mu\\{d'_i\\ne0\\}\\right)\n \\le C\\sum_j\\frac{1+h_j^*}{K_j}<\\infty.$$ Thus each of the transfer sums has only finitely many nonzero terms almost everywhere, including at the translated point $\\sigma x$.\n\n\nThe sum $\\sum_i h'_i$ is locally finite on $X\\setminus\\{0\\}$ because its successive blocks are supported in $O_j\\downarrow\\{0\\}$. It is therefore continuous there. Its integral is infinite: Tonelli’s theorem and $\\alpha_i>1/(m_jK_j)$ give $$\\label{eq:infinite-cost-integral}\n \\int_X\\sum_i h'_i\\,d\\mu\n =\\sum_j C_j\\sum_{i\\in J_j}\\alpha_i\n \\ge\\sum_j C_j/K_j=\\infty,$$ since $C_j\\ge K_j^2$. Local finiteness alone need not force divergence at $0$; the following background supplies that remaining property.",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        },
        {
          "anchor": "pm-h-29",
          "exact_quote": "#### A background cost diverging at the zero\n\nLet $W_j=2^{j+1}$. Choose continuous functions $0\\le v_j\\le1$ with $v_j(0)=1$, supported in closed arcs $I_j$ shrinking to $\\{0\\}$. Choose these arcs small enough that $\\sigma I_j,\\ldots,\\sigma^{W_j}I_j$ are pairwise disjoint and $$\\sum_j W_j\\mu(I_j)<\\infty.$$ This is possible because each finite set of distinct points $\\theta,2\\theta,\\ldots,W_j\\theta$ has positive minimum separation. For $x\\ne0$, put $$g_0(x)=1+\\sum_j\\left(v_j(x)-\\frac1{W_j}\n                              \\sum_{n=1}^{W_j}v_j(\\sigma^{-n}x)\\right).$$ For each $j$, disjointness bounds the norm of the subtracted function by $1/W_j$. These bounds sum to $1/2$, so the negative series converges uniformly. The positive series is locally finite away from $0$. Consequently $g_0$ is continuous there and $g_0\\ge1/2$. For every fixed integer $J$, the finite sum $\\sum_{j\\le J}v_j(x)$ tends to $J$ as $x\\to0$. The bounded negative series therefore implies $g_0(x)\\to\\infty$ as $x\\to0$. Its integral is finite, since $\\sum_j\\int v_j\\,d\\mu\\le\\sum_j\\mu(I_j)<\\infty$ and the negative series is uniformly bounded.\n\nDefine $$b_0(x)=\\sum_j\\frac1{W_j}\\sum_{n=1}^{W_j}\\sum_{r=1}^n\n                                      v_j(\\sigma^{-r}x).$$ The $j$th summand is supported in $\\bigcup_{r=1}^{W_j}\\sigma^r I_j$. The assumed summability makes this sum finite almost everywhere. On a conull set where the sums at $x$ and $\\sigma x$ are finite, telescoping shows $$g_0=1+b_0\\circ\\sigma-b_0.$$ Now let $$g(x)=g_0(x)+\\sum_i h'_i(x)\\quad(x\\ne0),\\qquad g(0)=\\infty,\n \\qquad b=b_0-\\sum_i(d_i^{\\rm sm}+d'_i).$$ The preceding arguments prove that $g$ is nonnegative, finite and continuous off $0$, tends to infinity at $0$, and has infinite integral. The transfer $b$ is real and finite almost everywhere. Summing <a href=\"#eq:smoothing-coboundary\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:smoothing-coboundary\">[eq:smoothing-coboundary]</a> and <a href=\"#eq:relocation-coboundary\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:relocation-coboundary\">[eq:relocation-coboundary]</a> is legitimate on the common conull set where the transfer sums and the original cost have finitely many nonzero terms. Together with the background identity this proves <a href=\"#eq:continuous-coboundary\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:continuous-coboundary\">[eq:continuous-coboundary]</a>.",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }
      ],

      "statement": "Smoothing and relocating the logarithmic drops require the prepared partition and summable exceptional support measures. The coboundary sums are legitimate on a common conull set where the transfer sums and original cost have finitely many nonzero terms.",

          "exact_quote": "#### A background cost diverging at the zero\n\nLet $W_j=2^{j+1}$. Choose these arcs small enough that $\\sigma I_j,\\ldots,\\sigma^{W_j}I_j$ are pairwise disjoint and $$\\sum_j W_j\\mu(I_j)<\\infty.$$ This is possible because each finite set of distinct points $\\theta,2\\theta,\\ldots,W_j\\theta$ has positive minimum separation. For $x\\ne0$, put $$g_0(x)=1+\\sum_j\\left(v_j(x)-\\frac1{W_j}\n                              \\sum_{n=1}^{W_j}v_j(\\sigma^{-n}x)\\right).$$ For each $j$, disjointness bounds the norm of the subtracted function by $1/W_j$. These bounds sum to $1/2$, so the negative series converges uniformly. The positive series is locally finite away from $0$. Consequently $g_0$ is continuous there and $g_0\\ge1/2$. For every fixed integer $J$, the finite sum $\\sum_{j\\le J}v_j(x)$ tends to $J$ as $x\\to0$. The bounded negative series therefore implies $g_0(x)\\to\\infty$ as $x\\to0$. Its integral is finite, since $\\sum_j\\int v_j\\,d\\mu\\le\\sum_j\\mu(I_j)<\\infty$ and the negative series is uniformly bounded.\n\nDefine $$b_0(x)=\\sum_j\\frac1{W_j}\\sum_{n=1}^{W_j}\\sum_{r=1}^n\n                                      v_j(\\sigma^{-r}x).$$ The $j$th summand is supported in $\\bigcup_{r=1}^{W_j}\\sigma^r I_j$. The assumed summability makes this sum finite almost everywhere. On a conull set where the sums at $x$ and $\\sigma x$ are finite, telescoping shows $$g_0=1+b_0\\circ\\sigma-b_0.$$ Now let $$g(x)=g_0(x)+\\sum_i h'_i(x)\\quad(x\\ne0),\\qquad g(0)=\\infty,\n \\qquad b=b_0-\\sum_i(d_i^{\\rm sm}+d'_i).$$ The preceding arguments prove that $g$ is nonnegative, finite and continuous off $0$, tends to infinity at $0$, and has infinite integral. The transfer $b$ is real and finite almost everywhere. Summing <a href=\"#eq:smoothing-coboundary\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:smoothing-coboundary\">[eq:smoothing-coboundary]</a> and <a href=\"#eq:relocation-coboundary\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:relocation-coboundary\">[eq:relocation-coboundary]</a> is legitimate on the common conull set where the transfer sums and the original cost have finitely many nonzero terms. Together with the background identity this proves <a href=\"#eq:continuous-coboundary\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:continuous-coboundary\">[eq:continuous-coboundary]</a>.",

      "statement": "Local finiteness of the relocated drops away from the distinguished point does not alone guarantee divergence at that point. The background cost supplies this additional property.",

    {
      "blocked_use": [
        "Replace the transport argument by bounded similarity without proving boundedness.",
        "Apply the result to an intertwiner with a nontrivial kernel.",
        "Ignore the domain intersection when defining the transported projection."
      ],
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-affiliated-transport",
          "exact_quote": "Let $(\\mathcal M,\\tau)$ be a finite von Neumann algebra acting on a Hilbert space, with faithful normal tracial state $\\tau$. Let $H$ be an injective positive self-adjoint operator affiliated with $\\mathcal M$, and let $A,B\\in\\mathcal M$. Suppose $$A(\\operatorname{Dom}H)\\subseteq\\operatorname{Dom}H,\n \\qquad HA\\zeta=BH\\zeta\\quad(\\zeta\\in\\operatorname{Dom}H).$$ For every projection $p\\in\\mathcal M$ with $A\\operatorname{ran}p\\subseteq\n\\operatorname{ran}p$, the projection $P$ onto $$\\overline{H(\\operatorname{ran}p\\cap\\operatorname{Dom}H)}$$ belongs to $\\mathcal M$, is invariant under $B$, and satisfies $\\tau(P)=\\tau(p)$.",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        },
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-affiliated-transport",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-affiliated-transport</summary>\n\n*Proof.* Every commutant unitary preserves $\\operatorname{ran}p$ and $\\operatorname{Dom}H$, and commutes with $H$ on that domain. It therefore maps the displayed closed space onto itself. Its projection commutes with the commutant, proving $P\\in\\mathcal M$. If $\\zeta\\in\\operatorname{ran}p\\cap\\operatorname{Dom}H$, both invariance assumptions give $A\\zeta\\in\\operatorname{ran}p\\cap\\operatorname{Dom}H$, and $BH\\zeta=HA\\zeta$. Thus $B$ preserves the unclosed image and, by boundedness, its closure.\n\nIt remains to prove the trace assertion; bounded similarity cannot be assumed. Put $E_N=\\mathbf1_{[1/N,N]}(H)$. Since $H$ is injective, $E_N\\uparrow1$. For $q_N=p\\wedge E_N$, the bounded element $Hq_N=(HE_N)q_N$ of $\\mathcal M$ is bounded below by $1/N$ on its initial space. Its image is closed, and its polar decomposition identifies the initial projection $q_N$ with its final projection, which is dominated by $P$. Therefore $$\\tau(P)\\ge\\tau(p\\wedge E_N)\n \\ge\\tau(p)+\\tau(E_N)-1.$$ The last inequality follows directly from the polar decomposition of $(1-E_N)p$: its initial support is $p-(p\\wedge E_N)$ and its final support is at most $1-E_N$. Letting $N$ increase gives $\\tau(P)\\ge\\tau(p)$.\n\nFor the reverse inequality, set $q'_N=(1-p)\\wedge E_N$. The bounded operator $H^{-1}q'_N=(H^{-1}E_N)q'_N$ is likewise bounded below on its initial space and has closed image. This image is orthogonal to $\\operatorname{ran}P$. Indeed, for $\\eta\\in\\operatorname{ran}q'_N$ and $\\zeta\\in\\operatorname{ran}p\\cap\\operatorname{Dom}H$, self-adjointness gives $$\\langle H^{-1}\\eta,H\\zeta\\rangle=\\langle\\eta,\\zeta\\rangle=0;$$ here $H^{-1}\\eta$ lies in $\\operatorname{Dom}H$. Polar decomposition and the same meet estimate yield $$\\tau(1-P)\\ge\\tau((1-p)\\wedge E_N)\n \\ge\\tau(1-p)+\\tau(E_N)-1.$$ Passing to the limit proves the opposite inequality for $\\tau(P)$. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }
      ],
      "id": "limit-affiliated-intertwining-domain",
      "kind": "hypothesis",
      "statement": "Trace-preserving transport requires an injective positive self-adjoint affiliated operator, a finite algebra with faithful normal tracial state, domain preservation and the intertwining equality on that domain. The transported range is the closure of the image of the domain intersection.",
      "support": "stated"
    },
    {
      "blocked_use": [
        "Replace uniform rotation averaging by convergence at sampled starting points.",
