      ],
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-cor-quasinilpotent",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-cor-quasinilpotent</summary>\n\n*Proof.* For $M>0$, the function $\\ell_M(x)=\\log\\max(f(e^{2\\pi i x}),e^{-M})$ is continuous. Its integrals decrease to $-\\infty$ by <a href=\"#eq:zero-log-integral\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:zero-log-integral\">[eq:zero-log-integral]</a>. Irrational rotation averages of a continuous function converge uniformly to its integral: this follows for Fourier monomials from their geometric sums and then for continuous functions by uniform trigonometric approximation. Hence, for any $a>0$, first choosing $M$ with $\\int_X\\ell_M\\,d\\mu<-2a$ and then taking $n$ sufficiently large gives $$\\sup_{x\\in X}\\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}f(e^{2\\pi i\\sigma^k x})\n \\le\\sup_{x\\in X}\\exp\\left(\\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\\ell_M(\\sigma^k x)\\right)\n \\le e^{-an}.$$ The expression on the left is $\\|T_f^n\\|$, because the power is a unitary $U^n$ times multiplication by that continuous product. The spectral-radius formula yields radius zero, since $a$ was arbitrary. Finally $T_f\\ne0$ because $f>0$ almost everywhere. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }
      ],
      "id": "limit-continuous-norm-averaging",
      "kind": "hypothesis",
      "statement": "The circle norm-quasinilpotence argument uses continuous truncated logarithms and uniform averaging under irrational rotation, together with the divergent negative logarithmic integral. Pointwise or average numerical decay is not the asserted operator-norm conclusion.",
      "support": "inferred"
    },
    {
      "blocked_use": [
        "Assert that every angle sequence in the allowed intervals satisfies the sampling theorem.",
        "Choose the rotation after inspecting a candidate invariant projection.",
        "Treat finitely many successful tests as certification of the probability-one intersection."
      ],
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-p-prop-sampling",
          "exact_quote": "With probability one in the choice of $\\theta$, the following holds for every finite $I\\subset\\mathbb N$. There are positive finite Borel measures $\\eta_{I,l}$ on $X\\times X$ converging weakly to $\\lambda_I$, each carried by ordered pairs of the form $$(x,y)=(x,\\sigma_\\theta^n x)$$ with $n$ in a finite set of positive integers. Both marginals of $\\eta_{I,l}$ have $L^2(\\mu)$ densities, and their $L^2$ norms are bounded uniformly in $l$. The bound may depend on $I$ and on the fixed realization of $\\theta$. Weak convergence here means convergence against every continuous function on the compact space $X\\times X$.",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        },
        {
          "anchor": "pm-h-37",
          "exact_quote": "###### One probability-one event and the weak limit\n\nFor each fixed finite $I$, orientation, and rational cylinder rectangle or absent test, Tonelli’s Theorem applied to <a href=\"#p:eq:sampling-summable\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:sampling-summable\">[p:eq:sampling-summable]</a> shows that $$\\sum_l\\|Z_l-Z_l^0\\|_2^2<\\infty\n \\quad\\text{almost surely},$$ where only the sufficiently large admissible stages are included. In particular, $\\|Z_l-Z_l^0\\|_2\\to0$. Finite subsets of $\\mathbb N$ form a countable family, as do finite cylinder rectangles with rational endpoints, and there are only two orientations. Intersect these countably many probability-one events. For the remainder of the proof take any $\\theta$ in this intersection.\n\nWithout a test, $Z_l$ is a marginal density of $\\widetilde\\eta_{I,l}$. Since $\\|Z_l^0\\|_2^2=A=O(1)$, the $L^2$ convergence just proved bounds both marginal norms uniformly in $l$. We may omit finitely many initial stages; alternatively, each of their densities is bounded for fixed $l$, so they can be included by enlarging the bound. The total masses of $\\widetilde\\eta_{I,l}$ are consequently uniformly bounded.\n\n\nFor a fixed cylinder rectangle $\\mathcal R$, using the first orientation with its test shows that $$\\left|\\widetilde\\eta_{I,l}(\\mathcal R)\n       -\\int_X Z_l^0\\,d\\mu\\right|\n \\le\\|Z_l-Z_l^0\\|_2\\longrightarrow0.$$ The ideal integral has a direct product interpretation on the first $d$ coordinates. For $i\\in I$, integration of $w_i(x_i,t)$ in $x_i,t$ gives exactly the triangular measure under $y_i=x_i+t$. For $i\\notin I$, first take uniform $x_i$, discard $\\Delta(x_i)<\\alpha$, and, otherwise, take $t$ uniform in $(0,h\\Delta(x_i))$, setting $y_i=x_i+t$. Before the discard this is a probability law (the value at the two endpoint values of $x_i$ may be defined arbitrarily), and $|y_i-x_i|\\le h/2$. The total loss caused by all cutoffs is at most $$2d\\alpha=2dm^{-2}\\longrightarrow0,$$ by the union bound, with the finite triangular factors having mass at most one. Each fixed tested coordinate outside $I$ therefore converges to its uniform diagonal law. More explicitly, outside an $h$-neighborhood of a tested interval’s endpoints, membership of $y_i$ in that interval agrees with membership of $x_i$; the neighborhood has Lebesgue measure $O(h)$. Since a fixed rectangle tests only finitely many coordinates, all such errors vanish. Untested coordinates contribute only their masses, and the total cutoff estimate controls their product even as $d$ increases. It follows that $$\\label{p:eq:sampling-rectangle-limit}\n \\widetilde\\eta_{I,l}(\\mathcal R)\\longrightarrow\n                  \\lambda_I(\\mathcal R).$$\n\nThese limits imply weak convergence on $X\\times X$. Here are the topological details. The boundaries of the interval rectangles have zero $\\lambda_I$ measure, since every single-coordinate marginal of $\\lambda_I$ is absolutely continuous. Every continuous function on the compact product can be uniformly approximated by a continuous function of finitely many coordinates: in a summable product metric, replace the tail coordinates by fixed points and use uniform continuity. On the resulting finite torus, a sufficiently fine rational grid gives a step function, a finite linear combination of rational rectangle indicators, with arbitrarily small uniform error. Thus <a href=\"#p:eq:sampling-rectangle-limit\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:sampling-rectangle-limit\">[p:eq:sampling-rectangle-limit]</a> and the uniform bound on total masses give convergence of integrals for every continuous function. In particular, $$\\widetilde\\eta_{I,l}\\ \\Longrightarrow\\ \\lambda_I.$$",
          "source_id": "PM-293.1"
        }

          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-p-prop-biased-identity</summary>\n\n\n*Proof.* Fix $j$. For $D\\ge1$, define $$y_i^{(D)}=\n \\begin{cases}\n  y_i,&i\\le D,\\\\\n  x_i,&i>D,\n \\end{cases}$$ and let $\\beta_D$ be the law of $(x,y^{(D)})$ under $\\mathbb P_j$. We claim that $$\\label{p:eq:finite-prefix-domination}\n \\beta_D\\ll\\sum_{I\\subseteq\\{1,\\ldots,D\\}}\\lambda_I.$$ Condition on a pattern $c$ of mixture labels in the first $D$ coordinates. There are finitely many patterns. Let $I(c)$ consist of the coordinates carrying replacement labels. At each $i\\in I(c)$ the conditioned pair has density $$r_{c,i}(t,z)=\n \\frac{\\mathbf 1_{U_{c,i}}(t)\\mathbf 1_{V_{c,i}}(z)}\n {|U_{c,i}|\\,|V_{c,i}|}$$ relative to the unnormalized triangular measure $\\mathbf 1_{\\{0\\le t<z<1\\}}\\,dt\\,dz$. Here $U_{c,i},V_{c,i}$ are the two intervals of the selected component. Their lengths are positive and their rectangle is supported on $t<z$. The remaining prefix coordinates are uniformly diagonal.\n\nFor the diagonal tail, let $g$ be the full $x$-marginal density from Lemma <a href=\"#p:lem:coupling-marginals\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"p:lem:coupling-marginals\">29</a>. Write $\\mu=\\mu_{\\le D}\\otimes\\mu_{>D}$ and define $$\\label{p:eq:tail-density}\n g_D(z)=\\int g(u,z)\\,d\\mu_{\\le D}(u),\n \\qquad\n \\|g_D\\|_{L^2(\\mu_{>D})}\\le\\|g\\|_{L^2(\\mu)}\\le H.$$ Fubini identifies $g_D$ as the density of $x_{>D}$, and Jensen’s inequality proves the norm bound. Independence across coordinates ensures that conditioning on $c$ leaves this tail law unchanged. Therefore the conditional law of $(x,y^{(D)})$ has density $$\\label{p:eq:conditioned-prefix-density}\n g_D(x_{>D})\\prod_{i\\in I(c)}r_{c,i}(x_i,y_i)$$ with respect to $\\lambda_{I(c)}$. This follows on cylinder sets from the coordinate construction, and hence on all Borel sets. The rectangle factors form a finite product and are bounded. The tail factor is integrable and need not equal one. Summing the conditional laws over the finitely many patterns proves <a href=\"#p:eq:finite-prefix-domination\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:finite-prefix-domination\">[p:eq:finite-prefix-domination]</a>.\n\nProposition <a href=\"#p:prop:pair-identity\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"p:prop:pair-identity\">28</a> now gives the pair identity $\\beta_D$-almost everywhere. To pass to the limit, the first marginal of $\\beta_D$ is the $x$-marginal of $\\nu_j$, while its second marginal uses the $y$-marginals in the first $D$ coordinates and the $x$-marginals thereafter. Lemma <a href=\"#p:lem:coupling-marginals\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"p:lem:coupling-marginals\">29</a> and <a href=\"#p:eq:uniform-absolute-continuity\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:uniform-absolute-continuity\">[p:eq:uniform-absolute-continuity]</a> therefore give uniform absolute continuity of both marginal families, and absolute continuity of the two marginals of $\\nu_j$.\n\nOn the common probability space, $y^{(D)}\\to y$ in the product topology. Since $X^2$ is compact metrizable, bounded convergence against continuous functions proves $\\beta_D\\Rightarrow\\nu_j$. Every one of these laws is carried by ordered pairs. Their absolutely continuous marginals put both endpoints in the common conull set on which the cocycles are finite and positive. The ordered-coordinate formula for the cocycle ratios consequently gives $0<R_{x,y^{(D)}}(k)\\le1$ and $0<R_{x,y}(k)\\le1$, simultaneously for every signed $k$. All hypotheses of Lemma <a href=\"#lem:compact-transfer\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:compact-transfer\">11</a> are now satisfied, with the fixed cap $B=1$. That lemma transfers the identity from $\\beta_D$ to $\\nu_j$, proving <a href=\"#p:eq:biased-identity\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"p:eq:biased-identity\">[p:eq:biased-identity]</a>. ◻",

### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 293 (`lean/docs/293.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [Invariant-projection counterexamples for every irrational rotation](../../preprints/Invariant-projection-counterexamples-for-every-irrational-rotation-September-27-2026/paper.pdf)
> - [Backward intertwiners and a transitive commutant](../../preprints/Backward-intertwiners-and-a-transitive-commutant-September-27-2026/paper.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> For every irrational rotation angle $\theta\in(0,1)$, the formalization constructs a continuous nonnegative circle weight with a single zero and logarithmic integral $-\infty$ whose weighted rotation is nonzero and quasinilpotent. In its associated hyperfinite type $\mathrm{II}_1$ factor, the operator has no nontrivial invariant projection: $(1-p)Tp=0$ forces $p=0$ or $p=1$. The linked statements also include an earlier existence construction, a product realization, and the selected product model's Brown measure $\delta_0$.
>
> The formalization for the companion paper [Backward intertwiners and a transitive commutant](../../preprints/Backward-intertwiners-and-a-transitive-commutant-September-27-2026/paper.pdf) gives, on every separable infinite-dimensional complex Hilbert space, a nonzero quasinilpotent operator whose commutant is transitive, proper, and closed in the strong operator topology. Thus no nontrivial closed subspace is invariant under every operator in the commutant.
>
> The hyperinvariant-subspace problem asks whether every bounded operator on a complex Hilbert space has a nontrivial closed subspace invariant under every operator that commutes with it. The formalization gives a negative answer on every separable infinite-dimensional complex Hilbert space: there is a nonzero quasinilpotent operator with a transitive commutant. Its commutant is a proper unital operator algebra closed in the strong operator topology. The construction therefore has no nonzero proper closed hyperinvariant subspace.

```lean
structure TracialState (A : Type*) [CStarAlgebra A] where
  toContinuousLinearMap : A →L[ℂ] ℂ
  map_one : toContinuousLinearMap 1 = 1
  map_star : ∀ a, toContinuousLinearMap (star a) = star (toContinuousLinearMap a)
  positive : ∀ a, 0 ≤ (toContinuousLinearMap (star a * a)).re
  tracial : ∀ a b, toContinuousLinearMap (a * b) = toContinuousLinearMap (b * a)
```

```lean
theorem prescribed_irrational_counterexample (θ : ℝ)
    (hθ0 : 0 < θ) (hθ1 : θ < 1) (hθ : Irrational θ) :
    ∃ f : C(Circle, ℝ), Rotation.IsContinuousCounterexample θ f
```

```lean
theorem product_realization :
    ∃ θ c : ℕ → ℝ, ProductModel.IsCounterexampleRealization θ c
```

The linked statements also include an earlier existence construction, a product realization, and the selected product model's Brown measure $\\delta_0$.\n\nThe formalization for the companion paper [Backward intertwiners and a transitive commutant](../../preprints/Backward-intertwiners-and-a-transitive-commutant-September-27-2026/paper.pdf) gives, on every separable infinite-dimensional complex Hilbert space, a nonzero quasinilpotent operator whose commutant is transitive, proper, and closed in the strong operator topology. Thus no nontrivial closed subspace is invariant under every operator in the commutant.\n\nThe hyperinvariant-subspace problem asks whether every bounded operator on a complex Hilbert space has a nontrivial closed subspace invariant under every operator that commutes with it. The formalization gives a negative answer on every separable infinite-dimensional complex Hilbert space: there is a nonzero quasinilpotent operator with a transitive commutant. <a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


For every irrational angle, we construct a continuous nonnegative circle weight with exactly one zero and logarithmic integral $`-\infty`$ whose weighted rotation has no nontrivial invariant projection in the associated hyperfinite type II<sub>1</sub> factor. This answers negatively the question of Zhu, Fang, and Shi, with the angle prescribed in advance. The resulting operator is nonzero and norm-quasinilpotent.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

Let $\theta\in(0,1)$ be irrational. In the tracial representation let $R_\theta$ be the von Neumann algebra generated by unitaries $U,V$ with $$VU=e^{2\pi i\theta}UV,\qquad
 \tau(U^mV^n)=0\quad((m,n)\in\mathbb Z^2\setminus\{(0,0)\}),\qquad\tau(1)=1.$$ This is the hyperfinite factor of type $\mathrm{II}_1$; the concrete representation used below is the group-measure-space representation of irrational rotation. For a continuous function $f$ on the unit circle $\mathbb T$, write $T_f=Uf(V)$. A closed invariant subspace is *affiliated with $R_\theta$* when its orthogonal projection belongs to $R_\theta$. A nontrivial such subspace is therefore equivalent to a projection $p\in R_\theta$ satisfying $$0<\tau(p)<1,\qquad (1-p)T_fp=0.$$ There is no requirement that $p$ commute with $T_f$.

Let $m$ denote normalized Haar measure on $\mathbb T$. The Fuglede–Kadison determinant of the scalar weight is $$\Delta(f(V))=\exp\left(\int_\mathbb T\log|f(z)|\,dm(z)\right),$$ with $\log0=-\infty$ and $\exp(-\infty)=0$. Zhu, Fang, and Shi asked whether $T_f$ must have a nontrivial affiliated invariant subspace if $f$ is nonzero almost everywhere, $|f|$ is nonconstant, and this determinant is zero (Zhu, Fang, and Shi 2017, 264, Question after Proposition 5.7) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[zfs](#ref-zfs)]. We answer their question negatively for every prescribed irrational angle.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *For every irrational $\theta\in(0,1)$ there exists a continuous function $f:\mathbb T\to[0,1]$ such that $$f^{-1}(\{0\})=\{1\},\qquad \int_\mathbb T\log f\,dm=-\infty,$$ and every projection $p\in R_\theta$ satisfying $(1-p)Uf(V)p=0$ is either $0$ or $1$.*

</div>

The angle is fixed before the weight is constructed; no Diophantine condition is imposed. The conclusion concerns projections in the specified containing factor. Arbitrary invariant subspaces of its Hilbert-space representation need not have their projections in that factor. Corollary <a href="#cor:quasinilpotent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:quasinilpotent">22</a> also shows directly that the constructed operator is nonzero and norm-quasinilpotent.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Hyperinvariant subspaces and commutants

For a bounded operator $T$ on a complex Hilbert space $\mathcal H$, write $\{T\}'=\{A\in B(\mathcal H):AT=TA\}$. A closed subspace is *hyperinvariant* for $T$ if every member of $\{T\}'$ leaves it invariant. An operator algebra is *transitive* if its only common closed invariant subspaces are $0$ and $\mathcal H$.

<a id="stmt-lem-commutant-algebra"></a>
<div id="lem:commutant-algebra" class="lemma">

**Lemma 2** (Commutant algebra). *If $T\in B(\mathcal H)$ is nonscalar, then $\{T\}'$ is a proper, strong-operator closed, unital complex algebra. It is transitive exactly when $T$ has no nontrivial hyperinvariant subspace.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-commutant-algebra"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-commutant-algebra</summary>

*Proof.* The commutation equation is preserved by addition, multiplication and scalar multiplication, and the identity commutes with $T$. If $A_\lambda\to A$ strongly and $A_\lambda T=TA_\lambda$, then for every $\zeta\in\mathcal H$ both sides converge, giving $AT\zeta=TA\zeta$. Thus the algebra is strongly closed. If every bounded operator commuted with $T$, then every rank-one orthogonal projection would commute with $T$, so every nonzero vector would be an eigenvector. Applying this to $x,y,x+y$ for linearly independent vectors shows that the eigenvalues are equal; hence $T$ would be scalar. Properness follows. The last assertion is precisely the two definitions. ◻

</details>

The bridge to the containing von Neumann algebra is the elementary observation recorded in Dykema (2005, Proposition 2.1) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[dykema2005](#ref-dykema2005)].

<a id="stmt-lem-hyperinvariant-projection"></a>
<div id="lem:hyperinvariant-projection" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3**. *Let $\mathcal M\subset B(\mathcal H)$ be a von Neumann algebra and $T\in\mathcal M$. The orthogonal projection onto any hyperinvariant subspace for $T$ belongs to $\mathcal M$ and is invariant for $T$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-hyperinvariant-projection"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-hyperinvariant-projection</summary>

*Proof.* Every unitary $W\in\mathcal M'$ and its inverse commute with $T$. Hyperinvariance therefore makes the subspace reducing for $W$. Its orthogonal projection commutes with all such unitaries and hence belongs to $\mathcal M''=\mathcal M$. Since $T\in\{T\}'$, its range is invariant under $T$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-cor-transitive-commutant"></a>
<div id="cor:transitive-commutant" class="corollary">

**Corollary 4**. *Every separable infinite-dimensional complex Hilbert space carries a nonzero norm-quasinilpotent operator with no nontrivial hyperinvariant subspace. Its commutant is a proper strongly closed transitive unital algebra.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-transitive-commutant"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-transitive-commutant</summary>

*Proof.* The tracial representation of $R_\theta$ is separable and infinite-dimensional. Apply Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, Corollary <a href="#cor:quasinilpotent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:quasinilpotent">22</a>, and Lemma <a href="#lem:hyperinvariant-projection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:hyperinvariant-projection">3</a> there. A nonzero quasinilpotent operator is nonscalar, so Lemma <a href="#lem:commutant-algebra" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:commutant-algebra">2</a> applies. Unitary equivalence transfers these properties to any separable infinite-dimensional complex Hilbert space. ◻

</details>

These conclusions do not exclude ordinary invariant subspaces in the representation. A separate companion gives a direct $2$-adic weighted shift with a transitive commutant, including an explicit norm estimate, backward intertwiners and a description of its polar conjugates (OpenAI 2026) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[direct-companion](#ref-direct-companion)]. It uses only Lemmas <a href="#lem:commutant-algebra" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:commutant-algebra">2</a> and <a href="#lem:scalar-multiplier-fields" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:scalar-multiplier-fields">9</a> from this paper. The present proof uses no result from that companion.

<a id="stmt-rem-ckl"></a>
<div id="rem:ckl" class="remark">

*Remark 5*. Corollary <a href="#cor:transitive-commutant" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:transitive-commutant">4</a> contradicts the hyperinvariant-subspace assertion of Chō, Ko, and Lee (2017, Corollary 2.8, p. 801) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[cho-ko-lee](#ref-cho-ko-lee)] at $\mu=1$: for the nonzero quasinilpotent $T$ just constructed and any conjugation $C$, their stated hypotheses apply to $I+T$, but $\{I+T\}'=\{T\}'$, so $I+T$ has no nontrivial hyperinvariant subspace. Only that hyperinvariant-subspace assertion is at issue here.

</div>

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Prior work

The determinant of Fuglede and Kadison (Fuglede and Kadison 1952) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[fuglede-kadison](#ref-fuglede-kadison)] and Brown’s spectral distribution (Brown 1986) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[brown](#ref-brown)] form a major route to invariant subspaces in finite factors. Haagerup and Schultz associate an affiliated hyperinvariant projection to each Borel subset of the plane, with trace equal to its Brown mass (Haagerup and Schultz 2009, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[haagerup-schultz](#ref-haagerup-schultz)]. Thus a Brown measure that is not a point mass gives a nontrivial projection. For a point-mass Brown measure, the trace formula gives only trivial projections.

Point-mass Brown measure can nevertheless coexist with many invariant subspaces. Dykema and Haagerup construct a one-parameter family of nontrivial affiliated hyperinvariant subspaces for the quasinilpotent DT-operator (Dykema and Haagerup 2004, Theorems 2.2 and 2.9) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[dykema-haagerup](#ref-dykema-haagerup)]. Thus degeneration of Brown measure to a point mass does not itself exclude invariant projections. Tucci also constructed quasinilpotent generators of the hyperfinite factor with nontrivial affiliated invariant projections (Tucci 2008, Theorems 3.1 and 3.3 and the discussion following Theorem 3.3) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Tucci2008](#ref-Tucci2008)]. These examples further distinguish quasinilpotence from the absence of factor-invariant projections.

For weighted irrational rotations, the measurable-action theorem of Dykema and Schultz identifies the Brown measure as uniform measure on the circle whose radius is the determinant (Dykema and Schultz 2009, Theorem 5.4) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[dykema-schultz](#ref-dykema-schultz)]. Zhu, Fang, and Shi give a direct proof, explicitly crediting that earlier result (Zhu, Fang, and Shi 2017, Theorem 5.3) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[zfs](#ref-zfs)]. At determinant zero this measure is the point mass at zero, so the Brown-measure projections are trivial.

Continuous weights with one zero and determinant zero were already exhibited by Zhu, Fang, and Shi (Zhu, Fang, and Shi 2017, 262–63) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[zfs](#ref-zfs)]. The additional conclusion of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> is the exclusion of every nontrivial invariant projection for a suitable weight at each prescribed angle. Zhu, Fang, and Shi’s spectral-radius formula (Zhu, Fang, and Shi 2017, Theorem 2.3) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[zfs](#ref-zfs)] implies quasinilpotence in this case. We give an elementary proof of this special case, using only uniform averaging of continuous functions under irrational rotation. Their positive-determinant invariant-subspace result (Zhu, Fang, and Shi 2017, Corollary 5.5) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[zfs](#ref-zfs)] has a hypothesis complementary to the one studied here.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Proof strategy

The obstruction begins with a measurable weight. Suppose its weighted rotation had a nontrivial invariant projection. In the shift representation, this projection becomes a field of projections. The finite trace and ergodicity force its central diagonal entry to lie strictly between zero and one almost everywhere. Thus both the projection and its complement have vectors with a nonzero central coordinate.

Invariance imposes weighted orthogonality identities on these vectors. We first obtain the identities along rotation orbits and then pass them to suitably coupled pairs of points. The weight has drops that are rare enough to leave a chosen finite window under control, but strong enough to suppress contributions outside that window. The central coordinates force at least one finite convolution coefficient to stay away from zero. The suppressed tail cannot cancel it, contradicting the exact orthogonality identity. Conditioning separately on each endpoint controls that tail even though it has infinitely many terms.

The measurable weight is then replaced by a continuous weight with one zero. Smoothing and moving the logarithmic drops changes their sum by a constant plus a measurable coboundary. This relation supplies an intertwining multiplier, possibly unbounded. A domain and trace argument shows that an invariant projection for the continuous operator would produce one for the measurable operator with exactly the same trace. This transfers the obstruction to the desired continuous weight.

The construction realizing this scheme is as follows. Use the additive circle $X=\mathbb R/\mathbb Z$, with Haar probability $\mu$, and write $\sigma x=x+\theta$. Write $\{t\}\in[0,1)$ for fractional part. We construct divisible integers $1=q_0\mid q_1\mid q_2\mid\cdots$ and positive costs $c_i$. The measurable model is the weight $$g_*(x)=\sum_{i\ge1}c_i\lfloor\{q_ix\}+\{q_i\theta\}\rfloor,
 \qquad f_*(x)=e^{-g_*(x)}.$$ The series is finite almost everywhere although its integral diverges. Large divisible frequencies allow its trial phases to be realized at the fixed angle $\theta$, while finitely many sampling tests approximate independent Haar coordinates. The circle characters themselves are dependent. A mixed-radix expansion supplies independent digits, from which we build the ordered couplings with controlled marginal densities. The prescribed-marginal viewpoint belongs to the classical coupling theory of Strassen and Edwards (Strassen 1965; Edwards 1978) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Strassen1965](#ref-Strassen1965), [Edwards1978](#ref-Edwards1978)]; here we also need approximation by positive orbit pairs.

Section <a href="#sec:parameters" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:parameters">2</a> fixes the frequencies and sampling powers. Sections <a href="#sec:fields" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:fields">3</a> and <a href="#sec:couplings" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:couplings">4</a> obtain the projection identities on the ordered pairs. Section <a href="#sec:obstruction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:obstruction">5</a> proves and applies the convolution obstruction, and Section <a href="#sec:continuity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:continuity">6</a> constructs the continuous weight and proves the trace-preserving transfer.

Appendix <a href="#p:sec:product" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:sec:product">7</a> gives a complete alternative realization on an infinite product of circles. Independent coordinates yield a finite-coordinate orbit-sampling theorem and a second application of the same convolution obstruction. That model also separates the atomic algebras generated by finitely many polar conjugates from the diffuse algebra generated by the full orbit. The prescribed-angle circle proof is complete before the appendix.

All identities involving measurable fields hold on appropriate conull sets. When integer iterates are used, we intersect the countably many translates of these sets. The constants implicit in $O(\cdot)$ below are absolute unless a fixed construction stage is specified. Every new ingredient needed for the proof is established in the paper.

<a id="sec:parameters"></a>
### Sampling at a prescribed irrational angle

Fix $X=\mathbb R/\mathbb Z$ with Haar probability $\mu$, and put $\sigma x=x+\theta$, where $\theta\in(0,1)$ is a fixed irrational number. We write $\{t\}\in[0,1)$ for fractional part. Our immediate aim is to construct rare logarithmic drops whose characters behave almost like independent coordinates in finitely many sampling tests. The frequencies will be divisible integers; the angle $\theta$ will never be changed.

We use the following elementary facts about irrational rotations. For each nonzero integer $a$, the average of $e^{2\pi ian\theta}$ over $1\le n\le N$ tends to zero, by the formula for a geometric sum. Trigonometric approximation gives equidistribution against continuous functions, and continuous upper and lower bounds give it for intervals. The same argument gives convergence uniform in the starting point for continuous functions. In particular, every positive orbit is dense. These facts also hold with $\theta$ replaced by any positive integer multiple of $\theta$.

<a id="eq:sampling-kernel"></a>
For an integer $m\ge200$, put $h=m^{-3/5}$ and $\Delta(z)=\min(\{z\},1-\{z\})$. Define on two circles $$\label{eq:sampling-kernel}
 w_m(z,v)=\mathbf1_{\{\Delta(z)\ge m^{-2}\}}
       \frac{\mathbf1_{\{0<v<h\Delta(z)\}}}{h\Delta(z)},
 \qquad
 w_m^{(1)}(z,v)=w_m(z,v),\quad
 w_m^{(2)}(z,v)=w_m(z-v,v),$$ where $v$ is represented in $[0,1)$ and the expression is zero when the cutoff fails. The first kernel forces a small positive move with no wrap around zero. The second is its density viewed from the endpoint.

The kernels have almost unit mass at either endpoint. We next control their second moments and the correlations produced by two separated integer powers. These estimates will let us average products over a whole block of coordinates. The scalar lemmas also serve the independent-coordinate construction in Appendix <a href="#p:sec:product" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:sec:product">7</a>.

<a id="stmt-lem-scalar-sampling-estimates"></a>
<div id="lem:scalar-sampling-estimates" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:kernel-estimates"></a>
**Lemma 6** (Scalar sampling estimates). *Let $m\ge200$ be an integer, put $h=m^{-3/5}$, and let $u=w_m^{(r)}$ for either $r=1$ or $r=2$. Write $\bar u(s)=\int_0^1u(s,v)\,dv$. Uniformly in $s$, the supremum plus total variation of $v\mapsto u(s,v)$ is $O(m^{13/5})$, hence $O(m^{10})$, and $$\label{eq:kernel-estimates}
 \begin{aligned}
 &\|\bar u\|_\infty\le2,\qquad
   \int_0^1\bar u=1-2m^{-2},\\
 &(1-2m^{-2})^2\le\int_0^1\bar u^2
       \le1+\frac{h^2}{(1-h)^2},\qquad
   \iint_{[0,1]^2}u^2=\frac2h\log\frac{m^2}{2}.
 \end{aligned}$$*

<a id="eq:scalar-marginal"></a>
<a id="eq:scalar-rectangles"></a>
<a id="eq:scalar-bv"></a>
*For the sampling estimate let $0<N_0\le N\le m^2$, $Q=m^{(7/10)m}$ and $H=m^{300}$. Let $\lambda$ be uniform on $[1/N_0,2/N_0]$. If $n\in[Q/2,Q]\cap\mathbb N$, the density $p_n$ of $\{n\lambda\}$ satisfies $$\label{eq:scalar-marginal}
 |p_n-1|\le N_0/n\quad\hbox{almost everywhere}.$$ If two distinct such powers $n,n'$ satisfy $$|vn+v'n'|>Nm^{1000}
 \quad(0<|v|+|v'|,\ |v|,|v'|\le H,\ v,v'\in\mathbb Z),$$ then, for intervals $I,J\subset[0,1)$, $$\label{eq:scalar-rectangles}
 \left|\mathbb P(\{n\lambda\}\in I,\{n'\lambda\}\in J)
             -|I||J|\right|=O(m^{-80}).$$ Consequently, for complex functions $f,g$ of bounded variation on $[0,1]$, with $B(f)=\|f\|_\infty+\operatorname{Var}(f)$, $$\label{eq:scalar-bv}
 \left|\mathbb E[f(\{n\lambda\})g(\{n'\lambda\})]
           -\left(\int_0^1f\right)\left(\int_0^1g\right)\right|
       \le C m^{-80}B(f)B(g).$$ All implicit constants are absolute, for sufficiently large $m$, and uniform in $N_0,N,n,n'$ and the intervals or functions.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-scalar-sampling-estimates"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-scalar-sampling-estimates</summary>

*Proof.* For the first orientation the function of $v$ is a constant height at most $m^{13/5}$ on an interval, so the variation bound is immediate. For the second, nonzero values require $v<s$ in representatives $s,v\in[0,1)$: if $v>s$, then $\Delta(s-v)\le v-s<v$, whereas $h<1$. On $0<v<s$, the conditions $\Delta(s-v)\ge m^{-2}$ and $v<h\Delta(s-v)$ describe an interval. The reciprocal of $\Delta(s-v)$ has at most two monotone pieces there, with the same height bound. This proves the asserted uniform bound in both orientations.

In the first orientation $\bar u$ is the cutoff indicator, and $$\iint u^2=\frac2h\int_{m^{-2}}^{1/2}\frac{ds}{s}
          =\frac2h\log(m^2/2).$$ The change $(s,v)\mapsto(s-v,v)$ preserves double integrals, giving the same total mass and square integral for the second orientation. To bound its averaged density, first omit the cutoff. The resulting density $d$ is the law of $z+t\Delta(z)$ for independent uniform $z\in[0,1)$ and $t\in(0,h)$. For fixed $t$ this is an increasing piecewise-linear bijection of $[0,1]$, with slopes $1+t$ and $1-t$. Thus $\int d=1$ and $|d-1|\le h/(1-h)$, whence $$\int d^2=1+\int(d-1)^2\le1+\frac{h^2}{(1-h)^2}.$$ Restoring the cutoff only decreases this nonnegative density; also $d\le(1-h)^{-1}\le2$. These observations give the upper bounds in <a href="#eq:kernel-estimates" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-estimates">[eq:kernel-estimates]</a>, while Cauchy–Schwarz gives the lower bound from its exact mass. The cancellation of the linear term here is essential when multiplying estimates over $m$ coordinates.

Periodizing a uniform interval of length $n/N_0$ gives <a href="#eq:scalar-marginal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scalar-marginal">[eq:scalar-marginal]</a>; in particular $p_n\le2$ for large $m$. For the separated powers and any permitted nonzero frequency pair, direct integration on $[1/N_0,2/N_0]$ gives $$\left|\mathbb E e^{2\pi i(vn+v'n')\lambda}\right|
 \le\frac{N_0}{\pi|vn+v'n'|}\le\frac1\pi m^{-1000}.$$ To pass from characters to rectangles, convolve each interval indicator with the Fejér kernel $$F_H(s)=\frac1H\left(\frac{\sin(\pi Hs)}{\sin(\pi s)}\right)^2.$$ Expanding the squared geometric sum shows that $F_H$ is nonnegative, has mass one, and has frequencies of magnitude below $H$. The convolved indicators lie in $[0,1]$, preserve their means, and have Fourier coefficients of modulus at most one. Outside strips of width $\eta=m^{-80}$ about interval endpoints, their error is $O((H\eta)^{-1})$, by integrating the kernel tail. The marginal bound $p_n,p_{n'}\le2$ shows that replacing both indicators in a product costs $O(\eta+(H\eta)^{-1})$, both for the actual joint law and for Haar product measure. The remaining Fourier error is $O(H^2m^{-1000})$. This proves <a href="#eq:scalar-rectangles" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scalar-rectangles">[eq:scalar-rectangles]</a>; no joint density, or independence of the sampled powers, is used.

Finally, a bounded-variation function is, up to a null set, a constant plus an integral of indicators of intervals $[a,1)$ against its complex Stieltjes measure. The constant and measure have total size at most $B(f)$; the same holds for $g$. Apply <a href="#eq:scalar-rectangles" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scalar-rectangles">[eq:scalar-rectangles]</a> to the resulting products of interval indicators, allowing the full interval. Integration against total variation gives <a href="#eq:scalar-bv" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scalar-bv">[eq:scalar-bv]</a>. Changes at the countably many possible discontinuities do not matter, because the marginals have densities. ◻

</details>

Before choosing the block scales, we record the finite-coefficient bound that will determine how much attenuation each block must supply.

<a id="stmt-lem-polynomial-coefficient"></a>
<div id="lem:polynomial-coefficient" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7** (A nonzero finite convolution). *For every integer $K\ge0$ and $D\ge\delta>0$ there is $\varepsilon=\varepsilon(K,D,\delta)>0$ such that complex Laurent polynomials $A,B$ supported in $[-K,K]$, with coefficients of modulus at most $D$ and $|A_0|,|B_0|\ge\delta$, satisfy $$\max_{0\le n\le2K}|(AB)_{-n}|\ge\varepsilon.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-polynomial-coefficient"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-polynomial-coefficient</summary>

*Proof.* The admissible polynomial pairs form a nonempty compact set. For each pair, the first nonzero exponents of $A$ and $B$ are at most zero, and their sum belongs to $[-2K,0]$. The corresponding product coefficient is nonzero. The continuous displayed maximum therefore has a positive minimum on the compact set. ◻

</details>

We now choose the parameters for the fixed circle rotation. First prepare data for the later continuity argument. Let $O_j$ be the open arc of radius $1/(10j)$ about zero. Choose a continuous nonnegative bump supported in $O_j$ and positive on a smaller arc. Finitely many of its translates $x\mapsto\psi(\sigma^n x)$, with $0\le n\le h_j^*$, have a strictly positive sum on $X$, by density and compactness. Normalize by this sum to obtain continuous functions $\phi_{j,n}\ge0$ such that <a id="eq:partition"></a>$$\label{eq:partition}
 \sum_{n=0}^{h_j^*}\phi_{j,n}=1,
 \qquad \sigma^n(\operatorname{supp}\phi_{j,n})\subset O_j.$$ All $h_j^*$ and $\phi_{j,n}$ are fixed before the construction below.

The parameters below are grouped into consecutive blocks of $m_j$ coordinates. In block $j$, $K_j$ is the cutoff for a finite convolution, $C_j$ is the cost of each logarithmic drop, and $L_j$ is the attenuation needed to make the omitted tail smaller than a finite polynomial coefficient. The total phase increment in the block is of order $1/K_j$. The sampling horizon $Q_j$ lies well below the next cutoff $K_{j+1}$, so sampling moves can be controlled in every future block. The two widths $a_j,b_j$ will provide crossings in complementary ranges of the tail index. The proposition states these requirements together; its proof shows that they can all be chosen successively.

<a id="stmt-prop-parameters"></a>
<div id="prop:parameters" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:spread"></a>
**Proposition 8**. *For the fixed $\theta$ there are positive integers $m_j,K_j,G_j,q_i$, positive real numbers $L_j,C_j,\varepsilon_j$, and consecutive blocks $J_j=\{d_{j-1}+1,\ldots,d_j\}$ of size $m_j$, with $d_0=0$, having the following properties. Set $$\begin{gathered}
 q_0=1,\qquad u_i(x)=\{q_ix\},\qquad\alpha_i=\{q_i\theta\},\\
 \kappa_j=(100j)^{-1},\qquad N_j=m_jK_j,\\
 a_j=G_j/m_j,\qquad b_j=m_j^{-19/20},\qquad Q_j=m_j^{(7/10)m_j}.
\end{gathered}$$ The frequencies are strictly increasing and divisible, and <a id="eq:phases"></a>$$\label{eq:phases}
 \frac1{N_j}<\alpha_i<\frac2{N_j},\qquad
 q_i\mid q_{i+1},\qquad \frac{q_{i+1}}{q_i}\ge N_j
 \quad(i\in J_j).$$ For every integer $k$ with $N_j/4\le |k|\le m_j^{(4/5)m_j}$, at least $m_j/20$ indices in $J_j$ satisfy $$\label{eq:spread}
 1-\{k\alpha_i\}\in[b_j,1-b_j].$$ If $G_jK_j\le |k|\le N_j/4$, every index in $J_j$ has $1-\{k\alpha_i\}\in[a_j,1-a_j]$.*

<a id="eq:scales"></a>
<a id="eq:polynomial"></a>
*The initial values are $K_1=100,G_1=1$. The numbers $L_j\ge1$ are nondecreasing, $C_j=\max(L_j,K_j^2)$, and the sizes satisfy $$\begin{aligned}
\label{eq:scales}
 &m_j\ge\max(K_j,2^j,4G_j,200),\qquad
 K_{j+1}=\lceil m_j^{(3/4)m_j}\rceil>2K_j,\notag\\
 &G_{j+1}K_{j+1}<m_j^{(4/5)m_j},\qquad
 \kappa_jm_j^{1/40}\ge L_j,\qquad
 \kappa_{j+1}\sqrt{G_{j+1}}\ge L_j,\notag\\
 &h_{j+1}^*/K_{j+1}\le2^{-j}.
\end{aligned}$$ Put $D_j=\exp((K_j+1)\sum_{s<j}C_sm_s)$. If $A,B$ are Laurent polynomials supported in $[-K_j,K_j]$, every coefficient has modulus at most $D_j$, and $|A_0|,|B_0|\ge1/j$, then $$\label{eq:polynomial}
 \max_{0\le n\le2K_j}|(AB)_{-n}|\ge\varepsilon_j,
 \qquad
 (2K_j+1)3e^{-L_j/10000}\le\varepsilon_j/j.$$*

<a id="eq:sampling"></a>
*Finally put $p_*=d_{j-1}$ and $\ell_j=((j+1)^2(1+p_*)q_{p_*})^{-1}$. For every $t=r/q_{p_*}$ with $r\in\mathbb Z$, $0\le r<q_{p_*}$, there is a finite nonempty set $\mathcal N_{j,t}$ of positive integers in $[Q_j/2,Q_j]$, with $\{n\theta-t\}\in(0,\ell_j)$, such that for $r'=1,2$, $$\label{eq:sampling}
 \left\|\frac1{|\mathcal N_{j,t}|}\sum_{n\in\mathcal N_{j,t}}
       \prod_{i\in J_j}w_{m_j}^{(r')}(u_i(x),\{n\alpha_i\})-1
       \right\|_{L^2(\mu)}<\frac1j.$$*

</div>

The proof separates two choices. We first choose independent trial phases to obtain the finite probabilistic tests. Only afterwards do we realize them, to sufficient accuracy, by characters of the prescribed rotation. Large gaps between successive frequencies also make those characters converge in distribution to independent Haar variables.

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-parameters"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-parameters</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose $K_j,G_j$ and all earlier blocks are fixed. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:polynomial-coefficient" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:polynomial-coefficient">7</a> with $K=K_j$, $D=D_j$ and $\delta=1/j$ to choose $\varepsilon_j$. Choose $L_j\ge\max(1,L_{j-1})$ to satisfy the second inequality in <a href="#eq:polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:polynomial">[eq:polynomial]</a> (omit $L_{j-1}$ when $j=1$), set $C_j=\max(L_j,K_j^2)$, and then choose an integer $G_{j+1}$ with $\kappa_{j+1}\sqrt{G_{j+1}}\ge L_j$. All requirements of <a href="#eq:scales" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scales">[eq:scales]</a> now hold for sufficiently large $m=m_j$. In what follows earlier data remain fixed while $m\to\infty$; in particular $p_*,q_{p_*},\ell_j$ and the finite list of shifts $t$ are fixed. Write $N=mK_j$, $Q=m^{(7/10)m}$, $a=G_j/m$, and $b=m^{-19/20}$.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
###### Separated sampling powers.

Equidistribution gives at least a fixed positive multiple of $\ell_j Q$ integers $n\in[Q/2,Q]$ with $\{n\theta-t\}\in(0,\ell_j)$ for each required $t$, once $m$ is sufficiently large. Set $H=m^{300}$ and $R=Nm^{1000}$. We can select at least $m^{0.69m}$ such powers, separately for every $t$, so that distinct selected powers obey <a id="eq:power-separation"></a>$$\label{eq:power-separation}
 |vn+v'n'|>R\quad
 (|v|,|v'|\le H,\ (v,v')\ne(0,0),\ v,v'\in\mathbb Z).$$ Indeed, if $v'\ne0$, each previously chosen $n$ excludes at most $2R+2$ integers $n'$ for each frequency pair $(v,v')$. There are at most $(2H+1)^2$ pairs, and $N\le m^2$. A greedy selection thus loses only a polynomial factor in $m$ from the available number. Since $Q=m^{0.70m}$, it eventually leaves at least $m^{0.69m}$ powers. If $v'=0$ and $v\ne0$, then $|vn|\ge Q/2>R$ for large $m$, so this case causes no exclusion. Fix the resulting sets $\mathcal N_{j,t}$.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
###### Trial phases and the spread condition.

For $i\in J_j$, take independent uniform $\lambda_i\in[1/N,2/N]$. For each integer $k$ in the range of <a href="#eq:spread" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:spread">[eq:spread]</a>, the law of $\{k\lambda_i\}$ has density at most $5$: it is the periodization of a uniform interval of length $|k|/N\ge1/4$, whose density is at most $1+N/|k|$. The probability that an index fails the strict interior version of <a href="#eq:spread" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:spread">[eq:spread]</a> is therefore at most $10b$. If fewer than $m/20$ indices succeed, more than $19m/20$ fail. Independence and a union bound give failure probability at most $2^m(10b)^{(19/20)m}$. A further union over both signs of $k$, up to $m^{(4/5)m}$, tends to zero: the coefficient of $m\log m$ in its logarithm is $4/5-(19/20)^2<0$. The lower range in the proposition requires no probabilistic choice. When $G_jK_j\le|k|\le N/4$, every trial phase has $a\le|k|\lambda_i\le1/2$; the same observation will hold for the eventual $\alpha_i$.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
###### One-coordinate and two-power estimates.

Fix an orientation, put $u=w_m^{(r')}$, and set $\bar u(s)=\int_0^1u(s,v)\,dv$ and $A_*=\int_0^1\bar u(s)^2\,ds$. Lemma <a href="#lem:scalar-sampling-estimates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:scalar-sampling-estimates">6</a>, with $N_0=N$, gives $\|\bar u\|_\infty\le2$, $(1-2m^{-2})^2\le A_*\le1+4h^2$ and $\iint u^2\le4h^{-1}\log m$, where $h=m^{-3/5}$. Its bounded-variation estimate also gives, uniformly in $s$, <a id="eq:two-power"></a>$$\label{eq:two-power}
 \mathbb E[u(s,\{n\lambda_i\})u(s,\{n'\lambda_i\})]
       =\bar u(s)^2+O(m^{-50})\quad(n\ne n').$$ Indeed, the error from <a href="#eq:scalar-bv" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scalar-bv">[eq:scalar-bv]</a> is $O(m^{-60})$. The one-power density estimate <a href="#eq:scalar-marginal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scalar-marginal">[eq:scalar-marginal]</a> likewise gives $\mathbb E[u(s,\{n\lambda_i\})\bar u(s)]
=\bar u(s)^2+O(m^{-50})$, since $n\ge Q/2$.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
###### The product sampling test.

We now have one-coordinate and two-power estimates. Their role is to control the second moment of an average of products over a whole block; the diagonal terms will be absorbed by the large number of sampling powers, while the distinct-power terms approach the ideal product law. For independent Haar variables $z=(z_i)_{i\in J_j}$, and a fixed power set of size $P$, put $$Z(z)=\frac1P\sum_{n\in\mathcal N_{j,t}}
                 \prod_{i\in J_j}u(z_i,\{n\lambda_i\}),
 \qquad Z^{\rm id}(z)=\prod_{i\in J_j}\bar u(z_i).$$ By independence of the trial phases over $i$, the distinct-power terms of $\mathbb E\|Z\|_2^2$, and the terms of $\mathbb E\langle Z,Z^{\rm id}\rangle$, are products of $m$ factors $A_*+O(m^{-50})$. They equal $A_*^m+O(m^{-49})$ by telescoping, because $(1+4h^2+O(m^{-50}))^m$ stays bounded. The equal-power terms are each at most $(8h^{-1}\log m)^m$ by <a href="#eq:kernel-estimates" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-estimates">[eq:kernel-estimates]</a> and the one-phase density bound, and their total averaging weight is $1/P$. Thus $$\mathbb E\|Z-Z^{\rm id}\|_2^2
 \le O(m^{-49})+P^{-1}(8h^{-1}\log m)^m\longrightarrow0,$$ since $P\ge m^{0.69m}$ and $h^{-1}=m^{0.60}$. Also $$\|Z^{\rm id}-1\|_2^2
 =A_*^m-2(1-2m^{-2})^m+1\longrightarrow0$$ by <a href="#eq:kernel-estimates" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:kernel-estimates">[eq:kernel-estimates]</a> and $mh^2=m^{-1/5}\to0$. It follows that $\mathbb E\|Z-1\|_2^2\to0$. There are only finitely many $t$ at this stage. Markov’s inequality, the two orientations, and the spread estimate therefore let us fix trial phases with strict spread inequalities and sampling norms below $1/(2j)$, simultaneously for every test. We can also avoid the finitely many equalities $n\lambda_i\in\mathbb Z$ and the endpoints $1/N,2/N$, since each has probability zero.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
###### Realizing the phases by circle characters.

Now fix this $m$ and these trial phases. Successively choose $q_i$ as multiples of the preceding frequency so that $\{q_i\theta\}$ is as close to $\lambda_i$ as desired and $q_i/q_{i-1}$ is as large as desired. This is possible because the positive multiples of $q_{i-1}\theta$ are dense. More formally, make a sequence of such finite block choices for which all new ratios tend to infinity and all $\alpha_i\to\lambda_i$. The Haar law of $(u_i(x))_{i\in J_j}$ then converges weakly to product Haar measure. For each fixed nonzero integer character vector, the last nonzero coefficient times its frequency dominates the sum of the earlier terms, once the ratios are sufficiently large. That Fourier coefficient is therefore exactly zero eventually. Trigonometric approximation proves the weak convergence. The joint law including the deterministic phases converges to product Haar in the coordinates and the fixed vector of trial phases.

For fixed $m$ the finitely many squared sampling tests are bounded functions of these coordinates and phases. Their discontinuities have zero limiting measure: every selected $\{n\lambda_i\}$ avoids the phase cut, and for that phase only finitely many coordinate values can lie on a cutoff or interval boundary. Weak convergence therefore gives convergence of their integrals. This familiar extension from continuous tests follows, for example, by continuous upper and lower approximations outside open neighborhoods of the null discontinuity sets. It applies to the squared differences from one as well. Choose a sufficiently late finite block choice. Its sampling norms are below $1/j$, all the strict spread and angle bounds persist, and all frequency ratios determined so far satisfy their required bounds. The ratio connecting the previous block to this one can be made large enough to meet that block’s outstanding condition; the ratio at the end of this block is imposed at the next stage. This completes the induction. ◻

</details>

Two consequences of the construction will be used repeatedly: <a id="eq:tail-sizes"></a>$$\label{eq:tail-sizes}
 \sum_{s>j}\frac1{K_s}\le\frac2{K_{j+1}},\qquad
 \sum_j\frac{1+h_j^*}{K_j}<\infty,\qquad
 \frac{Q_j}{K_{j+1}}\longrightarrow0.$$ They follow respectively from $K_{j+1}>2K_j$, the final inequality in <a href="#eq:scales" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scales">[eq:scales]</a>, and $7/10<3/4$. The parameters are now fixed independently of any putative invariant projection. We next turn their sampling identities into operator identities between pairs of points of the circle.

<a id="sec:fields"></a>
### Projection fields and transfer between endpoints

We now turn the parameters of Proposition <a href="#prop:parameters" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:parameters">8</a> into a weighted shift. The purpose of this section is to convert invariance under that shift into an identity between projection fields at two different points of the circle. We first obtain the identity along positive rotation orbits, and then use the sampling estimates to reach every translation by an integer multiple of $1/q_A$, for some $A\ge1$.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Fields determined by scalar multipliers

We first isolate the elementary field representation used here and in the direct weighted-shift construction. It depends only on commutation with scalar multipliers.

<a id="stmt-lem-scalar-multiplier-fields"></a>
<div id="lem:scalar-multiplier-fields" class="lemma">

**Lemma 9**. *Let $(Y,\nu)$ be a probability space, let $K=\ell^2(\mathbb Z)$, and let $A\in B(L^2(Y,\nu;K))$ commute with every scalar multiplication operator $M_g\zeta(y)=g(y)\zeta(y)$, $g\in L^\infty(Y,\nu)$. There is a field $A(y)\in B(K)$, unique almost everywhere, such that $$(A\zeta)(y)=A(y)\zeta(y),\qquad
 \|A(y)\|\le\|A\|\quad\text{almost everywhere}.$$ The field is measurable in the sense that $y\mapsto A(y)v$ is measurable for every $v\in K$. Products and adjoints of such operators are computed fiberwise. In particular, an orthogonal projection is represented by orthogonal projections outside one null set.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-scalar-multiplier-fields"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-scalar-multiplier-fields</summary>

*Proof.* If $B\in B(L^2(Y,\nu))$ commutes with all multipliers, put $b=B1$. For every measurable set $E$, $$B\mathbf1_E=\mathbf1_E b,\qquad
 \int_E|b|^2\,d\nu\le\|B\|^2\nu(E).$$ Thus $|b|\le\|B\|$ almost everywhere. The identity $Bf=bf$ follows first for simple functions and then, by density, for all $f\in L^2(Y)$. Apply this observation to the matrix entries of $A$ between the coordinate copies of $L^2(Y)$, obtaining measurable functions $a_{ij}$.

Let $v$ range over the countable set of finitely supported vectors with rational real and imaginary parts. Commutation with $M_{\mathbf1_E}$ and the operator bound give $$\int_E\sum_{i\in\mathbb Z}
       \left|\sum_j a_{ij}(y)v_j\right|^2d\nu(y)
 \le\|A\|^2\|v\|^2\nu(E).$$ Consequently the integrand is at most $\|A\|^2\|v\|^2$ almost everywhere. Outside the union of these countably many null sets, the matrix defines an operator $A(y)$ on $K$ with norm at most $\|A\|$. Its columns, and then its values on every vector, are measurable by finite-coordinate approximation. The represented field agrees with $A$ on finite-coordinate simple functions, hence on all of $L^2(Y;K)$.

Equality of two such fields follows by applying their represented operators to each constant basis field. Products act fiberwise by the representation formula, and the entries of the adjoint field are $\overline{a_{ji}}$. This proves the assertions about products and adjoints. The identities $p=p^*=p^2$ then give orthogonal projections on a common conull set. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### The tracial representation

We use the standard crossed-product representation for a probability-preserving action; see Anantharaman and Popa (n.d., sec. 1.4 and Proposition 1.4.5). We verify below the field and trace properties needed in the proof.

Write $X=\mathbb R/\mathbb Z$, let $\mu$ be Haar probability measure, and put $\sigma x=x+\theta$. On $\mathcal H=L^2(X,\mu;\ell^2(\mathbb Z))$, let $S e_k=e_{k+1}$ and define $$U(x)=S,\qquad
 \pi(a)(x)e_k=a(\sigma^k x)e_k\quad(a\in L^\infty(X,\mu)).$$ For $V=\pi(x\mapsto e^{2\pi i x})$, these operators satisfy $VU=e^{2\pi i\theta}UV$. We also write $e_0$ for the constant field with this value. The vector $U^mV^n e_0$ is the field $x\mapsto e^{2\pi i nx}e_m$. These vectors span a dense subspace by ordinary Fourier theory, and $\langle U^mV^n e_0,e_0\rangle$ equals $1$ for $(m,n)=(0,0)$ and $0$ otherwise. The commutation relation reduces every product of monomials to a scalar multiple of a monomial, so these moments identify all inner products of polynomial vectors with the prescribed tracial cyclic representation. The resulting unitary identifies the generated algebra $M$ with $R_\theta$ and its faithful normal trace with $\tau$. Measurable functional calculus for $V$ places every $\pi(a)$ in $M$.

<a id="eq:field-covariance"></a>
<a id="eq:field-trace"></a>
Every generator commutes with scalar multiplication by a function of $x$. Lemma <a href="#lem:scalar-multiplier-fields" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:scalar-multiplier-fields">9</a> therefore represents every $A\in M$ by an essentially bounded measurable field. The generators also commute with the unitary $J$ given by $(J\zeta)(x)=S\zeta(\sigma x)$: for $\pi(a)$ the coordinate calculation is $a(\sigma^{k-1}(\sigma x))=a(\sigma^kx)$. These commutations pass to their von Neumann algebra. Testing $AJ=JA$ on the constant basis fields gives $$\label{eq:field-covariance}
 A(\sigma x)=S^{-1}A(x)S\quad\text{for almost every }x.$$ The vector formula for the trace is now $$\label{eq:field-trace}
 \tau(A)=\int_X\langle A(x)e_0,e_0\rangle\,d\mu(x)
 \qquad(A\in M).$$ We choose Borel versions for projection fields and discard null sets and their countably many rotation translates when necessary. Irrational rotation is ergodic: all Fourier coefficients of nonzero index vanish for an invariant $L^2$ function. We use equidistribution in the form established at the beginning of Section <a href="#sec:parameters" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:parameters">2</a>.

The following consequence of covariance will also apply to the product model. It ensures that a nontrivial invariant projection has a nonzero zeroth coordinate at almost every endpoint, as does its complement.

<a id="stmt-lem-positive-diagonal"></a>
<div id="lem:positive-diagonal" class="lemma">

**Lemma 10**. *Let $\sigma$ be an invertible ergodic probability-preserving transformation of $(Y,\nu)$. On $L^2(Y,\nu;\ell^2(\mathbb Z))$, define $(U\zeta)(y)=S\zeta(y)$ and $\pi(a)(y)e_k=a(\sigma^ky)e_k$, where $Se_k=e_{k+1}$. Suppose a von Neumann algebra $M$ containing $U$ and $\pi(L^\infty(Y))$ consists of measurable fields with covariance $A(\sigma y)=S^{-1}A(y)S$ and has faithful normal tracial state $$\tau(A)=\int_Y\langle A(y)e_0,e_0\rangle\,d\nu(y).$$ Let $f\in L^\infty(Y,\nu)$ be positive almost everywhere, put $T=U\pi(f)$, and let $p\in M$ be an orthogonal projection with $(1-p)Tp=0$. If $0<\tau(p)<1$, then $0<\langle p(y)e_0,e_0\rangle<1$ almost everywhere.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-positive-diagonal"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-positive-diagonal</summary>

*Proof.* Put $r(y)=\langle p(y)e_0,e_0\rangle$ and $D_y=\operatorname{diag}_k f(\sigma^ky)$. Invariance and covariance give $D_y\operatorname{ran}p(y)\subseteq\operatorname{ran}p(\sigma y)$. If $r(\sigma y)=0$, every vector in the latter range has zero zeroth coordinate. Since $(D_yv)_0=f(y)v_0$ and $f(y)>0$, every vector in the former range also has zero zeroth coordinate, so $r(y)=0$. If $r(y)=1$, then $e_0$ belongs to the former range, and its image $f(y)e_0$ shows that $r(\sigma y)=1$.

Thus $\sigma^{-1}\{r=0\}\subseteq\{r=0\}$ and $\{r=1\}\subseteq\sigma^{-1}\{r=1\}$ modulo null sets. Probability preservation makes both inclusions equalities modulo null sets. Ergodicity makes each event null or conull. The trace formula and $0<\tau(p)<1$ exclude both conull cases. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### A compact-set transfer lemma

Projection fields need not be continuous. The following observation allows us to pass an identity to a weak limit of endpoint measures, provided that their marginals do not concentrate on small sets. A family of finite measures is *uniformly absolutely continuous* with respect to $\mu$ if, for each $\varepsilon>0$, one can choose $\delta>0$ such that every set of $\mu$-measure below $\delta$ has measure below $\varepsilon$ for every member of the family.

<a id="stmt-lem-compact-transfer"></a>
<div id="lem:compact-transfer" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:compact-transfer-identity"></a>
**Lemma 11**. *Let $X$ be a compact metric space with Borel probability measure $\mu$. Let $p(x)$ be a Borel field of orthogonal projections on $\ell^2(\mathbb Z)$, meaning that every column $x\mapsto p(x)e_k$ is Borel, and let $F_k:X\to(0,\infty)$, $k\in\mathbb Z$, be finite Borel functions. Suppose finite positive Borel measures $\beta_l$ on $X^2$ converge weakly to $\beta$. Assume that both families of marginals of $\beta_l$ are uniformly absolutely continuous with respect to $\mu$, and that both marginals of $\beta$ are absolutely continuous. Suppose also that some $0<B<\infty$ satisfies $$F_k(y)/F_k(x)\le B\quad(k\in\mathbb Z)$$ almost everywhere for each $\beta_l$ and for $\beta$. If $$\label{eq:compact-transfer-identity}
 (1-p(y))\operatorname{diag}_k\bigl(F_k(y)/F_k(x)\bigr)p(x)=0$$ holds $\beta_l$-almost everywhere for every $l$, then it holds $\beta$-almost everywhere.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-compact-transfer"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-compact-transfer</summary>

*Proof.* First replace every diagonal entry by its minimum with $B$, so that the diagonal field is bounded everywhere. Given an arbitrarily small $\varepsilon>0$, simultaneous Lusin approximation supplies a compact $Q\subset X$ on which every $F_k$ and every vector map $x\mapsto p(x)e_k$ is continuous, while $\mu(X\setminus Q)$ is sufficiently small to make the $\beta_l$-mass outside $Q^2$ less than $\varepsilon$ for all $l$. For completeness, the simultaneous assertion follows by applying Lusin’s property to the countably many maps with summable exceptional measures and intersecting the resulting compact sets. The usual property for maps into separable metric spaces follows by finite-valued approximation and measure regularity.

On $Q^2$, the capped diagonal field is strongly continuous: continuity on the basis vectors and its uniform bound imply continuity on every vector. The same argument applies to $p(x)$ and $p(y)$. Products of strongly continuous, uniformly bounded fields are strongly continuous. Therefore the zero set $Z_Q$ of the capped version of <a href="#eq:compact-transfer-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:compact-transfer-identity">[eq:compact-transfer-identity]</a> in $Q^2$ is compact. The assumed identity implies $\beta_l(Z_Q)\ge\beta_l(X^2)-\varepsilon$. The compact-set upper bound under weak convergence and convergence of total masses give $$\beta(Z_Q)\ge\limsup_l\beta_l(Z_Q)
 \ge\beta(X^2)-\varepsilon.$$ The capped global zero set thus has full $\beta$-measure, since $\varepsilon$ was arbitrary. It is measurable by its countably many matrix entries. Finally the cap changes no diagonal entry $\beta$-almost everywhere. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### The measurable weight and its cocycles

We return to the prescribed rotation on $X=\mathbb R/\mathbb Z$. The next formulas supply its positive weight and the diagonal ratios to which Lemma <a href="#lem:compact-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:compact-transfer">11</a> will apply. For the parameters of Proposition <a href="#prop:parameters" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:parameters">8</a>, put <a id="eq:measurable-weight"></a>$$\label{eq:measurable-weight}
 g_*(x)=\sum_j C_j\sum_{i\in J_j}
       \lfloor u_i(x)+\alpha_i\rfloor,\qquad
 f_*(x)=e^{-g_*(x)},\qquad T_*=U\pi(f_*).$$ The sum defining $g_*$ has only finitely many nonzero terms almost everywhere. Here $c_i=C_j$ for $i\in J_j$ in the introduction’s notation. To prove the finiteness assertion, observe that $$\sum_i\mu\{\lfloor u_i+\alpha_i\rfloor\ne0\}
 =\sum_i\alpha_i\le2\sum_j K_j^{-1}<\infty.$$ The elementary first Borel–Cantelli implication follows here simply by bounding the measure of a union of tail events by the sum of their measures. Thus $0<f_*\le1$ almost everywhere.

For each $k\in\mathbb Z$, define <a id="eq:cocycle-definition"></a>$$\label{eq:cocycle-definition}
 F_k(x)=\exp\left(-\sum_j C_j\sum_{i\in J_j}
                     \lfloor u_i(x)+k\alpha_i\rfloor\right).$$ These sums, too, have only finitely many nonzero terms almost everywhere: in the tail where $|k|\alpha_i<1$, the probability of a nonzero floor is $|k|\alpha_i$. After taking a countable intersection, there is an invariant conull set on which all $F_k$ are finite and strictly positive and <a id="eq:cocycle-identities"></a>$$\label{eq:cocycle-identities}
 F_{v+n}(x)=F_v(x)F_n(\sigma^v x),\qquad F_0=1,\qquad F_1=f_*.$$ Indeed $u_i(\sigma^v x)=\{u_i(x)+v\alpha_i\}$, so the addition formula for floors proves the identity term by term. Since $\alpha_i>0$, $F_k(x)$ is nonincreasing in $k$. Give these functions arbitrary positive finite Borel values on the remaining null set.

<a id="eq:ratio-crossing"></a>
For endpoints in the common conull set, write $$R_{x,y}(k)=F_k(y)/F_k(x).$$ If $u_i(x)\le u_i(y)$, the $i$th coordinate contributes at most $1$ to this ratio. For $i\in J_s$, it contributes $e^{-C_s}$ precisely when $$\label{eq:ratio-crossing}
 u_i(x)<1-\{k\alpha_i\}\le u_i(y).$$ When $k\alpha_i$ is integral the displayed event is empty, as it should be. Without an order assumption, an individual coordinate contributes at most $e^{C_s}$, since the two floors differ by at most one.

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### From invariant projections to endpoint identities

<a id="eq:positive-orbit-identity"></a>
Suppose $p\in M$ is a projection satisfying $(1-p)T_*p=0$. Its range is then invariant under every positive power of $T_*$. The formula $$T_*(x)^n=S^n\operatorname{diag}_v F_n(\sigma^v x)$$ and covariance <a href="#eq:field-covariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:field-covariance">[eq:field-covariance]</a> show that, for $y=\sigma^n x$, $$\label{eq:positive-orbit-identity}
 (1-p(y))\operatorname{diag}_v R_{x,y}(v)p(x)=0\qquad(n\ge1).$$ To verify the division involved, the cocycle identity gives $F_n(\sigma^v x)/F_n(x)=F_v(\sigma^n x)/F_v(x)$; the positive divisor $F_n(x)$ is finite. The resulting diagonal is bounded on each such fiber by $1/F_n(x)$, since $F_n\le1$. This bound need not be uniform in $x$.

<a id="eq:diagonal-interior"></a>
If $0<\tau(p)<1$, then $$\label{eq:diagonal-interior}
 0<r(x):=\langle p(x)e_0,e_0\rangle<1
 \quad\text{for almost every }x.$$ This is Lemma <a href="#lem:positive-diagonal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:positive-diagonal">10</a>, applied with $f=f_*$, the faithful trace <a href="#eq:field-trace" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:field-trace">[eq:field-trace]</a>, and covariance <a href="#eq:field-covariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:field-covariance">[eq:field-covariance]</a>.

The next lemma is the payoff from the sampling construction. It extends <a href="#eq:positive-orbit-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:positive-orbit-identity">[eq:positive-orbit-identity]</a> beyond actual orbit pairs, while retaining a bound on every diagonal entry at once.

<a id="stmt-lem-rational-graphs"></a>
<div id="lem:rational-graphs" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:rational-graph-identity"></a>
**Lemma 12**. *Let $p\in M$ be a projection satisfying $(1-p)T_*p=0$. For every integer $A\ge1$ and every $t\in X$ that is an integer multiple of $1/q_A$, the identity $$\label{eq:rational-graph-identity}
 (1-p(x+t))\operatorname{diag}_v R_{x,x+t}(v)p(x)=0$$ holds for almost every $x$. The exceptional sets may be chosen simultaneously for this countable family of translations.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-rational-graphs"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-rational-graphs</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $A,t$. In every sufficiently late stage $j$, put $a=d_{j-1}\ge A$. Since $q_A$ divides $q_a$, the sampling set $\mathcal N_{j,t}$ occurs in Proposition <a href="#prop:parameters" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:parameters">8</a>. Write $w=w_{m_j}$ and $w^{(2)}=w_{m_j}^{(2)}$ for this stage’s kernels in <a href="#eq:sampling-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sampling-kernel">[eq:sampling-kernel]</a>. Average over $n\in\mathcal N_{j,t}$ the graph measures $x\mapsto(x,\sigma^n x)$ with starting density $$\prod_{i\in J_j}w\bigl(u_i(x),\{n\alpha_i\}\bigr).$$ By <a href="#eq:sampling" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sampling">[eq:sampling]</a>, both marginal densities tend to $1$ in $L^2(\mu)$. For the second marginal this uses the second orientation $w^{(2)}(z,v)=w(z-v,v)$. As every translation $n\theta$ lies within $\ell_j$ of $t$ and $\ell_j\to0$, testing continuous functions on $X^2$ shows that these measures converge weakly to the Haar-first graph measure of $x\mapsto x+t$. At every point of positive density, $$0<\{n\alpha_i\}<m_j^{-3/5}\Delta(u_i(x))\quad(i\in J_j),$$ so there is no wrap and all current-block coordinates are ordered.

For every selected $n$, remove the same set of starting points, namely those satisfying at least one of $$\begin{array}{ll}
 u_i(x)\ge1-q_a\ell_j & (A\le i\le a),\\
 u_i(x)\ge1-2Q_j/N_s & (i\in J_s,\ s>j).
 \end{array}$$ The removed Haar measure is at most $$a q_a\ell_j+2Q_j\sum_{s>j}K_s^{-1}
 \le (j+1)^{-2}+4Q_j/K_{j+1}\longrightarrow0.$$ The $L^2$ bound on the averaged first marginal and Cauchy–Schwarz show that the removed weighted mass tends to zero. Thus the trimmed measures have the same weak limit. Each trimmed marginal is dominated by its untrimmed counterpart, so both retain uniform $L^2$ bounds and hence uniform absolute continuity.

For $A\le i\le a$, the number $q_i t$ is integral. The coordinate advance is therefore $q_i\{n\theta-t\}<q_a\ell_j$, and trimming prevents a wrap. For future coordinates it is $n\alpha_i<2Q_j/N_s$, again without a wrap. Together with the current block, this orders every coordinate $i\ge A$. Hence, on all trimmed measures, $$R_{x,y}(v)\le
 \exp\left(\sum_{i<A}C_{s(i)}\right)\quad(v\in\mathbb Z),
 \qquad i\in J_{s(i)}.$$ The bound is independent of $j$ and $v$. It also holds on the limiting graph, because $u_i(x+t)=u_i(x)$ for $i\ge A$. The positive-orbit identity holds on the approximants, so Lemma <a href="#lem:compact-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:compact-transfer">11</a> gives <a href="#eq:rational-graph-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rational-graph-identity">[eq:rational-graph-identity]</a>. There are only countably many pairs $(A,t)$ under consideration. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:couplings"></a>
### Ordered pairs from independent digits

The rational-translation identities of Lemma <a href="#lem:rational-graphs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:rational-graphs">12</a> will now be transferred to pairs with many opportunities to cross the thresholds in <a href="#eq:ratio-crossing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ratio-crossing">[eq:ratio-crossing]</a>. If both endpoint laws were Haar, coordinate order would make each nonnegative difference $u_i(y)-u_i(x)$ have mean zero, leaving no threshold crossings. We therefore allow a controlled change in the endpoint marginals. What matters is a uniform square-integrable bound for their marginal densities and a product structure that survives conditioning on either endpoint. We first isolate the density estimate, which also applies to the independent-coordinate construction in the appendix.

<a id="stmt-lem-product-density-bound"></a>
<div id="lem:product-density-bound" class="lemma">

**Lemma 13** (Densities of independent coordinates). *Let $(\Omega_i,\mathcal F_i,\mu_i)$, $i\ge1$, be probability spaces and let $h_i\ge0$ satisfy $\int h_i\,d\mu_i=1$. If $$\sum_{i\ge1}\|h_i-1\|_{L^2(\mu_i)}^2\le C<\infty,$$ then the product probability measure $\nu=\bigotimes_{i\ge1}(h_i\mu_i)$ has a density with respect to $\mu=\bigotimes_{i\ge1}\mu_i$ whose $L^2(\mu)$ norm is at most $e^{C/2}$.*

*In particular, suppose two probability densities $h_i^0,h_i^1$ at each coordinate satisfy $$\|h_i^a-1\|_{L^2(\mu_i)}^2\le b_i\quad(a=0,1),
 \qquad \sum_{i\ge1}b_i\le C.$$ For every deterministic sequence $\epsilon_i\in\{0,1\}$, the product law with coordinate densities $h_i^{\epsilon_i}$ has the same bound.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-product-density-bound"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-product-density-bound</summary>

*Proof.* The mean-one assumption gives $\|h_i\|_2^2=1+\|h_i-1\|_2^2$. Hence each finite product density obeys $$\left\|\prod_{i=1}^n h_i\right\|_{L^2(\mu)}^2
 =\prod_{i=1}^n\bigl(1+\|h_i-1\|_2^2\bigr)
 \le \exp\left(\sum_{i=1}^n\|h_i-1\|_2^2\right)\le e^C.$$ For a bounded cylinder function $\varphi$ depending on the first $n$ coordinates, integration under $\nu$ is therefore bounded in absolute value by $e^{C/2}\|\varphi\|_{L^2(\mu)}$. These cylinder functions are dense in $L^2(\mu)$, so the integration functional extends continuously to that space. The Riesz representation theorem gives a real $H\in L^2(\mu)$ with norm at most $e^{C/2}$ representing the extension. The finite signed measure $H\mu$ and the probability measure $\nu$ agree on all cylinder sets. Uniqueness of finite measures on the product sigma-algebra gives $H\mu=\nu$; thus $H\ge0$ almost everywhere and $\int H\,d\mu=1$. Applying the first assertion to $h_i=h_i^{\epsilon_i}$ proves the hybrid assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:digit-expansion"></a>
Write $r_i^*=q_{i+1}/q_i$. A Haar-uniform point of $X$ has the expansion $$\label{eq:digit-expansion}
 x=\frac{A_0}{q_1}+\sum_{i\ge1}\frac{A_i}{q_{i+1}},
 \qquad
 A_0\in\{0,\ldots,q_1-1\},\quad
 A_i\in\{0,\ldots,r_i^*-1\},$$ with independent uniform digits. Indeed, specifying a finite prefix selects an interval whose length is the reciprocal of the product of the relevant radices. Conversely these intervals determine the Haar measure. The ambiguity at endpoints of such intervals is a null set. In particular, $A_i=\lfloor r_i^*u_i(x)\rfloor$, and $u_i(x)$ is the tail of <a href="#eq:digit-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:digit-expansion">[eq:digit-expansion]</a> starting with digit $A_i$, after multiplication by $q_i$. We couple the independent digits; each character coordinate $u_i$ depends on an overlapping tail of these digits.

Fix a block index $j$. We construct a probability law $\mathbb P_j$ on a pair $(x,y)$, retaining auxiliary labels. Use the same uniform initial digit for both endpoints. Most coordinate pairs will have equal raw values. Rare top and bottom components force crossings even after one endpoint has been fixed, and an extra component in block $j$ places the endpoints close to opposite ends of the unit interval. These components give tail attenuation while keeping the marginal densities uniformly square integrable. Independently at every $i\in J_s$, choose a raw pair $(X_i,Y_i)$ by the following mixture, where $$\rho_s(w)=\kappa_s\sqrt{\frac{w}{m_s}}.$$ For each of the two labelled widths $w=a_s,b_s$, include these two components, each of probability $\rho_s(w)$: $$\begin{aligned}
 \text{top:}\quad &X_i\text{ uniform on }[0,1-w/2),
 &Y_i\text{ uniform on }[1-w/2,1),\\
 \text{bottom:}\quad &X_i\text{ uniform on }[0,w/2),
 &Y_i\text{ uniform on }[w/2,1).
\end{aligned}$$ The two choices within each displayed component are independent. Keep the width labels distinct even if their numerical values coincide. If $s=j$, also include a *tagged* component of probability $1/m_s$, with independent uniforms $$X_i\in[0,1/(2m_s)),\qquad
 Y_i\in[1-1/(2m_s),1).$$ With the remaining probability set $X_i=Y_i$ to a uniform point of $[0,1)$. These probabilities have a positive remainder: the parameter bounds give $\rho_s(w)/w\le\kappa_s$, $w\le1/4$, and $m_s\ge200$. Define the endpoint digits by $$A_i=\lfloor r_i^*X_i\rfloor,
 \qquad B_i=\lfloor r_i^*Y_i\rfloor,$$ and use <a href="#eq:digit-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:digit-expansion">[eq:digit-expansion]</a> to form $x$ from the $A_i$ and $y$ from the $B_i$. Thus $X_i,Y_i$ choose the first digit of the corresponding circle coordinates; they are not themselves those coordinates.

<a id="stmt-lem-digit-marginals"></a>
<div id="lem:digit-marginals" class="lemma">

**Lemma 14** (Uniform marginal bounds). *There is a finite constant $H_0$, independent of $j$, such that the marginal laws of $x$ and $y$ have densities with respect to $\mu$ of $L^2(\mu)$ norm at most $H_0$. The same assertion holds for an endpoint formed by any deterministic choice of the $x$-digit or the $y$-digit at each coordinate, retaining the common uniform initial digit.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-digit-marginals"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-digit-marginals</summary>

*Proof.* Either raw marginal density is at most $10$. For each width its top and bottom contributions together are at most $4\rho_s(w)/w$, the tag contributes at most $2$, and the diagonal component contributes at most $1$.

<a id="eq:one-digit-square"></a>
We also need a sharper integral estimate. A uniform density on an interval of length at least $w/2$ differs from $1$ by a mean-zero function of squared $L^2$ norm at most $2/w$. For a tagged interval the corresponding bound is $2m_s$. Express a raw marginal density minus $1$ as the sum of its at most five weighted mean-zero perturbations. The finite-sum Cauchy–Schwarz inequality gives $$\label{eq:one-digit-square}
 \int_0^1 d_i(t)^2\,dt
 \le 1+50\left(\frac{\kappa_s^2}{m_s}
          +\frac{\mathbf 1_{\{s=j\}}}{m_s}\right),
 \qquad i\in J_s.$$ In fact the four width terms contribute at most $40\kappa_s^2/m_s$, and the tag at most $10/m_s$. Passing from raw variables to digits replaces $d_i$ by its average on each radix bin, so Jensen’s inequality preserves <a href="#eq:one-digit-square" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:one-digit-square">[eq:one-digit-square]</a>.

Use the uniform law at each digit as the reference probability measure. The pairs of digits are independent across coordinates, and each endpoint digit density has mean one. Since $|J_s|=m_s$, summing <a href="#eq:one-digit-square" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:one-digit-square">[eq:one-digit-square]</a> minus one over the coordinates gives the common bound $50(\sum_s\kappa_s^2+1)$, for either endpoint and every deterministic choice between them. The initial digit has density one. Lemma <a href="#lem:product-density-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:product-density-bound">13</a> therefore gives the bound $$H_0^2:=\exp\left(50\left(\sum_{s\ge1}\kappa_s^2+1\right)\right)<\infty.$$ Outside the null sets of ambiguous expansions, the mixed-radix map is a measure-preserving bijection from the reference digit space to $X$. Transporting the density through this map gives a Haar density with the same $L^2$ norm. This proves the assertion for both endpoints and all the deterministic hybrids. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:coordinate-order"></a>
All the endpoint expansions consequently avoid their ambiguous values almost surely. Every raw pair satisfies $X_i\le Y_i$, so $A_i\le B_i$. The nonnegative tail expansion then gives $$\label{eq:coordinate-order}
 u_i(x)\le u_i(y),\qquad
 |u_i(x)-X_i|\le\frac1{r_i^*},\qquad
 |u_i(y)-Y_i|\le\frac1{r_i^*}\quad(i\ge1).$$ For $D\ge1$, let $y^{(D)}$ have $y$’s digits at indices $1,\ldots,D$ and $x$’s later digits. Then $u_i(x)\le u_i(y^{(D)})$ for all $i$, $y^{(D)}\to y$, and $y^{(D)}-x$ is a multiple of $1/q_{D+1}$.

<a id="stmt-prop-ordered-couplings"></a>
<div id="prop:ordered-couplings" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:coupling-identity"></a>
**Proposition 15** (Invariance on the ordered pairs). *Let $p\in M$ be a projection satisfying $(1-p)T_*p=0$. For every $j$, $$\label{eq:coupling-identity}
 (1-p(y))\operatorname{diag}_{k\in\mathbb Z}\bigl(R_{x,y}(k)\bigr)p(x)=0
 \quad\text{for }\mathbb P_j\text{-almost every pair}.$$ Moreover $0<R_{x,y}(k)\le1$ for all $k\in\mathbb Z$ almost surely.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-ordered-couplings"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-ordered-couplings</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $D$. The rational shifts $y^{(D)}-x$ have only finitely many possible values. Lemma <a href="#lem:rational-graphs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:rational-graphs">12</a> holds outside a common Haar-null set for all these shifts, so the absolute continuity of the $x$ marginal implies the required identity at $(x,y^{(D)})$. This remains valid even though the selected shift depends on the pair.

The coordinate order and <a href="#eq:ratio-crossing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ratio-crossing">[eq:ratio-crossing]</a> bound every ratio by $1$, both for these pairs and for their limit $(x,y)$. Their measures converge weakly by the almost-sure convergence $y^{(D)}\to y$. Lemma <a href="#lem:digit-marginals" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:digit-marginals">14</a> supplies uniform $L^2$ bounds for both marginals. In particular their masses on a set of Haar measure $\delta$ are at most $H_0\sqrt\delta$.
