Lemma <a href="#lem:compact-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:compact-transfer">11</a> now passes the identity to the limit. ◻

</details>

We have transferred the invariance identity to ordered pairs. The remaining reason for choosing these particular laws is that an interior threshold has a quantitatively positive chance of being crossed, even after one entire endpoint has been fixed.

<a id="stmt-lem-conditional-labels"></a>
<div id="lem:conditional-labels" class="lemma">

**Lemma 16** (Conditional labels and forced crossings). *Conditional on $x$, all coordinate labels are independent and each posterior depends only on the corresponding digit $A_i$. The analogous statement holds conditional on $y$, using $B_i$.*

*For $i\in J_s$, $w\in\{a_s,b_s\}$, and $u\in[w,1-w]$, the following bounds hold almost surely: <a id="eq:conditional-crossing"></a><a id="eq:conditional-crossing-bottom"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb P_j(\text{top label of width }w\text{ at }i\mid x)
   &\ge\rho_s(w)/10 &&\text{if }u_i(x)<u,
   \label{eq:conditional-crossing}\\
 \mathbb P_j(\text{bottom label of width }w\text{ at }i\mid y)
   &\ge\rho_s(w)/10 &&\text{if }u_i(y)\ge u.
   \label{eq:conditional-crossing-bottom}
\end{aligned}$$ In the first case that label forces $u_i(y)\ge u$; in the second it forces $u_i(x)<u$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-conditional-labels"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-conditional-labels</summary>

*Proof.* The entire endpoint determines all its digits almost surely. For any finite collection of labels, conditioning on all $x$-digits gives the product of the individual posteriors $$\frac{\mathbb P_j(\text{label }\ell\text{ at }i)\,
       \mathbb P_j(A_i=a\mid\text{label }\ell\text{ at }i)}
      {\mathbb P_j(A_i=a)}.$$ One verifies this formula first against cylinder tests in the digit sequence, using independence of coordinate pairs, and then against all bounded digit-measurable tests by the monotone class theorem. The common initial digit is independent of those pairs. This proves conditional independence given $x$; the proof for $y$ is the same. In particular, the dependence of $u_i(x)$ on later digits does not change the label posteriors after the entire endpoint has been conditioned on.

The parameters satisfy $$\frac1{r_i^*}\le\frac1{N_s}<\frac w2.$$ If $u_i(x)<u\le1-w$, the whole radix bin containing $X_i$ lies in $[0,1-w/2)$. On that bin the top component’s $X_i$-density is at least $1$, while the total raw marginal density is at most $10$. Its posterior probability is therefore at least $\rho_s(w)/10$. Under this label, <a href="#eq:coordinate-order" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:coordinate-order">[eq:coordinate-order]</a> gives $$u_i(y)\ge1-w/2-1/r_i^*>1-w\ge u.$$ For the second assertion, $u_i(y)\ge u\ge w$ places the entire $Y_i$ bin in $[w/2,1)$. The bottom component again has density at least $1$ there, so its posterior is at least $\rho_s(w)/10$. Its $X_i$ value satisfies $$u_i(x)\le w/2+1/r_i^*<w\le u.$$ These estimates include thresholds at radix-bin endpoints. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:obstruction"></a>
### The convolution obstruction

We can now exclude every nontrivial invariant projection for the measurable weight. The argument compares two estimates for a finite part of a convolution: polynomial compactness prevents that part from vanishing, whereas the ordered couplings make its complementary tail too small to cancel it.

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### A conditional convolution estimate

We isolate the common argument so that the product construction can use it without repeating the infinite-tail estimate. The probability space in the lemma may include auxiliary labels besides its endpoints.

<a id="stmt-lem-conditional-convolution"></a>
<div id="lem:conditional-convolution" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:generic-convolution"></a>
<a id="eq:generic-tail"></a>
<a id="eq:generic-obstruction"></a>
**Lemma 17** (Conditional convolution obstruction). *Let $K\ge1$ be an integer, $D\ge a>0$, $c>0$, and $\eta,\delta\ge0$. On a probability space with endpoints $x,y$, let $\xi(x),\gamma(y)$ be measurable vectors in the unit ball of $\ell^2(\mathbb Z)$, and let $F_k$ be positive finite measurable functions of an endpoint, with $F_0=1$. Set $$A_v=\frac{\xi_v(x)}{F_v(x)},\qquad
 B_w=F_{-w}(y)\overline{\gamma_{-w}(y)},\qquad
 R(k)=\frac{F_k(y)}{F_k(x)}.$$ Suppose, almost surely, for $0\le n\le2K$ and every $v\in\mathbb Z$, $$\label{eq:generic-convolution}
 \sum_{v\in\mathbb Z}A_vB_{-n-v}=0,\qquad
 0<\frac{F_{n+v}(y)}{F_v(x)}
       \le\min\{R(v),R(n+v)\}\le1.$$ Let $E$ be an event of probability at least $c$ on which $$|A_v|,|B_v|\le D\quad(|v|\le K),\qquad
 |A_0|,|B_0|\ge a.$$ For each $|k|>K$, suppose there are events $H_k^{(1)},H_k^{(2)}$ with $$\label{eq:generic-tail}
 R(k)\le\eta+\mathbf1_{H_k^{(1)}}+\mathbf1_{H_k^{(2)}}\quad\text{on }E,
 \qquad
 \mathbb P(H_k^{(1)}\mid x)\le\delta,\quad
 \mathbb P(H_k^{(2)}\mid y)\le\delta.$$ The conditional bounds are under the full probability law, before restriction to $E$. If $\varepsilon(K,D,a)$ is the positive constant from Lemma <a href="#lem:polynomial-coefficient" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:polynomial-coefficient">7</a>, then $$\label{eq:generic-obstruction}
 c\varepsilon(K,D,a)\le(2K+1)(\eta+2\sqrt\delta).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-conditional-convolution"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-conditional-convolution</summary>

*Proof.* The multiplier bound in <a href="#eq:generic-convolution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:generic-convolution">[eq:generic-convolution]</a> and Cauchy–Schwarz make every convolution there absolutely convergent. Put $$c_n=\sum_{\substack{|v|\le K\\|n+v|\le K}}A_vB_{-n-v}
       \quad(0\le n\le2K).$$ On $E$, Lemma <a href="#lem:polynomial-coefficient" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:polynomial-coefficient">7</a> applied to the two truncated Laurent polynomials gives $\sum_{n=0}^{2K}|c_n|\ge\varepsilon(K,D,a)$. Its expected value on $E$ is therefore at least $c\varepsilon(K,D,a)$.

<a id="eq:masked-tail"></a>
Fix $n$. For an omitted index $v$, choose $k(v)=v$ if $|v|>K$, and $k(v)=n+v$ otherwise. This is a deterministic choice with $|k(v)|>K$. The zero convolution and the two multiplier bounds give $$\label{eq:masked-tail}
 \mathbf1_E|c_n|\le\eta+
 \sum_{b=1}^2\sum_{v\text{ omitted}}
 |\xi_v(x)\gamma_{n+v}(y)|\mathbf1_{H_{k(v)}^{(b)}}.$$ The unmasked sum of products of vector coordinates is at most one, which accounts for $\eta$. We dropped $\mathbf1_E$ from the nonnegative exceptional terms so that their full-law conditional bounds remain applicable.

For the first exceptional sum, Cauchy–Schwarz on probability times counting measure bounds its expectation by $$\left(\mathbb E\sum_{v\text{ omitted}}
       |\xi_v(x)|^2\mathbf1_{H_{k(v)}^{(1)}}\right)^{1/2}
 \left(\mathbb E\sum_{v\text{ omitted}}
       |\gamma_{n+v}(y)|^2\right)^{1/2}
 \le\sqrt\delta.$$ Indeed, condition the first factor’s square on $x$, apply <a href="#eq:generic-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:generic-tail">[eq:generic-tail]</a> to each deterministic $k(v)$, and use $\sum_v|\xi_v(x)|^2\le1$. The second factor is at most one. For the other exceptional sum put its indicator with $|\gamma_{n+v}(y)|^2$ and condition on $y$, giving the same bound. Tonelli’s theorem justifies every interchange of the nonnegative sums and integrals. No sum of the individual exceptional probabilities is required. Taking expectations in <a href="#eq:masked-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:masked-tail">[eq:masked-tail]</a> and summing in $n$ proves <a href="#eq:generic-obstruction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:generic-obstruction">[eq:generic-obstruction]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### Application to the circle weight

<a id="stmt-thm-measurable"></a>
<div id="thm:measurable" class="theorem">

**Theorem 18**. *The bounded operator $T_*=U\pi(f_*)$ has no projection $p\in M$ satisfying $0<\tau(p)<1$ and $(1-p)T_*p=0$.*

</div>

Suppose, for contradiction, that such a projection exists. Put $$\xi(x)=p(x)e_0,\qquad \gamma(y)=(1-p(y))e_0,
 \qquad r(x)=p(x)_{0,0}.$$ Both vector norms are at most $1$. We shall use the laws $\mathbb P_j$ of Section <a href="#sec:couplings" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:couplings">4</a>; their expectations are denoted by $\mathbb E_j$.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### The exact convolution identities

<a id="eq:full-convolution"></a>
<a id="eq:ratio-minimum"></a>
By <a href="#eq:coupling-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:coupling-identity">[eq:coupling-identity]</a>, the vector $\operatorname{diag}_v(R_{x,y}(v))\xi(x)$ belongs to $\operatorname{ran}p(y)$. Applying $T_*(y)^n$, which preserves that range for $n\ge0$, and pairing with $\gamma(y)$ gives $$\label{eq:full-convolution}
 0=\sum_{v\in\mathbb Z}\frac{F_{n+v}(y)}{F_v(x)}
       \xi_v(x)\overline{\gamma_{n+v}(y)}
   =\sum_{v\in\mathbb Z} A_vB_{-n-v},\qquad n\ge0,$$ where, in this section, $$A_v=\frac{\xi_v(x)}{F_v(x)},\qquad
 B_v=F_{-v}(y)\overline{\gamma_{-v}(y)}.$$ All these identities hold simultaneously almost surely under each $\mathbb P_j$. They are absolutely convergent. Indeed, monotonicity of $F_k$ in $k$ and coordinate order imply $$\label{eq:ratio-minimum}
 \frac{F_{n+v}(y)}{F_v(x)}
 \le\min\{R_{x,y}(v),R_{x,y}(n+v)\}\le1,$$ and $\sum_v|\xi_v(x)\gamma_{n+v}(y)|\le1$ by Cauchy–Schwarz. The comparison with $R_{x,y}(v)$ follows from $F_{n+v}(y)\le F_v(y)$; the comparison with $R_{x,y}(n+v)$ follows from $F_{n+v}(x)\le F_v(x)$.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### A finite convolution cannot be too small

Fix $j$ and abbreviate $K=K_j$, $m=m_j$. Define an event $E_j$ by the following three conditions: $$\begin{aligned}
 &X_i<1-3/m,\quad Y_i>3/m\quad(i\in J_j),
       \quad\text{and at least one tag in }J_j;\\
 &X_i<1-3K/N_s,\quad Y_i>3K/N_s
       \quad(i\in J_s,\ s>j);\\
 &r(x)\ge1/j,\qquad 1-r(y)\ge1/j.
\end{aligned}$$ For $0\le n\le2K$, let <a id="eq:truncated-convolution"></a>$$\label{eq:truncated-convolution}
 c_{j,n}=\sum_{\substack{|v|\le K\\ |n+v|\le K}}A_vB_{-n-v}.$$

<a id="stmt-lem-truncation-bound"></a>
<div id="lem:truncation-bound" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:finite-lower-bound"></a>
**Lemma 19**. *There is a constant $c_*>0$ such that, for all sufficiently large $j$, $$\label{eq:finite-lower-bound}
 \mathbb P_j(E_j)\ge c_*,\qquad
 \mathbb E_j\left[\mathbf 1_{E_j}
          \sum_{n=0}^{2K_j}|c_{j,n}|\right]\ge c_*\varepsilon_j.$$ The point after which these inequalities hold may depend on $p$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-truncation-bound"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-truncation-bound</summary>

*Proof.* At a current-block coordinate, a tag has probability $1/m$ and satisfies both endpoint exclusions. Since each raw marginal density is at most $10$, the probability of an inadmissible outcome is at most $60/m$. Thus a nontagged admissible outcome has probability at least $1-61/m$. Independence across the block shows that exactly one tag and admissible nontagged outcomes at the other coordinates have probability at least $$m\frac1m(1-61/m)^{m-1}\ge e^{-90}\qquad(m\ge200).$$ For example, the last estimate follows from $\log(1-t)\ge-t/(1-t)$ with $t=61/m$.

The total probability excluded by the future-block inequalities is at most $$60K_j\sum_{s>j}\frac1{K_s}
 \le\frac{120K_j}{K_{j+1}}\longrightarrow0.$$ By <a href="#eq:diagonal-interior" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:diagonal-interior">[eq:diagonal-interior]</a>, $0<r<1$ Haar-almost everywhere. Lemma <a href="#lem:digit-marginals" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:digit-marginals">14</a> therefore bounds the probability lost from the last line of the definition of $E_j$ by $$H_0\mu\{r<1/j\}^{1/2}
 +H_0\mu\{1-r<1/j\}^{1/2}\longrightarrow0.$$ Consequently $c_*=e^{-90}/2$ works for all sufficiently large $j$.

On $E_j$, rounding changes the raw endpoint inequalities by at most $1/N_s$. Since $K\ge1$, they imply $$u_i(x)<1-2K/N_s,\qquad u_i(y)>2K/N_s
       \quad(i\in J_s,\ s\ge j).$$ For $0\le v\le K$, the bound $\alpha_i<2/N_s$ makes the floors of $u_i(x)+v\alpha_i$ and $u_i(y)-v\alpha_i$ zero in every such block. Earlier-block floors have absolute value at most $K+1$. Hence $$1/F_v(x)\le D_j,
 \qquad F_{-v}(y)\le D_j\quad(0\le v\le K).$$ For $v<0$ these two quantities are at most $1$, by monotonicity and $F_0=1$. Since the vector coordinates have modulus at most $1$, all coefficients of the Laurent polynomials $$A^{[K]}(z)=\sum_{|v|\le K}A_vz^v,
 \qquad B^{[K]}(z)=\sum_{|v|\le K}B_vz^v$$ have modulus at most $D_j$. Their constant coefficients are respectively $r(x)$ and $1-r(y)$, both at least $1/j$ on $E_j$. The coefficient of $z^{-n}$ in their product is $c_{j,n}$. The polynomial bound <a href="#eq:polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:polynomial">[eq:polynomial]</a> now gives $\sum_{n=0}^{2K}|c_{j,n}|\ge\varepsilon_j$ on $E_j$. Integration proves the second assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### Controlling every part of the infinite tail

To contradict <a href="#eq:finite-lower-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-lower-bound">[eq:finite-lower-bound]</a>, we must bound the omitted terms of <a href="#eq:full-convolution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:full-convolution">[eq:full-convolution]</a>. The important estimate is conditional on one endpoint. It will let us sum infinitely many terms without taking a union bound over their exceptional events.

<a id="stmt-lem-tail-attenuation"></a>
<div id="lem:tail-attenuation" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:conditional-bad-events"></a>
<a id="eq:tail-ratio-bound"></a>
**Lemma 20**. *For every $j$ and every integer $k$ with $|k|>K_j$, there are measurable events $H_k^{(1)},H_k^{(2)}$ in the labelled coupling space such that $$\label{eq:conditional-bad-events}
 \mathbb P_j(H_k^{(1)}\mid x)\le e^{-L_j/400},\qquad
 \mathbb P_j(H_k^{(2)}\mid y)\le e^{-L_j/400}
 \quad\text{almost surely},$$ and on $E_j$, $$\label{eq:tail-ratio-bound}
 R_{x,y}(k)\le e^{-L_j}
       +\mathbf 1_{H_k^{(1)}}+\mathbf 1_{H_k^{(2)}}.$$ The conditional bounds hold before any restriction to $E_j$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-tail-attenuation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-tail-attenuation</summary>

*Proof.* First suppose $K_j<|k|\le N_j/4$. A tagged coordinate in $J_j$ satisfies, after rounding, $$u_i(x)<1/m_j,\qquad u_i(y)>1-1/m_j.$$ Here $\beta=|k|\alpha_i$ lies in $(1/m_j,1/2)$. The threshold $1-\{k\alpha_i\}$ equals $1-\beta$ for $k>0$ and $\beta$ for $k<0$. It is crossed in either case, so <a href="#eq:ratio-crossing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ratio-crossing">[eq:ratio-crossing]</a> gives $R_{x,y}(k)\le e^{-C_j}\le e^{-L_j}$ on $E_j$. Set both exceptional events to be empty in this range.

We next cover all larger $|k|$. Put $M_s=m_s^{(4/5)m_s}$. The intervals $$[N_j/4,M_j],\qquad [G_sK_s,M_s]\quad(s>j)$$ cover $[N_j/4,\infty)$. Indeed the next left endpoint is below the preceding right endpoint by <a href="#eq:scales" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:scales">[eq:scales]</a>, the right endpoints tend to infinity, and $G_sK_s\le N_s/4$ because $m_s\ge4G_s$. Split each later interval at $N_s/4$. In its lower part the thresholds belong to $[a_s,1-a_s]$ at every coordinate of $J_s$. In its upper part, <a href="#eq:spread" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:spread">[eq:spread]</a> places them in $[b_s,1-b_s]$ at at least $m_s/20$ coordinates. The initial interval uses the upper-part assertion for $s=j$.

<a id="eq:crossing-intensity"></a>
Thus for each fixed $k$ in this tail we can choose, deterministically, a block $J_s$, a width $w\in\{a_s,b_s\}$, and a set $I\subset J_s$ of at least $m_s/20$ indices such that $$u_i^*:=1-\{k\alpha_i\}\in[w,1-w]\quad(i\in I).$$ The lower-part choice always has $s>j$. In either type of choice, $$\label{eq:crossing-intensity}
 m_s\rho_s(w)\ge L_j,
 \qquad C_s\ge L_j.$$ For $w=b_s$ the first quantity is $\kappa_sm_s^{1/40}\ge L_s\ge L_j$; for $w=a_s$ it is $\kappa_s\sqrt{G_s}\ge L_{s-1}\ge L_j$.

Let $H_k$ be the event of no crossing at any index in $I$, and define $$H_k^{(1)}=H_k\cap
    \left\{\#\{i\in I:u_i(x)<u_i^*\}\ge|I|/2\right\},
 \qquad H_k^{(2)}=H_k\setminus H_k^{(1)}.$$ Conditional on $x$, the first event forbids the top label at every index in the indicated $x$-measurable set. Conditional independence and Lemma <a href="#lem:conditional-labels" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:conditional-labels">16</a> therefore give $$\mathbb P_j(H_k^{(1)}\mid x)
 \le(1-\rho_s(w)/10)^{|I|/2}
 \le e^{-m_s\rho_s(w)/400}\le e^{-L_j/400}.$$ If the $x$-measurable set has fewer than $|I|/2$ elements, the event is empty, so the same bound holds.

For the other conditional estimate, note explicitly that coordinate order and the absence of crossings imply $$u_i(x)<u_i^*\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad u_i(y)<u_i^*
 \qquad(i\in I).$$ Thus $H_k^{(2)}$ requires at least $|I|/2$ indices with $u_i(y)\ge u_i^*$. Conditional on $y$, the bottom labels at all those indices are forbidden. The same product estimate proves the second bound in <a href="#eq:conditional-bad-events" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conditional-bad-events">[eq:conditional-bad-events]</a>. These arguments use the original law, without conditioning on $E_j$. Figure <a href="#fig:conditioning" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:conditioning">1</a> displays the complementary split.

<figure id="fig:conditioning">

<figcaption>The two parts of the no-crossing event are small after conditioning on complementary endpoints. Each estimate controls the matching masked square sum in Lemma <a href="#lem:conditional-convolution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:conditional-convolution">17</a>; no simultaneous avoidance over all indices is required.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-conditioning"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:conditioning`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  box/.style={draw,rounded corners,align=center,text width=5.3cm,
              minimum height=1.1cm,inner sep=6pt},
  arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=2mm]},thick}]
\node[box,text width=8.2cm] (b) at (0,0)
 {$H_k$: no listed threshold is crossed\\
  On this event, $u_i(x)<u_i^*\iff u_i(y)<u_i^*$.};
\node[box] (x) at (-3.1,-2)
 {$H_k^{(1)}$: at least $|I|/2$ of the $u_i(x)$ lie below $u_i^*$};
\node[box] (y) at (3.1,-2)
 {$H_k^{(2)}$: more than $|I|/2$ of the $u_i(y)$ lie above or at $u_i^*$};
\node[box] (cx) at (-3.1,-4)
 {Condition on all of $x$;\\
  top labels can cross.\\
  $\mathbb P_j(H_k^{(1)}\mid x)\le e^{-L_j/400}$};
\node[box] (cy) at (3.1,-4)
 {Condition on all of $y$;\\
  bottom labels can cross.\\
  $\mathbb P_j(H_k^{(2)}\mid y)\le e^{-L_j/400}$};
\draw[arr] (b.south) -- (x.north);
\draw[arr] (b.south) -- (y.north);
\draw[arr] (x) -- (cx);
\draw[arr] (y) -- (cy);
\end{tikzpicture}
```

Visual witness description of PDF page 22 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
Flowchart diagram showing the decomposition of the event $H_k$ into two branches across three levels:
- Top level: A single rectangular box containing:
  "$H_k$: no listed threshold is crossed"
  "On this event, $u_i(x) < u_i^* \iff u_i(y) < u_i^*$."
- Middle level: Two rectangular boxes connected from the top box by branching arrows:
  - Left branch arrow points to box: "$H_k^{(1)}$: at least $|I|/2$ of the $u_i(x)$ lie below $u_i^*$"
  - Right branch arrow points to box: "$H_k^{(2)}$: more than $|I|/2$ of the $u_i(y)$ lie above or at $u_i^*$"
- Bottom level: Two rectangular boxes connected by vertical downward arrows from the middle boxes:
  - Left box below $H_k^{(1)}$: "Condition on all of $x$; top labels can cross." and "$\mathbb{P}_j(H_k^{(1)} \mid x) \le e^{-L_j/400}$"
  - Right box below $H_k^{(2)}$: "Condition on all of $y$; bottom labels can cross." and "$\mathbb{P}_j(H_k^{(2)} \mid y) \le e^{-L_j/400}$"
```


Outside $H_k$, at least one chosen coordinate crosses. All coordinates are ordered, so <a href="#eq:ratio-crossing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ratio-crossing">[eq:ratio-crossing]</a> and <a href="#eq:crossing-intensity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:crossing-intensity">[eq:crossing-intensity]</a> give $R_{x,y}(k)\le e^{-L_j}$. On $H_k$ the ratio is at most $1$. This proves <a href="#eq:tail-ratio-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-ratio-bound">[eq:tail-ratio-bound]</a> and the lemma. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### Completion of the contradiction

Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:conditional-convolution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:conditional-convolution">17</a> to the labelled law $\mathbb P_j$, with $$K=K_j,\quad D=D_j,\quad a=1/j,\quad c=c_* =e^{-90}/2,
 \qquad \eta=e^{-L_j},\quad \delta=e^{-L_j/400}.$$ Equations <a href="#eq:full-convolution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:full-convolution">[eq:full-convolution]</a> and <a href="#eq:ratio-minimum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ratio-minimum">[eq:ratio-minimum]</a> verify its algebraic assumptions. The proof of Lemma <a href="#lem:truncation-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:truncation-bound">19</a> supplies the event of mass at least $c_*$ and all central-coefficient bounds for sufficiently large $j$. Lemma <a href="#lem:tail-attenuation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:tail-attenuation">20</a> gives the tail estimate with conditional probabilities under the full law. Consequently $$c_*\varepsilon_j
 \le(2K_j+1)\bigl(e^{-L_j}+2e^{-L_j/800}\bigr)
 \le\frac{\varepsilon_j}{j},$$ where the last inequality is the parameter choice <a href="#eq:polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:polynomial">[eq:polynomial]</a>. Since $\varepsilon_j>0$ and eventually $1/j<c_*$, this is impossible. This proves Theorem <a href="#thm:measurable" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:measurable">18</a>.

<a id="sec:continuity"></a>
### A continuous weight with one zero

The measurable obstruction of Theorem <a href="#thm:measurable" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:measurable">18</a> is now in place. We will replace its logarithmic cost $g_*$ by a function continuous off one point and tending to infinity at that point. The replacement changes the cost by the constant $1$ plus a measurable coboundary. Since its transfer function need not be bounded, we begin with the precise operator statement that will preserve the obstruction.

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### Transport by an affiliated positive operator

A positive self-adjoint operator is *affiliated* with a von Neumann algebra if all its spectral projections belong to that algebra. Such an operator commutes, including on its domain, with every unitary in the commutant. The action of an invertible affiliated operator on projection lattices also appears in Mori (2020, Proposition 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[mori](#ref-mori)]. The form needed here includes a specified intertwining domain and preservation of the trace; we give a direct spectral-cutoff proof.

<a id="stmt-lem-affiliated-transport"></a>
<div id="lem:affiliated-transport" class="lemma">

**Lemma 21**. *Let $(\mathcal M,\tau)$ be a finite von Neumann algebra acting on a Hilbert space, with faithful normal tracial state $\tau$. Let $H$ be an injective positive self-adjoint operator affiliated with $\mathcal M$, and let $A,B\in\mathcal M$. Suppose $$A(\operatorname{Dom}H)\subseteq\operatorname{Dom}H,
 \qquad HA\zeta=BH\zeta\quad(\zeta\in\operatorname{Dom}H).$$ For every projection $p\in\mathcal M$ with $A\operatorname{ran}p\subseteq
\operatorname{ran}p$, the projection $P$ onto $$\overline{H(\operatorname{ran}p\cap\operatorname{Dom}H)}$$ belongs to $\mathcal M$, is invariant under $B$, and satisfies $\tau(P)=\tau(p)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-affiliated-transport"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-affiliated-transport</summary>

*Proof.* Every commutant unitary preserves $\operatorname{ran}p$ and $\operatorname{Dom}H$, and commutes with $H$ on that domain. It therefore maps the displayed closed space onto itself. Its projection commutes with the commutant, proving $P\in\mathcal M$. If $\zeta\in\operatorname{ran}p\cap\operatorname{Dom}H$, both invariance assumptions give $A\zeta\in\operatorname{ran}p\cap\operatorname{Dom}H$, and $BH\zeta=HA\zeta$. Thus $B$ preserves the unclosed image and, by boundedness, its closure.

It remains to prove the trace assertion; bounded similarity cannot be assumed. Put $E_N=\mathbf1_{[1/N,N]}(H)$. Since $H$ is injective, $E_N\uparrow1$. For $q_N=p\wedge E_N$, the bounded element $Hq_N=(HE_N)q_N$ of $\mathcal M$ is bounded below by $1/N$ on its initial space. Its image is closed, and its polar decomposition identifies the initial projection $q_N$ with its final projection, which is dominated by $P$. Therefore $$\tau(P)\ge\tau(p\wedge E_N)
 \ge\tau(p)+\tau(E_N)-1.$$ The last inequality follows directly from the polar decomposition of $(1-E_N)p$: its initial support is $p-(p\wedge E_N)$ and its final support is at most $1-E_N$. Letting $N$ increase gives $\tau(P)\ge\tau(p)$.

For the reverse inequality, set $q'_N=(1-p)\wedge E_N$. The bounded operator $H^{-1}q'_N=(H^{-1}E_N)q'_N$ is likewise bounded below on its initial space and has closed image. This image is orthogonal to $\operatorname{ran}P$. Indeed, for $\eta\in\operatorname{ran}q'_N$ and $\zeta\in\operatorname{ran}p\cap\operatorname{Dom}H$, self-adjointness gives $$\langle H^{-1}\eta,H\zeta\rangle=\langle\eta,\zeta\rangle=0;$$ here $H^{-1}\eta$ lies in $\operatorname{Dom}H$. Polar decomposition and the same meet estimate yield $$\tau(1-P)\ge\tau((1-p)\wedge E_N)
 \ge\tau(1-p)+\tau(E_N)-1.$$ Passing to the limit proves the opposite inequality for $\tau(P)$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### Smoothing and moving the logarithmic drops

<a id="eq:continuous-coboundary"></a>
We claim that there exist a nonnegative function $g$, finite and continuous on $X\setminus\{0\}$ and tending to infinity at $0$, and an almost everywhere finite real measurable function $b$ such that $$\label{eq:continuous-coboundary}
 g=g_*+1+b\circ\sigma-b\quad\text{almost everywhere},
 \qquad \int_Xg\,d\mu=\infty.$$ The construction has three parts: smooth each drop, move its support into a shrinking arc, and add an integrable background cost that guarantees divergence at the center of those arcs. Apart from the constant $1$, all changes will be explicit coboundaries.

<a id="eq:smoothing-coboundary"></a>
Fix $i\in J_j$ and put $\delta_i=\alpha_i/4$. For $u\in X$, define the continuous periodic smoothing of the sawtooth by $$s_i(u)=\frac1{2\delta_i}\int_{-\delta_i}^{\delta_i}\{u+t\}\,dt.$$ It equals $\{u\}$ outside the $\delta_i$-neighborhood of the cut at $0$. Set $$h_i(x)=C_j\bigl(\alpha_i+s_i(u_i(x))
                         -s_i(u_i(x)+\alpha_i)\bigr).$$ The elementary identity $\lfloor\{u\}+\alpha_i\rfloor=
\alpha_i+\{u\}-\{u+\alpha_i\}$ shows that $h_i$ is the corresponding average of the original one-coordinate cost. It is nonnegative and continuous, with <a id="eq:smoothed-support"></a>$$\label{eq:smoothed-support}
 \int_X h_i\,d\mu=C_j\alpha_i,
 \qquad \mu(\operatorname{supp}h_i)\le\alpha_i+2\delta_i.$$ For $$d_i^{\rm sm}(x)=C_j\bigl(s_i(u_i(x))-u_i(x)\bigr),$$ we have the exact measurable identity $$\label{eq:smoothing-coboundary}
 h_i(x)=C_j\lfloor u_i(x)+\alpha_i\rfloor
                  +d_i^{\rm sm}(x)-d_i^{\rm sm}(\sigma x).$$ The support of $d_i^{\rm sm}$ has measure at most $2\delta_i$.

<a id="eq:relocation-coboundary"></a>
Recall the shrinking arcs $O_j$, the integers $h_j^*$, and the continuous partition of unity $\phi_{j,n}$, $0\le n\le h_j^*$, prepared in the parameter construction, with $\sigma^n(\operatorname{supp}\phi_{j,n})\subset O_j$. Define $$h'_i(x)=\sum_{n=0}^{h_j^*}(h_i\phi_{j,n})(\sigma^{-n}x).$$ This is continuous and nonnegative, supported in $O_j$, and has the same integral as $h_i$. Telescoping gives $$\label{eq:relocation-coboundary}
 h'_i=h_i+d'_i-d'_i\circ\sigma,
 \qquad
 d'_i(x)=\sum_{n=1}^{h_j^*}\sum_{r=1}^n
                      (h_i\phi_{j,n})(\sigma^{-r}x).$$ Although this transfer contains a double sum, its support is contained in just $h_j^*$ translates of $\operatorname{supp}h_i$, one for each possible $r$. Consequently $$\sum_i\left(\mu\{d_i^{\rm sm}\ne0\}+\mu\{d'_i\ne0\}\right)
 \le C\sum_j\frac{1+h_j^*}{K_j}<\infty.$$ Thus each of the transfer sums has only finitely many nonzero terms almost everywhere, including at the translated point $\sigma x$.

<a id="eq:infinite-cost-integral"></a>
The sum $\sum_i h'_i$ is locally finite on $X\setminus\{0\}$ because its successive blocks are supported in $O_j\downarrow\{0\}$. It is therefore continuous there. Its integral is infinite: Tonelli’s theorem and $\alpha_i>1/(m_jK_j)$ give $$\label{eq:infinite-cost-integral}
 \int_X\sum_i h'_i\,d\mu
 =\sum_j C_j\sum_{i\in J_j}\alpha_i
 \ge\sum_j C_j/K_j=\infty,$$ since $C_j\ge K_j^2$. Local finiteness alone need not force divergence at $0$; the following background supplies that remaining property.

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### A background cost diverging at the zero

Let $W_j=2^{j+1}$. Choose continuous functions $0\le v_j\le1$ with $v_j(0)=1$, supported in closed arcs $I_j$ shrinking to $\{0\}$. Choose these arcs small enough that $\sigma I_j,\ldots,\sigma^{W_j}I_j$ are pairwise disjoint and $$\sum_j W_j\mu(I_j)<\infty.$$ This is possible because each finite set of distinct points $\theta,2\theta,\ldots,W_j\theta$ has positive minimum separation. For $x\ne0$, put $$g_0(x)=1+\sum_j\left(v_j(x)-\frac1{W_j}
                              \sum_{n=1}^{W_j}v_j(\sigma^{-n}x)\right).$$ For each $j$, disjointness bounds the norm of the subtracted function by $1/W_j$. These bounds sum to $1/2$, so the negative series converges uniformly. The positive series is locally finite away from $0$. Consequently $g_0$ is continuous there and $g_0\ge1/2$. For every fixed integer $J$, the finite sum $\sum_{j\le J}v_j(x)$ tends to $J$ as $x\to0$. The bounded negative series therefore implies $g_0(x)\to\infty$ as $x\to0$. Its integral is finite, since $\sum_j\int v_j\,d\mu\le\sum_j\mu(I_j)<\infty$ and the negative series is uniformly bounded.

Define $$b_0(x)=\sum_j\frac1{W_j}\sum_{n=1}^{W_j}\sum_{r=1}^n
                                      v_j(\sigma^{-r}x).$$ The $j$th summand is supported in $\bigcup_{r=1}^{W_j}\sigma^r I_j$. The assumed summability makes this sum finite almost everywhere. On a conull set where the sums at $x$ and $\sigma x$ are finite, telescoping shows $$g_0=1+b_0\circ\sigma-b_0.$$ Now let $$g(x)=g_0(x)+\sum_i h'_i(x)\quad(x\ne0),\qquad g(0)=\infty,
 \qquad b=b_0-\sum_i(d_i^{\rm sm}+d'_i).$$ The preceding arguments prove that $g$ is nonnegative, finite and continuous off $0$, tends to infinity at $0$, and has infinite integral. The transfer $b$ is real and finite almost everywhere. Summing <a href="#eq:smoothing-coboundary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:smoothing-coboundary">[eq:smoothing-coboundary]</a> and <a href="#eq:relocation-coboundary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:relocation-coboundary">[eq:relocation-coboundary]</a> is legitimate on the common conull set where the transfer sums and the original cost have finitely many nonzero terms. Together with the background identity this proves <a href="#eq:continuous-coboundary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:continuous-coboundary">[eq:continuous-coboundary]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### Conclusion and spectral consequences

<a id="eq:zero-log-integral"></a>
Define the function on the unit circle by $$f(e^{2\pi i x})=e^{-g(x)},\qquad e^{-\infty}=0.$$ It is continuous, is positive away from $1$, and vanishes at $1$. Its modulus is nonconstant and its zero set has Haar measure zero. Moreover, $$\label{eq:zero-log-integral}
 \int_{\mathbb T}\log f\,dm=-\infty.$$ In particular, its Fuglede–Kadison determinant is zero (Fuglede and Kadison 1952) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[fuglede-kadison](#ref-fuglede-kadison)]: the spectral distribution of $\pi(f)$ is the Haar distribution of $f$, so the determinant definition is precisely the exponential of <a href="#eq:zero-log-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:zero-log-integral">[eq:zero-log-integral]</a>. Also $|U\pi(f)|=\pi(f)$, which gives the same determinant for $T_f=U\pi(f)$. This conclusion uses the nonnegative integral calculation <a href="#eq:infinite-cost-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:infinite-cost-integral">[eq:infinite-cost-integral]</a>; it does not require the transfer $b$ to be integrable.

Choose a finite Borel version of $b$ and discard all integer translates of the null set where the coboundary identity fails. Let $H_b$ be multiplication on the $k$th fiber coordinate by $e^{b(\sigma^k x)}$, with its natural square-integrability domain. It is positive, self-adjoint, and injective. Its spectral projections are $\pi$-images of spectral indicators of $e^b$, hence lie in $M$. Thus $H_b$ is affiliated with $M$. The coboundary identity gives the pointwise factor equality $$e^{b(\sigma^{k+1}x)}e^{-g(\sigma^k x)}
       =e^{-1}f_*(\sigma^k x)e^{b(\sigma^k x)}.$$ For every $\zeta\in\operatorname{Dom}H_b$, the right-hand side defines the $k+1$ coordinate of $e^{-1}T_*H_b\zeta$, a square-integrable vector because $T_*$ is bounded. Therefore $$T_f\zeta\in\operatorname{Dom}H_b,
 \qquad H_bT_f\zeta=e^{-1}T_*H_b\zeta.$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:affiliated-transport" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:affiliated-transport">21</a> sends any invariant projection of $T_f$ to an invariant projection of $e^{-1}T_*$ having the same trace. Multiplication by the nonzero scalar $e^{-1}$ does not change invariant ranges. Theorem <a href="#thm:measurable" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:measurable">18</a> therefore excludes traces strictly between $0$ and $1$. This proves Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> for the arbitrary irrational $\theta$ fixed at the start.

The same continuous weight has a stronger spectral property, which we record with an elementary proof. This is the zero-determinant case of Zhu, Fang, and Shi (2017, Theorem 2.3) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[zfs](#ref-zfs)]. We include the truncated-logarithm argument, which uses uniform averaging under irrational rotation.

<a id="stmt-cor-quasinilpotent"></a>
<div id="cor:quasinilpotent" class="corollary">

**Corollary 22**. *The constructed operator $T_f$ is nonzero and quasinilpotent.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-quasinilpotent"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-quasinilpotent</summary>

*Proof.* For $M>0$, the function $\ell_M(x)=\log\max(f(e^{2\pi i x}),e^{-M})$ is continuous. Its integrals decrease to $-\infty$ by <a href="#eq:zero-log-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:zero-log-integral">[eq:zero-log-integral]</a>. Irrational rotation averages of a continuous function converge uniformly to its integral: this follows for Fourier monomials from their geometric sums and then for continuous functions by uniform trigonometric approximation. Hence, for any $a>0$, first choosing $M$ with $\int_X\ell_M\,d\mu<-2a$ and then taking $n$ sufficiently large gives $$\sup_{x\in X}\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}f(e^{2\pi i\sigma^k x})
 \le\sup_{x\in X}\exp\left(\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\ell_M(\sigma^k x)\right)
 \le e^{-an}.$$ The expression on the left is $\|T_f^n\|$, because the power is a unitary $U^n$ times multiplication by that continuous product. The spectral-radius formula yields radius zero, since $a$ was arbitrary. Finally $T_f\ne0$ because $f>0$ almost everywhere. ◻

</details>

<a id="p:sec:product"></a>
### A product-rotation realization

The circle proof replaces independent coordinates by divisible characters and ordered digits. On a countable product of circles, independence is available directly. This gives a different sampling result: prescribed triangular laws in any finite set of coordinates are weak limits of positive orbit-pair measures, with both marginal densities uniformly bounded in $L^2$. We prove that result and use it to construct a second concrete model for the convolution obstruction.

All notation in this appendix is local. Let $$X=\mathbb T^{\mathbb N},\qquad \mathbb T=\mathbb R/\mathbb Z,$$ with product Haar probability $\mu$ and its compact metrizable product topology. Coordinates are represented in $[0,1)$, and $x\le y$ means $x_i\le y_i$ for every $i$. This order is Borel; its graph need not be closed at the circle cuts. For a sequence of angles $\theta=(\theta_i)$, write $\sigma_\theta x=x+\theta$. On $\mathcal H=L^2(X,\mu;\ell^2(\mathbb Z))$, put $$(\pi(a)\zeta)(x)_k=a(\sigma_\theta^k x)\zeta(x)_k,
 \qquad (U\zeta)(x)=S\zeta(x),\qquad Se_k=e_{k+1},$$ and let $M=W^*(U,\pi(L^\infty(X)))$.

<a id="stmt-p-thm-model"></a>
<div id="p:thm:model" class="theorem">

**Theorem 23** (Product realization). *There are angles $0<\theta_i<1$ and costs $c_i>0$, with $\sum_i\theta_i<\infty$ and $1,\theta_1,\theta_2,\ldots$ rationally independent, such that the measurable weight $$f(x)=\exp\left(-\sum_{i\ge1}c_i\lfloor x_i+\theta_i\rfloor\right)$$ is positive almost everywhere and has $\int_X\log f\,d\mu=-\infty$. The algebra $M$ is a type $\mathrm{II}_1$ factor with separable predual, and $T=U\pi(f)$ is nonzero and norm-quasinilpotent, with $\Delta(T)=0$. Every projection $p\in M$ satisfying $(1-p)Tp=0$ is $0$ or $1$.*

*Moreover, $|T|$ has pure-point spectral type. The abelian algebra generated by any finite family of polar conjugates $U^n|T|U^{-n}$ is atomic, whereas $W^*(U^n|T|U^{-n}:n\in\mathbb Z)$ is diffuse.*

</div>

The last statement concerns spectral type: it does not require every point of the spectrum of $|T|$ to be an eigenvalue. In particular, zero can be a spectral accumulation point although $f>0$ almost everywhere. As in the principal construction, the absence of invariant projections refers to the specified containing factor. For instance, the subspaces $L^2(X,\mu;\overline{\operatorname{span}}\{e_k:k\ge m\})$ are invariant under the represented forward weighted shift, but their constant-field projections do not satisfy the covariance required for membership in $M$.

The proof has three product-specific tasks. We first choose angles for which the finite-coordinate sampling theorem holds. We then pass the invariant projection identity from those measures to biased product couplings. Finally we verify the positive-event and tail-attenuation hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#lem:conditional-convolution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:conditional-convolution">17</a>. The field decomposition, compact-set transfer, and scalar sampling and density estimates are the lemmas already proved in the main text; no part of this appendix is needed for the prescribed-angle theorem.

<a id="p:sec:scales"></a>
#### Scales and independent angles

We choose the finite polynomial accuracy before choosing each new block. This leaves enough freedom to obtain both the sampling estimates and the threshold crossings needed by Lemma <a href="#lem:conditional-convolution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:conditional-convolution">17</a>. All choices in this subsection are independent of any invariant projection.

Partition $\mathbb N=\{1,2,\ldots\}$ into consecutive finite blocks $J_j$ of length $m_j$, writing $$d_0=0,\qquad d_j=\sum_{s\le j}m_s,\qquad
 J_j=\{d_{j-1}+1,\ldots,d_j\}.$$ The cutoff $K_j$ will specify the finite coefficient window in the contradiction, while $D_j$ and $\varepsilon_j$ will give the coefficient bound and the product lower bound from Lemma <a href="#lem:polynomial-coefficient" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:polynomial-coefficient">7</a>. After these are fixed, $L_j$ specifies the required decay of the omitted terms; the logarithmic drop $C_j$ and the block length $m_j$ are chosen to achieve that decay while retaining uniform bounds on the coupling marginals.

We now choose these parameters. Put $\kappa_j=1/(100j)$ and start with $K_1=100$ and $G_1=1$. At stage $j$, the numbers $K_j,G_j$ and all parameters of earlier blocks have already been fixed. Make the following choices in the stated order.

1.  Define $$D_j=\exp\left((K_j+1)\sum_{s<j}C_sm_s\right).$$ Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:polynomial-coefficient" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:polynomial-coefficient">7</a> with $K=K_j$, $D=D_j$, and $\delta=1/j$ to choose $\varepsilon_j>0$. This is possible because $D_j\ge1\ge1/j$. Consequently, <a id="p:eq:poly-lower"></a>$$\label{p:eq:poly-lower}
     \max_{0\le n\le2K_j}|(AB)_{-n}|\ge\varepsilon_j$$ whenever $A,B$ are supported in $[-K_j,K_j]$, their coefficients have modulus at most $D_j$, and $|A_0|,|B_0|\ge1/j$.

<a id="p:eq:epsilon-choice"></a>
2.  Choose $L_j\ge1$, increasing with $j$, so large that $$\label{p:eq:epsilon-choice}
     (2K_j+1)3\exp(-L_j/10000)\le\frac{\varepsilon_j}{j}.$$ Set $C_j=\max\{L_j,K_j^2\}$, and choose a finite $G_{j+1}\ge1$ such that <a id="p:eq:G-choice"></a>$$\label{p:eq:G-choice}
     \kappa_{j+1}\sqrt{G_{j+1}}\ge L_j.$$

<a id="p:eq:scale-conditions"></a>
3.  Choose an integer $m=m_j$ sufficiently large, and set $$K_{j+1}=\left\lceil m^{(3/4)m}\right\rceil,$$ subject to $$\label{p:eq:scale-conditions}
     \begin{gathered}
     m\ge\max\{K_j,2^j,4G_j,100\},\qquad d_{j-1}\le m/100,\\
     \kappa_jm^{1/40}\ge L_j,\qquad
     G_{j+1}K_{j+1}<m^{(4/5)m}.
     \end{gathered}$$

Every choice is finite and feasible. Indeed, the first two steps do not depend on the new $m_j$. All but the final requirement in <a href="#p:eq:scale-conditions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:scale-conditions">[p:eq:scale-conditions]</a> are lower bounds on $m$. For the final one, the already fixed number $G_{j+1}$ satisfies $$\frac{G_{j+1}\lceil m^{(3/4)m}\rceil}{m^{(4/5)m}}
 \le 2G_{j+1}m^{-m/20}\longrightarrow0.$$ There is therefore no upper restriction conflicting with the lower bounds.

<a id="p:eq:scale-consequences"></a>
Write <a id="p:eq:N-and-widths"></a>$$\label{p:eq:N-and-widths}
 N_j=m_jK_j,\qquad a_j=G_j/m_j,\qquad b_j=m_j^{-19/20}.$$ The construction gives the useful consequences $$\label{p:eq:scale-consequences}
 \begin{gathered}
 m_j\ge2^j,\qquad d_j\le1.01m_j,\qquad N_j\le m_j^2,\\
 K_{j+1}>2K_j,\qquad
 \sum_{s>j}\frac1{K_s}\le\frac2{K_{j+1}},\qquad
 \frac1{m_j}\le a_j,b_j\le\frac14.
 \end{gathered}$$ For example, $m_j\ge100$ and $m_j\ge K_j$ imply $K_{j+1}\ge m_j^{(3/4)m_j}>2m_j\ge2K_j$, and the reciprocal estimate follows by summing a geometric majorant. Also $m_{j+1}\ge K_{j+1}>m_j$, so each of $m_j,K_j,N_j$ is increasing. The upper bound for $a_j$ follows from $m_j\ge4G_j$; the other width bounds follow from $G_j\ge1$ and $m_j\ge100$.

Use the coordinate order on $X$ defined above.

On a separate product probability space choose independent random angles $\theta_i$, with $\theta_i$ uniform on $[1/N_j,2/N_j]$ when $i\in J_j$, and let $$\sigma_\theta x=x+\theta\quad\text{in }X.$$ For every realization, <a id="p:eq:angle-summability"></a>$$\label{p:eq:angle-summability}
 \sum_{i=1}^\infty\theta_i
 \le2\sum_{j=1}^\infty\frac{m_j}{N_j}
 =2\sum_{j=1}^\infty\frac1{K_j}<\infty.$$ Almost surely $1,\theta_1,\theta_2,\ldots$ are rationally independent. To see this, multiply any proposed finite rational relation by a common denominator. If some angle has a nonzero integer coefficient, conditioning on all other angles leaves one prescribed value of an atomless random variable, an event of probability zero. A relation with no angle term cannot hold unless its constant term is zero. There are only countably many nontrivial finite integer relations.

For a rationally independent realization the rotation is ergodic and free. Indeed, the characters $$x\longmapsto\exp\left(2\pi i\sum_i v_ix_i\right),
 \qquad v_i\in\mathbb Z,\quad v_i=0\text{ for all but finitely many }i,$$ form an orthonormal basis of $L^2(\mu)$: finite-coordinate functions are dense, and the usual Fourier bases are complete on each finite torus. Translation multiplies this character by $\exp(2\pi i\sum_i v_i\theta_i)$, which equals $1$ only for the constant character. Hence the invariant $L^2$ functions are constant, which is ergodicity. Also $n\theta_1\notin\mathbb Z$ for every $n\in\mathbb Z\setminus\{0\}$, so $\sigma_\theta^n x\ne x$ for every $x\in X$.

<a id="stmt-p-prop-phases"></a>
<div id="p:prop:phases" class="proposition">

**Proposition 24** (Phase ranges). *Almost surely, for every sufficiently large $j$ the following hold simultaneously for all integers $k$ in the indicated ranges, with $m=m_j$:*

1.  *If $G_jK_j\le |k|\le N_j/4$, then every $i\in J_j$ satisfies $$1-\{k\theta_i\}\in[a_j,1-a_j].$$*

2.  *If $N_j/4\le |k|\le m^{(4/5)m}$, then at least $m/20$ coordinates $i\in J_j$ satisfy $$1-\{k\theta_i\}\in[b_j,1-b_j].$$*

*The first assertion in fact holds at every stage and for every realization of the angles in their prescribed intervals.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-p-prop-phases"><summary>Proof of stmt-p-prop-phases</summary>

*Proof.* In the first range, put $t=|k|\theta_i$. The angle bounds give $$a_j=G_j/m_j\le t\le1/2.$$ For $k>0$ the threshold is $1-t$, and for $k<0$ it is $t$. Both belong to $[a_j,1-a_j]$ because $a_j\le1/4$.

For the second range, the uniform law of $k\theta_i$ on an interval of length $|k|/N_j$ has, after reduction modulo one, a density bounded by $$1+N_j/|k|\le5.$$ This follows by counting the integer translates covering a point: the covering multiplicity of an interval of length $r$ is at most $r+1$. The statement also applies to negative $k$ by reflection. Thus a given coordinate misses $[b_j,1-b_j]$ with probability at most $10b_j$. If fewer than $m/20$ coordinates satisfy the assertion, more than $19m/20$ coordinates miss the interval. Independence over $i\in J_j$ and a union bound over the possible sets of misses show that, for a fixed $k$, this failure probability is at most $2^m(10b_j)^{(19/20)m}$ for large $m$. A further union bound over the at most $2m^{(4/5)m}$ nonzero integers in the range gives $$\mathbb P(\text{failure at stage }j)
 \le2m^{(4/5)m}2^m(10b_j)^{(19/20)m}
 =\exp\left(-\frac{41}{400}m\log m+O(m)\right).$$ These probabilities are summable because $m_j\ge2^j$. The first Borel–Cantelli Lemma now gives the second assertion for all sufficiently large $j$, simultaneously for every integer in each stated range. ◻

</details>

We retain the random angles until the sampling construction below has supplied one further probability-one property.

<a id="p:sec:sampling"></a>
#### Sampling ordered orbit pairs

We next approximate prescribed changes in finitely many coordinates by positive orbit moves. Both endpoint distributions must remain controlled: this will allow the projection identity to pass from orbit pairs to the couplings used in the product construction. The scalar estimates of Lemma <a href="#lem:scalar-sampling-estimates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:scalar-sampling-estimates">6</a> apply coordinate by coordinate; we prove here the growing-product estimate and the ordered weak limit.

The coordinate order is only Borel in the product-circle topology: already in one circle, the ordered pairs $(1/2,1-1/n)$ converge to the unordered pair $(1/2,0)$. Thus the closed-relation hypothesis in the classical coupling theorem (Edwards 1978, Corollary 3.2) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Edwards1978](#ref-Edwards1978)] does not apply directly. The result below also requires approximating measures supported on positive orbit pairs, with both marginal densities controlled; those are additional conclusions proved by the sampling construction.

For a finite set $I\subset\mathbb N$, define a finite Borel measure $\lambda_I$ on $X\times X$ as follows. In each coordinate $i\in I$ use the unnormalized triangular measure $$\mathbf 1_{\{0\le x_i<y_i<1\}}\,dx_i\,dy_i,$$ and in each other coordinate use the law of $(x_i,x_i)$ for uniform $x_i\in[0,1)$. Take the product over all coordinates. Equivalently, one can first normalize the finitely many triangular factors and then multiply the resulting probability measure by $2^{-|I|}$. In particular, $$\lambda_I(X\times X)=2^{-|I|}.$$ Its two marginal densities relative to $\mu$ are, respectively, $\prod_{i\in I}(1-x_i)$ and $\prod_{i\in I}y_i$; they are bounded by $1$. The measure is carried by $x\le y$.

<a id="stmt-p-prop-sampling"></a>
<div id="p:prop:sampling" class="proposition">

**Proposition 25** (Ordered orbit-pair sampling). *With probability one in the choice of $\theta$, the following holds for every finite $I\subset\mathbb N$. There are positive finite Borel measures $\eta_{I,l}$ on $X\times X$ converging weakly to $\lambda_I$, each carried by ordered pairs of the form $$(x,y)=(x,\sigma_\theta^n x)$$ with $n$ in a finite set of positive integers. Both marginals of $\eta_{I,l}$ have $L^2(\mu)$ densities, and their $L^2$ norms are bounded uniformly in $l$. The bound may depend on $I$ and on the fixed realization of $\theta$. Weak convergence here means convergence against every continuous function on the compact space $X\times X$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-p-prop-sampling"><summary>Proof of stmt-p-prop-sampling</summary>

*Proof.* We give the construction and its estimates before choosing the probability-one event. The notation $O(\cdot)$ in this proof has absolute constants, uniform in the coordinate, the selected powers, and the interval endpoints involved in a rectangle test.

We average many separated orbit powers so that both marginal densities approach those of an ideal model with independent uniform phases. The coordinate weights impose triangular laws on $I$ and concentrate near the diagonal elsewhere, while their marginal bounds remain uniform in the growing product. A final restriction to ordered tail coordinates removes asymptotically negligible mass.

<a id="pm-h-34"></a>
###### Separated powers and phase discrepancy

Take $l$ sufficiently large that $m_l\ge200$, as required by the scalar lemma. At stage $l$ write $m=m_l$, $d=d_l$, and set $$Q=m^{(7/10)m},\qquad H=m^{300},\qquad R=N_lm^{1000}.$$ For all sufficiently large $l$ we can choose, deterministically, a set $\mathcal N_l$ of integers in $[Q/2,Q]$ such that <a id="p:eq:sampling-separation"></a>$$\label{p:eq:sampling-separation}
 \begin{gathered}
 P_l:=|\mathcal N_l|\ge m^{0.69m},\\
 |vn+v'n'|>R\quad
 \left(\begin{array}{l}
 n,n'\in\mathcal N_l,\ n\ne n',\\
 v,v'\in\mathbb Z,\ |v|,|v'|\le H,\ (v,v')\ne(0,0)
 \end{array}\right).
 \end{gathered}$$ Indeed, given a previously selected $n$, a fixed frequency pair with $v'\ne0$ forbids an interval of possible $n'$ of length $2R/|v'|\le2R$, hence at most $2R+2$ integers. There are $O(H^2)$ frequency pairs. Since $N_l\le m^2$, each selection therefore excludes at most $O(m^{1602})$ integers. When $v'=0$ and $v\ne0$, the inequality is automatic because $n\ge Q/2>R$ eventually; likewise, a zero first frequency creates no obstruction. The constraints are symmetric in the selected powers, since the full set of frequency pairs is used. Greedy selection from the $\asymp Q$ available integers yields at least $cQ/m^{1602}\ge m^{0.69m}$ choices for large $m$.

<a id="p:eq:single-phase-density"></a>
<a id="p:eq:bv-discrepancy"></a>
<a id="p:eq:single-phase-test"></a>
For a coordinate $i\in J_s$ with $s\le l$, the angle $\theta_i$ is uniform on $[1/N_s,2/N_s]$. Apply the separated-phase part of Lemma <a href="#lem:scalar-sampling-estimates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:scalar-sampling-estimates">6</a> with $$N_0=N_s\le N_l=N\le m^2.$$ The powers just selected satisfy its separation hypothesis. Thus $t_n=\{n\theta_i\}$ has a density $\varphi_{i,n}$ satisfying $$\label{p:eq:single-phase-density}
 \|\varphi_{i,n}-1\|_\infty\le N_s/n\le N_l/n.$$ In particular, that density is at most two for large $l$. For distinct selected powers and functions $g,g'$ of bounded variation, the same lemma gives $$\label{p:eq:bv-discrepancy}
 \begin{split}
 \left|\mathbb E_\theta g(t_n)g'(t_{n'})
       -\left(\int_0^1g\right)\left(\int_0^1g'\right)\right|
 &\le c m^{-80}
   (\|g\|_\infty+\operatorname{Var}g)
   (\|g'\|_\infty+\operatorname{Var}g').
 \end{split}$$ No independence between $t_n$ and $t_{n'}$ is asserted. The imported estimate uses their Fourier coefficients and one-dimensional marginal densities, and so applies even when their joint law is singular. We shall also use the direct consequence of <a href="#p:eq:single-phase-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:single-phase-density">[p:eq:single-phase-density]</a> $$\label{p:eq:single-phase-test}
 \left|\mathbb E_\theta g(t_n)-\int_0^1g(t)\,dt\right|
 \le\frac{N_l}{n}\int_0^1|g(t)|\,dt.$$

<a id="pm-h-35"></a>
###### The weighted measures and both marginal orientations

Fix a finite $I$ and restrict to sufficiently large $l$ that $I\subset\{1,\ldots,d\}$. Set $$h=m^{-3/5},\qquad \Delta(s)=\min(s,1-s),\qquad \alpha=m^{-2}.$$ For $0\le s,t<1$ define <a id="p:eq:sampling-weights"></a>$$\label{p:eq:sampling-weights}
 w_i(s,t)=
 \begin{cases}
 \mathbf 1_{\{0<t<1-s\}},&i\in I,\\[1mm]
 \displaystyle\mathbf 1_{\{\Delta(s)\ge\alpha\}}
 \frac{\mathbf 1_{\{0<t<h\Delta(s)\}}}{h\Delta(s)},&i\notin I.
 \end{cases}$$ The second line is defined to be zero if $\Delta(s)<\alpha$. Let $\widetilde\eta_{I,l}$ be the average, over $n\in\mathcal N_l$, of the push-forward under $x\mapsto(x,\sigma_\theta^n x)$ of <a id="p:eq:untrimmed-weight"></a>$$\label{p:eq:untrimmed-weight}
 W_n(x)\,d\mu(x),\qquad
 W_n(x)=\prod_{i\le d}w_i(x_i,\{n\theta_i\}).$$ Each weight is bounded for fixed $l$, so these are finite positive measures. They are carried by pairs satisfying $x_i<y_i$ for $i\le d$, since $h\Delta(x_i)\le h(1-x_i)<1-x_i$ and $h<1$.

We will treat the marginals both without a test and after restriction to a cylinder rectangle $$\mathcal R=\{(x,y):x_i\in E_i^x,\ y_i\in E_i^y
                         \text{ for every }i\},$$ where the $E_i^x,E_i^y$ are intervals with rational endpoints and all but finitely many are the whole circle. Take $l$ large enough that all tested coordinates are at most $d$. A missing test is represented by using the whole circle for every interval. For the first and second marginal, respectively, define <a id="p:eq:oriented-weights"></a>$$\label{p:eq:oriented-weights}
 \begin{split}
 u_i^{(1)}(s,t)
 &=w_i(s,t)\mathbf 1_{E_i^x}(s)\mathbf 1_{E_i^y}(\{s+t\}),\\
 u_i^{(2)}(s,t)
 &=w_i(\{s-t\},t)\mathbf 1_{E_i^x}(\{s-t\})\mathbf 1_{E_i^y}(s).
 \end{split}$$ Changing variables $y=\sigma_\theta^n x$ preserves Haar measure. It follows that the two tested marginal densities are exactly the averages of the products of the corresponding $u_i^{(r)}$. There is no factor from coordinates past $d$, even in the second orientation, since their unweighted Haar law is translation invariant.

<a id="p:eq:weight-variation"></a>
We check the extra factors introduced by triangular laws and interval tests. Write $u_i$ for either orientation and put $$\bar u_i(s)=\int_0^1u_i(s,t)\,dt,\qquad
 A_i=\int_0^1\bar u_i(s)^2\,ds.$$ Uniformly in $s$, the coordinate, and the test intervals, we have $$\label{p:eq:weight-variation}
 \|u_i(s,\cdot)\|_\infty+
       \operatorname{Var}_t u_i(s,\cdot)
 =O(m^{13/5})=O(m^{10}).$$ For an untested triangular factor, the bound is $O(1)$: in the first orientation its support in $t$ is $0<t<1-s$, and in the second it is $0<t\le s$. The possible value at $t=s$ affects no integral. For the untested near-diagonal factors, Lemma <a href="#lem:scalar-sampling-estimates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:scalar-sampling-estimates">6</a> gives the bound $O(m^{13/5})$. To include the tests, in either orientation the extra factor is a constant in $\{0,1\}$ times an interval indicator translated or reflected modulo one. Its supremum is at most one and its variation is bounded by an absolute constant. The inequality $$\operatorname{Var}(ab)\le
 \|a\|_\infty\operatorname{Var}(b)
 +\|b\|_\infty\operatorname{Var}(a)$$ therefore proves <a href="#p:eq:weight-variation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:weight-variation">[p:eq:weight-variation]</a>, including all jumps at the test endpoints.

<a id="p:eq:one-coordinate-moments"></a>
The moment bounds needed below are $$\label{p:eq:one-coordinate-moments}
 0\le\bar u_i(s)\le2,\qquad
 0\le A_i\le1+O(h^2),\qquad
 \int_0^1\int_0^1u_i(s,t)^2\,ds\,dt
       =O(h^{-1}\log m).$$ For an untested triangular factor the two averages are exactly $1-s$ and $s$, respectively. In either orientation its total integral and squared integral are $1/2$, while $A_i=1/3$. For an untested near-diagonal factor, Lemma <a href="#lem:scalar-sampling-estimates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:scalar-sampling-estimates">6</a> gives $$\|\bar u_i\|_\infty\le2,\qquad
 A_i\le1+\frac{h^2}{(1-h)^2},\qquad
 \iint u_i(s,t)^2\,ds\,dt=\frac2h\log\frac1{2\alpha}.$$ Since tests only decrease the nonnegative factors and their averages, these statements imply <a href="#p:eq:one-coordinate-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:one-coordinate-moments">[p:eq:one-coordinate-moments]</a> for every tested factor. Their actual masses need not agree; in particular, the near-diagonal integral identities are not being imposed on the triangular factors or on the tested kernels. The quadratic error $O(h^2)$ is what keeps the product of the moment bounds controlled as the number of coordinates grows.

<a id="pm-h-36"></a>
###### The estimate in the growing product dimension

<a id="p:eq:sampling-L2"></a>
For either fixed orientation and a fixed rectangle test or no test, write $$\begin{aligned}
 q_n(z)&=\prod_{i\le d}u_i(z_i,\{n\theta_i\}),&
 Z_l(z)&=\frac1{P_l}\sum_{n\in\mathcal N_l}q_n(z),\\
 Z_l^0(z)&=\prod_{i\le d}\bar u_i(z_i),&
 A&=\prod_{i\le d}A_i=\|Z_l^0\|_2^2.
 \end{aligned}$$ We prove the estimate $$\label{p:eq:sampling-L2}
 \mathbb E_\theta\|Z_l-Z_l^0\|_{L^2(\mu)}^2
 \le O(dm^{-40})+\frac{(c h^{-1}\log m)^d}{P_l}.$$ The independence used here is independence across coordinates $\theta_i$. In a single coordinate two phases may still be dependent, as already accounted for in <a href="#p:eq:bv-discrepancy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:bv-discrepancy">[p:eq:bv-discrepancy]</a>.

For distinct $n,n'$, Tonelli’s Theorem, product Haar measure, and coordinate independence yield the exact factorization $$\mathbb E_\theta\langle q_n,q_{n'}\rangle
 =\prod_{i\le d}\int_0^1
   \mathbb E_\theta\bigl[
    u_i(s,\{n\theta_i\})u_i(s,\{n'\theta_i\})\bigr]\,ds.$$ By <a href="#p:eq:weight-variation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:weight-variation">[p:eq:weight-variation]</a> and <a href="#p:eq:bv-discrepancy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:bv-discrepancy">[p:eq:bv-discrepancy]</a>, its $i$th factor is $A_i+e_i$ with $|e_i|\le c m^{-50}$. Similarly, $$\mathbb E_\theta\langle q_n,Z_l^0\rangle
 =\prod_{i\le d}\int_0^1
       \bar u_i(s)\mathbb E_\theta u_i(s,\{n\theta_i\})\,ds.$$ Here <a href="#p:eq:single-phase-test" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:single-phase-test">[p:eq:single-phase-test]</a> and $\bar u_i\le2$ bound the error in each factor from $A_i$ by $4N_l/n=O(m^{-50})$. Thus both sorts of factors have the same stated error bound.

<a id="p:eq:sampling-product-error"></a>
The product errors do not require any lower bound on $A_i$. Telescoping gives $$\label{p:eq:sampling-product-error}
 \left|\prod_{i\le d}(A_i+e_i)-\prod_{i\le d}A_i\right|
 \le cdm^{-50}(1+ch^2+cm^{-50})^{d-1}
 =O(dm^{-50}),$$ because <a href="#p:eq:scale-consequences" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:scale-consequences">[p:eq:scale-consequences]</a> gives $d\le1.01m$, and hence $dh^2\le1.01m^{-1/5}$. In particular, $A\le(1+ch^2)^d=O(1)$.

For a self-pairing, use only the one-phase density bound at most $2$ and <a href="#p:eq:one-coordinate-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:one-coordinate-moments">[p:eq:one-coordinate-moments]</a>: $$\mathbb E_\theta\|q_n\|_2^2
 =\prod_{i\le d}\int_0^1
            \mathbb E_\theta u_i(s,\{n\theta_i\})^2\,ds
 \le(c h^{-1}\log m)^d.$$ To see the cancellation of all other main terms explicitly, expand the squared norm. The distinct-pair terms have total averaging weight $1-1/P_l$ and main term $(1-1/P_l)A$. The cross terms with $Z_l^0$ have main term $-2A$, and the final squared norm of $Z_l^0$ is $A$. Their sum is $-A/P_l\le0$. By <a href="#p:eq:sampling-product-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:sampling-product-error">[p:eq:sampling-product-error]</a>, their remaining contribution has absolute value $O(dm^{-50})$. The self-pairings have total weight $1/P_l$. Dropping $-A/P_l$ proves the slightly weaker bound <a href="#p:eq:sampling-L2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:sampling-L2">[p:eq:sampling-L2]</a>.

<a id="p:eq:sampling-summable"></a>
This estimate is summable in $l$. Indeed, $$\log_m (c h^{-1}\log m)^d
 \le1.01m\left(\frac35+\log_m(c\log m)\right)
 \le0.65m$$ for large $m$, whereas $P_l\ge m^{0.69m}$. Thus $$\label{p:eq:sampling-summable}
 \mathbb E_\theta\|Z_l-Z_l^0\|_2^2
 \le O(m^{-39})+m^{-0.04m},$$ whose sum is finite by $m_l\ge2^l$.

<a id="pm-h-37"></a>
###### One probability-one event and the weak limit

For each fixed finite $I$, orientation, and rational cylinder rectangle or absent test, Tonelli’s Theorem applied to <a href="#p:eq:sampling-summable" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:sampling-summable">[p:eq:sampling-summable]</a> shows that $$\sum_l\|Z_l-Z_l^0\|_2^2<\infty
 \quad\text{almost surely},$$ where only the sufficiently large admissible stages are included. In particular, $\|Z_l-Z_l^0\|_2\to0$. Finite subsets of $\mathbb N$ form a countable family, as do finite cylinder rectangles with rational endpoints, and there are only two orientations. Intersect these countably many probability-one events. For the remainder of the proof take any $\theta$ in this intersection.

Without a test, $Z_l$ is a marginal density of $\widetilde\eta_{I,l}$. Since $\|Z_l^0\|_2^2=A=O(1)$, the $L^2$ convergence just proved bounds both marginal norms uniformly in $l$. We may omit finitely many initial stages; alternatively, each of their densities is bounded for fixed $l$, so they can be included by enlarging the bound. The total masses of $\widetilde\eta_{I,l}$ are consequently uniformly bounded.

<a id="p:eq:sampling-rectangle-limit"></a>
For a fixed cylinder rectangle $\mathcal R$, using the first orientation with its test shows that $$\left|\widetilde\eta_{I,l}(\mathcal R)
       -\int_X Z_l^0\,d\mu\right|
 \le\|Z_l-Z_l^0\|_2\longrightarrow0.$$ The ideal integral has a direct product interpretation on the first $d$ coordinates. For $i\in I$, integration of $w_i(x_i,t)$ in $x_i,t$ gives exactly the triangular measure under $y_i=x_i+t$. For $i\notin I$, first take uniform $x_i$, discard $\Delta(x_i)<\alpha$, and, otherwise, take $t$ uniform in $(0,h\Delta(x_i))$, setting $y_i=x_i+t$. Before the discard this is a probability law (the value at the two endpoint values of $x_i$ may be defined arbitrarily), and $|y_i-x_i|\le h/2$. The total loss caused by all cutoffs is at most $$2d\alpha=2dm^{-2}\longrightarrow0,$$ by the union bound, with the finite triangular factors having mass at most one. Each fixed tested coordinate outside $I$ therefore converges to its uniform diagonal law. More explicitly, outside an $h$-neighborhood of a tested interval’s endpoints, membership of $y_i$ in that interval agrees with membership of $x_i$; the neighborhood has Lebesgue measure $O(h)$. Since a fixed rectangle tests only finitely many coordinates, all such errors vanish. Untested coordinates contribute only their masses, and the total cutoff estimate controls their product even as $d$ increases. It follows that $$\label{p:eq:sampling-rectangle-limit}
 \widetilde\eta_{I,l}(\mathcal R)\longrightarrow
                  \lambda_I(\mathcal R).$$

These limits imply weak convergence on $X\times X$. Here are the topological details. The boundaries of the interval rectangles have zero $\lambda_I$ measure, since every single-coordinate marginal of $\lambda_I$ is absolutely continuous. Every continuous function on the compact product can be uniformly approximated by a continuous function of finitely many coordinates: in a summable product metric, replace the tail coordinates by fixed points and use uniform continuity. On the resulting finite torus, a sufficiently fine rational grid gives a step function, a finite linear combination of rational rectangle indicators, with arbitrarily small uniform error. Thus <a href="#p:eq:sampling-rectangle-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:sampling-rectangle-limit">[p:eq:sampling-rectangle-limit]</a> and the uniform bound on total masses give convergence of integrals for every continuous function. In particular, $$\widetilde\eta_{I,l}\ \Longrightarrow\ \lambda_I.$$

<a id="pm-h-38"></a>
###### Trimming the tail to impose order everywhere

<a id="p:eq:sampling-tail-trim"></a>
The untrimmed measures now have the required weak limit and both marginal bounds. It remains to impose order past the first $d$ coordinates. Define $\eta_{I,l}$ by restricting $\widetilde\eta_{I,l}$ to the Borel set where $x_i\le y_i$ also for every $i>d$. For a fixed selected $n$, the weight $W_n$ depends only on the first $d$ coordinates, so the tail $x$-coordinates remain independent uniform coordinates under that summand, apart from multiplication by its total mass. For a uniform tail coordinate, the probability that $x_i>\{x_i+n\theta_i\}$ is exactly $\{n\theta_i\}\le n\theta_i$. Consequently, uniformly over $n\in\mathcal N_l$, the relative mass discarded from this summand is at most $$\label{p:eq:sampling-tail-trim}
 \begin{split}
 \sum_{i>d}n\theta_i
 &\le 2m^{(7/10)m}\sum_{s>l}\frac1{K_s}\\
 &\le\frac{4m^{(7/10)m}}{K_{l+1}}
 \le4m^{-m/20}\longrightarrow0.
 \end{split}$$ The union bound is valid for the countable tail by increasing finite unions. More precisely, the discarded mass of a summand equals its total mass times the Haar probability of its tail failure event, because the weight and that event depend on disjoint coordinates. This statement also holds for a summand of mass zero. Averaging over $n$ shows that the total discarded mass is bounded by <a href="#p:eq:sampling-tail-trim" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:sampling-tail-trim">[p:eq:sampling-tail-trim]</a> times the uniformly bounded untrimmed mass. Hence the difference of the two measures tends to zero in total variation and $\eta_{I,l}\Longrightarrow\lambda_I$.

Each trimmed marginal is a submeasure of the corresponding untrimmed marginal. Its Radon–Nikodym density is therefore nonnegative and bounded almost everywhere by the untrimmed density, so its $L^2$ norm cannot increase. Trimming preserves the finite set of positive orbit powers and now imposes order in every coordinate. All conclusions hold on the single probability-one event already constructed, for every finite $I$, proving the Proposition. ◻

</details>

We now fix one realization $\theta$ for which rational independence, Proposition <a href="#p:prop:phases" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:prop:phases">24</a>, and Proposition <a href="#p:prop:sampling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:prop:sampling">25</a> all hold, and henceforth write $\sigma=\sigma_\theta$. The scales and this rotation are fixed before any candidate invariant projection is introduced. For this fixed rotation, each sampling sequence has actual weak convergence against every continuous function; its validity is no longer restricted to the countable tests used to obtain the probability-one event. Thus later choices of continuous tests or compact sets depending on a candidate projection require no further intersection of events in the angle probability space.

<a id="p:sec:operator"></a>
#### The product factor and its weighted shift

We now turn the fixed rotation into an operator in a finite factor. The goal is to express invariance as an identity on ordered pairs, where the sampling result can act. Use the rotation $\sigma$ fixed at the end of Section <a href="#p:sec:sampling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:sec:sampling">7.2</a>, and write $\mathcal K=\ell^2(\mathbb Z)$. The operators $U,\pi(g)$ and their generated algebra $M$ are those defined at the beginning of the appendix. This is the usual regular representation of a crossed product by a measure-preserving transformation (Anantharaman and Popa, n.d., sec. 1.4 and Proposition 1.4.5); the class of operators $U\pi(f)$ is also considered in (Dykema and Schultz 2009, sec. 5) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[dykema-schultz](#ref-dykema-schultz)]. For completeness, we verify the properties of $M$ needed here.

<a id="stmt-p-lem-factor"></a>
<div id="p:lem:factor" class="lemma">

<a id="p:eq:covariance"></a>
**Lemma 26**. *The algebra $M$ is a factor of type $\mathrm{II}_1$ with separable predual. Its elements are essentially bounded measurable fields $H(x)\in\mathcal B(\mathcal K)$ satisfying $$\label{p:eq:covariance}
 H(\sigma x)=S^{-1}H(x)S\quad\text{almost everywhere},$$ and its faithful normal tracial state is $$\tau(H)=\int_X H(x)_{0,0}\,d\mu(x).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-p-lem-factor"><summary>Proof of stmt-p-lem-factor</summary>

*Proof.* Each generator commutes with scalar multiplication by any function of $x$. It also commutes with the unitary $(J\zeta)(x)=S\zeta(\sigma x)$. These commutation relations therefore hold for every $H\in M$. Lemma <a href="#lem:scalar-multiplier-fields" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:scalar-multiplier-fields">9</a> therefore represents $H$ by an essentially bounded measurable field $H(x)$, with products and adjoints computed fiberwise. Commutation with $J$ gives <a href="#p:eq:covariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:covariance">[p:eq:covariance]</a>.

Conversely, let $H$ be an essentially bounded measurable field satisfying <a href="#p:eq:covariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:covariance">[p:eq:covariance]</a>. All its integer-iterate covariance identities hold on one conull set. Put $h_r(x)=H(x)_{r,0}$ for $r\in\mathbb Z$. Then $$H(x)_{k+r,k}=h_r(\sigma^k x),$$ so the $r$th matrix diagonal of $H$ is $U^r\pi(h_r)\in M$. To recover $H$ from these diagonals, use the constant-field unitaries $D_t e_k=e^{2\pi i kt}e_k$, $t\in\mathbb T$. The strong integral $$H_N=\int_0^1\mathcal F_N(t)D_tHD_t^*\,dt
 =\sum_{|r|<N}\left(1-\frac{|r|}{N}\right)U^r\pi(h_r)$$ belongs to $M$, where $\mathcal F_N$ is the Fejér kernel. The map $t\mapsto D_tHD_t^*$ is strongly continuous and uniformly bounded. The positivity, unit integral and concentration at zero of $\mathcal F_N$ imply $H_N\to H$ strongly. The strong closure of $M$ therefore proves the converse field characterization.

The displayed state is the vector state associated with the constant field $e_0$, hence is normal. If $H\geq0$ and $\tau(H)=0$, its $00$ entry vanishes almost everywhere. Covariance then forces every diagonal entry to vanish almost everywhere. Positivity, or the inequality $|H_{k,l}|^2\leq H_{k,k}H_{l,l}$, implies $H=0$. Thus the state is faithful. For $H,H'\in M$, covariance and the change of variable $z=\sigma^r x$ give $$\int_X H(x)_{0,r}H'(x)_{r,0}\,d\mu(x)
 =\int_X H'(z)_{0,-r}H(z)_{-r,0}\,d\mu(z).$$ The sums over $r$ are absolutely integrable by the row and column $\ell^2$ bounds and Cauchy–Schwarz. Summing proves $\tau(HH')=\tau(H'H)$.

The rotation $\sigma$ is ergodic by Section <a href="#p:sec:scales" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:sec:scales">7.1</a>. If $H$ is central, it commutes with $\pi(x\mapsto e^{2\pi i x_1})$. In each fiber this last operator has distinct diagonal entries, because $\theta_1$ is irrational; hence $H(x)$ is diagonal. Commutation with $U$ makes all its diagonal entries equal. Equation <a href="#p:eq:covariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:covariance">[p:eq:covariance]</a> and ergodicity make their common value constant almost everywhere. The center of $M$ is therefore scalar. The algebra contains the infinite-dimensional abelian algebra $\pi(L^\infty(X))$, so this finite factor is not a matrix algebra and is of type $\mathrm{II}_1$. Finally, the predual of a von Neumann algebra on a separable Hilbert space is a quotient of the separable trace class, and is separable. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-40"></a>
##### Weights and cocycles

<a id="p:eq:weights"></a>
<a id="p:eq:cocycle"></a>
Using representatives $x_i\in[0,1)$ for the circle coordinates, put $$\label{p:eq:weights}
 f(x)=\exp\!\left(-\sum_{j\geq1}C_j
                    \sum_{i\in J_j}\lfloor x_i+\theta_i\rfloor\right),
 \qquad T=U\pi(f).$$ Since $\sum_i\theta_i\leq2\sum_jK_j^{-1}<\infty$, the first Borel–Cantelli Lemma shows that only finitely many terms in this sum are nonzero almost everywhere. Thus $0<f\leq1$ almost everywhere and $T$ is a nonzero bounded operator. For every $k\in\mathbb Z$ define $$F_k(x)=\exp\!\left(-\sum_{j\geq1}C_j
                    \sum_{i\in J_j}\lfloor x_i+k\theta_i\rfloor\right).$$ For a fixed $k$, all sufficiently late coordinates have $|k|\theta_i<1$, and the probability that their floor is nonzero is $|k|\theta_i$. Borel–Cantelli again proves that $F_k$ is positive and finite almost everywhere. Taking a countable intersection and all its $\sigma$-translates gives an invariant Borel set $X_0$ of full measure on which all these formulas hold and $$\label{p:eq:cocycle}
 F_0=1,\qquad F_{k+n}(x)=F_n(\sigma^k x)F_k(x)
 \quad(k,n\in\mathbb Z).$$ Indeed, the identity follows coordinatewise from $\lfloor x_i+(k+n)\theta_i\rfloor
 =\lfloor x_i+k\theta_i\rfloor
  +\lfloor\{x_i+k\theta_i\}+n\theta_i\rfloor$. Extend each $F_k$ to be positive and finite on $X\setminus X_0$; its values there will never affect an integral.

<a id="p:eq:ratio"></a>
For $x,y\in X_0$ with $x\leq y$ coordinatewise, set $$\label{p:eq:ratio}
 R_{x,y}(k)=\frac{F_k(y)}{F_k(x)}\leq1,\qquad k\in\mathbb Z.$$ The inequality holds for negative as well as positive $k$, since the floor function is nondecreasing. More precisely, each coordinate $i\in J_s$ with <a id="p:eq:threshold-crossing"></a>$$\label{p:eq:threshold-crossing}
 x_i<1-\{k\theta_i\}\leq y_i$$ contributes a factor $e^{-C_s}$ to this ratio. All other coordinates contribute either $1$ or another such factor.

<a id="stmt-p-lem-quasinilpotent"></a>
<div id="p:lem:quasinilpotent" class="lemma">

**Lemma 27**. *The weight satisfies $\int_X\log f\,d\mu=-\infty$ and $\Delta(T)=0$. The operator $T$ is nonzero and norm-quasinilpotent: $\lim_{n\to\infty}\|T^n\|^{1/n}=0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-p-lem-quasinilpotent"><summary>Proof of stmt-p-lem-quasinilpotent</summary>

*Proof.* For $n\geq1$, direct multiplication and <a href="#p:eq:cocycle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:cocycle">[p:eq:cocycle]</a> give $T^n=U^n\pi(F_n)$. All the floors defining $F_n$ are nonnegative. For each fixed $q$, the estimate $\lfloor x_i+n\theta_i\rfloor\geq n\theta_i-1$ on the first $q$ blocks therefore gives $$\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|T^n\|^{1/n}
 \leq \exp\!\left(-\sum_{s\leq q}C_s
                              \sum_{i\in J_s}\theta_i\right)
 \leq \exp\!\left(-\sum_{s\leq q}\frac{C_s}{K_s}\right).$$ Since $C_s\geq K_s^2$, the last expression tends to zero as $q\to\infty$. Also, Tonelli’s Theorem and the same angle bounds give $$-\int_X\log f\,d\mu
 =\sum_s C_s\sum_{i\in J_s}\theta_i
 \ge\sum_s\frac{C_s}{K_s}=\infty.$$ Since $|T|=\pi(f)$ and the trace on $\pi(L^\infty(X))$ is integration against $\mu$, the determinant formula gives $\Delta(T)=0$. Positivity of $f$ almost everywhere ensures $T\ne0$. ◻

</details>

The uniform norm estimate also gives $\sigma(T)=\{0\}$ and Brown measure $\delta_0$.

<a id="pm-h-41"></a>
##### From orbit identities to triangular laws

The sampled measures now allow us to use Lemma <a href="#lem:compact-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:compact-transfer">11</a>. The order supplies its uniform cap, and the two marginal $L^2$ bounds supply uniform absolute continuity.

<a id="stmt-p-prop-pair-identity"></a>
<div id="p:prop:pair-identity" class="proposition">

<a id="p:eq:pair-identity"></a>
**Proposition 28**. *Suppose $p\in M$ is a projection and $(1-p)Tp=0$. For every finite $I\subset\mathbb N$, $$\label{p:eq:pair-identity}
 (1-p(y))\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}_{k\in\mathbb Z}\bigl(R_{x,y}(k)\bigr)p(x)=0
 \quad\text{for }\lambda_I\text{-almost every }(x,y).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-p-prop-pair-identity"><summary>Proof of stmt-p-prop-pair-identity</summary>

*Proof.* Fix this $p$ and a Borel version of its projection field. We may shrink $X_0$ to a common invariant Borel set of full measure on which covariance, the cocycle identities, and all the following countably many power identities hold. Invariance of $\mathop{\mathrm{ran}}p(x)$ under $T(x)$ gives invariance under $T(x)^n$, for each $n\geq1$. Applying $S^{-n}$ and using covariance yields <a id="p:eq:orbit-inclusion"></a>$$\label{p:eq:orbit-inclusion}
 (1-p(\sigma^n x))
 \mathop{\mathrm{diag}}_{k\in\mathbb Z}\bigl(F_n(\sigma^k x)\bigr)p(x)=0.$$ Division by $F_n(x)>0$ changes the diagonal entries to $F_k(\sigma^n x)/F_k(x)$ by <a href="#p:eq:cocycle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:cocycle">[p:eq:cocycle]</a>. Proposition <a href="#p:prop:sampling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:prop:sampling">25</a> gives ordered orbit-pair measures converging to $\lambda_I$ with uniformly $L^2$-bounded marginal densities. Their first marginals are absolutely continuous, so the countable collection of orbit identities holds almost everywhere on each approximant. The marginals of $\lambda_I$ are absolutely continuous. The uniform $L^2$ bounds give uniform absolute continuity by Cauchy–Schwarz. Orderedness and <a href="#p:eq:ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:ratio">[p:eq:ratio]</a> give the common cap $B=1$ on every ratio, under both the approximants and the limit. Lemma <a href="#lem:compact-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:compact-transfer">11</a> now proves <a href="#p:eq:pair-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:pair-identity">[p:eq:pair-identity]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="p:eq:strict-diagonal"></a>
For later use, the common strict-diagonal lemma applies here: the base is ergodic, the integral trace is faithful, and $f>0$ almost everywhere. Thus, for any projection $p\in M$ with $0<p<1$ and $(1-p)Tp=0$, Lemma <a href="#lem:positive-diagonal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:positive-diagonal">10</a> gives $$\label{p:eq:strict-diagonal}
 0<r(x):=p(x)_{0,0}<1\quad\text{for almost every }x.$$

<a id="p:sec:couplings"></a>
#### Biased couplings of product coordinates

The measures $\lambda_I$ give the pair identity when only finitely many coordinates are replaced. We now construct ordered probability laws that give every block opportunities to cross its thresholds. Their endpoint densities remain uniformly bounded in $L^2(\mu)$, and the crossing estimates remain valid after either entire endpoint is fixed. These are the properties needed for the convolution obstruction.

An ordered coupling with both marginals equal to Haar measure would give $x=y$ almost surely: in every coordinate, $y_i-x_i\ge0$ and $\mathbb E(y_i-x_i)=0$. We therefore allow a controlled change in the endpoint marginals. Fix a block index $j\ge1$. For $i\in J_s$, write $m=m_s$ and set <a id="p:eq:rho-definition"></a>$$\label{p:eq:rho-definition}
