 \rho_s(w)=\kappa_s\sqrt{w/m},\qquad w=a_s,b_s.$$ Choose the coordinate pair $(x_i,y_i)$ by the following mixture:

- For each of the two labelled widths $w=a_s,b_s$, include a top component of probability $\rho_s(w)$. In this component $x_i$ and $y_i$ are independent uniforms on $[0,1-w/2)$ and $[1-w/2,1)$, respectively.

- For each labelled width $w=a_s,b_s$, include a bottom component of probability $\rho_s(w)$, with independent uniforms on $[0,w/2)$ and $[w/2,1)$, respectively.

- If $s=j$, include a tagged component of probability $1/m$, with independent uniforms on $[0,1/m)$ and $[1-1/m,1)$, respectively.

- With the remaining probability <a id="p:eq:diagonal-probability"></a>$$\label{p:eq:diagonal-probability}
    q_{j,s}=1-2\rho_s(a_s)-2\rho_s(b_s)
                    -\mathbf 1_{\{s=j\}}/m,$$ choose $x_i=y_i$ uniformly in $[0,1)$.

The width labels remain distinct even if their numerical values agree. The scale bounds imply <a id="p:eq:width-bounds"></a>$$\label{p:eq:width-bounds}
 \frac1m\le a_s,b_s\le\frac14,
 \qquad
 \frac{\rho_s(w)}{w}=\frac{\kappa_s}{\sqrt{mw}}
       \le\kappa_s\le\frac1{100}.$$ Since $m\ge100$, it follows that $q_{j,s}\ge1-\kappa_s-1/m\ge0.98$.

Make the choices independently over all coordinates. More precisely, let $(\Omega_j,\mathbb P_j)$ be the countable product of the spaces carrying the mixture label and its coordinate pair. Let $x,y:\Omega_j\to X$ be the endpoint maps, and let $\nu_j$ be the law of $(x,y)$ on $X^2$. The labels, including the tags, are thus retained as events on $\Omega_j$. These are the actual product coordinates of the endpoints. Each component is supported on $x_i\le y_i$, so $x\le y$ almost surely.

<a id="stmt-p-lem-coupling-marginals"></a>
<div id="p:lem:coupling-marginals" class="lemma">

**Lemma 29** (Marginals and conditional crossings). *Put <a id="p:eq:uniform-product-density"></a>$$\label{p:eq:uniform-product-density}
 H^2=\exp\left(20\left(2\sum_{s\ge1}\kappa_s^2+1\right)\right)<\infty.$$ For every $j\ge1$, the law just constructed has the following properties.*

1.  *Every one-coordinate endpoint density is at most $10$. Both marginals of $\nu_j$ have $L^2(\mu)$ densities of norm at most $H$. The same bound holds for the product law formed by any deterministic choice of the $x$-marginal or the $y$-marginal at each coordinate.*

<a id="p:eq:crossing-given-x"></a>
<a id="p:eq:crossing-given-y"></a>
2.  *Conditional on $x$, the coordinates $y_i$ are independent. Conditional on $y$, the coordinates $x_i$ are independent. If $i\in J_s$, $w=a_s$ or $b_s$, and $u\in[w,1-w]$, then $$\begin{aligned}
      \mathbb P_j(x_i<u\le y_i\mid x)&\ge\rho_s(w)/10
           &&\text{on }\{x_i<u\},\label{p:eq:crossing-given-x}\\
      \mathbb P_j(x_i<u\le y_i\mid y)&\ge\rho_s(w)/10
           &&\text{on }\{y_i\ge u\}.
           \label{p:eq:crossing-given-y}
     
    \end{aligned}$$ The assertions hold almost surely, simultaneously for any specified countable family of such thresholds.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-p-lem-coupling-marginals"><summary>Proof of stmt-p-lem-coupling-marginals</summary>

*Proof.* For a top or bottom component of width $w$, each endpoint interval has length at least $w/2$, so its density is at most $2/w$. A tagged endpoint density is at most $m$. Consequently either one-coordinate marginal density $h_i$ satisfies $$0<q_{j,s}\le h_i
 \le1+4\sum_{w=a_s,b_s}\frac{\rho_s(w)}w+\mathbf 1_{\{s=j\}}
 \le2+8\kappa_s<10.$$ Throughout these sums the two widths are counted with their labels.

<a id="p:eq:uniform-absolute-continuity"></a>
If $v$ is the uniform density on an interval of length at least $w/2$, then $\int(v-1)=0$ and $\|v-1\|_2^2\le2/w$. The corresponding bound for a tagged interval is $m$. Subtracting $1$ from $h_i$ expresses it as the sum of four weighted mean-zero perturbations and, when $s=j$, one tagged perturbation. Cauchy–Schwarz for these at most five summands gives <a id="p:eq:marginal-energy"></a>$$\label{p:eq:marginal-energy}
 \begin{split}
 \|h_i-1\|_2^2
 &\le20\sum_{w=a_s,b_s}\frac{\rho_s(w)^2}{w}
                    +5\frac{\mathbf 1_{\{s=j\}}}{m_s}\\
 &\le20\left(\frac{2\kappa_s^2}{m_s}
                    +\frac{\mathbf 1_{\{s=j\}}}{m_s}\right).
 \end{split}$$ This estimate holds for either endpoint. Summing over the $m_s$ coordinates of every block gives the bound $20(2\sum_s\kappa_s^2+1)$ for every deterministic selection of endpoint marginals. Each selected coordinate density is nonnegative and has integral one, and the coordinate laws are independent. Thus Lemma <a href="#lem:product-density-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:product-density-bound">13</a> supplies the asserted $L^2$ density with norm at most $H$. In particular, for each such product law $\eta$ and every Borel set $A\subseteq X$, $$\label{p:eq:uniform-absolute-continuity}
 \eta(A)\le H\mu(A)^{1/2}.$$

<a id="p:eq:conditional-kernel"></a>
To verify the assertions after conditioning on an entire endpoint, we describe the conditional kernels. At coordinate $i\in J_s$, let $\ell$ run through the replacement labels, including the tag when present. Write $\pi_\ell$ for its mixture probability and $u_\ell,v_\ell$ for the normalized interval densities of its two endpoints. The marginal densities in this coordinate are $$h_i^x(t)=q_{j,s}+\sum_\ell\pi_\ell u_\ell(t),
 \qquad
 h_i^y(t)=q_{j,s}+\sum_\ell\pi_\ell v_\ell(t).$$ They are strictly positive. A conditional law of $y_i$ given $x_i=t$ is $$\label{p:eq:conditional-kernel}
 K_i^x(t,dz)=\frac{q_{j,s}}{h_i^x(t)}\delta_t(dz)
       +\sum_\ell\frac{\pi_\ell u_\ell(t)}{h_i^x(t)}
                              v_\ell(z)\,dz.$$ The reverse kernel $K_i^y$ interchanges $u_\ell$ and $v_\ell$. Independence of the coordinate pairs shows, first for cylinder tests, that $\bigotimes_i K_i^x(x_i,\cdot)$ is a conditional law of $y$ given $x$. The monotone class theorem extends the defining identity to all Borel tests on $X^2$. The corresponding product of reverse kernels is a conditional law of $x$ given $y$. These formulas prove both assertions of conditional independence.

If $x_i=t<u\le1-w$, then $t\in[0,1-w/2)$, where the top component of width $w$ has $x_i$-density $(1-w/2)^{-1}\ge1$. Its whole $y_i$ interval lies above $u$. Since $h_i^x\le10$, its contribution to <a href="#p:eq:conditional-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:conditional-kernel">[p:eq:conditional-kernel]</a> is at least $\rho_s(w)/10$. This proves <a href="#p:eq:crossing-given-x" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:crossing-given-x">[p:eq:crossing-given-x]</a>. If $y_i=t\ge u\ge w$, the bottom component has $y_i$-density $(1-w/2)^{-1}\ge1$ at $t$, and its whole $x_i$ interval lies below $u$. The reverse kernel therefore proves <a href="#p:eq:crossing-given-y" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:crossing-given-y">[p:eq:crossing-given-y]</a>. Intersecting the full-measure sets for any specified countable family of thresholds gives the final assertion. ◻

</details>

It remains to establish the pair identity under these probability laws. We first replace a finite prefix of $x$ by coordinates of $y$. The resulting law is dominated by a finite sum of the triangular laws $\lambda_I$, but its diagonal tail retains the biased $x$-distribution. Keeping that tail density explicit permits the transfer to infinitely many replacements.

<a id="stmt-p-prop-biased-identity"></a>
<div id="p:prop:biased-identity" class="proposition">

<a id="p:eq:biased-identity"></a>
**Proposition 30** (The identity under the biased couplings). *Let $p\in M$ be an orthogonal projection satisfying $(1-p)Tp=0$ in the product model. For every $j\ge1$, $$\label{p:eq:biased-identity}
 (1-p(y))\operatorname{diag}_{k\in\mathbb Z}\bigl(R_{x,y}(k)\bigr)p(x)=0
 \qquad\text{for }\nu_j\text{-almost every }(x,y).$$ Moreover, $0<R_{x,y}(k)\le1$ for all $k\in\mathbb Z$ almost surely.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-p-prop-biased-identity"><summary>Proof of stmt-p-prop-biased-identity</summary>

<a id="p:eq:finite-prefix-domination"></a>
*Proof.* Fix $j$. For $D\ge1$, define $$y_i^{(D)}=
 \begin{cases}
  y_i,&i\le D,\\
  x_i,&i>D,
 \end{cases}$$ and let $\beta_D$ be the law of $(x,y^{(D)})$ under $\mathbb P_j$. We claim that $$\label{p:eq:finite-prefix-domination}
 \beta_D\ll\sum_{I\subseteq\{1,\ldots,D\}}\lambda_I.$$ Condition on a pattern $c$ of mixture labels in the first $D$ coordinates. There are finitely many patterns. Let $I(c)$ consist of the coordinates carrying replacement labels. At each $i\in I(c)$ the conditioned pair has density $$r_{c,i}(t,z)=
 \frac{\mathbf 1_{U_{c,i}}(t)\mathbf 1_{V_{c,i}}(z)}
 {|U_{c,i}|\,|V_{c,i}|}$$ relative to the unnormalized triangular measure $\mathbf 1_{\{0\le t<z<1\}}\,dt\,dz$. Here $U_{c,i},V_{c,i}$ are the two intervals of the selected component. Their lengths are positive and their rectangle is supported on $t<z$. The remaining prefix coordinates are uniformly diagonal.

For the diagonal tail, let $g$ be the full $x$-marginal density from Lemma <a href="#p:lem:coupling-marginals" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:lem:coupling-marginals">29</a>. Write $\mu=\mu_{\le D}\otimes\mu_{>D}$ and define <a id="p:eq:tail-density"></a>$$\label{p:eq:tail-density}
 g_D(z)=\int g(u,z)\,d\mu_{\le D}(u),
 \qquad
 \|g_D\|_{L^2(\mu_{>D})}\le\|g\|_{L^2(\mu)}\le H.$$ Fubini identifies $g_D$ as the density of $x_{>D}$, and Jensen’s inequality proves the norm bound. Independence across coordinates ensures that conditioning on $c$ leaves this tail law unchanged. Therefore the conditional law of $(x,y^{(D)})$ has density <a id="p:eq:conditioned-prefix-density"></a>$$\label{p:eq:conditioned-prefix-density}
 g_D(x_{>D})\prod_{i\in I(c)}r_{c,i}(x_i,y_i)$$ with respect to $\lambda_{I(c)}$. This follows on cylinder sets from the coordinate construction, and hence on all Borel sets. The rectangle factors form a finite product and are bounded. The tail factor is integrable and need not equal one. Summing the conditional laws over the finitely many patterns proves <a href="#p:eq:finite-prefix-domination" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:finite-prefix-domination">[p:eq:finite-prefix-domination]</a>.

Proposition <a href="#p:prop:pair-identity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:prop:pair-identity">28</a> now gives the pair identity $\beta_D$-almost everywhere. To pass to the limit, the first marginal of $\beta_D$ is the $x$-marginal of $\nu_j$, while its second marginal uses the $y$-marginals in the first $D$ coordinates and the $x$-marginals thereafter. Lemma <a href="#p:lem:coupling-marginals" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:lem:coupling-marginals">29</a> and <a href="#p:eq:uniform-absolute-continuity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:uniform-absolute-continuity">[p:eq:uniform-absolute-continuity]</a> therefore give uniform absolute continuity of both marginal families, and absolute continuity of the two marginals of $\nu_j$.

On the common probability space, $y^{(D)}\to y$ in the product topology. Since $X^2$ is compact metrizable, bounded convergence against continuous functions proves $\beta_D\Rightarrow\nu_j$. Every one of these laws is carried by ordered pairs. Their absolutely continuous marginals put both endpoints in the common conull set on which the cocycles are finite and positive. The ordered-coordinate formula for the cocycle ratios consequently gives $0<R_{x,y^{(D)}}(k)\le1$ and $0<R_{x,y}(k)\le1$, simultaneously for every signed $k$. All hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#lem:compact-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:compact-transfer">11</a> are now satisfied, with the fixed cap $B=1$. That lemma transfers the identity from $\beta_D$ to $\nu_j$, proving <a href="#p:eq:biased-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:biased-identity">[p:eq:biased-identity]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="p:sec:obstruction"></a>
#### Verifying the convolution obstruction

It remains to exclude invariant projections. We verify the hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#lem:conditional-convolution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:conditional-convolution">17</a> for the product couplings. The infinite-sum estimate is already contained in that lemma; the task here is to bound the finite coefficients on an event of fixed positive probability and to attenuate every omitted index.

The preceding sections have constructed the factor $M$, its bounded operator $T$, and all the couplings, with the scales and angles fixed independently of any projection. Let $p\in M$ be an orthogonal projection satisfying $(1-p)Tp=0$, and suppose, toward a contradiction, that $0<p<1$. Use the common invariant Borel conull set for this fixed $p$ and the cocycles. Equation <a href="#p:eq:strict-diagonal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:strict-diagonal">[p:eq:strict-diagonal]</a> gives $$0<r(x)=p(x)_{0,0}<1\qquad\text{for }\mu\text{-almost every }x.$$ Every coupling used below has absolutely continuous endpoint marginals, so its endpoints belong to this set almost surely. All pointwise operator and cocycle statements below are understood on these sets. Only countably many indices occur, so they may be required simultaneously.

<a id="pm-h-44"></a>
###### Convolution identities.

<a id="p:eq:convolution-identity"></a>
<a id="p:eq:coefficient-sequences"></a>
<a id="p:eq:two-ratio-bounds"></a>
Set $$\xi(x)=p(x)e_0,\qquad \gamma(y)=(1-p(y))e_0.$$ These measurable vectors have norms at most one, with $\xi_0(x)=r(x)$ and $\gamma_0(y)=1-r(y)$. Fix $j$. By Proposition <a href="#p:prop:biased-identity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:prop:biased-identity">30</a>, the vector $$\zeta=\operatorname{diag}_{v\in\mathbb Z}\bigl(R_{x,y}(v)\bigr)\xi(x)$$ belongs to $\operatorname{ran}p(y)$ for $\nu_j$-almost every $(x,y)$. The same range is invariant under the fiber $T(y)$ and therefore under all its nonnegative powers. By the cocycle identity, the coordinate $n+v$ of $T(y)^n\zeta$ is $$F_n(\sigma^v y)\,R_{x,y}(v)\,\xi_v(x)
 =\frac{F_{n+v}(y)}{F_v(x)}\xi_v(x),\qquad n\ge0.$$ Taking its inner product with $\gamma(y)$ yields $$\label{p:eq:convolution-identity}
 0=\sum_{v\in\mathbb Z}\frac{F_{n+v}(y)}{F_v(x)}
                  \xi_v(x)\overline{\gamma_{n+v}(y)}
   =\sum_{v\in\mathbb Z}A_vB_{-n-v},\qquad n\ge0,$$ where $$\label{p:eq:coefficient-sequences}
 A_v=\frac{\xi_v(x)}{F_v(x)},\qquad
 B_v=F_{-v}(y)\overline{\gamma_{-v}(y)}.$$ These sequences themselves need not be square summable. Every convolution in <a href="#p:eq:convolution-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:convolution-identity">[p:eq:convolution-identity]</a>, however, is absolutely convergent. Indeed $F_{k+1}(z)=f(\sigma^k z)F_k(z)\le F_k(z)$ for every integer $k$, so for $n\ge0$, $$\label{p:eq:two-ratio-bounds}
 0<\frac{F_{n+v}(y)}{F_v(x)}
 \le\min\{R_{x,y}(v),R_{x,y}(n+v)\}\le1.$$ The first bound uses $F_{n+v}(y)\le F_v(y)$ and the second uses $F_v(x)\ge F_{n+v}(x)$. Cauchy–Schwarz now bounds the absolute sum by $\|\xi(x)\|_2\|\gamma(y)\|_2\le1$.

<a id="pm-h-45"></a>
###### An event of uniformly positive probability.

Write $K=K_j$ and $m=m_j$. On the augmented probability space $(\Omega_j,\mathbb P_j)$, define $E_j$ by the following conditions:

1.  For every $i\in J_j$, $x_i<1-2/m$ and $y_i>2/m$, and at least one coordinate in $J_j$ has the tagged label.

2.  For every $s>j$ and $i\in J_s$, $x_i<1-2K/N_s$ and $y_i>2K/N_s$.

3.  $r(x)\ge1/j$ and $1-r(y)\ge1/j$.

<a id="p:eq:positive-event"></a>
We claim that there is a constant $c_*>0$ such that $$\label{p:eq:positive-event}
 \mathbb P_j(E_j)\ge c_*
 \qquad\text{for all sufficiently large }j.$$ The constant can be chosen independently of $p$; the point beyond which the bound holds may depend on $p$.

<a id="p:eq:tag-event"></a>
<a id="p:eq:future-event-loss"></a>
At one coordinate of $J_j$, a tagged choice has probability $1/m$ and satisfies both of the first condition’s inequalities. By the one-coordinate density bound in Lemma <a href="#p:lem:coupling-marginals" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:lem:coupling-marginals">29</a>, the probability of a nontagged choice satisfying those inequalities is at least $$1-\frac1m-10\frac2m-10\frac2m=1-\frac{41}{m}.$$ The disjoint events specifying exactly one tag and admissible nontagged choices at the other coordinates consequently give the lower bound $$\label{p:eq:tag-event}
 m\frac1m\left(1-\frac{41}{m}\right)^{m-1}
 =\left(1-\frac{41}{m}\right)^{m-1}\ge e^{-82}.$$ Here $m\ge100$ and $\log(1-t)\ge-2t$ for $0\le t\le1/2$. The probability that the second condition fails is at most $$\label{p:eq:future-event-loss}
 40K\sum_{s>j}\frac{m_s}{N_s}
 =40K\sum_{s>j}\frac1{K_s}
 \le\frac{80K_j}{K_{j+1}}\longrightarrow0.$$ This is a countable union bound with a convergent sum. The intervals involved have length $2K/N_s$; all are less than one by the scale conditions.

The two endpoint losses in the third condition are bounded, using <a href="#p:eq:uniform-absolute-continuity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:uniform-absolute-continuity">[p:eq:uniform-absolute-continuity]</a>, by $$\mathbb P_j\{r(x)<1/j\}\le H\mu\{r<1/j\}^{1/2},
 \qquad
 \mathbb P_j\{1-r(y)<1/j\}\le H\mu\{1-r<1/j\}^{1/2}.$$ Both tend to zero because $0<r<1$ almost everywhere. Subtracting these losses and <a href="#p:eq:future-event-loss" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:future-event-loss">[p:eq:future-event-loss]</a> from <a href="#p:eq:tag-event" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:tag-event">[p:eq:tag-event]</a> proves <a href="#p:eq:positive-event" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:positive-event">[p:eq:positive-event]</a>, for example with $c_*=e^{-82}/2$. No independence between these conditions is needed.

<a id="pm-h-46"></a>
###### The finite coefficients.

<a id="p:eq:middle-coefficients"></a>
On $E_j$ the coefficients in <a href="#p:eq:coefficient-sequences" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:coefficient-sequences">[p:eq:coefficient-sequences]</a> satisfy $$\label{p:eq:middle-coefficients}
 |A_v|,|B_v|\le D_j\quad(|v|\le K),
 \qquad |A_0|,|B_0|\ge1/j.$$ To verify all signs, first let $0\le v\le K$. For $i\in J_j$, $v\theta_i\le2/m$, and for $i\in J_s$ with $s>j$, $v\theta_i\le2K/N_s$. The defining endpoint exclusions in $E_j$ therefore give $$\lfloor x_i+v\theta_i\rfloor=0,
 \qquad \lfloor y_i-v\theta_i\rfloor=0
 \quad(i\in J_s,\ s\ge j).$$ At each of the finitely many earlier coordinates, these floors have absolute value at most $K+1$, since $0<\theta_i<1$. Thus $$\frac1{F_v(x)}\le
 \exp\left((K+1)\sum_{s<j}C_sm_s\right)=D_j,
 \qquad F_{-v}(y)\le D_j,$$ which bounds $A_v$ and $B_v$. For the negative coefficient indices, monotonicity gives $F_{-v}(x)\ge1$ and $F_v(y)\le1$, and hence $$|A_{-v}|\le1,\qquad |B_{-v}|\le1\qquad(0\le v\le K).$$ Finally $A_0=r(x)$ and $B_0=1-r(y)$, proving <a href="#p:eq:middle-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:middle-coefficients">[p:eq:middle-coefficients]</a>.

Thus the finite-coefficient hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#lem:conditional-convolution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:conditional-convolution">17</a> hold with $D=D_j$ and $a=1/j$. We next verify its attenuation hypothesis outside this window.

<a id="pm-h-47"></a>
###### Attenuation at every omitted index.

<a id="p:eq:tag-attenuation"></a>
We next control $R_{x,y}(k)$ when $|k|>K$. If $K<|k|\le N_j/4$, choose a tagged coordinate in $J_j$ on $E_j$. At that coordinate $$\frac1m<|k|\theta_i\le\frac12,
 \qquad x_i<\frac1m,\qquad y_i\ge1-\frac1m.$$ For $k>0$ the threshold $1-\{k\theta_i\}$ lies in $[1/2,1-1/m)$; for $k<0$ it equals $|k|\theta_i$ and lies in $(1/m,1/2]$. The tagged pair crosses it in both cases. The coordinate contribution to the ratio gives $$\label{p:eq:tag-attenuation}
 R_{x,y}(k)\le e^{-C_j}\le e^{-L_j}
 \quad\text{on }E_j,\qquad K<|k|\le N_j/4.$$

Fix $j$ sufficiently large that the phase conclusions of Proposition <a href="#p:prop:phases" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:prop:phases">24</a> hold in every block $s\ge j$. For $|k|>N_j/4$ we can assign a block $J_s$ and a width $w$ so that either the second phase range is used with $s\ge j$, $w=b_s$, or the first phase range is used with $s>j$, $w=a_s$. Here is the coverage explicitly. Put $$\alpha_s=G_sK_s,\qquad \beta_s=N_s/4,
 \qquad Q_s=m_s^{(4/5)m_s}.$$ The scale conditions imply $$\alpha_s\le\beta_s<Q_s,\qquad \alpha_{s+1}<Q_s,
 \qquad Q_s\longrightarrow\infty.$$ Start with $[\beta_j,Q_j]$, where the second phase range applies. For each $s>j$, the first and second phase ranges together cover $[\alpha_s,Q_s]$, divided at $\beta_s$. Consecutive intervals overlap by $\alpha_{s+1}<Q_s$, so their union with the initial interval covers $[\beta_j,\infty)$. These are real intervals; in particular every integer magnitude in question is covered.

<a id="p:eq:tail-thresholds"></a>
<a id="p:eq:tail-crossing-attenuation"></a>
Choose one such assignment deterministically for each $k$, and let $I(k)\subseteq J_s$ be a deterministic list of qualifying coordinates. The choice can, for example, use the least available block and then increasing coordinate order. With $q=|I(k)|$ we have $$\label{p:eq:tail-thresholds}
 q\ge m_s/20,\qquad
 u_i:=1-\{k\theta_i\}\in[w,1-w]\quad(i\in I(k)).$$ All choices depend only on $j,k$ and the already fixed parameters and angles, not on the sampled endpoints. In the second phase range, $$m_s\rho_s(b_s)=\kappa_sm_s^{1/40}\ge L_s\ge L_j;$$ in the first phase range, where $s>j$, $$m_s\rho_s(a_s)=\kappa_s\sqrt{G_s}\ge L_{s-1}\ge L_j.$$ Also $C_s\ge L_s\ge L_j$ in either case. If any listed threshold is crossed, then $$\label{p:eq:tail-crossing-attenuation}
 R_{x,y}(k)\le e^{-C_s}\le e^{-L_j}.$$

Let $\mathcal B_k$ be the event that none of the thresholds in <a href="#p:eq:tail-thresholds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:tail-thresholds">[p:eq:tail-thresholds]</a> is crossed, and define $$a_k(x)=\#\{i\in I(k):x_i<u_i\},$$ $$\begin{aligned}
 H_k^{(1)}&=\mathcal B_k\cap\{a_k(x)\ge q/2\},\\
 H_k^{(2)}&=\mathcal B_k\cap\{a_k(x)<q/2\}.
 \end{aligned}$$ Given $x$, the eligible coordinates in $H_k^{(1)}$ are fixed. Conditional independence and <a href="#p:eq:crossing-given-x" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:crossing-given-x">[p:eq:crossing-given-x]</a> imply <a id="p:eq:first-bad-probability"></a>$$\label{p:eq:first-bad-probability}
 \mathbb P_j(H_k^{(1)}\mid x)
 \le(1-\rho_s(w)/10)^{q/2}
 \le\exp(-m_s\rho_s(w)/400)
 \le e^{-L_j/400}.$$ For the other orientation, orderedness gives on $\mathcal B_k$ the exact equivalence $$x_i<u_i\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad y_i<u_i
 \qquad(i\in I(k)).$$ Indeed the forward implication follows from the absence of a crossing, and the reverse implication from $x_i\le y_i$. Thus <a id="p:eq:second-bad-event"></a>$$\label{p:eq:second-bad-event}
 H_k^{(2)}
 =\mathcal B_k\cap
   \left\{\#\{i\in I(k):y_i\ge u_i\}>q/2\right\}
 \quad\text{almost surely}.$$ This is the same complementary conditioning split shown in Figure <a href="#fig:conditioning" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:conditioning">1</a>, with independent coordinates in place of digit labels. The eligible coordinates in this expression are determined by $y$. The reverse conditional independence and <a href="#p:eq:crossing-given-y" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:crossing-given-y">[p:eq:crossing-given-y]</a> give <a id="p:eq:second-bad-probability"></a>$$\label{p:eq:second-bad-probability}
 \mathbb P_j(H_k^{(2)}\mid y)\le e^{-L_j/400}.$$ These estimates hold under the full probability law $\mathbb P_j$, before any restriction to $E_j$. There are countably many $k$, so the conditional estimates can be taken simultaneously outside endpoint null sets. No union estimate over their bad events has been made.

For the near-window indices in <a href="#p:eq:tag-attenuation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:tag-attenuation">[p:eq:tag-attenuation]</a>, set both exceptional events equal to the empty set. Combining that estimate with <a href="#p:eq:tail-crossing-attenuation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:tail-crossing-attenuation">[p:eq:tail-crossing-attenuation]</a> and the two conditional bounds, we have, for every $|k|>K_j$, $$R_{x,y}(k)\le e^{-L_j}
       +\mathbf 1_{H_k^{(1)}}+\mathbf 1_{H_k^{(2)}}\quad\text{on }E_j,$$ where $$\mathbb P_j(H_k^{(1)}\mid x)\le e^{-L_j/400},\qquad
 \mathbb P_j(H_k^{(2)}\mid y)\le e^{-L_j/400}$$ hold under the full law. All inputs to Lemma <a href="#lem:conditional-convolution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:conditional-convolution">17</a> are now verified: the vectors, convolutions and ratio bounds are those of <a href="#p:eq:convolution-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:convolution-identity">[p:eq:convolution-identity]</a>–<a href="#p:eq:two-ratio-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:two-ratio-bounds">[p:eq:two-ratio-bounds]</a>; the event and finite coefficient bounds are <a href="#p:eq:positive-event" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:positive-event">[p:eq:positive-event]</a> and <a href="#p:eq:middle-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:middle-coefficients">[p:eq:middle-coefficients]</a>. For every sufficiently large fixed $j$, the lemma therefore gives <a id="p:eq:final-contradiction"></a>$$\label{p:eq:final-contradiction}
 \begin{split}
 c_*\varepsilon_j
 &\le (2K_j+1)\bigl(e^{-L_j}+2e^{-L_j/800}\bigr)\\
 &\le (2K_j+1)3e^{-L_j/10000}
 \le \frac{\varepsilon_j}{j},
 \end{split}$$ by <a href="#p:eq:epsilon-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:epsilon-choice">[p:eq:epsilon-choice]</a>. Since $\varepsilon_j>0$, cancellation at this fixed stage gives $c_*\le1/j$, impossible once $j>1/c_*$. No uniform lower bound on $(\varepsilon_j)$ is used. The scales, angles, operator and coupling laws were fixed before the arbitrary projection $p$; only its conull representatives and the eventual threshold in <a href="#p:eq:positive-event" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:positive-event">[p:eq:positive-event]</a> depended on $p$. This excludes every nontrivial invariant projection in $M$.

<a id="p:sec:structure"></a>
#### Finite atomic algebras and a diffuse limit

The model also distinguishes the spectral information in finitely many polar conjugates from that in the full orbit. This distinction follows from the explicit weight and the rotation, independently of the convolution argument.

<a id="stmt-p-prop-joint-structure"></a>
<div id="p:prop:joint-structure" class="proposition">

**Proposition 31**. *For the operator $T$ constructed above, its polar decomposition is $T=U|T|$, with $|T|=\pi(f)$ and $\ker|T|=\{0\}$. The spectral measure of $|T|$ is countably atomic. For every finite $F\subset\mathbb Z$, the abelian von Neumann algebra $$\mathcal A_F=W^*(U^n|T|U^{-n}:n\in F)$$ is atomic. The full algebra $$\mathcal A=W^*(U^n|T|U^{-n}:n\in\mathbb Z)$$ is diffuse.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-p-prop-joint-structure"><summary>Proof of stmt-p-prop-joint-structure</summary>

*Proof.* Since $f>0$ almost everywhere, $T^*T=\pi(f)^2$ has zero kernel, and $|T|=\pi(f)$. The unitary $U$ is therefore its polar factor. On the conull set where the sum in <a href="#p:eq:weights" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="p:eq:weights">[p:eq:weights]</a> has finite support, the value of $f$ is determined by a finite subset of the countable coordinate set. There are only countably many such subsets. Consequently $f$ takes values in a countable subset of $(0,1]$, up to a null set. Its nonnull level sets give countably many spectral projections whose sum is $1$, which proves the assertion about spectral type.

The representation convention gives $$U\pi(g)U^*=\pi(g\circ\sigma^{-1}).$$ For a finite $F$, the tuple $(f\circ\sigma^{-n})_{n\in F}$ has countable range on a common conull set. The nonnull joint level sets therefore give an atomic partition of the generated algebra $\mathcal A_F$. This includes $F=\varnothing$, when the generated unital algebra is scalar.

For the infinite family, $\mathcal A$ is an invariant abelian subalgebra of $\pi(L^\infty(X))$. The rotation is totally ergodic. Indeed, for any integer $q\ne0$ and any nonconstant finite-support character indexed by $v=(v_i)$, rational independence implies $q\sum_i v_i\theta_i\notin\mathbb Z$. The Fourier argument used in Section <a href="#p:sec:scales" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:sec:scales">7.1</a> thus proves ergodicity of $\sigma^q$.

Suppose $e$ were an atom of $\mathcal A$. Its integer translates under $\operatorname{Ad}U$ are atoms of equal positive trace. Distinct atoms in an abelian algebra are orthogonal, so only finitely many can occur. The orbit of $e$ is therefore finite, and for some positive integer $q$, $U^q eU^{-q}=e$. Because $e\in\pi(L^\infty(X))$, total ergodicity forces $e=1$. An abelian algebra whose identity is an atom is scalar. But $\mathcal A$ contains the nonconstant multiplication operator $\pi(f)$: a positive almost-everywhere constant would have finite logarithmic integral, contrary to Lemma <a href="#p:lem:quasinilpotent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:lem:quasinilpotent">27</a>. Hence $\mathcal A$ has no atoms, as claimed. ◻

</details>

The factor construction, the norm and determinant calculation, the exclusion of invariant projections, and Proposition <a href="#p:prop:joint-structure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:prop:joint-structure">31</a> prove Theorem <a href="#p:thm:model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="p:thm:model">23</a>, with $c_i=C_j$ for $i\in J_j$. The diffuseness statement is only about $\mathcal A$; it does not identify $\mathcal A$ with all of $\pi(L^\infty(X))$.

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "The two parts of the no-crossing event are small after conditioning on complementary endpoints. Each estimate controls the matching masked square sum in Lemma 17; no simultaneous avoidance over all indices is required.",
      "figure_id": "fig:conditioning",
      "pdf_page": 22,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:conditioning",
          "exact_quote": "The two parts of the no-crossing event are small after\nconditioning on complementary endpoints. Each estimate controls the\nmatching masked square sum in Lemma~\\ref{lem:conditional-convolution}; no\nsimultaneous avoidance over all indices is ",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[\n  box/.style={draw,rounded corners,align=center,text width=5.3cm,\n              minimum height=1.1cm,inner sep=6pt},\n  arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=2mm]},thick}]\n\\node[box,text width=8.2cm] (b) at (0,0)\n {$H_k$: no listed threshold is crossed\\\\\n  On this event, $u_i(x)<u_i^*\\iff u_i(y)<u_i^*$.};\n\\node[box] (x) at (-3.1,-2)\n {$H_k^{(1)}$: at least $|I|/2$ of the $u_i(x)$ lie below $u_i^*$};\n\\node[box] (y) at (3.1,-2)\n {$H_k^{(2)}$: more than $|I|/2$ of the $u_i(y)$ lie above or at $u_i^*$};\n\\node[box] (cx) at (-3.1,-4)\n {Condition on all of $x$;\\\\\n  top labels can cross.\\\\\n  $\\mathbb P_j(H_k^{(1)}\\mid x)\\le e^{-L_j/400}$};\n\\node[box] (cy) at (3.1,-4)\n {Condition on all of $y$;\\\\\n  bottom labels can cross.\\\\\n  $\\mathbb P_j(H_k^{(2)}\\mid y)\\le e^{-L_j/400}$};\n\\draw[arr] (b.south) -- (x.north);\n\\draw[arr] (b.south) -- (y.north);\n\\draw[arr] (x) -- (cx);\n\\draw[arr] (y) -- (cy);\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Flowchart diagram showing the decomposition of the event $H_k$ into two branches across three levels:\n- Top level: A single rectangular box containing:\n  \"$H_k$: no listed threshold is crossed\"\n  \"On this event, $u_i(x) < u_i^* \\iff u_i(y) < u_i^*$.\"\n- Middle level: Two rectangular boxes connected from the top box by branching arrows:\n  - Left branch arrow points to box: \"$H_k^{(1)}$: at least $|I|/2$ of the $u_i(x)$ lie below $u_i^*$\"\n  - Right branch arrow points to box: \"$H_k^{(2)}$: more than $|I|/2$ of the $u_i(y)$ lie above or at $u_i^*$\"\n- Bottom level: Two rectangular boxes connected by vertical downward arrows from the middle boxes:\n  - Left box below $H_k^{(1)}$: \"Condition on all of $x$; top labels can cross.\" and \"$\\mathbb{P}_j(H_k^{(1)} \\mid x) \\le e^{-L_j/400}$\"\n  - Right box below $H_k^{(2)}$: \"Condition on all of $y$; bottom labels can cross.\" and \"$\\mathbb{P}_j(H_k^{(2)} \\mid y) \\le e^{-L_j/400}$\""
    }
  ],
